CCFP(EM) Answer Keys and Model Answers

Scan and read: no access code needed. Numbered to match your printed study guide.

Part 2: SAMP Case Model Answers (140 Cases)

Each case below repeats its scenario stem, then gives each sub-question's model write-in answer (matching the real exam's short word/phrase/list response format) followed by a brief marking-guide style rationale.

Chapter 2: Cardiovascular Emergencies (16 Cases)

  1. Case 1: 62-Year-Old Man with Exertional Chest Pain

    A 62-year-old man presents with 45 minutes of central crushing chest pain that started while shovelling snow, radiating to his left arm, associated with diaphoresis and nausea. He has a history of hypertension, type 2 diabetes, and smoking. Vitals: BP 148/92, HR 98, RR 20, sat 96 percent on room air. He looks pale and uncomfortable but is not in acute distress. Heart and lung exam are unremarkable, no murmurs, no crackles.

    1. What is the single most important initial investigation?

    12-lead ECG within 10 minutes

    Rapid ECG is mandatory in any presentation concerning for ACS to identify STEMI candidates for immediate reperfusion.

    2. List three other investigations you would order.

    Troponin, CBC, electrolytes/creatinine (also chest x-ray)

    Serial troponin confirms myocardial injury while baseline bloods assess renal function before contrast or anticoagulation and rule out other causes.

    3. The ECG shows 3mm ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. What is your immediate management?

    ASA 160-325mg chewed, activate cath lab for primary PCI, second antiplatelet (ticagrelor or clopidogrel), heparin

    Inferior STEMI requires urgent reperfusion; primary PCI is preferred over fibrinolysis when available within guideline time targets.

    4. Before giving nitroglycerin, what must you assess?

    Right ventricular involvement (right-sided ECG leads), blood pressure, recent PDE5-inhibitor use

    Inferior STEMI can involve the right ventricle, making patients preload-dependent; nitrates can cause profound hypotension in this setting.

  2. Case 2: 28-Year-Old Woman with Sharp Chest Pain and Recent Travel

    A 28-year-old woman presents with sudden pleuritic right-sided chest pain and mild shortness of breath that began this morning. She returned two days ago from a 10-hour flight and takes an oral contraceptive. She denies cough, fever, or leg swelling. Vitals: BP 118/74, HR 112, RR 22, sat 94 percent on room air, afebrile. Lungs are clear, no calf tenderness or swelling.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Pulmonary embolism

    Pleuritic chest pain with tachycardia, hypoxia, recent immobility, and estrogen use is a classic PE presentation.

    2. Calculate her risk using a validated clinical decision rule and name it.

    Wells score, high pretest probability (score 4.5: PE is the leading diagnosis +3, heart rate >100 +1.5)

    PE-as-leading-diagnosis (+3) plus tachycardia (+1.5) already total 4.5, which is 'PE likely' on the Wells score without needing to count her flight. Her 10-hour flight two days ago is a recognized general VTE risk factor, but it does not meet the Wells score's specific immobilization criterion, which requires bedridden immobilization for at least 3 days or surgery within the previous 4 weeks. The Wells score standardizes pretest probability and determines whether to proceed to D-dimer or directly to imaging.

    3. What is the definitive investigation of choice?

    CT pulmonary angiogram

    CTPA is the gold-standard imaging test to confirm or exclude PE in patients with moderate-to-high pretest probability.

    4. CTPA confirms a segmental PE. She is hemodynamically stable. What is your initial treatment?

    Therapeutic anticoagulation (e.g., DOAC or LMWH)

    Hemodynamically stable PE is managed with anticoagulation alone; thrombolysis is reserved for massive PE with shock.

    5. What disposition is appropriate?

    Discharge home on anticoagulation with close follow-up if low-risk features (e.g., low PESI score)

    Select stable low-risk PE patients can be safely managed as outpatients, reducing unnecessary admission.

  3. Case 3: 45-Year-Old Man with Tearing Chest and Back Pain

    A 45-year-old man with poorly controlled hypertension presents with sudden severe tearing chest pain radiating to his back, maximal at onset. He appears anxious and diaphoretic. Vitals: BP 178/60 in the right arm, 142/58 in the left arm, HR 104, RR 20, sat 97 percent. A soft diastolic murmur is heard at the left sternal border. Pulses are diminished in the left radial artery compared to the right.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Acute aortic dissection

    Tearing pain radiating to the back, blood pressure differential between arms, and a new diastolic murmur strongly suggest proximal aortic dissection.

    2. What is the most appropriate imaging study?

    CT angiogram of the chest, abdomen, and pelvis

    CT angiography rapidly identifies dissection extent and involvement of branch vessels and is widely available emergently.

    3. What two classes of medication are first-line to control blood pressure and heart rate before any intervention?

    Beta-blocker (e.g., esmolol or labetalol), then vasodilator if needed

    Rate control must precede vasodilator therapy to avoid reflex tachycardia that increases aortic wall shear stress.

    4. The scan shows a Stanford type A dissection. What is the definitive management?

    Emergent cardiothoracic surgery consult for surgical repair

    Type A dissections involving the ascending aorta carry high mortality and require emergent surgical intervention, unlike type B which is often managed medically.

  4. Case 4: 35-Year-Old Man with Chest Pain After Viral Illness

    A 35-year-old previously healthy man presents with sharp, retrosternal chest pain that worsens with lying flat and deep inspiration, improved by sitting forward. He had a flu-like illness one week ago. Vitals: BP 122/76, HR 88, RR 18, sat 98 percent, afebrile. A friction rub is heard on cardiac auscultation. ECG shows diffuse concave ST elevation and PR depression.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Acute pericarditis

    Positional pleuritic chest pain, a friction rub, and diffuse ST elevation with PR depression following a viral illness are classic for pericarditis.

    2. What investigation should be done to assess for a complication, and what would you look for?

    Bedside echocardiogram, assess for pericardial effusion or tamponade

    Pericarditis can be complicated by effusion progressing to tamponade, which changes management and disposition.

    3. What is the first-line treatment?

    NSAID plus colchicine

    Combination NSAID and colchicine reduces symptom duration and recurrence risk compared to NSAID alone.

    4. Name one clinical clue that would make you reconsider the diagnosis in favour of myocardial infarction.

    Localized rather than diffuse ST elevation with reciprocal changes

    MI typically produces regional ST elevation with reciprocal depression in other leads, unlike the diffuse, non-territorial pattern of pericarditis.

  5. Case 5: 70-Year-Old Woman with Atypical Presentation of ACS

    A 70-year-old woman with diabetes presents with several hours of vague epigastric discomfort, nausea, and fatigue, without classic chest pain. She is diaphoretic and appears unwell. Vitals: BP 100/64, HR 102, RR 22, sat 94 percent. ECG shows new left bundle branch block. Initial troponin is markedly elevated.

    1. Why might this presentation be missed, and which patient groups are at risk of atypical ACS presentations?

    Diabetics, elderly, and women often present atypically with fatigue, nausea, or epigastric pain instead of classic chest pain

    Autonomic neuropathy in diabetes and atypical symptom reporting in elderly and female patients frequently delay recognition of ACS.

    2. How should a new LBBB with a clinical picture of ACS be managed?

    Assess modified Sgarbossa criteria; activate cath lab given elevated troponin and hemodynamic compromise

    New LBBB alone is no longer treated as an automatic STEMI equivalent since the 2013 ACCF/AHA guideline update, since most such patients do not have acute coronary occlusion. The modified Sgarbossa criteria (concordant ST elevation, concordant ST depression in V1-V3, or excessively discordant ST elevation) are now used to identify STEMI physiology in the presence of LBBB. Regardless of the LBBB debate, his markedly elevated troponin and hypotension independently justify emergent cath lab activation.

    3. List three components of initial medical management pending PCI.

    ASA, second antiplatelet, anticoagulation (heparin)

    Dual antiplatelet therapy plus anticoagulation reduces further thrombus propagation while awaiting definitive reperfusion.

    4. Given her hypotension, what medication should be used cautiously or avoided?

    Nitroglycerin

    Nitrates can worsen hypotension in a preload-dependent or hemodynamically borderline patient and should be used cautiously or withheld.

  6. Case 6: 55-Year-Old Man with Non-ST Elevation ACS

    A 55-year-old man with a history of hyperlipidemia presents with two episodes of exertional chest tightness over the past 24 hours, now resolved. Vitals are normal. ECG shows T-wave inversions in leads V2-V4. Initial troponin is mildly elevated and rises on repeat testing two hours later.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Non-ST elevation myocardial infarction (NSTEMI)

    A rising troponin with ischemic ECG changes and no ST elevation defines NSTEMI. Note the T-wave inversions in V2-V4 with resolved chest tightness also overlap with the classic Wellens' syndrome pattern (critical proximal LAD stenosis), which regardless of the NSTEMI label should prompt urgent cardiology involvement and avoidance of exercise stress testing given the risk of critical LAD occlusion.

    2. List two risk stratification tools used in this setting.

    TIMI score, GRACE score

    These validated scores estimate risk of further ischemic events and guide timing of invasive management.

    3. What is the initial antiplatelet and anticoagulant regimen?

    ASA plus P2Y12 inhibitor (ticagrelor or clopidogrel), plus anticoagulation (heparin)

    Dual antiplatelet therapy with anticoagulation is standard for NSTEMI to prevent thrombus progression while awaiting risk-stratified angiography.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition?

    Admit to cardiology/CCU for risk-stratified angiography

    NSTEMI patients require admission for monitoring and timing of coronary angiography based on risk category.

  7. Case 7: 48-Year-Old Man with Cocaine-Associated Chest Pain

    A 48-year-old man presents with severe chest pain after using cocaine two hours ago. He is agitated, diaphoretic, and hypertensive. Vitals: BP 190/110, HR 128, RR 24, sat 98 percent. ECG shows ST elevation in the anterior leads.

    1. What medication class is contraindicated in this presentation and why?

    Beta-blockers, due to risk of unopposed alpha stimulation causing worsened vasospasm and hypertension

    Beta-blockade in cocaine toxicity can leave alpha-adrenergic effects unchecked, worsening coronary vasospasm and hypertension.

    2. What is the first-line treatment for the agitation, hypertension, and chest pain in this patient?

    Benzodiazepines (e.g., lorazepam), plus nitroglycerin and ASA

    Benzodiazepines address sympathetic overdrive, reducing both agitation and cardiovascular strain, while nitrates treat vasospasm and ASA treats potential thrombosis.

    3. Despite benzodiazepines and nitrates, ST elevation persists. What is the next step?

    Activate cath lab for emergent coronary angiography/PCI

    Persistent STEMI despite treating vasospasm suggests true coronary thrombosis or refractory spasm requiring emergent angiography.

  8. Case 8: 58-Year-Old Woman with Chest Pain and Normal Coronaries Suspected

    A 58-year-old woman presents with sudden severe chest pain after the recent death of her husband. Vitals: BP 130/80, HR 96, RR 20, sat 97 percent. ECG shows anterior ST elevation and deep T-wave inversions. Troponin is elevated. Bedside echo shows apical ballooning with preserved basal contractility.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Takotsubo (stress) cardiomyopathy

    Emotional stressor, apical ballooning on echo, and ECG/troponin changes mimicking STEMI are classic for takotsubo cardiomyopathy.

    2. What must be excluded first before confirming this diagnosis?

    Acute coronary syndrome via coronary angiography

    Takotsubo cardiomyopathy is a diagnosis of exclusion; angiography must show no obstructive coronary disease to explain the wall motion abnormality.

    3. List two potential complications to monitor for.

    Cardiogenic shock, left ventricular outflow tract obstruction (also arrhythmia or mural thrombus)

    Takotsubo can be complicated by acute heart failure, dynamic outflow obstruction, and arrhythmias requiring close monitoring.

  9. Case 9: 72-Year-Old Woman with Rapid Irregular Palpitations

    A 72-year-old woman presents with two hours of palpitations and mild dyspnea. She is alert and talking. Vitals: BP 110/70, HR 152, RR 20, sat 96 percent. ECG shows an irregularly irregular narrow-complex rhythm with no discernible P waves.

    1. What is the rhythm diagnosis?

    Atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response

    Irregularly irregular narrow-complex tachycardia without P waves is diagnostic of atrial fibrillation.

    2. Since she is hemodynamically stable, what is your initial treatment strategy?

    Rate control with IV beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker

    Stable AF with RVR is managed first with rate control agents rather than immediate cardioversion.

    3. Before considering cardioversion, what key question about symptom duration must you determine and why?

    Onset time; cardioversion is only considered without anticoagulation workup if onset is clearly under 48 hours

    Unknown or greater than 48-hour duration of AF carries significant thromboembolic risk with cardioversion unless a TEE excludes atrial thrombus.

    4. What long-term therapy should be addressed before discharge?

    Anticoagulation based on CHADS-65 or CHA2DS2-VASc criteria

    Stroke risk stratification determines the need for long-term anticoagulation in patients with atrial fibrillation.

  10. Case 10: 19-Year-Old Man with Sudden Palpitations

    A 19-year-old healthy man presents with sudden-onset palpitations and lightheadedness while at rest, ongoing for 20 minutes. Vitals: BP 118/76, HR 190, RR 18, sat 99 percent. ECG shows a regular narrow-complex tachycardia at 190 with no visible P waves.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Supraventricular tachycardia (AVNRT)

    A regular narrow-complex tachycardia in a young healthy patient without visible P waves is classic for AV nodal reentrant tachycardia.

    2. What is your first-line non-pharmacologic treatment?

    Vagal maneuvers (modified Valsalva)

    Vagal maneuvers are safe first-line therapy that can terminate reentrant SVT by increasing vagal tone at the AV node.

    3. Vagal maneuvers fail. What medication and dosing strategy would you use next?

    Adenosine 6mg rapid IV push, followed by 12mg if unsuccessful

    Adenosine transiently blocks AV nodal conduction, terminating most reentrant SVTs, given as a rapid push with saline flush.

    4. The patient should be warned about what transient side effect of adenosine?

    Brief flushing, chest discomfort, and a feeling of impending doom

    Adenosine causes transient asystole-like sensations and chest tightness lasting seconds, which should be explained in advance to reduce patient distress.

  11. Case 11: 80-Year-Old Man with Syncope and Bradycardia

    An 80-year-old man is brought in after a witnessed syncopal episode. He is now awake but fatigued. Vitals: BP 90/60, HR 38, RR 16, sat 95 percent. ECG shows complete heart block with a wide escape rhythm at 36 beats per minute.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Third-degree (complete) AV block

    Complete dissociation between P waves and QRS complexes with a slow escape rhythm defines third-degree AV block.

    2. Given his hypotension, what is the immediate pharmacologic treatment?

    Atropine 1mg IV

    Atropine is first-line for symptomatic bradycardia, though it is often ineffective in infranodal complete heart block.

    3. Atropine is ineffective. What is the next step in management?

    Transcutaneous pacing, with dopamine or epinephrine infusion as a bridge

    When atropine fails, especially in infranodal block, transcutaneous pacing or chronotropic infusions are needed while arranging transvenous pacing.

    4. What definitive treatment does this patient require?

    Permanent pacemaker

    Complete heart block is generally an irreversible conduction disease requiring permanent pacemaker implantation.

  12. Case 12: 65-Year-Old Man with Wide Complex Tachycardia

    A 65-year-old man with a prior myocardial infarction presents with palpitations and chest discomfort. Vitals: BP 82/54, HR 178, RR 24, sat 93 percent, mottled skin and altered mental status. ECG shows a regular wide-complex tachycardia.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis and why?

    Ventricular tachycardia, given prior MI (scar-related reentry) and regular wide-complex morphology

    A regular wide-complex tachycardia in a patient with structural heart disease should be assumed VT until proven otherwise.

    2. Given his hemodynamic instability, what is the immediate treatment?

    Synchronized cardioversion

    Unstable wide-complex tachycardia requires immediate synchronized electrical cardioversion rather than pharmacologic treatment.

    3. If he were stable instead, what medication could be considered?

    Amiodarone IV

    Amiodarone is a reasonable first-line antiarrhythmic for stable monomorphic VT.

    4. After successful cardioversion, what investigation should be arranged to evaluate underlying etiology?

    Echocardiogram and cardiology referral for possible ICD evaluation

    Assessing ejection fraction and scar burden guides decisions about implantable defibrillator therapy for secondary prevention.

  13. Case 13: 78-Year-Old Man with Acute Dyspnea

    A 78-year-old man with known heart failure presents with acute severe shortness of breath overnight, worse lying flat. He is sitting upright, using accessory muscles. Vitals: BP 176/94, HR 110, RR 32, sat 86 percent on room air. Exam reveals bilateral crackles to mid-lung fields and jugular venous distension.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema

    Orthopnea, bilateral crackles, JVD, and hypertension in a known heart failure patient is classic for decompensated cardiogenic pulmonary edema.

    2. What is your first-line respiratory support intervention?

    Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP/CPAP)

    NIPPV reduces preload and afterload while improving oxygenation, decreasing need for intubation in acute pulmonary edema.

    3. Given his hypertension, what medication class should be given early?

    Nitrates (IV or sublingual nitroglycerin)

    Nitrates rapidly reduce preload and afterload, improving symptoms in hypertensive acute pulmonary edema.

    4. What diuretic would you also administer and why?

    IV furosemide, to promote diuresis and reduce volume overload

    Loop diuretics reduce intravascular volume, complementing nitrate-induced vasodilation in fluid overloaded heart failure.

  14. Case 14: 84-Year-Old Woman with Flash Pulmonary Edema and Hypotension

    An 84-year-old woman presents with sudden dyspnea and pink frothy sputum. Vitals: BP 88/58, HR 118, RR 34, sat 82 percent on room air. She is diaphoretic and cool peripherally. Bedside echo shows severely reduced ejection fraction.

    1. What is this presentation called and what does it imply about her hemodynamic status?

    Cardiogenic shock with pulmonary edema; wet and cold profile

    Hypotension with poor perfusion and pulmonary congestion indicates cardiogenic shock, a wet-and-cold hemodynamic profile.

    2. Why are nitrates and diuretics relatively contraindicated as first-line here?

    She is hypotensive, and preload reduction could worsen shock

    In cardiogenic shock, aggressive preload reduction can further drop cardiac output and blood pressure.

    3. What is the appropriate initial pharmacologic support?

    Inotropic support (e.g., dobutamine or norepinephrine)

    Inotropes improve contractility and perfusion in cardiogenic shock rather than reducing preload further.

    4. What consult is essential given likely need for mechanical circulatory support?

    Cardiology/cardiac critical care for consideration of mechanical circulatory support

    Refractory cardiogenic shock may require intra-aortic balloon pump or other mechanical support, warranting early specialist involvement.

  15. Case 15: 60-Year-Old Woman with Pulmonary Edema After Missed Dialysis

    A 60-year-old woman with end-stage renal disease on hemodialysis presents with dyspnea after missing her last two dialysis sessions. Vitals: BP 168/96, HR 104, RR 30, sat 89 percent. Exam shows bilateral crackles and pitting edema to the knees.

    1. What is the most likely cause of her pulmonary edema?

    Fluid overload from missed dialysis

    Volume overload from inadequate ultrafiltration in a dialysis-dependent patient is a common cause of pulmonary edema distinct from primary cardiac failure.

    2. What critical lab must be checked immediately given her renal failure?

    Serum potassium

    Missed dialysis risks life-threatening hyperkalemia, which must be identified and treated urgently.

    3. Why is furosemide unlikely to be effective as sole therapy?

    She is likely anuric or has minimal residual renal function

    Loop diuretics require functioning nephrons to work; end-stage renal disease patients often have negligible urine output.

    4. What is the definitive treatment for her fluid overload?

    Emergent hemodialysis

    Urgent dialysis directly removes excess fluid and corrects electrolyte abnormalities when the kidneys cannot respond to diuretics.

  16. Case 16: 50-Year-Old Man with Flash Pulmonary Edema and New Murmur

    A 50-year-old man presents with acute severe dyspnea and diaphoresis three days after an anterior myocardial infarction managed with PCI. Vitals: BP 92/60, HR 122, RR 34, sat 84 percent. A new harsh holosystolic murmur is heard at the left sternal border with a palpable thrill.

    1. What mechanical complication of MI is most likely?

    Ventricular septal rupture

    A new holosystolic murmur with thrill days after anterior MI is classic for post-infarction ventricular septal defect.

    2. What bedside investigation would confirm this?

    Echocardiogram

    Echocardiography with Doppler can directly visualize the septal defect and left-to-right shunt.

    3. What is the emergent management priority?

    Hemodynamic stabilization (inotropes, possible intra-aortic balloon pump) and emergent cardiac surgery consult

    Mechanical complications of MI carry high mortality and require urgent surgical repair alongside hemodynamic support.

Chapter 3: Respiratory Emergencies (8 Cases)

  1. Case 17: 22-Year-Old Woman with Severe Asthma Exacerbation

    A 22-year-old woman with known asthma presents with severe shortness of breath and wheeze after running out of her inhaler three days ago. She can only speak in short phrases. Vitals: BP 128/80, HR 122, RR 30, sat 90 percent on room air. Diffuse wheeze is heard bilaterally with poor air entry.

    1. List three first-line treatments to start immediately.

    Salbutamol and ipratropium via nebulizer, systemic corticosteroids, supplemental oxygen

    Combined bronchodilators with systemic steroids and oxygen are the core initial therapies for moderate to severe asthma exacerbations.

    2. What clinical sign would indicate impending respiratory failure?

    Decreased wheeze with reduced air entry (silent chest) and altered mental status

    A silent chest indicates minimal air movement despite severe obstruction, a red flag for imminent respiratory arrest rather than improvement.

    3. Despite maximal nebulized therapy, she remains in severe distress. What adjunct medication would you add?

    IV magnesium sulfate

    IV magnesium provides bronchial smooth muscle relaxation and is recommended as adjunct therapy in severe asthma unresponsive to initial treatment.

    4. She continues to deteriorate with rising CO2 and fatigue. What is your next step?

    Prepare for intubation; consider a trial of BiPAP first if tolerated

    Rising CO2 with fatigue signals impending respiratory failure requiring escalation to non-invasive or invasive ventilatory support.

  2. Case 18: 68-Year-Old Man with COPD Exacerbation

    A 68-year-old man with severe COPD on home oxygen presents with increased dyspnea, purulent sputum, and cough for three days. Vitals: BP 138/84, HR 104, RR 26, sat 88 percent on his home 2L nasal cannula, temp 37.6. He is using accessory muscles with pursed-lip breathing.

    1. List the three core treatments for an acute COPD exacerbation.

    Bronchodilators (salbutamol/ipratropium), systemic corticosteroids, antibiotics

    The classic triad of bronchodilators, steroids, and antibiotics (given purulent sputum) treats the inflammatory and infectious components of AECOPD.

    2. What target oxygen saturation should you aim for and why?

    88 to 92 percent, to avoid worsening hypercapnia from suppressed hypoxic respiratory drive

    Overzealous oxygenation in chronic CO2 retainers can blunt hypoxic drive and worsen hypercapnic respiratory failure.

    3. He develops worsening respiratory acidosis on blood gas despite medical therapy. What is the next step?

    Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP)

    BiPAP reduces work of breathing and improves ventilation in acute hypercapnic respiratory failure from COPD, reducing intubation rates.

    4. What chest x-ray finding would suggest an alternative or additional diagnosis requiring different management?

    Focal consolidation suggesting pneumonia

    A focal infiltrate suggests superimposed pneumonia, which may warrant broader antibiotic coverage and closer monitoring.

  3. Case 19: 16-Year-Old Boy with Exercise-Induced Wheeze

    A 16-year-old boy presents with wheeze and chest tightness that occurs consistently during soccer practice, resolving within 30 minutes of stopping activity. He has no daytime symptoms otherwise and normal exam today. Vitals are all normal.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Exercise-induced bronchoconstriction

    Symptoms specifically triggered by exertion and resolving with rest, without other daily symptoms, suggest exercise-induced bronchoconstriction as a form of asthma.

    2. What is the recommended pre-exercise treatment?

    Short-acting beta-agonist (salbutamol) 15 minutes before exercise

    Pretreatment with a SABA prevents bronchospasm triggered by exercise in most patients.

    3. What outpatient investigation would help confirm the diagnosis if it were unclear?

    Spirometry with exercise or bronchoprovocation challenge testing

    Objective lung function testing before and after exercise can demonstrate reversible airflow obstruction to confirm the diagnosis.

  4. Case 20: 74-Year-Old Man with COPD and Pneumothorax

    A 74-year-old man with severe emphysema presents with sudden worsening dyspnea and right-sided pleuritic chest pain. Vitals: BP 100/68, HR 116, RR 28, sat 84 percent on his home oxygen. Decreased breath sounds and hyperresonance are noted on the right side, trachea is midline.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Spontaneous pneumothorax (secondary to underlying emphysema)

    Sudden dyspnea with unilateral decreased breath sounds and hyperresonance in a patient with bullous emphysema is classic for secondary spontaneous pneumothorax.

    2. What bedside investigation can rapidly confirm the diagnosis?

    Point-of-care ultrasound (absent lung sliding) or chest x-ray

    Bedside ultrasound is fast and sensitive for pneumothorax, while chest x-ray remains a standard confirmatory test if the patient is stable enough.

    3. Given the size and his underlying lung disease, what is the definitive management if the pneumothorax is large?

    Chest tube (tube thoracostomy) insertion

    Secondary spontaneous pneumothorax in patients with underlying lung disease is generally managed with chest tube drainage rather than observation or simple aspiration.

    4. The trachea shifts to the left and he becomes hypotensive. What is the diagnosis and immediate action?

    Tension pneumothorax; immediate needle decompression or finger thoracostomy

    Tracheal deviation with hemodynamic instability indicates tension physiology requiring immediate decompression before imaging.

  5. Case 21: 30-Year-Old Woman with Progressive Dyspnea and Anemia

    A 30-year-old woman presents with progressive exertional dyspnea and fatigue over three weeks, with heavy menstrual bleeding. Vitals: BP 100/64, HR 118, RR 22, sat 97 percent. She appears pale with conjunctival pallor. Lungs are clear, heart sounds show a flow murmur.

    1. What is the most likely underlying cause of her dyspnea?

    Severe iron deficiency anemia from menorrhagia

    Progressive fatigue, pallor, tachycardia, and a flow murmur with a history of heavy menses point to significant anemia rather than a primary cardiopulmonary process.

    2. What investigation confirms the diagnosis and severity?

    Complete blood count (hemoglobin level)

    CBC quantifies the degree of anemia and guides urgency of transfusion versus outpatient iron repletion.

    3. Her hemoglobin returns at 58 g/L. What is the immediate management?

    Packed red blood cell transfusion

    Severe symptomatic anemia at this level with tachycardia warrants transfusion rather than oral iron alone.

    4. What consult should be arranged for the underlying cause of bleeding?

    Gynecology for evaluation and management of menorrhagia

    Addressing the source of chronic blood loss is necessary to prevent recurrence after acute correction of anemia.

  6. Case 22: 55-Year-Old Man with Dyspnea and Diffuse Crackles

    A 55-year-old man with no prior cardiac history presents with three days of fever, cough, and progressive dyspnea. Vitals: BP 112/70, HR 108, RR 26, sat 89 percent on room air, temp 38.9. Exam reveals focal crackles at the right lung base with dullness to percussion.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Community-acquired pneumonia

    Fever, focal crackles, dullness, and hypoxia with a subacute course point to bacterial pneumonia rather than cardiac causes.

    2. What severity score would help guide disposition?

    CURB-65

    CURB-65 incorporates confusion, urea, respiratory rate, blood pressure, and age to stratify pneumonia severity and need for admission.

    3. What investigations would you order to confirm the diagnosis and assess severity?

    Chest x-ray, CBC, blood cultures if admitted, lactate

    Chest x-ray confirms the infiltrate while blood work and cultures assess severity and guide antibiotic therapy in more unwell patients.

    4. What empiric antibiotic regimen would be appropriate for admission?

    Beta-lactam plus macrolide (e.g., ceftriaxone plus azithromycin)

    Combination therapy covers typical and atypical pathogens per community-acquired pneumonia guidelines for hospitalized patients.

  7. Case 23: 40-Year-Old Woman with Anxiety-Related Dyspnea

    A 40-year-old woman presents with acute shortness of breath, perioral tingling, and a sense of impending doom during a stressful work meeting. Vitals: BP 130/82, HR 100, RR 28, sat 99 percent on room air. She appears anxious but exam and ECG are normal.

    1. What is an important diagnosis to exclude before attributing this to anxiety?

    Pulmonary embolism (and other organic causes such as ACS)

    Panic attack is a diagnosis of exclusion; organic causes of dyspnea and tachypnea, particularly PE, must be reasonably ruled out first based on risk factors.

    2. What is the physiologic explanation for her perioral tingling?

    Hyperventilation causing respiratory alkalosis and hypocalcemia-like symptoms

    Hyperventilation lowers CO2, causing respiratory alkalosis that reduces ionized calcium and produces paresthesias.

    3. What is the appropriate management once organic causes are excluded?

    Reassurance, coaching controlled breathing, consider benzodiazepine for acute anxiety

    Once serious pathology is ruled out, panic attacks are managed supportively with breathing techniques and short-term anxiolytics if needed.

  8. Case 24: 70-Year-Old Man with Dyspnea and Barrel Chest, Found to Have Lung Mass

    A 70-year-old man with a 50 pack-year smoking history presents with progressive dyspnea and unintentional weight loss over two months, now with new hemoptysis. Vitals: BP 124/78, HR 96, RR 22, sat 93 percent. Chest x-ray shows a right upper lobe mass with hilar lymphadenopathy.

    1. What is the leading diagnosis?

    Lung cancer

    Weight loss, hemoptysis, and a mass with lymphadenopathy in a heavy smoker are highly concerning for malignancy.

    2. What immediate complication of his mass should be considered given his dyspnea, and what physical exam finding would suggest it?

    Superior vena cava obstruction; facial/upper extremity swelling and distended neck/chest wall veins

    Central masses can compress the SVC, causing venous congestion presenting with facial swelling and dilated collateral veins.

    3. What is the appropriate ED disposition and next steps?

    Admission for CT chest, tissue biopsy planning, and oncology referral

    A new suspicious lung mass requires staging imaging and biopsy coordination, generally best facilitated through admission or urgent oncology pathway referral.

Chapter 4: Neurological Emergencies (12 Cases)

  1. Case 25: 68-Year-Old Man with Sudden Left-Sided Weakness

    A 68-year-old man was last seen normal 90 minutes ago and now has sudden right facial droop, right arm weakness, and slurred speech. Vitals: BP 168/94, HR 88, RR 18, sat 97 percent, glucose 6.2. NIHSS is calculated at 12.

    1. What is the first investigation required before any treatment decision?

    Non-contrast CT head

    Non-contrast CT is mandatory to exclude hemorrhage before considering thrombolysis or thrombectomy.

    2. CT shows no hemorrhage. What treatment should be considered given the time window?

    IV tenecteplase or alteplase (thrombolysis)

    He is within the standard window for thrombolytic therapy with no contraindication identified, making him a reperfusion candidate.

    3. Given his NIHSS and likely large vessel involvement, what additional imaging and treatment should be arranged?

    CT angiogram to assess for large vessel occlusion, and consider mechanical thrombectomy

    High NIHSS suggests a large vessel occlusion, which may benefit from endovascular thrombectomy in addition to thrombolysis.

    4. List two absolute contraindications to thrombolysis you would screen for.

    Active internal bleeding, recent intracranial hemorrhage (also recent major surgery, severe uncontrolled hypertension)

    Screening for bleeding risk is essential before administering a thrombolytic agent that could cause catastrophic hemorrhage.

  2. Case 26: 75-Year-Old Woman with Wake-Up Stroke Symptoms

    A 75-year-old woman wakes up with left arm weakness and dysarthria. She was last seen normal at bedtime 8 hours ago. Vitals: BP 172/98, HR 82, RR 18, sat 96 percent. NIHSS is 8.

    1. Why is this case more complex regarding thrombolysis eligibility?

    Unknown exact time of symptom onset, outside the standard thrombolysis window

    Wake-up strokes have an unclear true onset time, complicating standard time-based eligibility for thrombolysis.

    2. What advanced imaging could still make her eligible for treatment?

    MRI with diffusion-weighted imaging/FLAIR mismatch, or CT perfusion

    Perfusion or DWI-FLAIR mismatch imaging can estimate the likely time since onset, extending eligibility for reperfusion therapy in select patients.

    3. What blood pressure management is appropriate if she is not a thrombolysis candidate?

    Permissive hypertension, treat only if above 220/120

    In acute ischemic stroke without thrombolysis, blood pressure is generally allowed to remain elevated to maintain cerebral perfusion unless severely high.

  3. Case 27: 58-Year-Old Woman with Transient Weakness that Resolved

    A 58-year-old woman reports an episode of right hand weakness and word-finding difficulty that lasted 20 minutes and completely resolved before arrival. She has hypertension and atrial fibrillation, not on anticoagulation. Exam is now entirely normal.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Transient ischemic attack (TIA)

    Fully resolved focal neurologic symptoms without infarct represent a TIA, a warning sign for impending stroke.

    2. What score would you use to estimate her short-term stroke risk?

    ABCD2 score

    ABCD2 incorporates age, blood pressure, clinical features, duration, and diabetes to estimate risk of stroke in the days following a TIA.

    3. What investigations should be arranged urgently given her atrial fibrillation?

    CT/MRI brain, carotid imaging, echocardiogram, and urgent anticoagulation planning

    Identifying a cardioembolic source and carotid disease guides secondary prevention, particularly starting anticoagulation for her known AF.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition?

    Urgent stroke clinic referral or admission depending on ABCD2 risk and local protocol

    High-risk TIA patients require expedited workup, either through rapid outpatient stroke clinics or admission, to prevent an impending stroke.

  4. Case 28: 45-Year-Old Man with Sudden Neck Pain and Stroke Symptoms

    A 45-year-old man presents with sudden severe neck pain following a chiropractic neck manipulation two days ago, now with new left-sided weakness and ataxia. Vitals: BP 148/88, HR 80, RR 16, sat 98 percent. Exam reveals a partial Horner syndrome on the right and left-sided limb weakness.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Vertebral artery dissection

    Neck manipulation, neck pain, Horner syndrome, and posterior circulation stroke symptoms are classic for vertebral artery dissection.

    2. What imaging would confirm this diagnosis?

    CT angiogram or MR angiogram of the neck

    Vascular imaging of the neck vessels can directly visualize the dissection flap or intramural hematoma.

    3. What is the general management approach for a confirmed dissection with stroke?

    Antithrombotic therapy (antiplatelet or anticoagulation per neurology) and stroke unit admission

    Dissection-related stroke is typically managed with antithrombotic therapy to prevent further embolization, guided by neurology/stroke specialists.

  5. Case 29: 34-Year-Old Woman with Worst Headache of Her Life

    A 34-year-old woman presents with sudden, severe headache reaching maximal intensity within seconds while exercising, associated with neck stiffness and one episode of vomiting. Vitals: BP 152/90, HR 92, RR 18, sat 98 percent. She is alert, no focal deficits, mild neck stiffness on exam.

    1. What diagnosis must be excluded first?

    Subarachnoid hemorrhage

    A thunderclap headache reaching maximal severity within seconds is the hallmark presentation of subarachnoid hemorrhage until proven otherwise.

    2. What is the first-line investigation, and within what time frame does it have the highest sensitivity?

    Non-contrast CT head, within 6 hours of headache onset

    CT sensitivity for SAH approaches near 100 percent within 6 hours of onset, decreasing thereafter as blood is resorbed.

    3. The CT is negative and it has been 10 hours since onset. What is the next step?

    Lumbar puncture to assess for xanthochromia and red blood cells

    Beyond the early CT window, lumbar puncture is needed to detect blood breakdown products missed on imaging.

    4. If SAH is confirmed, what is the immediate management priority?

    Urgent neurosurgery consult, blood pressure control, and CT angiogram to identify an aneurysm

    Confirmed SAH requires urgent identification of the aneurysmal source and neurosurgical involvement to prevent rebleeding and secure the aneurysm.

  6. Case 30: 26-Year-Old Woman with Recurrent Migraine

    A 26-year-old woman presents with a throbbing unilateral headache with photophobia, phonophobia, and nausea, similar to her usual migraines but more severe. She reports visual aura beforehand. Vitals are normal, neurological exam is normal.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Migraine with aura

    A throbbing unilateral headache with photophobia, phonophobia, and a preceding visual aura consistent with her usual pattern confirms migraine.

    2. List two first-line abortive treatments for the ED.

    IV/oral antiemetic (e.g., metoclopramide or prochlorperazine), NSAID

    Dopamine antagonist antiemetics have independent analgesic effect in migraine and are combined with NSAIDs as first-line ED therapy.

    3. What adjunct is often given alongside the antiemetic to reduce akathisia?

    Diphenhydramine

    Diphenhydramine reduces the risk of akathisia and dystonic reactions associated with dopamine antagonist antiemetics.

    4. What red flag feature, if present, would change your workup despite her history of migraines?

    New neurological deficit, sudden thunderclap onset, or change in headache pattern

    Even known migraineurs require further investigation if red flags suggest a secondary cause rather than a typical migraine.

  7. Case 31: 70-Year-Old Woman with Headache and Jaw Claudication

    A 70-year-old woman presents with a new temporal headache over two weeks, jaw pain while chewing, and scalp tenderness. She reports blurred vision in her right eye this morning. Vitals: BP 138/84, HR 88, RR 16, sat 98 percent. Temporal artery is tender and non-pulsatile.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Giant cell (temporal) arteritis

    New temporal headache, jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, and visual symptoms in an elderly patient are classic for giant cell arteritis.

    2. What is the most urgent complication to prevent?

    Permanent vision loss

    GCA can cause irreversible blindness from ischemic optic neuropathy if untreated, making it a true ophthalmologic and rheumatologic emergency.

    3. What investigation supports the diagnosis and what is the immediate treatment, without waiting for biopsy?

    ESR/CRP; start high-dose corticosteroids immediately

    Elevated inflammatory markers support the diagnosis, but treatment with high-dose steroids should begin immediately given the risk of vision loss, without waiting for biopsy confirmation.

    4. What definitive diagnostic test should be arranged, and does it need to happen before starting steroids?

    Temporal artery biopsy; no, steroids should not be delayed for biopsy

    Biopsy confirms the diagnosis but findings persist for days to weeks after starting steroids, so treatment should never be withheld awaiting biopsy.

  8. Case 32: 29-Year-Old Woman with Headache and Papilledema

    A 29-year-old obese woman presents with daily headache, transient visual graying episodes, and pulsatile tinnitus. Vitals: BP 128/78, HR 76, RR 16, sat 99 percent. Fundoscopy reveals bilateral papilledema. Neurological exam is otherwise normal.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Idiopathic intracranial hypertension

    Headache with papilledema, transient visual obscurations, and pulsatile tinnitus in an obese woman of childbearing age is classic for idiopathic intracranial hypertension.

    2. What imaging must be done before lumbar puncture and why?

    CT or MRI brain/venogram, to exclude a mass lesion or venous sinus thrombosis

    Imaging excludes structural causes of raised intracranial pressure and rules out cerebral venous sinus thrombosis before proceeding to LP.

    3. What diagnostic and therapeutic procedure would you perform after imaging is reassuring?

    Lumbar puncture with opening pressure measurement, with therapeutic drainage of CSF

    Measuring elevated opening pressure confirms the diagnosis, and removing CSF can transiently relieve symptoms.

    4. What medication is used for long-term management?

    Acetazolamide

    Acetazolamide reduces CSF production and is first-line medical therapy to lower intracranial pressure in IIH.

  9. Case 33: 24-Year-Old Man with First Unprovoked Seizure

    A 24-year-old man is brought in after a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure lasting 2 minutes, now postictal but improving. He has no prior seizure history and no substance use reported by witnesses. Vitals: BP 128/78, HR 96, RR 18, sat 97 percent, glucose 5.8.

    1. List three initial investigations to identify a provoking cause.

    Glucose, electrolytes, CT head

    Common reversible causes such as hypoglycemia, electrolyte disturbance, and structural lesions must be excluded after a first seizure.

    2. Once he returns to baseline with a normal workup, what is the disposition?

    Discharge home with neurology follow-up and outpatient EEG/MRI, driving restriction counseling

    A first unprovoked seizure with a normal ED workup and full return to baseline can be safely discharged with outpatient follow-up and driving safety counseling.

    3. What specific counseling point is medicolegally important before discharge?

    Provincial driving restriction requirements after a seizure

    Physicians have reporting obligations and patients must be counseled on driving restrictions to reduce risk to themselves and others.

  10. Case 34: 40-Year-Old Man in Status Epilepticus

    A 40-year-old man with known epilepsy is brought in actively seizing for the past 8 minutes despite two doses of lorazepam given by paramedics. Vitals: BP 148/90, HR 128, RR 22, sat 91 percent on non-rebreather, glucose 6.0.

    1. What is this condition called?

    Status epilepticus

    Continuous seizure activity for more than 5 minutes, or failure to return to baseline between seizures, defines status epilepticus.

    2. Having already received benzodiazepines, what is the next line of treatment?

    IV second-line agent such as levetiracetam, fosphenytoin, or valproate

    After adequate benzodiazepine dosing fails, a second-line antiepileptic loading dose is the next step per status epilepticus protocols.

    3. The seizure continues despite the second-line agent. What is the next step?

    Rapid sequence intubation and induction with an anesthetic agent (e.g., propofol or midazolam infusion) for refractory status epilepticus

    Refractory status epilepticus unresponsive to first and second-line agents requires airway control and continuous anesthetic infusion, typically with EEG monitoring.

    4. What investigation should be arranged once he is stabilized to look for an underlying cause?

    CT head, and consider EEG and toxicology/metabolic screen

    Identifying an underlying structural, metabolic, or toxic trigger is essential to guide further management and prevent recurrence.

  11. Case 35: 35-Year-Old Woman with Seizure in Pregnancy

    A 35-year-old woman at 34 weeks gestation presents after a witnessed generalized seizure at home. She has had headache and swelling of her hands and face over the past week. Vitals: BP 168/108, HR 96, RR 20, sat 96 percent. She is now postictal.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Eclampsia

    New seizure in the setting of hypertension, headache, and edema in a pregnant woman beyond 20 weeks defines eclampsia.

    2. What is the first-line medication to prevent further seizures?

    Magnesium sulfate

    Magnesium sulfate is the standard of care for both seizure prophylaxis and treatment in eclampsia, superior to other anticonvulsants.

    3. What is the definitive treatment for eclampsia?

    Delivery of the fetus

    Eclampsia resolves only with delivery, which is the definitive treatment once the mother is stabilized.

    4. What consult must be activated immediately?

    Obstetrics (and consider anesthesia/NICU)

    Urgent obstetric involvement is essential for maternal and fetal monitoring and to coordinate emergent delivery if needed.

  12. Case 36: 20-Year-Old Woman with Psychogenic Non-Epileptic Events

    A 20-year-old woman with a history of anxiety and prior similar episodes presents with 15 minutes of asynchronous limb thrashing with pelvic thrusting, eyes closed tightly, and no post-ictal confusion. Vitals are normal throughout. Bystanders report she responded to voice during the event.

    1. What clinical features suggest a psychogenic non-epileptic event rather than true epileptic seizure?

    Asynchronous thrashing, forced eye closure, retained awareness/responsiveness, no true post-ictal confusion

    These features are classic distinguishing findings between psychogenic non-epileptic seizures and true generalized tonic-clonic seizures.

    2. What investigation, ideally with video, would provide the definitive diagnosis?

    Video EEG monitoring

    Video EEG capturing an event without corresponding epileptiform activity is the gold standard to confirm psychogenic non-epileptic seizures.

    3. What is an important next step in her ED management and disposition?

    Rule out organic causes as needed, then provide sensitive communication of findings and refer to psychiatry/neurology for outpatient management

    A careful, non-dismissive explanation and referral to appropriate specialists supports engagement in treatment rather than alienating the patient.

Chapter 5: Altered Mental Status and Behavioral Emergencies (12 Cases)

  1. Case 37: 55-Year-Old Man Found Unresponsive with Pinpoint Pupils

    A 55-year-old man is found unresponsive at home by family, with an empty pill bottle nearby. Vitals: BP 98/60, HR 58, RR 8, sat 88 percent on room air. Pupils are pinpoint and minimally reactive. GCS is 6.

    1. What is the most likely toxidrome?

    Opioid toxicity

    Respiratory depression, pinpoint pupils, and decreased level of consciousness form the classic opioid toxidrome.

    2. What is the immediate antidote and route?

    Naloxone, IV or IM/intranasal

    Naloxone rapidly reverses opioid-induced respiratory depression and should be given promptly given his hypoventilation and hypoxia.

    3. Given his GCS of 6 and respiratory rate of 8, what airway intervention should be prioritized alongside naloxone?

    Bag-valve-mask ventilation while preparing naloxone/further airway management

    Securing oxygenation and ventilation takes priority over waiting for the antidote to take effect in a critically hypoventilating patient.

    4. After initial reversal, why must he be observed for an extended period?

    Risk of re-sedation as naloxone's duration is shorter than many opioids

    Naloxone has a shorter half-life than most opioids, risking recurrent respiratory depression once it wears off if the ingested opioid remains active.

  2. Case 38: 70-Year-Old Woman with Decreased LOC and Fever

    A 70-year-old woman is brought from her nursing home with confusion progressing to unresponsiveness over 12 hours. Vitals: BP 84/50, HR 122, RR 26, sat 92 percent, temp 39.2. She has nuchal rigidity and a petechial rash on her trunk.

    1. What is the most concerning diagnosis?

    Bacterial meningitis with sepsis

    Fever, nuchal rigidity, altered consciousness, and petechial rash strongly suggest meningococcal or other bacterial meningitis with septic shock.

    2. What is the priority intervention, and should it be delayed for lumbar puncture or imaging?

    Empiric IV antibiotics plus fluid resuscitation, given immediately without delay for LP or CT

    In suspected bacterial meningitis with sepsis, antibiotics must not be delayed for diagnostic testing given the high mortality of delayed treatment.

    3. What adjunct medication should be given alongside or just before antibiotics?

    Dexamethasone

    Early corticosteroids reduce neurological complications in bacterial meningitis, particularly pneumococcal, and are given with or just before the first antibiotic dose.

    4. What investigation would you still pursue once she is stabilized to confirm the organism?

    Lumbar puncture with CSF analysis and cultures, blood cultures

    Once stabilized, CSF and blood cultures identify the causative organism to tailor antibiotic therapy and guide public health measures.

  3. Case 39: 60-Year-Old Man with Known Diabetes and Altered LOC

    A 60-year-old man with type 1 diabetes is found confused and diaphoretic by his wife. Vitals: BP 118/74, HR 110, RR 18, sat 98 percent. He is tremulous and combative, unable to answer questions appropriately. Point-of-care glucose reads 1.8 mmol/L.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Severe hypoglycemia

    Markedly low point-of-care glucose in a confused diabetic patient on insulin confirms hypoglycemia as the cause of altered mental status.

    2. What is the immediate treatment given he cannot safely take oral intake?

    IV dextrose (D50W)

    IV dextrose rapidly corrects severe hypoglycemia in a patient unable to safely swallow due to altered mental status.

    3. After his glucose normalizes and mental status improves, what must you investigate to prevent recurrence?

    Recent insulin dosing, oral intake, and any precipitating illness or renal function change

    Identifying the precipitant, whether dosing error, missed meal, or new renal impairment, is essential to prevent repeat hypoglycemic episodes.

    4. What length of observation is appropriate if he was on a long-acting insulin?

    Extended observation given risk of recurrent hypoglycemia from long-acting insulin

    Long-acting insulins have prolonged effects, so a brief response to dextrose does not exclude the risk of recurrent hypoglycemia later.

  4. Case 40: 45-Year-Old Man with Decreased LOC After Head Trauma

    A 45-year-old man is brought in after a fall from a ladder with head strike, initially alert then progressively less responsive over one hour. Vitals: BP 160/70, HR 52, RR 14, sat 97 percent. GCS has dropped from 15 to 9. Left pupil is now dilated and sluggish.

    1. What is the classic triad suggested by his vitals, and what does it indicate?

    Cushing's triad (hypertension, bradycardia, irregular respirations), indicating raised intracranial pressure

    Widening pulse pressure, bradycardia, and irregular breathing reflect the body's compensatory response to rising intracranial pressure.

    2. What is the most likely underlying diagnosis given the lucid interval and unilateral pupil change?

    Epidural hematoma with uncal herniation

    A lucid interval followed by rapid deterioration and a blown pupil is the classic presentation of an expanding epidural hematoma causing uncal herniation.

    3. What immediate investigation and consult are needed?

    STAT non-contrast CT head and emergent neurosurgery consult

    Rapid imaging confirms the bleed while neurosurgery is needed emergently for potential surgical decompression.

    4. What temporizing medical measure can reduce intracranial pressure while awaiting surgery?

    Hyperosmolar therapy (mannitol or hypertonic saline), head of bed elevation, and controlled hyperventilation if herniating

    Osmotic agents and positioning reduce cerebral edema and ICP temporarily to buy time before definitive surgical decompression.

  5. Case 41: 82-Year-Old Woman with Acute Confusion

    An 82-year-old woman with baseline mild dementia is brought from her care home with a two-day history of new confusion, agitation, and disturbed sleep-wake cycle. Vitals: BP 108/64, HR 102, RR 20, sat 95 percent, temp 38.4. She is intermittently combative and disoriented.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Delirium, likely secondary to infection

    Acute fluctuating confusion superimposed on baseline cognitive status, with fever and tachycardia, suggests delirium from an underlying infectious trigger.

    2. List three investigations to identify the underlying cause.

    Urinalysis/urine culture, CBC, chest x-ray (also electrolytes, blood cultures)

    Common precipitants of delirium in elderly patients include urinary tract infection, pneumonia, and metabolic derangements, all screened with these basic tests.

    3. What non-pharmacologic strategies should be prioritized for her agitation?

    Reorientation, familiar objects/family presence, minimizing noise and restraints, correcting sensory deficits

    Non-pharmacologic measures are first-line for delirium management and reduce the need for sedating medications that can worsen confusion in the elderly.

    4. If pharmacologic treatment becomes necessary for severe agitation, what medication is preferred?

    Low-dose haloperidol or an atypical antipsychotic

    Low-dose antipsychotics are preferred over benzodiazepines for delirium-related agitation, as benzodiazepines can worsen confusion except in withdrawal states.

  6. Case 42: 50-Year-Old Man with Agitation and Tremor After Stopping Alcohol

    A 50-year-old man with chronic alcohol use presents with agitation, tremor, and visual hallucinations two days after his last drink. Vitals: BP 168/98, HR 128, RR 22, sat 96 percent, temp 38.0. He is diaphoretic and tremulous, oriented only to person.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Alcohol withdrawal delirium (delirium tremens)

    Autonomic hyperactivity, hallucinations, and confusion two to three days after cessation of heavy alcohol use is classic for delirium tremens.

    2. What is the first-line medication class for treatment?

    Benzodiazepines

    Benzodiazepines are first-line for alcohol withdrawal, cross-tolerant with alcohol at the GABA receptor and effective for both symptoms and seizure prevention.

    3. What vitamin should be given before any glucose administration and why?

    Thiamine, to prevent precipitating Wernicke encephalopathy

    Giving glucose before thiamine in a thiamine-deficient patient can precipitate Wernicke encephalopathy by consuming remaining thiamine stores.

    4. What validated tool can help guide symptom-triggered dosing of benzodiazepines?

    CIWA-Ar scale

    The CIWA-Ar score objectively quantifies withdrawal severity, allowing symptom-triggered dosing rather than fixed scheduling.

  7. Case 43: 19-Year-Old Man with Severe Agitation After Stimulant Use

    A 19-year-old man is brought in by police after being found running through traffic, extremely agitated, diaphoretic, and yelling incoherently. Friends report he used a stimulant drug at a party. Vitals: BP 178/104, HR 142, RR 26, sat 96 percent, temp 39.4. He requires four staff to restrain him safely.

    1. What life-threatening condition must be considered given his presentation?

    Excited delirium/severe sympathomimetic toxicity with hyperthermia

    Extreme agitation, hyperthermia, tachycardia, and diaphoresis after stimulant use signal a life-threatening sympathomimetic crisis requiring urgent intervention.

    2. What is the priority initial treatment for his agitation?

    Rapid sedation with benzodiazepines (e.g., IM/IV midazolam or lorazepam)

    Aggressive benzodiazepine sedation controls agitation, reduces muscular heat generation, and lowers risk of complications like rhabdomyolysis.

    3. Given his temperature, what cooling measures should be initiated immediately?

    Active external cooling (ice packs, cooling blankets, misting with fans)

    Rapid cooling reduces the risk of hyperthermia-related complications such as rhabdomyolysis, seizures, and multi-organ failure.

    4. What lab tests should be sent given his presentation to monitor for complications?

    CK (rhabdomyolysis), electrolytes/creatinine, and consider troponin

    Severe agitation and hyperthermia can cause rhabdomyolysis and acute kidney injury, warranting monitoring of CK and renal function.

  8. Case 44: 72-Year-Old Man with Agitation and Urinary Retention

    A 72-year-old man with benign prostatic hyperplasia presents with new agitation and confusion over the past day, refusing to be examined. Vitals: BP 148/86, HR 110, RR 20, sat 96 percent, afebrile. Abdominal exam reveals a distended, tender suprapubic mass.

    1. What is the likely cause of his delirium?

    Acute urinary retention

    A distended painful bladder is a common, easily reversible cause of acute delirium in elderly men, particularly those with BPH.

    2. What bedside investigation and intervention would you perform first?

    Bladder scan, followed by urinary catheterization

    Confirming retention with a bladder scan and promptly relieving it with catheterization can rapidly resolve the delirium and prevent renal injury.

    3. After decompressing the bladder, what should you reassess before assuming the delirium is fully explained?

    Reassess mental status and screen for other contributing causes (infection, medications)

    Delirium is often multifactorial in the elderly, so full resolution should be confirmed rather than assuming a single cause explains everything.

  9. Case 45: 17-Year-Old Girl Presenting After Overdose

    A 17-year-old girl is brought in by her mother after admitting to taking an unknown number of acetaminophen tablets three hours ago following a breakup. She is medically stable, tearful, and states she wishes she had died. Vitals are normal.

    1. What is the priority initial investigation given her ingestion?

    Acetaminophen level (drawn at or after 4 hours post-ingestion) and plot on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram

    Acetaminophen levels are only interpretable using the nomogram starting at 4 hours post-ingestion to guide need for N-acetylcysteine.

    2. What immediate safety measure must be implemented while she is in the department?

    One-to-one observation/constant supervision and removal of means

    Continuous observation prevents further self-harm attempts while she remains in a vulnerable acute suicidal state.

    3. What structured assessment should be completed before disposition?

    Formal suicide risk assessment including intent, plan, means, and protective factors

    A thorough risk assessment is required to determine safety for discharge versus need for psychiatric admission.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition given her expressed wish to have died and an impulsive overdose?

    Psychiatric consultation for further risk assessment and disposition planning, likely inpatient psychiatric admission if risk remains high

    An impulsive overdose with ongoing suicidal ideation warrants specialist psychiatric evaluation before any discharge decision is made.

  10. Case 46: 45-Year-Old Man with Suicidal Ideation and a Firearm at Home

    A 45-year-old man presents at his family's urging after expressing thoughts of ending his life following a recent job loss and divorce. He reports a specific plan and access to a firearm at home. He appears withdrawn and admits to feeling hopeless.

    1. List three high-risk features identified in this history.

    Specific plan, access to lethal means (firearm), recent major losses/hopelessness

    A specific plan combined with access to a highly lethal method and significant recent psychosocial stressors are strong indicators of imminent risk.

    2. What is the single most important immediate safety intervention regarding his firearm?

    Means restriction, arranging removal or secure storage of the firearm, involving police/family as needed

    Restricting access to lethal means is one of the most effective interventions to reduce completed suicide risk during a crisis period.

    3. What is the appropriate disposition?

    Urgent psychiatric assessment, likely involuntary admission if he is unwilling to accept voluntary care and remains high risk

    High imminent risk with a specific plan and access to means generally requires involuntary hold under provincial mental health legislation if he declines voluntary treatment.

  11. Case 47: 60-Year-Old Man with Chronic Illness and Passive Death Wish

    A 60-year-old man with metastatic cancer presents for unrelated abdominal pain. During your assessment, he mentions he sometimes wishes he would simply not wake up, though he denies any plan or intent to harm himself and states he wants to see his grandchild's graduation.

    1. What is this presentation called, and how does it differ from active suicidal ideation?

    Passive suicidal ideation; involves a wish to be dead without active intent or plan to cause death

    Passive ideation reflects a wish not to be alive rather than active intent or planning, which changes the urgency and management approach.

    2. What should you still do despite the absence of active plan or intent?

    Complete a full risk assessment and screen for underlying depression or demoralization

    Even passive ideation warrants careful evaluation, as it can indicate underlying untreated depression or existential distress requiring support.

    3. What resource or referral would be appropriate given his terminal illness context?

    Palliative care and psychosocial/psychiatric support for coping with terminal illness

    Addressing distress in the context of terminal illness benefits from integrated palliative and mental health support rather than emergency psychiatric hold.

  12. Case 48: 30-Year-Old Woman with Repeated Self-Harm Presentations

    A 30-year-old woman with a history of borderline personality disorder presents after superficial cutting on her forearms following an argument with her partner. This is her sixth ED presentation this year for similar behaviour. She denies suicidal intent, stating the cutting relieves emotional tension.

    1. What is this behaviour best classified as?

    Non-suicidal self-injury

    Self-harm performed to relieve emotional distress without intent to die is classified as non-suicidal self-injury, distinct from a suicide attempt.

    2. Despite the denial of intent, what must still be formally assessed at every visit?

    Suicide risk assessment, since NSSI increases long-term suicide risk

    Repeated non-suicidal self-injury is a known risk factor for eventual suicide attempts, so risk must be reassessed every presentation rather than assumed low.

    3. What is the most appropriate long-term management approach for her recurrent presentations?

    Referral to dialectical behaviour therapy or specialized personality disorder treatment program

    DBT has strong evidence for reducing self-harm frequency in patients with borderline personality disorder and addresses underlying emotion regulation difficulties.

    4. What wound care management is needed today?

    Local wound cleaning and closure as needed (steri-strips or sutures) with tetanus status review

    Superficial lacerations require standard wound care and tetanus prophylaxis assessment regardless of the psychiatric context.

Chapter 6: Trauma and Musculoskeletal Injury (16 Cases)

  1. Case 49: Restrained Driver in High-Speed MVC

    A 34-year-old man is brought in by paramedics after his vehicle struck a guardrail at highway speed. He was restrained. On arrival he is unresponsive, HR 128, BP 88/54, RR 26, SpO2 91% on a non-rebreather mask. He has a seatbelt sign across his chest and abdomen, decreased breath sounds on the left, and a tender distended abdomen. He withdraws to pain but does not open his eyes or speak.

    1. What is your first priority in assessing this patient?

    Airway with C-spine control

    In any trauma patient with altered mental status, airway protection and cervical spine immobilization take precedence over all other interventions per ATLS primary survey sequencing.

    2. Name two immediate bedside investigations to identify the source of hypotension.

    Extended FAST scan, chest X-ray

    eFAST rapidly assesses for hemoperitoneum, hemothorax, and pneumothorax, while a portable chest X-ray identifies tension pneumothorax or hemothorax contributing to shock.

    3. The left chest has absent breath sounds and hyperresonance with worsening hypotension. What is the immediate management?

    Needle or finger thoracostomy, then chest tube

    Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate decompression before imaging, as delay can cause cardiac arrest from obstructive shock.

    4. Despite decompression, the patient remains hypotensive with a positive FAST. What is the definitive next step?

    Emergent surgical consultation for laparotomy

    A hemodynamically unstable patient with intra-abdominal free fluid on FAST requires immediate operative intervention rather than further imaging.

  2. Case 50: Pedestrian Struck by Vehicle

    A 62-year-old woman is struck by a car while crossing the street at low speed. She is alert and oriented, complaining of right hip and pelvic pain. HR 104, BP 108/70, RR 18. Exam reveals pelvic instability to gentle compression and a shortened, externally rotated right leg. She has no head injury and no abdominal tenderness.

    1. What immediate measure should be applied given the exam finding of pelvic instability?

    Pelvic binder

    Early pelvic binding reduces pelvic volume and can control venous and bony bleeding from an unstable pelvic fracture, limiting ongoing hemorrhage.

    2. List two investigations to further evaluate her injuries.

    Pelvic X-ray, CT abdomen/pelvis with contrast

    Plain pelvic X-ray quickly confirms fracture pattern while CT with contrast identifies active arterial bleeding and associated visceral injury for surgical or interventional radiology planning.

    3. What examination must be performed before and after any manipulation of the injured limb?

    Neurovascular exam (distal pulses, sensation, motor function)

    Documenting neurovascular status before and after reduction protects against missed vascular or nerve injury and medicolegal risk from manipulation.

    4. If she remains hemodynamically unstable despite binder placement and fluids, what is the next management step?

    Interventional radiology for angioembolization or emergent surgery

    Ongoing instability despite pelvic binding suggests arterial hemorrhage, which is best controlled by angioembolization or surgical packing rather than further fluid resuscitation alone.

  3. Case 51: Fall From Ladder With Delayed Presentation

    A 45-year-old man fell approximately 4 meters from a ladder two hours ago and was reluctant to seek care. He now presents with worsening abdominal pain, mild confusion, and left shoulder tip pain. HR 118, BP 96/60, RR 22. His abdomen is diffusely tender with guarding. He has bruising over his left lower ribs.

    1. What does left shoulder tip pain in this context suggest?

    Splenic injury causing diaphragmatic irritation (Kehr sign)

    Referred shoulder pain from diaphragmatic irritation is a classic sign of splenic injury with subphrenic blood, especially with associated left-sided rib fractures.

    2. List two investigations to assess for intra-abdominal injury.

    FAST scan, CT abdomen/pelvis with IV contrast

    FAST provides rapid bedside detection of hemoperitoneum while CT with contrast characterizes solid organ injury grade if the patient is stable enough to be scanned.

    3. What is the initial fluid resuscitation approach while awaiting definitive management?

    Balanced crystalloid boluses titrated to mental status and blood pressure, activate massive transfusion protocol if unresponsive

    Judicious crystalloid with early blood product availability avoids dilutional coagulopathy while maintaining perfusion in a patient with suspected ongoing hemorrhage.

    4. He responds transiently to fluids then decompensates again. What is the appropriate disposition?

    Emergent surgical consultation for laparotomy or angioembolization

    Transient responders with recurrent hypotension have ongoing hemorrhage and require definitive hemorrhage control rather than repeated fluid boluses.

  4. Case 52: Motorcyclist With Chest and Head Injury

    A 27-year-old man was thrown from his motorcycle at moderate speed, wearing a helmet. He is agitated, GCS 13, with a laceration to his scalp and paradoxical chest wall movement on the right with crepitus. HR 122, BP 100/64, RR 30 with reduced air entry on the right. SpO2 88% on room air.

    1. What is the diagnosis suggested by paradoxical chest wall movement?

    Flail chest

    Multiple adjacent rib fractures in two or more places create a free-floating chest wall segment that moves paradoxically with respiration, often with underlying pulmonary contusion.

    2. What is the immediate airway and breathing management?

    High-flow oxygen, consider early intubation if respiratory failure worsens

    Flail chest with hypoxia and pulmonary contusion can rapidly progress to respiratory failure, so supplemental oxygen and a low threshold for definitive airway control are essential.

    3. Name two investigations to assess for associated injuries.

    CT head, CT chest

    CT head evaluates for intracranial injury given his altered GCS, while CT chest characterizes rib fracture pattern, pulmonary contusion, and occult pneumothorax.

    4. What analgesic strategy is preferred for his rib fractures to reduce pulmonary complications?

    Regional analgesia such as an intercostal or epidural block, multimodal analgesia

    Adequate analgesia, ideally regional, improves ventilation and reduces the risk of atelectasis and pneumonia compared to opioids alone, which can suppress respiratory drive.

  5. Case 53: Fall on Outstretched Hand in Elderly Woman

    A 72-year-old woman presents after a fall onto her outstretched right hand. She has a dorsally displaced deformity of the distal forearm described as a dinner fork deformity, with swelling and tenderness. She has normal sensation but reports mild numbness in her thumb and index finger.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Colles fracture (distal radius fracture)

    The dorsally angulated dinner fork deformity after a fall on an outstretched hand is the classic presentation of a Colles fracture.

    2. What nerve is at risk of injury or compression with this fracture?

    Median nerve

    Distal radius fractures can cause acute median nerve compression from swelling or fragment displacement, presenting with thumb and index finger numbness.

    3. What investigation should be ordered?

    Wrist X-ray (AP and lateral)

    Plain radiographs confirm the fracture pattern, degree of displacement, and angulation needed to guide reduction.

    4. What must be documented before and after closed reduction?

    Neurovascular status of the hand

    Documenting sensation, motor function, and distal pulses before and after any manipulation protects the patient from missed nerve injury and protects against medicolegal liability.

  6. Case 54: Adolescent With Twisted Ankle Playing Basketball

    A 16-year-old boy twists his ankle landing awkwardly during a basketball game. He has immediate pain and swelling over the lateral malleolus and is unable to bear weight for more than a few steps. There is tenderness over the distal fibula but no bony tenderness at the base of the fifth metatarsal or navicular.

    1. What clinical decision rule determines whether an ankle X-ray is needed?

    Ottawa Ankle Rules

    The Ottawa Ankle Rules use bony tenderness at specific landmarks and inability to bear weight to determine which patients require radiography, reducing unnecessary imaging.

    2. Based on his inability to bear weight, what investigation is indicated?

    Ankle X-ray series

    Inability to bear weight for four steps immediately and in the emergency department meets Ottawa Ankle Rule criteria for imaging regardless of tenderness location.

    3. The X-ray shows no fracture. What is the initial management?

    RICE (rest, ice, compression, elevation), brace or air cast, weight-bearing as tolerated, follow-up

    A negative X-ray with a lateral ligament sprain is managed conservatively with functional support and early mobilization as tolerated.

    4. What associated injury pattern should be considered in a high-energy ankle injury with widening of the mortise?

    Syndesmotic (high ankle) injury or Maisonneuve fracture

    Mortise widening suggests syndesmotic disruption, which can be associated with a proximal fibular fracture (Maisonneuve fracture) that is easily missed if only the ankle is imaged.

  7. Case 55: Shoulder Pain After Seizure

    A 38-year-old man is brought in after a witnessed generalized seizure. He now complains of severe right shoulder pain and holds his arm adducted and internally rotated, unable to externally rotate or abduct. On exam there is a flattened deltoid contour and a prominent coracoid process.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis given the mechanism and exam?

    Posterior shoulder dislocation

    Seizures classically cause posterior shoulder dislocation because the internal rotators overpower the external rotators, presenting with a locked internally rotated arm rather than the typical anterior dislocation appearance.

    2. What imaging view is essential to avoid missing this diagnosis?

    Axillary or scapular Y view

    Posterior dislocations can appear deceptively normal on a standard AP shoulder X-ray alone, and an axillary or Y view is needed to confirm the direction of dislocation.

    3. What nerve should be assessed before and after reduction?

    Axillary nerve

    The axillary nerve is at risk with shoulder dislocations and reduction attempts, and testing regimental patch sensation and deltoid function documents its status.

    4. After successful reduction, what is the disposition?

    Sling immobilization and orthopedic follow-up

    Following confirmed reduction with restored neurovascular status, the shoulder is immobilized and the patient referred for outpatient orthopedic follow-up given the higher risk of recurrent instability.

  8. Case 56: Child With Elbow Injury After Fall

    A 6-year-old girl falls from monkey bars onto an outstretched hand. She presents with a swollen, painful elbow held in slight flexion, refusing to move it. X-ray shows a posterior fat pad sign but no obvious fracture line. Her radial pulse is present and she has normal finger sensation.

    1. What does a posterior fat pad sign on elbow X-ray suggest?

    Occult elbow fracture, most likely supracondylar in a child

    A posterior fat pad is abnormal and indicates an elbow joint effusion, most commonly from an occult supracondylar fracture in this age group even without a visible fracture line.

    2. What nerve is most commonly injured with a supracondylar fracture?

    Anterior interosseous nerve (branch of median nerve)

    The anterior interosseous nerve is the most frequently injured nerve in supracondylar fractures, tested by asking the child to make an OK sign.
