    3. What vascular complication must be assessed and monitored closely?

    Brachial artery injury or compromise

    Displaced supracondylar fractures can compress or injure the brachial artery, risking compartment syndrome, so pulses and capillary refill must be serially monitored.

    4. Given the fat pad sign with no displaced fracture, what is appropriate management?

    Long-arm splint immobilization and orthopedic follow-up

    A nondisplaced occult fracture suggested by an isolated fat pad sign is treated with splinting in a position of comfort and outpatient orthopedic reassessment.

  9. Case 57: Bite Wound to the Hand From a Fight

    A 24-year-old man presents with a laceration over the dorsum of his right hand near the fourth and fifth metacarpophalangeal joints, sustained when he punched someone in the mouth. The wound is 1.5 cm, minimally bleeding, and he has full range of motion but reports the injury happened six hours ago.

    1. What is this injury commonly called and why is it high risk?

    Fight bite (clenched fist injury), high risk for joint and tendon inoculation with oral flora

    The mechanism drives the tooth through skin, extensor tendon, and often into the joint capsule, seeding it with polymicrobial oral bacteria including Eikenella corrodens.

    2. What investigation should be considered given the mechanism?

    Hand X-ray to look for retained tooth fragment or joint involvement

    Retained tooth fragments are radiopaque and must be excluded, and imaging can also reveal an occult metacarpal fracture from the punch.

    3. Should this wound be closed primarily?

    No, leave open for delayed closure

    Fight bites are high risk for deep infection, and primary closure traps bacteria in the joint or tendon sheath, so the wound should be explored, irrigated, and left open.

    4. What antibiotic coverage is indicated?

    Amoxicillin-clavulanate covering oral flora including Eikenella

    Empiric prophylactic antibiotics targeting oral flora, particularly Eikenella corrodens, are indicated for all clenched fist injuries regardless of how benign the wound appears.

  10. Case 58: Deep Laceration From Broken Glass

    A 29-year-old woman lacerates her forearm on broken glass while doing dishes. The wound is 4 cm, gaping, over the volar surface. She reports numbness in her thumb and inability to flex her thumb tip. There is brisk bleeding controlled with direct pressure.

    1. What two structures must be specifically examined given her symptoms?

    Median nerve and flexor pollicis longus tendon

    Thumb numbness and inability to flex the thumb interphalangeal joint suggest injury to the median nerve and flexor pollicis longus tendon, which run in this volar forearm location.

    2. What must be done before closing this wound?

    Wound exploration and irrigation to assess for foreign body and structural injury

    Glass lacerations require careful exploration under adequate anesthesia and lighting to rule out retained glass fragments and to directly visualize tendon and nerve integrity.

    3. What imaging can help if a retained foreign body is suspected but not visualized on exploration?

    Soft tissue X-ray or ultrasound of the wound

    Most glass is radiopaque and visible on plain X-ray, while ultrasound can detect radiolucent fragments not seen on X-ray.

    4. Given the confirmed tendon and nerve injury, what is the disposition?

    Urgent hand surgery consultation for operative repair

    Combined tendon and nerve lacerations require specialist surgical repair and should not be closed primarily in the emergency department.

  11. Case 59: Facial Laceration in a Child After Fall

    A 4-year-old boy falls onto a coffee table edge and sustains a 3 cm laceration through his right eyebrow. The wound edges are well approximated with no active bleeding, and there is no eyelid or globe involvement. He is otherwise well and cooperative with a parent present.

    1. What structure must not be shaved when preparing this wound?

    The eyebrow

    Eyebrow hair should never be shaved because it may not regrow and the hairline serves as a critical landmark for precise wound edge alignment.

    2. What examination should be performed to exclude a more serious injury?

    Full ocular exam including visual acuity and assessment for globe injury or eyelid laceration involving the margin

    Periorbital lacerations require careful evaluation to rule out occult globe rupture, canalicular injury, or levator involvement that would need ophthalmology referral.

    3. What closure technique is appropriate for this straightforward laceration?

    Simple interrupted sutures with careful edge alignment along the eyebrow margin

    Meticulous approximation of the eyebrow border with fine sutures minimizes visible scarring and preserves natural contour in this cosmetically important area.

    4. When should sutures be removed from the face in a child this age?

    In 5 to 7 days

    Facial sutures are removed earlier than other body sites to minimize scarring and track marks given the thinner skin and rich vascular supply that promotes faster healing.

  12. Case 60: Puncture Wound to the Foot From a Rusty Nail

    A 19-year-old man steps on a rusty nail while walking in an old barn, sustaining a puncture wound to the plantar surface of his foot through his sneaker. The wound is small with minimal bleeding. He is unsure of his tetanus immunization history and has mild local tenderness only.

    1. What organism is a specific concern with puncture wounds through footwear?

    Pseudomonas aeruginosa

    Pseudomonas can be harbored in the foam of shoe soles and is a classic cause of osteomyelitis or cellulitis following plantar puncture wounds through sneakers.

    2. What is the appropriate tetanus prophylaxis given his uncertain immunization status and this wound type?

    Tetanus toxoid vaccine and tetanus immunoglobulin

    A contaminated puncture wound in a patient with unknown or incomplete tetanus vaccination status warrants both active immunization and passive immunoglobulin for immediate protection.

    3. What wound care step is essential for this puncture wound?

    Irrigation and exploration to remove any retained foreign material such as sock or shoe fragments

    Puncture wounds can drive foreign debris deep into tissue, and thorough exploration reduces the risk of retained foreign body and subsequent infection.

    4. What discharge instruction regarding follow-up should be given?

    Return if increasing redness, swelling, pain, or fever develop, suggesting infection or osteomyelitis

    Puncture wounds carry a delayed risk of deep infection including osteomyelitis, so patients need clear return precautions since early infection can be subtle.

  13. Case 61: House Fire With Facial Burns

    A 40-year-old man is rescued from a house fire with burns to his face, neck, and chest, estimated at 25% total body surface area, partial and full thickness. He has singed nasal hairs, soot in his oropharynx, and a hoarse voice. He is anxious but currently maintaining his airway, RR 24, SpO2 96% on room air.

    1. What is the most urgent concern in this patient?

    Impending airway obstruction from inhalation injury

    Singed nasal hairs, soot in the oropharynx, and hoarseness are signs of inhalation injury that can progress to rapid airway edema and obstruction, warranting early definitive airway management.

    2. What is the recommended management for his airway at this stage?

    Early elective intubation before edema progresses

    Airway swelling from inhalation injury can progress over hours, and early intubation while the airway is still accessible is safer than waiting for decompensation.

    3. From what time point should fluid resuscitation calculations be based?

    Time of the burn injury, not time of arrival

    Burn resuscitation formulas calculate fluid requirements from the actual time of injury, and any delay to presentation must be accounted for by adjusting the infusion rate for time already elapsed.

    4. What laboratory test should be checked given his fire exposure and hoarseness?

    Carboxyhemoglobin level

    Enclosed fire exposure raises concern for carbon monoxide poisoning, which can cause hypoxia not reflected accurately by standard pulse oximetry.

  14. Case 62: Toddler With Scald Burn

    A 2-year-old girl is brought in with a scald burn to her lower leg and foot after reportedly pulling a cup of hot tea off a table. The burn has a clear demarcation line on the foot with uniform depth and sharp stocking-like distribution. The parents' account seems inconsistent with the pattern observed.

    1. What concerning feature of this burn pattern raises suspicion for non-accidental injury?

    Sharp stocking or glove distribution with uniform depth suggesting forced immersion

    A clearly demarcated, uniform burn in a stocking or glove pattern is inconsistent with an accidental spill and is a classic finding of intentional forced immersion in hot liquid.

    2. What must be done given this concern?

    Report to child protective services and document findings thoroughly

    Suspected non-accidental injury mandates reporting to child protection authorities and careful documentation of the history, exam, and any inconsistencies for medicolegal purposes.

    3. What should be assessed to estimate burn severity and body surface area in a child this size?

    Use the Lund and Browder chart rather than the rule of nines

    The rule of nines is inaccurate in young children because of their proportionally larger head and smaller legs, so the Lund and Browder chart is used for accurate pediatric burn surface area estimation.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition for this child?

    Admission for further assessment and involvement of child protection team

    A suspected abusive burn in a young child requires admission for safety, thorough skeletal survey consideration, and coordinated child protection evaluation rather than outpatient discharge.

  15. Case 63: Chemical Burn to the Eye

    A 35-year-old construction worker splashes concentrated alkali cleaning solution into his right eye. He presents with severe eye pain, blepharospasm, and blurred vision. On exam there is conjunctival injection and a hazy cornea. He has not rinsed the eye since the exposure ten minutes ago.

    1. What is the immediate priority before any other examination?

    Copious irrigation of the eye

    Immediate and continuous irrigation dilutes and removes the causative chemical, and treatment should never be delayed for visual acuity testing or detailed exam in a true chemical burn.

    2. How is adequate irrigation endpoint determined?

    Irrigate until pH is neutral, checked with litmus paper

    Irrigation should continue until the conjunctival pH normalizes to roughly 7 to 7.4, confirmed with litmus paper testing, rather than for a fixed arbitrary time.

    3. Why are alkali burns generally more severe than acid burns to the eye?

    Alkali causes liquefactive necrosis allowing deeper tissue penetration, while acid causes coagulative necrosis that limits its own spread

    Alkali substances continue to penetrate deeper tissues through liquefactive necrosis, causing progressive damage, whereas acids form a protein barrier that limits further penetration.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition after initial irrigation and stabilization?

    Urgent ophthalmology consultation

    Chemical eye injuries, especially alkali burns with corneal haziness, require urgent ophthalmology evaluation for ongoing management and to assess for limbal ischemia.

  16. Case 64: Electrical Burn From Workplace Injury

    A 29-year-old electrician sustains a high-voltage electrical injury while working on a live circuit. He has small entry and exit wound burns on his hand and foot, appearing deceptively minor. He complains of significant forearm pain out of proportion to the visible injury, and his urine appears dark red-brown.

    1. What does the dark red-brown urine suggest?

    Myoglobinuria from rhabdomyolysis

    High-voltage electrical injury causes deep muscle damage along the current pathway leading to rhabdomyolysis, with myoglobin release causing pigmented urine and risk of acute kidney injury.

    2. What complication should be suspected given pain out of proportion in the forearm?

    Compartment syndrome

    Deep tissue injury from high-voltage current, worse than the skin surface suggests, can cause muscle swelling within fascial compartments leading to compartment syndrome.

    3. What cardiac investigation is essential regardless of symptoms?

    ECG monitoring

    Electrical current passing through the body can cause arrhythmias including ventricular fibrillation, so continuous cardiac monitoring and an ECG are mandatory after any significant electrical injury.

    4. What is the fluid resuscitation goal in this patient given the suspected rhabdomyolysis?

    Aggressive IV fluids targeting higher urine output to prevent renal injury

    Electrical burns with myoglobinuria require more aggressive fluid resuscitation than surface burn area alone would suggest, aiming for brisk urine output to flush myoglobin and protect the kidneys.

Chapter 7: Abdominal and Gastrointestinal Emergencies (8 Cases)

  1. Case 65: Young Woman With Right Lower Quadrant Pain

    A 24-year-old woman presents with two days of worsening right lower quadrant pain, now constant, with anorexia and low-grade fever. She has migratory pain that started periumbilically. Her last menstrual period was three weeks ago and she is sexually active without contraception. Exam reveals right lower quadrant tenderness with guarding.

    1. List three items on the differential diagnosis given her demographic.

    Appendicitis, ectopic pregnancy, ovarian torsion or cyst

    Any woman of reproductive age with lower abdominal pain must have gynecologic causes considered alongside appendicitis given overlapping presentations and the danger of missing ectopic pregnancy.

    2. What single test must be done early regardless of other findings?

    Urine or serum beta-hCG

    Pregnancy status must be established first in any reproductive-age woman with abdominal pain because it changes the entire differential and imaging approach, particularly to exclude ectopic pregnancy.

    3. The pregnancy test is negative. What imaging is preferred to evaluate for appendicitis in this patient?

    CT abdomen/pelvis with contrast, or ultrasound as an alternative

    CT is highly sensitive and specific for appendicitis, though ultrasound may be preferred first-line in some settings to avoid radiation, particularly if gynecologic pathology is also suspected.

    4. Imaging confirms acute appendicitis without perforation. What is the management?

    Surgical consultation for appendectomy and IV antibiotics

    Confirmed appendicitis requires surgical referral for appendectomy along with broad-spectrum antibiotics covering gram-negative and anaerobic organisms.

  2. Case 66: Elderly Man With Sudden Severe Abdominal Pain

    A 74-year-old man with a history of atrial fibrillation on no anticoagulation presents with sudden onset severe diffuse abdominal pain three hours ago, out of proportion to a relatively benign exam. He has had one episode of bloody diarrhea. Vitals show HR 110, BP 128/78. His abdomen is soft with mild diffuse tenderness.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis given his risk factors and pain out of proportion to exam?

    Acute mesenteric ischemia

    Atrial fibrillation without anticoagulation predisposes to embolic occlusion of the mesenteric vessels, and severe pain disproportionate to a benign-appearing exam is the classic early presentation.

    2. What investigation is the imaging modality of choice to confirm this diagnosis?

    CT angiography of the abdomen

    CT angiography can directly visualize mesenteric vessel occlusion and is the preferred rapid diagnostic test when mesenteric ischemia is suspected.

    3. What laboratory finding, though nonspecific, may support this diagnosis?

    Elevated lactate

    An elevated serum lactate reflects bowel hypoperfusion and tissue ischemia, though a normal lactate does not exclude early mesenteric ischemia.

    4. What is the immediate next step in management once suspected?

    Urgent surgical consultation and vascular or interventional radiology involvement

    Mesenteric ischemia is a surgical emergency with high mortality, and early surgical and vascular consultation is critical to prevent bowel infarction while resuscitation continues.

  3. Case 67: Middle-Aged Woman With Right Upper Quadrant Pain

    A 52-year-old woman presents with two hours of severe right upper quadrant pain radiating to her right shoulder blade after eating a fatty meal, associated with nausea and vomiting. She is febrile at 38.6C, HR 100, with positive Murphy sign on exam. She has no jaundice.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Acute cholecystitis

    Fever, right upper quadrant pain after a fatty meal, and a positive Murphy sign are the classic clinical triad suggesting acute cholecystitis.

    2. What is the first-line imaging investigation?

    Right upper quadrant ultrasound

    Ultrasound is the preferred initial test for suspected cholecystitis as it is fast, avoids radiation, and can identify gallstones, wall thickening, and pericholecystic fluid.

    3. What laboratory tests should be ordered to assess for a complication such as choledocholithiasis?

    Liver enzymes, bilirubin, and lipase

    Elevated bilirubin and liver enzymes raise concern for common bile duct obstruction, while lipase screens for concurrent gallstone pancreatitis.

    4. What is the initial management while awaiting surgical consultation?

    IV fluids, analgesia, antiemetics, and empiric IV antibiotics

    Acute cholecystitis is managed with supportive care and antibiotics targeting biliary flora pending surgical evaluation for cholecystectomy.

  4. Case 68: Young Man With Epigastric Pain and Vomiting

    A 30-year-old man with a history of heavy alcohol use presents with severe constant epigastric pain radiating to his back, associated with repeated vomiting, starting after a night of heavy drinking. He is tachycardic at 115 and appears uncomfortable, leaning forward. His abdomen is tender epigastrically without rebound.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Acute pancreatitis

    Epigastric pain radiating to the back with a relieving forward-leaning posture following an alcohol binge is a classic presentation of acute pancreatitis.

    2. What laboratory test confirms this diagnosis?

    Lipase

    Serum lipase is more sensitive and specific than amylase for diagnosing acute pancreatitis and remains elevated longer in the disease course.

    3. Why should imaging not be relied on alone to exclude severe disease early?

    CT findings can lag behind clinical severity in the first 24 to 48 hours

    Early CT imaging can underestimate necrosis and severity, so clinical and laboratory criteria such as Ranson or BISAP scores guide initial severity assessment rather than imaging alone.

    4. What is the cornerstone of initial management?

    Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation, analgesia, and antiemetics

    Early goal-directed fluid resuscitation is the primary intervention shown to reduce complications in acute pancreatitis, alongside pain and nausea control.

  5. Case 69: Cirrhotic Patient With Hematemesis

    A 58-year-old man with known alcoholic cirrhosis presents with two episodes of large-volume hematemesis. He is pale and diaphoretic, HR 128, BP 84/50. He has palmar erythema, spider angiomata, and a distended abdomen with shifting dullness. His hemoglobin is 78 g/L.

    1. What is the most likely cause of his bleeding given his history?

    Bleeding esophageal varices

    Known cirrhosis with portal hypertension signs makes variceal hemorrhage the most likely and most immediately life-threatening cause of upper GI bleeding in this patient.

    2. What is the initial resuscitation priority?

    Airway protection and large-bore IV access with crystalloid and blood product resuscitation

    Massive hematemesis risks airway compromise from aspiration, so airway protection alongside aggressive volume and blood resuscitation is the immediate priority before definitive therapy.

    3. What pharmacologic therapy should be started early in suspected variceal bleeding?

    Octreotide and prophylactic antibiotics

    Octreotide reduces splanchnic blood flow and portal pressure, while prophylactic antibiotics such as ceftriaxone reduce mortality and rebleeding risk in cirrhotic patients with GI bleeding.

    4. What definitive intervention is required after resuscitation?

    Urgent upper endoscopy

    Endoscopy allows direct diagnosis and treatment of varices through banding or sclerotherapy and should be performed urgently once the patient is stabilized.

  6. Case 70: Elderly Woman on Anticoagulation With Melena

    An 81-year-old woman on warfarin for atrial fibrillation presents with three days of black tarry stools and progressive weakness. She denies vomiting blood. HR 102, BP 100/62. She appears pale with a soft nontender abdomen. Her INR returns at 4.8.

    1. What is the significance of melena in this presentation?

    Melena typically indicates an upper gastrointestinal bleed source

    Black tarry stool results from digested blood, most commonly indicating bleeding proximal to the ligament of Treitz, though it can occur from a slow right-sided colonic bleed as well.

    2. What immediate reversal strategy is indicated for her elevated INR with active bleeding?

    Prothrombin complex concentrate and vitamin K

    Life-threatening or significant bleeding on warfarin requires rapid reversal with PCC for immediate factor replacement plus vitamin K for sustained reversal.

    3. Name two initial investigations to guide resuscitation.

    Hemoglobin and type and crossmatch

    Hemoglobin establishes baseline severity while type and crossmatch ensures blood products are readily available given her ongoing bleeding and anticoagulation reversal needs.

    4. What definitive diagnostic and therapeutic procedure should be arranged once she is stabilized?

    Upper endoscopy

    Upper endoscopy identifies the bleeding source such as peptic ulcer disease and allows for hemostatic intervention in this likely upper GI bleed.

  7. Case 71: Young Man With Painless Hematochezia

    A 45-year-old man presents with several episodes of bright red blood per rectum over the past day, painless, without hematemesis. He feels lightheaded on standing. HR 108, BP 96/60 sitting, dropping further on standing. He has a history of diverticulosis noted on prior colonoscopy.

    1. What does his orthostatic vital sign change indicate?

    Significant volume depletion from blood loss

    A marked drop in blood pressure with standing reflects intravascular volume depletion, indicating a clinically significant amount of blood loss requiring resuscitation.

    2. What is the most likely cause of his bleeding given his history?

    Diverticular bleeding

    Diverticulosis is the most common cause of painless lower GI bleeding in adults, often presenting with sudden bright red hematochezia without abdominal pain.

    3. Despite the presentation, what must be excluded before assuming a lower source?

    Brisk upper GI bleed presenting as hematochezia

    A brisk upper GI bleed can transit quickly through the bowel and present as bright red blood per rectum, so an upper source should be considered, especially with hemodynamic instability, sometimes clarified by nasogastric aspirate or early upper endoscopy.

    4. What is the appropriate initial management after resuscitation begins?

    Type and crossmatch, IV fluids, and gastroenterology consultation for colonoscopy

    After hemodynamic stabilization, colonoscopy is the diagnostic and therapeutic procedure of choice for suspected lower GI bleeding once the bowel can be adequately prepared or urgently if bleeding continues.

  8. Case 72: Young Woman With NSAID Use and Epigastric Pain

    A 34-year-old woman with chronic low back pain treated with frequent ibuprofen presents with several weeks of epigastric discomfort and now one episode of coffee-ground emesis. She denies alcohol use. HR 92, BP 110/70. She appears mildly pale but hemodynamically stable with a soft abdomen.

    1. What is the most likely underlying diagnosis given her NSAID use?

    NSAID-induced peptic ulcer disease with upper GI bleeding

    Chronic NSAID use damages the gastric mucosal barrier and is a well-established risk factor for peptic ulcer formation and subsequent bleeding.

    2. What score can help risk-stratify her for need of urgent endoscopy versus outpatient management?

    Glasgow-Blatchford score

    The Glasgow-Blatchford score uses clinical and laboratory variables without requiring endoscopy to identify low-risk patients who may be safely managed as outpatients versus those needing urgent inpatient endoscopy.

    3. What medication class should be started empirically?

    Proton pump inhibitor

    Empiric PPI therapy reduces the risk of rebleeding and can downstage ulcer severity findings at subsequent endoscopy in suspected peptic ulcer bleeding.

    4. What advice regarding her chronic pain medication should be given going forward?

    Discontinue NSAIDs and consider alternative analgesics or gastroprotection if NSAIDs are unavoidable

    Stopping the offending NSAID is essential to ulcer healing and preventing recurrence, with gastroprotective co-therapy considered only if NSAID use cannot be avoided.

Chapter 8: Toxicology, Environmental Emergencies, and Anaphylaxis (12 Cases)

  1. Case 73: Intentional Acetaminophen Overdose

    A 19-year-old woman presents six hours after ingesting an estimated 15 grams of acetaminophen in a suicide attempt. She is currently asymptomatic with normal vitals. She reports no other co-ingestions but is tearful and states she wanted to end her life.

    1. What investigation determines need for treatment?

    Serum acetaminophen level plotted on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram

    A level drawn at four hours or later post-ingestion plotted against time on the nomogram determines whether treatment threshold has been crossed and antidote is required.

    2. What is the antidote if her level is above the treatment line?

    N-acetylcysteine

    N-acetylcysteine replenishes glutathione stores and prevents hepatotoxicity from the toxic acetaminophen metabolite NAPQI when given within the appropriate treatment window.

    3. What other laboratory tests should be ordered given the ingestion?

    Liver enzymes, INR, and creatinine

    Baseline liver function, coagulation, and renal function help assess for early hepatotoxicity and guide ongoing management and prognosis.

    4. What must be arranged before discharge given the intentional nature of the ingestion?

    Psychiatric assessment and safety planning

    Any intentional overdose requires a formal psychiatric evaluation for suicide risk and safety planning before the patient can be safely discharged.

  2. Case 74: Unresponsive Patient Found With Pinpoint Pupils

    A 26-year-old man is found unresponsive by a friend, with a needle nearby. He has RR 6, SpO2 84% on room air, and pinpoint pupils. He responds only to painful stimuli. His skin is cool and he has track marks on both arms.

    1. What toxidrome is present?

    Opioid toxidrome

    The combination of respiratory depression, miosis, and decreased level of consciousness with a history of injection drug use is classic for opioid overdose.

    2. What is the immediate management?

    Bag-valve-mask ventilation and naloxone

    Airway and breathing support takes priority, and naloxone rapidly reverses opioid-induced respiratory depression by competitively displacing the opioid from receptors.

    3. Why might repeated doses of naloxone or an infusion be needed?

    Naloxone has a shorter half-life than many opioids, risking re-sedation

    Long-acting opioids or high doses can outlast the effect of a single naloxone dose, so patients require observation and possibly repeat dosing or an infusion to prevent re-narcotization.

    4. What co-ingestion or additional exposure should be considered given current drug supply trends?

    Fentanyl or other potent synthetic opioid contamination

    Illicit opioid supplies are frequently contaminated with fentanyl or its analogues, which can cause more profound and prolonged toxicity requiring higher or repeated naloxone doses.

  3. Case 75: Elderly Man With Confusion and Anticholinergic Signs

    A 78-year-old man is brought in confused and agitated after apparently taking extra doses of his diphenhydramine for insomnia. He has dry flushed skin, dilated pupils, urinary retention, tachycardia at 128, and is picking at the air. His temperature is 38.9C.

    1. What toxidrome does this presentation represent?

    Anticholinergic toxidrome

    Dry flushed skin, mydriasis, urinary retention, tachycardia, hyperthermia, and agitated delirium together form the classic anticholinergic toxidrome, often summarized as hot as a hare, dry as a bone, red as a beet, blind as a bat, and mad as a hatter.

    2. What ECG finding should specifically be assessed for given diphenhydramine's properties?

    QRS widening from sodium channel blockade

    Diphenhydramine has sodium channel blocking properties similar to tricyclic antidepressants, so QRS widening should be assessed as it indicates risk of ventricular arrhythmia.

    3. If QRS widening is present, what is the treatment?

    Sodium bicarbonate

    Sodium bicarbonate increases extracellular sodium concentration and alkalinizes serum, counteracting sodium channel blockade and narrowing the QRS complex.

    4. What management addresses his agitation and hyperthermia?

    Benzodiazepines and active cooling measures

    Benzodiazepines reduce agitation and associated hyperthermia from muscular activity, while active cooling directly addresses his elevated temperature. Physostigmine is generally avoided here despite being useful for pure anticholinergic delirium, since this patient already has diphenhydramine-induced sodium channel blockade with QRS widening, and physostigmine can precipitate asystole or seizures in that setting.

  4. Case 76: Adolescent With ASA Overdose

    A 17-year-old girl presents four hours after ingesting a large quantity of aspirin tablets. She is tachypneic, RR 32, and complains of tinnitus and nausea. She is anxious and diaphoretic. Her venous blood gas shows a mixed respiratory alkalosis and metabolic acidosis.

    1. What acid-base pattern is classically seen in salicylate toxicity?

    Mixed respiratory alkalosis and anion gap metabolic acidosis

    Salicylates directly stimulate the respiratory center causing hyperventilation and respiratory alkalosis, while simultaneously causing an anion gap metabolic acidosis from uncoupling oxidative phosphorylation, together producing this classic mixed picture.

    2. What investigation confirms the diagnosis and guides severity?

    Serum salicylate level

    A quantitative salicylate level confirms toxic ingestion and helps guide the intensity of treatment, with serial levels needed since absorption can be delayed.

    3. What is the mainstay treatment to enhance elimination?

    Urinary alkalinization with sodium bicarbonate infusion

    Alkalinizing the urine ion-traps salicylate in its ionized form, preventing renal tubular reabsorption and significantly enhancing elimination.

    4. What is a critical error to avoid regarding her ventilation if intubation becomes necessary?

    Avoid under-ventilating her, since compensatory hyperventilation must be maintained or worsening acidosis can be fatal

    If intubated, failure to match her compensatory respiratory alkalosis with adequate ventilator settings can cause a rapid drop in serum pH and precipitous clinical deterioration.

  5. Case 77: Marathon Runner With Altered Mental Status

    A 28-year-old man collapses near the finish line of a summer marathon. He is confused, combative, and his skin is hot and dry. Rectal temperature is 41.2C. HR 140, BP 92/58. He has stopped sweating despite the heat and exertion.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Exertional heat stroke

    Core temperature above 40C with altered mental status following strenuous exertion in a hot environment defines exertional heat stroke, a true medical emergency.

    2. What is the single most important immediate intervention?

    Rapid cooling, ideally cold water immersion

    The speed of cooling is the primary determinant of outcome in heat stroke, and cold water immersion is the fastest and most effective method available.

    3. What target temperature and timeframe should cooling aim for?

    Cool to approximately 38.5 to 39C within 30 minutes

    Rapid cooling to this target within the first 30 minutes has been shown to significantly reduce mortality and organ damage in heat stroke.

    4. Name one laboratory complication that should be monitored for in exertional heat stroke.

    Rhabdomyolysis (or coagulopathy or acute kidney injury)

    Severe heat stress and muscle breakdown from exertion can precipitate rhabdomyolysis, disseminated intravascular coagulation, and acute kidney injury, all of which require monitoring and management.

  6. Case 78: Hiker Found With Severe Hypothermia

    A 55-year-old man is found by search and rescue after being lost overnight in freezing temperatures. He is minimally responsive, with a core temperature of 27C. He has a slow, faint pulse and is breathing shallowly. His ECG shows Osborn waves.

    1. What ECG finding is characteristic of hypothermia and what does it represent?

    Osborn (J) waves, a positive deflection at the J point

    Osborn waves are a hallmark ECG finding in hypothermia, appearing as an extra deflection at the junction of the QRS complex and ST segment, and their prominence often correlates with the degree of hypothermia.

    2. Given his severity, what rewarming method is indicated?

    Active internal (core) rewarming, such as warmed IV fluids and extracorporeal rewarming if available

    Severe hypothermia with an unstable patient requires active internal rewarming methods, and extracorporeal membrane oxygenation or cardiopulmonary bypass is the most effective option when accessible for cardiac arrest or severe cases.

    3. Why must this patient be handled gently during assessment and transport?

    Risk of precipitating ventricular fibrillation from a hypothermic irritable myocardium

    The hypothermic heart is highly irritable and prone to ventricular fibrillation with rough handling or unnecessary movement, so gentle handling is critical throughout care.

    4. If he were found in cardiac arrest, what important principle guides resuscitation decisions?

    A patient is not considered dead until warm and dead, continue resuscitation during rewarming

    Hypothermia can be neuroprotective, and patients have survived prolonged arrest with good outcomes after rewarming, so resuscitation efforts should continue until adequate core rewarming is achieved before declaring death.

  7. Case 79: Scuba Diver With Joint Pain and Neurological Symptoms

    A 33-year-old recreational diver surfaces rapidly after a dive to 30 meters due to a low air supply alarm. Within an hour he develops severe shoulder and knee joint pain, along with new onset weakness in both legs and difficulty urinating. He denies chest pain.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Decompression sickness

    A rapid ascent from depth causing nitrogen bubble formation in tissues and the spinal cord classically presents with joint pain and neurological deficits shortly after surfacing.

    2. What definitive treatment is required given his neurological symptoms?

    Hyperbaric oxygen therapy (recompression)

    Hyperbaric oxygen recompresses and reduces bubble size while improving tissue oxygenation, and is the definitive treatment especially for neurological decompression sickness.

    3. What immediate measure should be started while arranging transfer to a hyperbaric facility?

    High-flow 100% oxygen and IV fluids

    High concentration oxygen helps reduce nitrogen bubble size and improve oxygen delivery to ischemic tissue, while IV fluids address hemoconcentration commonly seen in decompression sickness.

    4. What must be avoided during transport of this patient to a hyperbaric center?

    Avoid air transport at altitude, or use a pressurized aircraft, or fly at the lowest safe altitude

    Ascending to altitude further reduces ambient pressure and can worsen bubble expansion and symptoms, so unpressurized air transport at altitude must be avoided.

  8. Case 80: Construction Worker With High-Voltage Electrical Contact

    A 41-year-old man contacts a high-voltage power line while operating a crane. He is found unconscious with visible entry and exit burn wounds. On arrival his cardiac monitor shows ventricular fibrillation. Bystander CPR was started immediately.

    1. What is the first management priority given his rhythm?

    Immediate defibrillation and continued ACLS

    Ventricular fibrillation from high-voltage electrical injury is treated identically to any other cause, with immediate defibrillation and standard ACLS protocol taking priority.

    2. What associated injury must always be assumed and evaluated in electrical injuries of this nature?

    Cervical spine and other trauma from a fall or violent muscle contraction

    High-voltage shocks can cause violent muscle contractions or falls from height, so trauma including cervical spine injury must be assumed and evaluated alongside the electrical injury itself.

    3. What investigation should be checked to assess for muscle breakdown from the current pathway?

    Creatine kinase and urinalysis for myoglobinuria

    Current traveling through deep tissue can cause extensive muscle necrosis, so creatine kinase and urine myoglobin assessment guide fluid resuscitation to prevent renal injury.

    4. Once return of spontaneous circulation is achieved, what should be arranged next?

    Post-arrest care including targeted temperature management consideration and burn or trauma surgery consultation

    Following ROSC, standard post-cardiac arrest care applies, and given his severe burns and trauma risk, early surgical involvement for burn and possible compartment syndrome management is essential.

  9. Case 81: Child With Peanut Exposure at School

    An 8-year-old boy develops facial swelling, hives, and vomiting within minutes of accidentally eating a peanut-containing snack at school. On arrival he has audible stridor, is anxious, HR 130, BP 88/54, and diffuse urticaria. He has a known peanut allergy but did not have his auto-injector with him.

    1. What is the diagnosis and what criteria support it?

    Anaphylaxis, meeting criteria with skin findings plus respiratory and cardiovascular involvement after a likely allergen exposure

    Anaphylaxis is diagnosed clinically when skin or mucosal involvement occurs with either respiratory compromise or hypotension following exposure to a likely allergen, as seen here with stridor and hypotension.

    2. What is the immediate first-line treatment?

    Intramuscular epinephrine

    Intramuscular epinephrine in the anterolateral thigh is the first-line and most time-critical treatment for anaphylaxis, and should never be delayed for antihistamines or steroids.

    3. What adjunct therapies should also be given?

    IV fluids, antihistamines, and corticosteroids

    IV fluids support blood pressure while antihistamines and corticosteroids are adjuncts that address cutaneous symptoms and may reduce the risk of a biphasic reaction, though they do not replace epinephrine.

    4. What must be arranged before discharge given the risk of recurrence?

    Observation period, prescription for an epinephrine auto-injector, and an allergy action plan with follow-up

    Patients require observation for biphasic reactions and must be discharged with an epinephrine auto-injector, clear instructions, and allergy specialist follow-up given his known allergy.

  10. Case 82: Adult With Isolated Gastrointestinal Symptoms After Shellfish

    A 36-year-old woman presents with severe abdominal cramping, vomiting, and diarrhea that began fifteen minutes after eating shrimp at a restaurant. She has no visible hives or lip swelling and denies difficulty breathing. She reports a similar milder reaction to shellfish once before. HR 108, BP 82/50.

    1. Should this presentation without skin findings be considered anaphylaxis?

    Yes, anaphylaxis can be diagnosed with severe GI symptoms plus hypotension after a likely allergen even without skin involvement

    Per NIAID/FAAN criteria, anaphylaxis can be diagnosed when two organ systems are involved after exposure to a likely allergen: here her severe GI symptoms (cramping, vomiting, diarrhea) plus a hypotensive blood pressure of 82/50 (systolic <90) both qualify, even without any skin findings, avoiding a missed or delayed diagnosis.

    2. What is the first-line treatment given this recognition?

    Intramuscular epinephrine

    Once anaphylaxis is recognized regardless of the specific symptom combination, intramuscular epinephrine remains the immediate first-line treatment.

    3. What is a key reason clinicians may under-treat presentations like this one?

    Absence of urticaria or angioedema can lead to under-recognition and diagnostic delay

    Incomplete or atypical anaphylaxis presentations lacking the classic skin findings are a well-documented cause of delayed epinephrine administration and worse outcomes.

    4. What follow-up should be arranged given her recurrent shellfish reactions?

    Referral to an allergist and prescription of an epinephrine auto-injector

    A second confirmed reaction to shellfish warrants formal allergy assessment and ensures she has emergency medication available for future accidental exposures.

  11. Case 83: Patient With Biphasic Reaction After Bee Sting

    A 47-year-old man was stung by a bee two hours ago, developed hives and throat tightness, and was treated in the field with epinephrine and antihistamines by a bystander with improvement. He now returns to the emergency department four hours after the initial reaction with recurrent hives, wheeze, and hypotension despite feeling well in between.

    1. What phenomenon does this recurrence represent?

    Biphasic anaphylactic reaction

    A biphasic reaction is a recurrence of anaphylactic symptoms after apparent resolution, typically occurring within several hours without any further allergen exposure.

    2. What is the immediate management of this recurrence?

    Repeat intramuscular epinephrine

    A biphasic reaction is treated identically to the initial anaphylactic episode, with repeat epinephrine as the first-line therapy regardless of prior treatment.

    3. What observation period is generally recommended after an anaphylactic reaction given this risk?

    Typically 4 to 6 hours, longer if severe or risk factors present

    Because biphasic reactions can occur up to many hours after the initial event, an observation period of several hours is recommended, extended further for severe reactions or those requiring multiple epinephrine doses.

    4. What discharge plan is appropriate after this biphasic episode resolves?

    Extended observation, epinephrine auto-injector prescription, and clear return precautions with allergy follow-up

    Given the demonstrated biphasic course, this patient needs longer observation, ensured access to epinephrine, explicit instructions to return for any recurrence, and outpatient allergy referral.

  12. Case 84: Postoperative Patient With Intraoperative Anaphylaxis

    A 55-year-old woman develops sudden hypotension, bronchospasm, and flushing shortly after induction of general anesthesia for an elective procedure. Her BP drops to 70/40 and peak airway pressures rise sharply. The anesthesia team suspects a drug reaction to a neuromuscular blocking agent.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis in this intraoperative setting?

    Anaphylaxis to a perioperative medication, most commonly a neuromuscular blocking agent

    Neuromuscular blocking agents are the most common cause of perioperative anaphylaxis, presenting with sudden cardiovascular collapse and bronchospasm shortly after administration.

    2. What is the immediate treatment?

    Intravenous epinephrine titrated to effect, given the operative and monitored setting

    In a monitored perioperative setting with IV access already established and ongoing severe hypotension, titrated IV epinephrine is used rather than the standard intramuscular dose used outside hospital settings.

    3. What laboratory test drawn shortly after the event can help confirm anaphylaxis retrospectively?

    Serum tryptase level

    Serum tryptase rises during mast cell degranulation in anaphylaxis and, when drawn within a few hours of the event, helps confirm the diagnosis retrospectively.

    4. What must be arranged for this patient after recovery to prevent future perioperative reactions?

    Referral to an allergist for perioperative drug allergy testing and clear documentation of the suspected agent

    Identifying the specific causative agent through allergy testing and clearly documenting it in her chart is essential to avoid re-exposure during any future anesthetic.

Chapter 9: Obstetric, Pediatric, and Vulnerable Populations (16 Cases)

  1. Case 85: 28-Year-Old Woman, 7 Weeks Pregnant, with Vaginal Bleeding and Unilateral Abdominal Pain

    A 28-year-old G2P1 woman presents with 2 days of light vaginal spotting and new right lower quadrant pain that started 3 hours ago. Her last menstrual period was 7 weeks ago. She has a history of chlamydia treated 2 years ago. Vitals: HR 104, BP 108/68, RR 18, afebrile. Abdominal exam shows mild right adnexal tenderness without peritoneal signs. Speculum exam shows scant blood, cervical os closed. A urine beta-hCG is positive.

    1. List three items on your differential diagnosis.

    Ectopic pregnancy, threatened miscarriage, corpus luteum cyst

    A positive pregnancy test with unilateral pain and bleeding must always trigger consideration of ectopic pregnancy first, alongside more benign causes.

    2. What is the single most important investigation to order now?

    Transvaginal pelvic ultrasound

    Ultrasound is required to localize the pregnancy and rule out ectopic implantation before any reassurance can be given.

    3. If ultrasound shows no intrauterine or adnexal pregnancy and the patient is hemodynamically stable, what is this finding called and what is the next step?

    Pregnancy of unknown location; serial quantitative beta-hCG in 48 hours

    A single ultrasound cannot definitively rule out ectopic pregnancy; serial beta-hCG trending helps determine if the pregnancy is viable, failing, or ectopic.

    4. What additional test result determines whether she needs Rh immunoglobulin?

    Rh (blood) type; give if Rh-negative

    Rh-negative women with any first-trimester bleeding should receive Rh immunoglobulin to prevent alloimmunization in future pregnancies.

  2. Case 86: 32-Year-Old Woman, 6 Weeks Pregnant, with Heavy Bleeding and Passage of Clots

    A 32-year-old woman presents with 6 hours of heavy vaginal bleeding with clots and crampy suprapubic pain. She confirmed pregnancy 2 weeks ago at 4 weeks gestation by home test. She has soaked 3 pads in the last 4 hours. Vitals: HR 96, BP 112/70, afebrile. Speculum exam reveals blood and clot in the vaginal vault with the cervical os open and tissue visible at the os.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Inevitable/incomplete miscarriage

    An open cervical os with passage of tissue in the setting of heavy bleeding indicates the pregnancy cannot continue.

    2. What immediate bedside intervention should be performed if bleeding is heavy and tissue is visible at the os?

    Ring forceps removal of tissue from the os

    Removing retained products of conception visible at the cervical os often reduces ongoing bleeding and cramping quickly.

    3. List two investigations to order.

    CBC, blood type and Rh screen

    Hemoglobin assesses degree of blood loss and Rh status determines need for immunoglobulin, both essential before disposition.

    4. What outpatient options should be discussed for management of an incomplete miscarriage?

    Expectant management, medical management with misoprostol, or surgical dilation and curettage

    All three approaches are evidence-based options and the choice should be shared with the patient based on her preference and clinical stability.

  3. Case 87: 24-Year-Old Woman with Syncope, Unknown Last Menstrual Period, and Vaginal Bleeding

    A 24-year-old woman is brought in by EMS after collapsing at home. She reports 1 day of light spotting and severe diffuse abdominal pain with shoulder tip pain. She is unsure of her last menstrual period. Vitals: HR 128, BP 82/50, RR 24, pale and diaphoretic. Abdomen is diffusely tender with guarding. A bedside urine beta-hCG is positive.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis and why is shoulder tip pain significant?

    Ruptured ectopic pregnancy; shoulder tip pain indicates diaphragmatic irritation from hemoperitoneum

    Referred shoulder pain from free intraperitoneal blood is a classic sign of significant hemorrhage from a ruptured ectopic.

    2. What is your immediate management priority?

    Resuscitate with IV crystalloid, activate massive transfusion protocol if needed, emergent obstetrics/gynecology and surgical consult

    This patient is in hemorrhagic shock and requires simultaneous resuscitation and emergent surgical intervention rather than awaiting confirmatory imaging.

    3. Should you wait for formal ultrasound before consulting surgery?

    No

    In an unstable patient with a positive pregnancy test and signs of hemoperitoneum, clinical diagnosis and immediate surgical consultation take priority over imaging confirmation.

    4. What bedside test can support the diagnosis while awaiting definitive care?

    Bedside FAST ultrasound for free fluid

    FAST can rapidly confirm hemoperitoneum in the resuscitation bay without delaying transfer to the operating room.

  4. Case 88: 35-Year-Old Woman, Uncertain Dates, with Light Spotting and Indeterminate Ultrasound

    A 35-year-old G3P2 woman presents with 1 day of light brown spotting and no pain. Her dates are uncertain due to irregular cycles, with a last menstrual period suggesting roughly 5 to 6 weeks gestation. Vitals are normal. Speculum exam shows scant old blood, closed os. Transvaginal ultrasound shows an intrauterine gestational sac without a clearly visible fetal pole or yolk sac. Beta-hCG is 1800 IU/L.

    1. What is this ultrasound finding classified as?

    Pregnancy of uncertain viability

    An intrauterine sac without a visible embryo at this hCG level is too early to call definitively viable or nonviable.

    2. What is the appropriate next step in management?

    Repeat transvaginal ultrasound in 7 to 14 days with serial beta-hCG

    Guidelines caution against declaring nonviability from a single early scan; repeat imaging avoids inappropriately terminating a viable pregnancy.

    3. What discharge advice should be given regarding activity and follow-up?

    Pelvic rest, return precautions for heavy bleeding or pain, scheduled repeat ultrasound

    Clear return precautions ensure she seeks care promptly if symptoms worsen while awaiting definitive imaging.

    4. What test should be checked before discharge to determine need for prophylaxis?

    Rh type

    Rh-negative patients with any bleeding in pregnancy require immunoglobulin regardless of eventual pregnancy outcome.

  5. Case 89: 32-Week Pregnant Woman with Headache and Normal Blood Pressure

    A 29-year-old woman at 32 weeks gestation presents with 2 days of persistent frontal headache and one episode of vomiting. She denies visual changes. Vitals: BP 128/82, HR 88, afebrile. Fundal height is appropriate. There is no papilledema. She has mild right upper quadrant tenderness on exam.

    1. Despite a normal blood pressure, why must pre-eclampsia remain on your differential?

    Atypical or normotensive presentations of pre-eclampsia and HELLP syndrome occur and can progress rapidly

    Relying solely on blood pressure to screen out pre-eclampsia misses cases that present primarily with headache, epigastric pain, or visual symptoms.

    2. List three investigations to order.

    Urine protein-to-creatinine ratio, CBC with platelets, liver enzymes (AST/ALT)

    These tests screen for proteinuria, thrombocytopenia, and hepatic involvement seen in pre-eclampsia and HELLP syndrome.

    3. What diagnosis must be ruled out first given the right upper quadrant tenderness?

    HELLP syndrome

    Right upper quadrant pain in pregnancy with nonspecific symptoms should prompt urgent evaluation for HELLP given its high morbidity if missed.

    4. Who should be consulted urgently regardless of blood pressure reading?

    Obstetrics

    Suspected pre-eclampsia or HELLP requires obstetric involvement for maternal and fetal monitoring and delivery timing decisions.

  6. Case 90: 38-Week Pregnant Woman with Severe Hypertension and Proteinuria

    A 34-year-old G1P0 woman at 38 weeks gestation presents with a severe headache and swelling of her hands and face over 3 days. Vitals: BP 168/108, HR 92, RR 18. Urine dipstick shows 3+ protein. She has brisk deep tendon reflexes. Fetal heart rate is 150 with reactive tracing.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Pre-eclampsia with severe features

    Severe range blood pressure plus significant proteinuria and hyperreflexia meets criteria for severe pre-eclampsia.

    2. What is your immediate first-line antihypertensive management?

    IV labetalol or hydralazine

    These agents rapidly lower severe range blood pressure in pregnancy while preserving uteroplacental perfusion.

    3. What medication should be given to prevent seizures?

    Magnesium sulfate

    Magnesium sulfate is the standard of care for seizure prophylaxis in severe pre-eclampsia.

    4. What is the definitive treatment for severe pre-eclampsia at this gestational age?

    Delivery of the fetus

    At 38 weeks with severe features, expedited delivery is indicated as the only definitive treatment for pre-eclampsia.

  7. Case 91: 34-Week Pregnant Woman with Epigastric Pain and Nausea

    A 27-year-old woman at 34 weeks gestation presents with 1 day of epigastric pain, nausea, and vomiting that she attributes to heartburn. Vitals: BP 142/94, HR 100. Exam reveals epigastric tenderness without rebound. Labs show platelets 90, AST 210, LDH elevated.

    1. What diagnosis do these findings support?

    HELLP syndrome

    Hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets in a pregnant patient with epigastric pain define HELLP syndrome.

    2. Why is this presentation dangerous if attributed only to heartburn?

    HELLP can be mistaken for a benign gastrointestinal complaint, delaying recognition of a life-threatening obstetric emergency

    Nonspecific gastrointestinal symptoms are a well-documented trap that delays HELLP diagnosis and worsens maternal outcomes.

    3. List two additional investigations needed to complete workup.

    Peripheral blood smear for schistocytes, coagulation profile

    Schistocytes confirm microangiopathic hemolysis and coagulation studies assess for DIC, both important in HELLP evaluation.

    4. What is the immediate disposition?

    Urgent obstetric consultation for admission and consideration of delivery

    HELLP syndrome requires urgent obstetric management as maternal and fetal complications can progress rapidly.

  8. Case 92: Postpartum Woman with Seizure Three Days After Delivery

    A 31-year-old woman presents 3 days after an uncomplicated vaginal delivery with a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure at home lasting 2 minutes, now postictal. She had no hypertension during pregnancy. Vitals on arrival: BP 152/98, HR 104. She is confused but arousable.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Postpartum eclampsia

    Eclampsia can occur up to several weeks postpartum even without a prior history of hypertension in pregnancy.

    2. What is the first-line treatment to prevent recurrent seizures?

    Magnesium sulfate

    Magnesium sulfate remains first-line for both treatment and prevention of recurrent eclamptic seizures, including postpartum.

    3. List two other diagnoses to consider in a postpartum seizure.

    Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis, intracranial hemorrhage

    The postpartum state is a hypercoagulable period and these diagnoses must be excluded, especially with atypical or refractory seizures.

    4. What imaging should be considered if the presentation is atypical or she fails to improve?

    CT or MRI brain with venous imaging

    Imaging is warranted to exclude venous sinus thrombosis or hemorrhage when the clinical course does not follow the typical eclampsia pattern.

  9. Case 93: 3-Week-Old Infant with Fever of 38.2C

    A 3-week-old male infant is brought in for fever measured at home as 38.2C rectal. He is feeding slightly less but has wet diapers. Vitals: HR 168, RR 44, temp 38.3C in the department. He is fussy but consolable, with a soft anterior fontanelle and no rash.

    1. Why does this infant's age alone mandate a full workup regardless of appearance?

    Infants under 60 days have immature immune systems and higher risk of serious bacterial infection even when well-appearing

    Age-based risk stratification, not clinical appearance alone, drives the decision for full sepsis workup in neonates.

    2. List the components of a full septic workup in this age group.

    Blood culture, urine culture (catheter specimen), CBC, lumbar puncture with CSF studies

    All four components are needed to adequately screen for bacteremia, urinary tract infection, and meningitis in this high-risk age group.

    3. What is the appropriate empiric antibiotic management while awaiting results?

    Empiric IV ampicillin and cefotaxime (or gentamicin), admit

    Empiric broad-spectrum coverage for common neonatal pathogens including Listeria and group B Streptococcus is started pending culture results.

    4. Is outpatient management appropriate for this infant even if all initial tests are reassuring?

    No

    Infants under 28 to 60 days with fever are generally admitted for observation and empiric therapy regardless of initial reassuring tests, per age-based protocols.

  10. Case 94: 18-Month-Old with Fever and Diffuse Viral Rash

    An 18-month-old girl presents with 2 days of fever to 39C, mild cough, and a diffuse maculopapular rash that started on her trunk yesterday. She is playful, drinking well, and interactive. Vitals: HR 130, RR 26, temp 38.9C. No petechiae, no meningismus, capillary refill under 2 seconds.

    1. What features support a viral exanthem over a more concerning bacterial illness?

    Well appearance, normal perfusion, maculopapular non-petechial rash, no meningismus

    These reassuring clinical features distinguish a benign viral exanthem from serious bacterial infections presenting with rash.

    2. What is the appropriate antibiotic management?

    None; supportive care only

    Antibiotics are not indicated for a likely viral illness and unnecessary use contributes to antimicrobial resistance.

    3. How should antipyretic dosing be determined for this child?

    Weight-based dosing, not age-based

    Acetaminophen and ibuprofen dosing must be calculated by weight to ensure safety and efficacy rather than using age-based approximations.

    4. What return precautions should be given to the caregiver?

    Return if petechial rash develops, lethargy, poor intake, or difficulty breathing

    Clear safety netting ensures early return if the illness evolves into something more serious than a benign viral exanthem.

  11. Case 95: 4-Year-Old with Fever and Petechial Rash

    A 4-year-old boy presents with 6 hours of high fever, lethargy, and a rapidly spreading petechial rash on his legs and trunk. Vitals: HR 168, BP 78/44, RR 32, temp 39.8C, capillary refill 4 seconds. He is difficult to rouse and has neck stiffness.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Meningococcemia

    A rapidly spreading petechial rash with fever, shock, and meningismus in a child is classic for meningococcal sepsis until proven otherwise.

    2. What is your immediate first step in management?

    IV/IO access, fluid resuscitation, and empiric antibiotics without delay

    Antibiotics must not be delayed for confirmatory testing given the rapid mortality of meningococcemia; resuscitation is initiated simultaneously.

    3. Name the empiric antibiotic of choice.

    Ceftriaxone

    Ceftriaxone provides appropriate empiric coverage for Neisseria meningitidis and other likely pathogens in pediatric bacterial sepsis.

    4. What public health action is required for close contacts?

    Post-exposure chemoprophylaxis for close contacts (e.g., rifampin or ciprofloxacin) and public health notification

    Meningococcal disease is reportable and close contacts require prophylaxis to prevent secondary cases.

  12. Case 96: 3-Month-Old, Well-Appearing, with Fever

    A 3-month-old female infant presents with fever to 38.4C for 12 hours. She is breastfeeding well and has normal urine output. Vitals: HR 150, RR 36, temp 38.5C. She appears well, is smiling, and has a normal exam including a soft fontanelle. Her mother reports no sick contacts.

    1. Does a well appearance eliminate the need for investigations in this age group?

    No

    Well-appearing infants aged 29 to 90 days can still harbor serious bacterial infection, so risk stratification tools are required rather than clinical gestalt alone.

    2. Name a validated tool used to risk-stratify febrile infants in this age range.

    Step-by-Step approach or PECARN prediction rule

    These validated clinical decision tools combine age, appearance, and laboratory markers to identify infants at low risk of serious bacterial infection.

    3. List the baseline investigations that should be performed.

    Urinalysis and urine culture, CBC, blood culture, inflammatory markers (procalcitonin/CRP)

    These tests are the foundation of risk stratification tools used to decide whether lumbar puncture and admission are needed.

    4. If all criteria are met for low risk, what disposition may be considered?

    Outpatient management with close follow-up, without lumbar puncture or antibiotics

    Validated low-risk criteria allow safe outpatient management with close follow-up, avoiding unnecessary invasive testing.

  13. Case 97: 29-Year-Old Woman with Bruising in Various Stages of Healing

    A 29-year-old woman presents for a minor hand laceration. On exam you note bruising over her upper arms and back in various stages of healing that she attributes to 'being clumsy.' She appears anxious and avoids eye contact when you ask about her home situation. Her partner is waiting in the hallway and repeatedly enters the room during the visit.

    1. What features raise concern for intimate partner violence?

    Bruising in multiple stages of healing, inconsistent explanation, partner's controlling behavior

    These are recognized red flags that should prompt a dedicated, private screening conversation for intimate partner violence.

    2. How should you arrange the environment to screen appropriately?

    Speak with the patient alone, without the partner present

    Screening must occur in private since disclosure is unsafe or unlikely if the potential abuser is present.

    3. What approach should you use when asking about abuse?

    Direct, non-judgmental, open-ended questions in a supportive manner

    A nonjudgmental approach increases the likelihood of disclosure and preserves the therapeutic relationship.

    4. What must be included in your documentation?

    Detailed description and body diagram of injuries, patient's own words, and objective findings

    Thorough, objective documentation is essential for the patient's safety planning and any future legal proceedings.

    5. If she discloses abuse but declines police involvement, what is your next step?

    Offer referral to social work and a domestic violence support service, and safety planning

    Respecting patient autonomy while connecting her to resources and multidisciplinary support is the appropriate approach when she does not want police involved.

  14. Case 98: 82-Year-Old Woman Brought in by Family with Weight Loss and Pressure Ulcers

    An 82-year-old woman with dementia is brought in by a neighbour who reports concern for her wellbeing. She has lost significant weight over 2 months, is unkempt, and has a stage 2 sacral pressure ulcer. Her son, who is her caregiver, states she 'just doesn't eat much anymore.' She is unable to give a reliable history.

    1. What is your leading concern given these findings?

    Elder neglect/abuse

    Unexplained weight loss, poor hygiene, and a pressure ulcer in a dependent elderly patient are recognized markers of neglect.

    2. List two investigations to assess her medical status.

    CBC and electrolytes, albumin/nutritional markers

    These help evaluate for dehydration, malnutrition, and underlying medical contributors while corroborating the concern for neglect.

    3. What is a key principle in your interview approach with a dependent elderly patient?

    Interview the patient separately from the caregiver when possible

    Speaking with the patient alone, when cognitively feasible, reduces the risk of caregiver influence over disclosure.

    4. What is your reporting obligation in suspected elder abuse or neglect?

    Report to adult protective services or the relevant local authority per jurisdictional requirements

    Most jurisdictions mandate or strongly encourage reporting suspected abuse or neglect of a vulnerable adult to protective services.

  15. Case 99: 8-Month-Old Infant with a Femur Fracture and Inconsistent History

    An 8-month-old non-ambulatory infant is brought in after his father reports he 'rolled off the couch.' X-ray shows a spiral femur fracture. The infant is not yet crawling or pulling to stand. There is a delay of 2 days between the reported injury and presentation. The infant is fussy but otherwise appears well nourished.

    1. Why is this history inconsistent with the injury pattern?

    A non-ambulatory infant cannot generate the rotational force typically needed for a spiral femur fracture from a simple fall

    Spiral fractures in non-mobile infants are highly specific for non-accidental trauma given the mechanism required to produce them.

    2. What additional workup is indicated?

    Skeletal survey, CT head, ophthalmology exam for retinal hemorrhages

    A full occult injury workup is required whenever non-accidental trauma is suspected, since additional injuries are common and often clinically silent.

    3. What is your reporting obligation?

    Mandatory report to child protective services

    Suspected child abuse must be reported by law regardless of parental consent or the physician's degree of certainty.

    4. What is the appropriate disposition while investigation is pending?

    Admit the infant to a safe environment pending child protection assessment

    The infant should not be discharged to a potentially unsafe environment before protective services can assess the situation.

  16. Case 100: 24-Year-Old Pregnant Woman with Abdominal Trauma from Reported 'Fall'

    A 24-year-old woman at 22 weeks gestation presents with abdominal pain after reportedly 'falling down the stairs.' She has bruising on her upper arms consistent with grip marks and appears withdrawn. Her partner insists on staying in the room and answers questions on her behalf. Fetal heart tones are present and normal.

    1. What population-specific risk factor makes screening for intimate partner violence essential here?

    Pregnancy is a recognized high-risk period for onset or escalation of intimate partner violence

    Violence during pregnancy poses risk to both maternal and fetal wellbeing and screening should be routine at every pregnancy-related visit.

    2. How should you separate her from her partner to allow disclosure?

    Use a pretext such as an ultrasound or private exam to speak with her alone

    Creating a private, safe opportunity for disclosure is essential when a partner is controlling the interaction.

    3. What obstetric investigation should be performed given the abdominal trauma?

    Fetal monitoring/cardiotocography and obstetric ultrasound for placental abruption

    Blunt abdominal trauma in pregnancy requires fetal monitoring to detect abruption or fetal distress that may not be immediately apparent.

    4. What multidisciplinary resources should be offered on disclosure?

    Social work, domestic violence advocate, and safety planning with obstetric follow-up

    A coordinated multidisciplinary approach addresses both the immediate safety needs and ongoing obstetric care.

Chapter 10: Shock, Sepsis, and Thromboembolic Disease (12 Cases)

  1. Case 101: 8-Month-Old Infant with Vomiting, Diarrhea, and Lethargy

    An 8-month-old infant presents with 2 days of vomiting and watery diarrhea, now lethargic and refusing feeds. Vitals: HR 190, RR 44, BP 70/40, capillary refill 4 seconds, sunken fontanelle, dry mucous membranes, no tears.

    1. What is the clinical diagnosis?

    Hypovolemic shock from severe dehydration

    Tachycardia, hypotension, delayed capillary refill, and sunken fontanelle together indicate decompensated hypovolemic shock in an infant.

    2. What is your immediate management?

    IV/IO access and rapid isotonic crystalloid bolus (normal saline or Ringer's lactate) 20 mL/kg

    Rapid isotonic fluid resuscitation is the priority intervention to restore circulating volume in hypovolemic shock.

    3. Why is isotonic fluid preferred over hypotonic fluid in this resuscitation?

    Hypotonic fluids risk precipitating hyponatremia and cerebral edema and do not expand intravascular volume as effectively

    Isotonic solutions remain in the intravascular space longer and avoid the electrolyte derangements associated with hypotonic fluids.

    4. What reassessment should follow each fluid bolus?

    Reassess heart rate, capillary refill, mental status, and urine output

    Frequent reassessment guides whether further boluses are needed and identifies non-hypovolemic causes if the child fails to respond.

  2. Case 102: 84-Year-Old Man with Confusion and Normal Temperature

    An 84-year-old man is brought from a long-term care facility with 1 day of decreased oral intake and new confusion. Temperature is 36.4C. Vitals: HR 108, BP 88/54, RR 24. He has a chronic indwelling urinary catheter with cloudy, foul-smelling urine. Skin is mottled on his lower extremities.

    1. Why should sepsis remain on your differential despite a normal temperature?

    Elderly patients often fail to mount a febrile response and can present with hypothermia or normothermia despite severe infection

    Atypical presentations of sepsis are common in the elderly, and relying on fever alone will miss significant infections in this population.

    2. What is the most likely source of infection?

    Catheter-associated urinary tract infection/urosepsis

    The indwelling catheter with cloudy foul-smelling urine strongly suggests a urinary source in this septic presentation.

    3. What is your immediate management?

    IV fluid resuscitation and early empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics

    Early aggressive fluid resuscitation and antibiotics within the first hour improve survival in septic shock regardless of patient age.

    4. If blood pressure fails to improve after adequate fluid resuscitation, what is the next step?

    Start vasopressor support, typically norepinephrine

    Norepinephrine is the first-line vasopressor for septic shock refractory to fluid resuscitation.

  3. Case 103: 19-Year-Old Athlete with Collapse During a Summer Marathon

    A 19-year-old male collapses near the finish line of a marathon on a hot humid day. He is confused and combative on EMS arrival. Vitals: HR 160, BP 90/56, RR 28, rectal temperature 41.2C. Skin is hot and dry. He has not urinated in several hours.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Exertional heat stroke

    Core temperature above 40C with altered mental status following exertion in a hot environment defines heat stroke.

    2. What is the single most important immediate intervention?

    Rapid cooling, ideally cold water immersion

    Rapid, aggressive cooling is the intervention most strongly linked to survival and reduces the duration of hyperthermia-induced organ damage.

    3. What fluid should be used for volume resuscitation and why?

    Isotonic crystalloid, since he is also volume depleted from dehydration

    Concurrent dehydration from sweat losses requires isotonic fluid resuscitation alongside active cooling measures.

    4. What complication should be monitored for given his temperature and presentation?

    Rhabdomyolysis and acute kidney injury

    Severe exertional heat stroke commonly causes muscle breakdown, and monitoring creatine kinase and renal function is essential.

  4. Case 104: 12-Year-Old Girl with Known Type 1 Diabetes and Vomiting

    A 12-year-old girl with type 1 diabetes presents with 2 days of vomiting, abdominal pain, and lethargy. Vitals: HR 132, BP 92/58, RR 32 and deep. Glucose is 28 mmol/L, and she has fruity breath. She is drowsy but arousable, with dry mucous membranes.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Diabetic ketoacidosis with dehydration

    Hyperglycemia, ketotic breath odor, deep respirations (Kussmaul breathing), and dehydration together define DKA.

    2. What is the priority initial fluid management step?

    Cautious isotonic saline bolus, avoiding overly rapid or excessive fluid administration

    Fluid resuscitation in pediatric DKA must be judicious because overly aggressive rehydration is associated with cerebral edema.

    3. What is the most feared neurological complication of pediatric DKA treatment and how is it minimized?

    Cerebral edema; minimized by gradual fluid correction and avoiding rapid glucose or osmolality shifts

    Cerebral edema is a leading cause of DKA-related mortality in children and is linked to rapid correction of fluid and electrolyte deficits.

    4. When should insulin infusion typically be started relative to fluid resuscitation?

    After initial fluid resuscitation has begun, usually delayed by about 1 hour

    Starting fluids before insulin helps prevent a precipitous drop in osmolality that can contribute to cerebral edema.

  5. Case 105: 45-Year-Old Man with Rapidly Spreading Leg Pain After a Minor Cut

    A 45-year-old man with diabetes presents with severe pain in his lower leg, out of proportion to a small scratch he sustained gardening 2 days ago. The skin is dusky with bullae and crepitus is palpable. Vitals: HR 128, BP 92/58, temp 38.9C. He appears toxic and confused.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Necrotizing fasciitis

    Pain out of proportion, bullae, crepitus, and systemic toxicity following minor trauma are classic red flags for necrotizing soft tissue infection.

    2. What is your immediate management?

    IV fluid resuscitation, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, and emergent surgical consultation for debridement

    Necrotizing fasciitis requires urgent surgical debridement as source control alongside resuscitation and antibiotics; delay increases mortality significantly.

    3. What does this case teach about 'minor' infections?

    Seemingly minor skin injuries can progress to life-threatening infection and must not be dismissed based on the trivial mechanism

    Recognizing red flag signs regardless of how minor the inciting injury seemed is critical to avoid missed or delayed diagnosis.

    4. What imaging, if any, should be obtained if the diagnosis is uncertain and it does not delay surgical consultation?

    CT of the affected limb showing fascial gas or fluid tracking

    Imaging can support the diagnosis in equivocal cases but should never delay emergent surgical consultation when clinical suspicion is high.

  6. Case 106: 26-Year-Old with Sore Throat and Cough

    A 26-year-old woman presents with 3 days of sore throat, rhinorrhea, and mild cough. She has no fever, no tonsillar exudate, and no cervical lymphadenopathy. Vitals are normal. She requests antibiotics because she 'always gets a chest infection.'

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Viral upper respiratory tract infection

    The absence of fever, exudate, and lymphadenopathy makes bacterial pharyngitis unlikely, favoring a self-limited viral illness.

    2. What clinical decision tool can help assess the likelihood of streptococcal pharyngitis?

    Centor criteria (modified)

    The Centor score uses fever, exudate, lymphadenopathy, and absence of cough to estimate probability of group A strep pharyngitis and guide testing or treatment.

    3. What is the appropriate antibiotic management?

    None; supportive care and symptomatic treatment

    Prescribing antibiotics for a likely viral illness provides no benefit and promotes antimicrobial resistance.

    4. How should you address her request for antibiotics?

    Explain the viral etiology and lack of benefit from antibiotics, and provide symptomatic care recommendations

    Patient education on antibiotic stewardship maintains trust while avoiding inappropriate prescribing.

  7. Case 107: 38-Year-Old Man with Fever, Headache, and Neck Stiffness

    A 38-year-old man presents with 12 hours of fever, severe headache, photophobia, and neck stiffness. Vitals: HR 118, BP 100/64, temp 39.4C. He has nuchal rigidity and a positive Kernig sign. There is no rash.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Bacterial meningitis

    Fever, headache, photophobia, and meningismus in an acutely ill adult are classic for bacterial meningitis.

    2. What is the priority in management timing?

    Give empiric antibiotics immediately, without waiting for lumbar puncture or CT results

    Delaying antibiotics for imaging or lumbar puncture significantly worsens outcomes; empiric therapy should be given as soon as bacterial meningitis is suspected.

    3. Name the empiric antibiotic regimen for a healthy adult.

    Ceftriaxone plus vancomycin (add ampicillin if Listeria risk)

    This combination covers the most common bacterial pathogens causing meningitis in adults, including resistant Streptococcus pneumoniae.

    4. What adjunctive medication should be considered alongside antibiotics?
