    Dexamethasone

    Adjunctive corticosteroids given with or before the first antibiotic dose reduce neurological complications in bacterial meningitis.

  8. Case 108: Nurse with Needlestick Injury from a Patient with Unknown HIV Status

    A 29-year-old nurse presents 1 hour after sustaining a needlestick injury while drawing blood from a patient with unknown HIV and hepatitis status. The needle was visibly contaminated with blood. She has washed the site with soap and water.

    1. What immediate wound care was appropriately performed?

    Washing the site with soap and water

    Prompt cleansing of the exposure site is the correct first step, avoiding harmful practices such as squeezing or using caustic agents.

    2. What is the time-sensitive medication decision that must be made?

    Whether to start HIV post-exposure prophylaxis

    HIV PEP is most effective when started as soon as possible, ideally within hours and no later than 72 hours after exposure.

    3. What baseline investigations should be done on both the nurse and, if possible, the source patient?

    Baseline HIV, hepatitis B and C serology, and source patient testing if consent obtainable

    Baseline testing establishes pre-exposure status and helps guide need for ongoing prophylaxis and follow-up testing.

    4. What follow-up is required after starting prophylaxis?

    Occupational health follow-up with repeat serology at intervals and monitoring for medication side effects

    Structured follow-up ensures medication adherence, monitors for seroconversion, and manages side effects of prophylactic therapy.

  9. Case 109: 58-Year-Old Man with Unilateral Leg Swelling After Knee Surgery

    A 58-year-old man presents with 2 days of right calf swelling and pain, 10 days after knee replacement surgery. He is otherwise well. Vitals are normal. Exam shows calf circumference 4 cm greater than the left side, with tenderness along the deep venous system.

    1. What clinical decision rule should be applied?

    Wells score for DVT

    The Wells score stratifies pretest probability of DVT and guides the appropriate diagnostic pathway.

    2. Given his recent surgery, what is his pretest probability category?

    High probability

    Recent major surgery combined with unilateral swelling and tenderness places him in a high pretest probability category on the Wells score.

    3. What is the appropriate first-line diagnostic test given his risk category?

    Compression ultrasound of the leg (not D-dimer)

    D-dimer is not useful in high pretest probability patients due to poor specificity; imaging should proceed directly to compression ultrasound.

    4. If ultrasound confirms DVT, what is the initial management?

    Start therapeutic anticoagulation

    Confirmed DVT requires prompt initiation of anticoagulation to prevent clot propagation and pulmonary embolism.

  10. Case 110: 31-Year-Old Pregnant Woman with Sudden Dyspnea

    A 31-year-old woman at 26 weeks gestation presents with sudden onset shortness of breath and pleuritic chest pain. Vitals: HR 112, RR 26, SpO2 94% on room air, BP 118/74. She has mild swelling of the right calf.

    1. What is the leading diagnosis?

    Pulmonary embolism

    Pregnancy is a hypercoagulable state, and sudden dyspnea with pleuritic pain and unilateral leg swelling raises strong concern for PE.

    2. Why is D-dimer not useful for ruling out PE in this patient?

    D-dimer is physiologically elevated throughout pregnancy, reducing its specificity and negative predictive value

    Normal pregnancy-related increases in D-dimer make a negative result unreliable for excluding PE in this population.

    3. What is the preferred initial imaging test in pregnancy when leg symptoms are present?

    Compression ultrasound of the symptomatic leg

    If DVT is confirmed on leg ultrasound, PE can be diagnosed indirectly without further radiation exposure to the fetus.

    4. If leg ultrasound is negative and suspicion remains, what imaging is used, and why is it preferred to minimize fetal and maternal radiation risk?

    Ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) scan, preferred over CT pulmonary angiogram because it delivers less breast tissue and comparable fetal radiation

    V/Q scanning is often preferred in pregnancy to reduce maternal breast tissue radiation exposure while still providing diagnostic accuracy.

  11. Case 111: 70-Year-Old Man with Pulmonary Embolism and Renal Failure

    A 70-year-old man with known chronic kidney disease (creatinine clearance 22 mL/min) is diagnosed with a segmental pulmonary embolism after presenting with pleuritic chest pain and hypoxia. He is hemodynamically stable.

    1. Why does his renal function limit standard anticoagulant choice?

    Most direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) are renally cleared and are contraindicated or require dose adjustment in severe renal impairment

    Reduced renal clearance increases bleeding risk with standard DOAC dosing, requiring an alternative anticoagulation strategy.

    2. What anticoagulant is preferred in this setting?

    Unfractionated heparin infusion

    Unfractionated heparin is not renally dependent for clearance and its effect can be titrated and reversed, making it preferred in severe renal impairment.

    3. What monitoring is required with this anticoagulant choice?

    Serial activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) or anti-Xa levels

    Unfractionated heparin requires close laboratory monitoring to ensure therapeutic anticoagulation without excessive bleeding risk.

    4. What disposition is appropriate?

    Admission for anticoagulation management given renal impairment

    Patients requiring heparin infusion with careful titration and renal considerations are generally not candidates for outpatient management.

  12. Case 112: 40-Year-Old Woman with Mild Calf Pain and No Risk Factors

    A 40-year-old healthy woman presents with 1 day of mild left calf discomfort after a long car ride. She has no swelling, no prior DVT, no cancer, no recent surgery, and full range of motion. Exam shows minimal tenderness with no swelling or erythema.

    1. What is her Wells score risk category?

    Low probability

    The absence of major risk factors, swelling, or significant clinical findings places her in the low pretest probability category.

    2. What is the appropriate first diagnostic test?

    D-dimer

    In low pretest probability patients, a negative D-dimer has a high negative predictive value and can safely exclude DVT without imaging.

    3. If the D-dimer is negative, what is the next step?

    DVT is excluded; no further imaging or anticoagulation needed

    A negative D-dimer in a low-risk patient reliably rules out venous thromboembolism, avoiding unnecessary ultrasound and treatment.

    4. If the D-dimer were positive instead, what would be the next step?

    Proceed to compression ultrasound of the leg

    A positive D-dimer requires confirmatory imaging since it lacks specificity for DVT even at low pretest probability.

Chapter 11: Airway Management, Procedures, and Ophthalmology (12 Cases)

  1. Case 113: 34-Year-Old Trauma Patient Requiring Airway Assessment

    A 34-year-old man is brought in after a motorcycle collision with facial trauma and a Glasgow Coma Scale of 9. He has active oral bleeding and a beard. Vitals: HR 116, BP 104/68, SpO2 92% on high-flow oxygen. You are preparing for possible intubation.

    1. List two elements of a rapid difficult airway assessment.

    3-3-2 rule (mouth opening, hyoid-mental distance, thyroid-mental distance), Mallampati score

    These bedside assessments predict difficulty with laryngoscopy and mask ventilation and guide preparation before attempting intubation.

    2. What acronym is used to specifically predict difficult mask ventilation?

    LEMON (or the beard/obesity/no teeth/snoring criteria within it)

    The LEMON assessment (Look, Evaluate 3-3-2, Mallampati, Obstruction, Neck mobility) identifies features predicting a difficult airway, including his beard as a mask seal risk factor.

    3. Given his facial trauma and declining GCS, what is your management trigger?

    Proceed with early definitive airway control rather than waiting for further deterioration

    Facial trauma with bleeding and declining consciousness predicts airway compromise, so pre-emptive intubation is safer than a reactive approach.

    4. What backup plan should be prepared given predicted difficulty?

    Video laryngoscopy and a surgical airway kit at bedside

    Anticipating a difficult airway means having rescue and surgical airway equipment immediately available before the first attempt.

  2. Case 114: 26-Year-Old with Anaphylaxis and Worsening Stridor

    A 26-year-old woman develops facial and tongue swelling with stridor and wheeze 15 minutes after eating shellfish. Vitals: HR 130, BP 88/56, SpO2 90%. Her voice is becoming increasingly hoarse.

    1. What is your immediate first-line medication?

    Intramuscular epinephrine

    Epinephrine is the first-line, life-saving treatment for anaphylaxis and should be given immediately, not delayed for other therapies.

    2. What airway decision must be made early given her progressive stridor and hoarseness?

    Proceed with early elective intubation before complete airway obstruction develops

    Progressive angioedema can rapidly obstruct the airway; waiting until obstruction is complete makes intubation far more dangerous or impossible.

    3. Why is early intubation preferred over a wait-and-see approach here?

    Airway edema can progress rapidly to complete obstruction, and a controlled early intubation is safer than an emergent attempt later

    Anticipating deterioration and acting before the airway becomes anatomically distorted improves the chance of first-pass success.

    4. What backup should be immediately available given the difficult airway anatomy?

    Surgical airway (cricothyroidotomy) setup at bedside

    Angioedema can distort normal anatomy, so a surgical airway backup must be ready in case laryngoscopy or intubation fails.

  3. Case 115: 55-Year-Old with Renal Failure Requiring Rapid Sequence Intubation

    A 55-year-old man with end-stage renal disease on hemodialysis presents in respiratory failure requiring intubation. His last dialysis session was 3 days ago. Point-of-care potassium is 6.4 mmol/L.

    1. Which standard RSI paralytic is contraindicated here and why?

    Succinylcholine, because it can cause a further rise in serum potassium

    Succinylcholine causes potassium efflux from cells and can precipitate dangerous hyperkalemia or cardiac arrest in patients with existing hyperkalemia or renal failure.

    2. What paralytic should be used instead?

    Rocuronium

    Rocuronium is a non-depolarizing agent that does not raise serum potassium and is the preferred paralytic in patients with hyperkalemia or renal failure.

    3. What induction agent consideration is relevant given his renal disease and likely hemodynamic status?

    Use a hemodynamically neutral induction agent such as ketamine or etomidate at an appropriately reduced dose

    Renal failure patients may be volume overloaded or unstable, so choosing an induction agent with minimal hemodynamic effect reduces peri-intubation cardiovascular collapse.

    4. What immediate treatment should be given concurrently for his hyperkalemia?

    Calcium gluconate, insulin with glucose, and consideration of emergent dialysis

    Hyperkalemia this severe requires immediate cardiac membrane stabilization and potassium shifting therapy alongside airway management.

  4. Case 116: 62-Year-Old Post-Intubation with Subtle Signs of Tube Misplacement

    A 62-year-old man was intubated for respiratory failure. Shortly after, his oxygen saturation begins to fall from 96% to 88% and his abdomen appears mildly distended. Breath sounds are difficult to assess due to background noise. End-tidal capnography shows a low, gradually decreasing waveform rather than a flat line.

    1. What complication should be suspected despite the presence of a capnography waveform?

    Esophageal intubation, or a profound low-cardiac-output state (e.g. cardiac arrest, severe shock, massive PE) reducing CO2 delivery to the lungs

    A truly absent waveform is the classic sign of esophageal intubation, but a low or progressively decaying waveform can also reflect esophageal placement with some initial exhaled gas washing out, or a state of severely reduced pulmonary blood flow (arrest, severe shock, massive PE) limiting CO2 delivery to the alveoli. A right mainstem intubation still ventilates one lung and typically shows a preserved capnogram; it presents instead with unilateral breath sounds and asymmetric chest rise, not a falling waveform.

    2. What bedside findings support this subtle presentation?

    Progressive desaturation, abdominal distension, and a low or falling capnography trace

    Abdominal distension from gastric insufflation combined with declining oxygenation despite intubation should prompt immediate tube position reassessment.

    3. What is your immediate next step?

    Direct or video laryngoscopy to visually confirm tube position

    Direct visualization is the definitive way to confirm or exclude esophageal or malpositioned tube placement when other signs are equivocal.

    4. If misplacement is confirmed, what is the appropriate action?

    Remove the tube, re-oxygenate with bag-mask ventilation, and reattempt intubation

    A misplaced tube must be removed immediately and the patient re-oxygenated before a safer, better-prepared reattempt at intubation.

  5. Case 117: 16-Year-Old with a Displaced Forearm Fracture Requiring Reduction

    A 16-year-old boy presents with a deformed, painful forearm after falling off his bike. X-ray confirms a displaced distal radius fracture requiring closed reduction. He rates his pain 9 out of 10. He has eaten a meal 1 hour ago and has no significant medical history.

    1. What structured tool should you use to assess his pain?

    A validated numeric or visual analog pain scale

    Structured pain scoring guides analgesic choice and allows objective reassessment after treatment.

    2. What pre-sedation assessment must be completed before procedural sedation?

    ASA classification, NPO status, airway assessment, and baseline vitals

    A structured pre-sedation risk assessment identifies patients at higher risk of sedation-related complications, including his recent meal.

    3. Does his recent meal absolutely contraindicate procedural sedation?

    No, but it should factor into risk-benefit discussion and choice of agent/depth of sedation

    Recent oral intake increases aspiration risk but does not automatically preclude sedation, especially for urgent painful procedures; risk must be weighed against benefit.

    4. What monitoring is required after the procedure before discharge?

    Continuous vital sign and oxygenation monitoring until back to baseline mental status, with defined discharge criteria

    Post-sedation monitoring ensures full recovery of airway reflexes and mental status before the patient can be safely discharged.

  6. Case 118: 88-Year-Old Woman with Dementia and a Suspected Hip Fracture

    An 88-year-old woman with advanced dementia is brought in after a fall, unable to verbally describe her pain. She grimaces and guards her right leg, which is shortened and externally rotated. X-ray confirms a hip fracture.

    1. Why is pain assessment challenging in this patient?

    Her dementia limits her ability to verbally communicate pain severity

    Nonverbal or cognitively impaired patients cannot reliably self-report pain using standard numeric scales, requiring an alternative approach.

    2. What tool can be used to assess pain in a nonverbal patient?

    A behavioral pain scale (e.g., PAINAD)

    Behavioral observation tools assess facial expression, body movements, and vocalization to estimate pain severity in patients who cannot self-report.

    3. What analgesic approach is appropriate for a hip fracture in an elderly patient?

    Titrated low-dose IV opioids or a regional nerve block (e.g., fascia iliaca block)

    Regional nerve blocks and cautious titrated opioids provide effective analgesia while minimizing the risk of delirium and hemodynamic side effects in elderly patients.

    4. What should be monitored closely given her age and analgesic choice?

    Respiratory rate, sedation level, and blood pressure

    Elderly patients are at higher risk of opioid-induced respiratory depression and hypotension, requiring close monitoring after analgesia.

  7. Case 119: 9-Year-Old After Procedural Sedation for Fracture Reduction

    A 9-year-old boy underwent procedural sedation with ketamine for reduction of a displaced elbow fracture. The procedure is now complete, and he remains drowsy 20 minutes later. Vitals are stable with SpO2 98% on room air.

    1. What is the minimum monitoring required before considering discharge?

    Continuous cardiorespiratory and oxygen saturation monitoring until he returns to baseline mental status

    Ongoing monitoring is essential since sedative effects and airway reflex recovery can lag behind apparent clinical improvement.

    2. List two criteria that must be met before this child can be safely discharged.

    Return to baseline mental status/ability to sit unsupported, and stable vital signs without supplemental oxygen

    These are standard components of validated post-sedation discharge criteria that confirm resolution of sedation effects.

    3. What discharge instructions should be given to the caregiver?

    Supervise closely for the rest of the day, avoid activities requiring coordination, and return if vomiting or excessive drowsiness occurs

    Residual sedative effects can persist after discharge criteria are met, so caregivers need clear safety instructions for continued observation at home.

    4. What known side effect of the sedative agent used should be anticipated during recovery?

    Emergence reactions (agitation, vivid dreams, hallucinations)

    Ketamine is well known to cause emergence phenomena during recovery, and caregivers and staff should be prepared to manage this calmly.

  8. Case 120: 45-Year-Old with Acute Renal Colic

    A 45-year-old man presents writhing in pain with left flank pain radiating to the groin, unable to find a comfortable position. He has nausea and gross hematuria. Vitals are normal aside from tachycardia from pain.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Renal colic from ureteric stone

    Colicky flank pain radiating to the groin with hematuria and an inability to lie still is classic for ureterolithiasis.

    2. What is the preferred first-line analgesic class for this presentation?

    NSAIDs (e.g., IV ketorolac)

    NSAIDs reduce ureteric spasm and inflammation and are as effective as or more effective than opioids for renal colic pain.

    3. What should be added if pain is not adequately controlled with NSAIDs alone?

    Opioid analgesia (e.g., IV morphine or hydromorphone)

    Combination therapy is appropriate when monotherapy fails to provide adequate analgesia for severe colic pain.

    4. What antiemetic consideration should be made given his nausea?

    Give an antiemetic such as ondansetron alongside analgesia

    Treating nausea improves comfort and tolerance of oral intake and reduces vomiting-related complications.

  9. Case 121: 62-Year-Old Woman with Red Eye Thought to Be Conjunctivitis

    A 62-year-old woman presents with a painful red right eye, headache, nausea, and blurred vision that started 4 hours ago after being in a dark movie theatre. She was told by a walk-in clinic it was conjunctivitis and given drops with no improvement. The pupil is mid-dilated and sluggish, and the eye feels firm on palpation.

    1. What is the correct diagnosis, not conjunctivitis?

    Acute angle closure glaucoma

    A painful red eye with headache, nausea, mid-dilated sluggish pupil, and a firm globe are classic for acute angle closure glaucoma rather than benign conjunctivitis.

    2. What bedside investigation should be performed urgently?

    Intraocular pressure measurement (tonometry)

    Measuring intraocular pressure confirms the diagnosis and quantifies severity, guiding urgency of treatment.

    3. What is your immediate management?

    Topical beta-blocker, topical alpha-agonist, topical pilocarpine, and IV acetazolamide, with urgent ophthalmology consultation

    Rapid pharmacologic lowering of intraocular pressure is required to prevent permanent vision loss while arranging emergent ophthalmology involvement.

    4. Why was the prior diagnosis of conjunctivitis dangerous?

    It delayed recognition of a sight-threatening emergency and any topical steroid or reassurance-only approach would allow ongoing pressure damage

    Misdiagnosing angle closure glaucoma as a benign eye condition delays vision-saving treatment and risks permanent optic nerve damage.

  10. Case 122: 24-Year-Old with Bilateral Red, Watery Eyes

    A 24-year-old man presents with 2 days of bilateral red, watery, itchy eyes with mild crusting on waking. He has a preauricular lymph node palpable on the right and a recent upper respiratory infection. Visual acuity is normal and there is no photophobia.

    1. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Viral conjunctivitis

    Bilateral watery discharge, preauricular adenopathy, and a preceding viral upper respiratory illness are classic for viral conjunctivitis.

    2. What examination findings support ruling out a more serious cause?

    Normal visual acuity and absence of photophobia or corneal staining

    Preserved vision and lack of photophobia argue against keratitis, uveitis, or other sight-threatening causes of red eye.

    3. Why should topical corticosteroids be avoided here?

    They can worsen or prolong viral infections, including undiagnosed herpes simplex keratitis, and risk serious complications

    Topical steroids can exacerbate viral keratitis and should not be prescribed for undifferentiated red eye without ophthalmology guidance.

    4. What advice should be given regarding contagion?

    Frequent hand hygiene and avoiding shared towels or close contact, as viral conjunctivitis is highly contagious

    Viral conjunctivitis spreads easily through contact, and patient education helps prevent transmission to others.

  11. Case 123: 19-Year-Old with Eye Pain After Being Struck by a Squash Ball

    A 19-year-old man presents with right eye pain and blurred vision after being struck by a squash ball 1 hour ago. Visual acuity is decreased to counting fingers. Exam reveals blood layering in the anterior chamber and a sluggish pupil.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Traumatic hyphema

    Blood layering in the anterior chamber after blunt ocular trauma defines hyphema.

    2. What is a critical additional injury to rule out with this mechanism?

    Globe rupture and orbital fracture

    Blunt high-velocity trauma to the eye can cause globe rupture, which must be excluded before further examination or pressure is applied to the eye.

    3. What positioning and activity restriction should be advised?

    Keep the head elevated at 30 to 45 degrees and restrict activity/rest

    Head elevation helps blood settle and reduces intraocular pressure, and activity restriction reduces the risk of rebleeding.

    4. What complication of hyphema must be monitored for?

    Elevated intraocular pressure and rebleeding

    Hyphema increases the risk of secondary glaucoma from elevated intraocular pressure and rebleeding, both of which require close ophthalmologic follow-up.

  12. Case 124: 72-Year-Old Man with Sudden Painless Vision Loss in One Eye

    A 72-year-old man presents with sudden painless loss of vision in his left eye that began 30 minutes ago. He has hypertension and atrial fibrillation, on no anticoagulation. Fundoscopic exam reveals a pale retina with a cherry red spot at the macula.

    1. What is the diagnosis?

    Central retinal artery occlusion

    Sudden painless monocular vision loss with a cherry red spot and pale retina is the classic finding of central retinal artery occlusion.

    2. Why is time critical in this presentation?

    Retinal tissue is highly sensitive to ischemia, and irreversible vision loss occurs within a short window, similar to a stroke

    Central retinal artery occlusion is considered an ocular stroke, and treatments are only potentially effective if initiated within a narrow time window.

    3. What underlying systemic workup should be initiated given his risk factors?

    Stroke/cardiovascular workup including ECG, carotid imaging, and consideration of giant cell arteritis screening (ESR/CRP)

    Central retinal artery occlusion shares embolic and vasculitic etiologies with stroke, and identifying the source is important for secondary prevention.

    4. Who should be consulted emergently?

    Ophthalmology and consideration of stroke neurology

    Emergent ophthalmology involvement may allow attempts at reperfusion, while neurology involvement addresses the shared vascular risk with stroke.

Chapter 12: Systems-Based Practice and EMS (16 Cases)

  1. Case 125: EMS Pre-Arrival Notification for a High-Speed Motor Vehicle Collision

    You receive an EMS pre-arrival call for a 35-year-old man involved in a high-speed motor vehicle collision with prolonged extrication, reported hypotension, and a GCS of 12 en route, arriving in 8 minutes.

    1. List three preparations you should make before the patient arrives.

    Activate the trauma team, prepare blood products/massive transfusion protocol, ready airway and resuscitation equipment

    Pre-arrival preparation based on EMS report allows rapid, coordinated resuscitation instead of scrambling for resources on arrival.

    2. What additional information should you request from EMS if not already given?

    Mechanism details, vital sign trends, interventions already performed, and estimated time of arrival

    Detailed mechanism and trend information allows better anticipation of injury patterns and ongoing resuscitation needs.

    3. Why is prolonged extrication clinically significant?

    It increases suspicion for significant crush or entrapment injury and prolonged hypoperfusion time

    Extended extrication time correlates with more severe mechanism and possible crush syndrome, informing resuscitation priorities.

    4. What team roles should be assigned before arrival?

    Airway lead, resuscitation/procedures lead, and a scribe/communication coordinator

    Predefining roles prevents confusion and improves efficiency during the critical first minutes of a major trauma resuscitation.

  2. Case 126: EMS Radio Report for a Suspected STEMI

    EMS radios in a 58-year-old man with acute crushing chest pain and an ECG showing ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF, 15 minutes from arrival. He has taken aspirin already.

    1. What focused history should you obtain from EMS on arrival?

    Time of symptom onset, medications given, allergies, and contraindications to fibrinolysis or antiplatelet therapy

    This focused handover information directly determines eligibility for time-sensitive reperfusion therapy.

    2. What is your priority action based on this EMS report?

    Activate the cardiac catheterization lab (primary PCI) prior to patient arrival if available

    Pre-notification and early activation based on a prehospital ECG reduces door-to-balloon time and improves outcomes.

    3. Why is an inferior STEMI clinically significant for your initial management?

    Right ventricular involvement is common, making the patient preload-dependent and at risk of hypotension with nitrates

    Inferior STEMIs are associated with right ventricular infarction, and nitrate administration can cause profound hypotension in this setting.

    4. What medication should be used cautiously or avoided given the likely inferior/right ventricular involvement?

    Nitroglycerin

    Nitrates reduce preload and can precipitate severe hypotension in right ventricular infarction, so they should be used cautiously or avoided.

  3. Case 127: Deciding on Interfacility Transfer for a Patient with a Subdural Hematoma

    A 68-year-old man presents to a rural emergency department after a fall, with a GCS of 13 and CT showing a small acute subdural hematoma without midline shift. There is no neurosurgery on site. The nearest tertiary centre is 3 hours away by ground.

    1. What are the key risks to weigh in deciding whether to transfer?

    Risk of neurological deterioration during transport versus benefit of neurosurgical availability at the receiving centre

    Interfacility transfer decisions require balancing the danger of deterioration en route against the need for specialist care unavailable locally.

    2. What monitoring and preparation should occur before transfer?

    Serial neurological assessments, secured airway if indicated, and appropriately trained transport personnel

    Ensuring the patient is stabilized and appropriately monitored reduces risk of deterioration during a long transport.

    3. What mode of transport should be considered given the distance and acuity?

    Air medical transport if available and time-critical

    Faster transport reduces time to definitive neurosurgical care when clinical deterioration risk is significant.

    4. What should be communicated to the receiving centre before transfer?

    Full clinical picture, imaging findings, current neurological status, and estimated time of arrival

    Clear handover communication ensures the receiving team is prepared for immediate assessment and intervention on arrival.

  4. Case 128: EMS Field Triage Direction During a Multi-Vehicle Highway Collision

    You are the base hospital physician receiving a call from EMS at the scene of a multi-vehicle highway collision with an estimated 12 patients of varying severity. EMS requests guidance on triage and transport destinations.

    1. What triage framework should EMS use at the scene?

    START (Simple Triage and Rapid Treatment) or an equivalent mass casualty triage system

    A standardized field triage system allows rapid categorization of patients by acuity when resources are limited.

    2. What guidance should you give regarding distribution of patients to hospitals?

    Distribute patients across multiple receiving facilities based on capacity and level of care needed, avoiding overwhelming a single centre

    Spreading the patient load prevents any single hospital from being overwhelmed and ensures each patient reaches an appropriately resourced facility.

    3. What information should you request to coordinate the response effectively?

    Number and acuity of patients, resources on scene, and estimated transport times to available facilities

    This information is essential to direct appropriate destination assignment and prepare receiving hospitals.

    4. What internal action should you take at your own hospital in anticipation of incoming patients?

    Activate the hospital's mass casualty/disaster response plan

    Early activation of the internal disaster plan mobilizes staff and resources ahead of patient arrival.

  5. Case 129: Three Trauma Patients Arriving Simultaneously After a Rollover Collision

    Three patients arrive simultaneously from a single rollover collision. Patient A has a GCS of 6 with unequal pupils. Patient B has stable vitals with an isolated closed forearm fracture. Patient C has tachycardia, hypotension, and a distended abdomen. You are the only physician immediately available.

    1. How should you prioritize your immediate attention among these three patients?

    Patient C first (unstable abdomen/possible hemorrhage), then Patient A (head injury), Patient B last (stable isolated injury)

    Prioritization should follow physiologic instability and reversible life threats first; an unstable abdomen suggesting hemorrhage takes priority over a head injury without other unstable findings, while the isolated fracture can safely wait.

    2. What should you do about the patients you are not immediately assessing?

    Delegate ongoing assessment and monitoring to nursing staff or other available providers

    Effective delegation ensures all patients continue to be monitored even while your direct attention is on the most unstable patient.

    3. What resource should be mobilized immediately given the scenario?

    Additional physician support and trauma team activation

    A multi-patient trauma presentation exceeding single-provider capacity requires immediate escalation of staffing and resources.

    4. What ongoing reassessment principle applies across all three patients?

    Continuously reassess and be ready to re-prioritize as clinical status changes

    Triage priority is dynamic, and any patient's condition can change rapidly, requiring frequent reassessment of the overall priority order.

  6. Case 130: Emergency Department Overwhelmed During a Norovirus Outbreak

    Your emergency department census has doubled overnight due to a community norovirus outbreak, with all beds full and a growing waiting room. Nursing staff report they are stretched beyond capacity, and several patients are waiting over 4 hours for reassessment.

    1. What immediate department-level action should you consider when capacity is exceeded?

    Activate the hospital's surge or overcapacity protocol

    Formal surge protocols mobilize additional staffing, space, and resources when routine capacity is exceeded.

    2. What is an important safety measure for patients waiting to be seen?

    Ensure ongoing triage reassessment of waiting room patients for clinical deterioration

    Patients waiting long periods can deteriorate, so structured reassessment prevents missed decompensation.

    3. What resource request should you make to hospital administration?

    Request additional nursing and physician staffing, and expedite inpatient bed availability to reduce boarding

    Requesting additional staffing and reducing ED boarding directly addresses the root causes of overcapacity.

    4. What communication should occur with the rest of the care team during this surge?

    Regular team huddles to redistribute workload and identify the sickest patients

    Frequent team communication ensures workload is balanced and no critically ill patient is overlooked during a surge.

  7. Case 131: Delegating Tasks During Simultaneous Resuscitations

    You are managing a cardiac arrest in resuscitation bay 1 when a second patient in bay 2 develops sudden severe respiratory distress requiring urgent airway management. You have one additional physician and two nurses available.

    1. How should you delegate management of the second patient?

    Assign the second physician to lead the airway management of the patient in bay 2 while you continue directing the arrest

    Delegating leadership of the second critical event to another qualified provider allows both patients to receive simultaneous, dedicated attention.

    2. What should you communicate clearly to the team during this delegation?

    Explicit roles, who is leading each resuscitation, and how they will communicate updates to each other

    Clear role delineation prevents confusion and ensures accountability during simultaneous critical events.

    3. What additional resource should be requested given two simultaneous critical events?

    Call for additional physician, nursing, or respiratory therapy support

    Two simultaneous resuscitations exceed baseline staffing needs and require immediate additional support to maintain safe care.

    4. What should happen once both situations stabilize?

    Debrief with the team to review decision-making and identify any systems issues

    A structured debrief after high-acuity simultaneous events helps identify improvement opportunities and supports team wellbeing.

  8. Case 132: Simultaneous Suspected Stroke and Acute Coronary Syndrome Patients

    Two patients arrive within minutes of each other: a 74-year-old woman with acute right-sided weakness and slurred speech starting 45 minutes ago, and a 60-year-old man with crushing chest pain and ST elevation on ECG. You are the sole physician on shift with one nurse per patient.

    1. Can both patients be managed simultaneously with appropriate delegation?

    Yes, by assigning a dedicated nurse to initiate the respective time-critical pathway for each patient while you personally oversee both

    Both conditions are time-critical, and using trained nursing staff to initiate parallel protocol steps (stroke workup, STEMI activation) allows simultaneous progress.

    2. What time-critical actions should be initiated immediately for the stroke patient?

    Stat CT head, blood glucose check, and stroke team activation

    These steps must occur rapidly to determine eligibility for thrombolysis or thrombectomy within the treatment window.

    3. What time-critical action should be initiated immediately for the cardiac patient?

    Cardiac catheterization lab activation for primary PCI

    Rapid reperfusion therapy activation minimizes myocardial damage in ST elevation myocardial infarction.

    4. What resource should be requested given two simultaneous time-critical emergencies?

    Additional physician or charge nurse support to help coordinate both pathways

    Requesting additional support ensures neither time-critical pathway is delayed due to competing demands on a single provider.

  9. Case 133: Wrong Medication Dose Administered Due to a Look-Alike Vial

    A nurse reports that a patient received 10 times the intended dose of an intravenous medication because two vials with similar packaging were stored adjacent to each other. The patient is stable but required close monitoring. The error was caught before further harm occurred.

    1. What is your immediate responsibility regarding disclosure?

    Disclose the error to the patient and family in a timely, honest, and transparent manner

    Patients have a right to know when an error affecting their care has occurred, and transparent disclosure is an ethical and professional obligation.

    2. What analytical process should be used to identify the underlying cause of this error?

    Root cause analysis

    Root cause analysis systematically identifies underlying system failures, such as medication storage practices, rather than attributing the error solely to individual fault.

    3. What systems-based intervention could reduce recurrence of this specific error?

    Separate storage of look-alike/sound-alike medications and improved labeling

    Physical separation and enhanced labeling address the root cause directly, reducing the chance of a similar mix-up in the future.

    4. Who should be involved in reviewing this incident beyond the immediate care team?

    Hospital quality and patient safety committee

    Formal incident review through a patient safety committee ensures systemic learning and broader implementation of preventive measures.

  10. Case 134: Designing a Quality-of-Care Review for Delayed Sepsis Recognition

    Your department has noted several cases over the past 6 months where patients with sepsis had delayed antibiotic administration. Leadership asks you to help design a review to understand why this is happening and how to improve it.

    1. What type of quality improvement study design would be appropriate here?

    A retrospective chart audit/case review of sepsis presentations, potentially followed by a plan-do-study-act (PDSA) cycle

    Reviewing existing cases identifies patterns in delay before testing a targeted intervention using an iterative improvement cycle.

    2. What specific process metrics should be measured?

    Time to triage recognition, time to physician assessment, and time to antibiotic administration

    Measuring each step of the sepsis care pathway identifies exactly where delays are occurring.

    3. What is an example of an intervention that could be tested using this framework?

    Implementation of a sepsis screening tool or alert at triage

    A standardized screening trigger can improve early recognition, the step most commonly responsible for delayed treatment.

    4. How should the effectiveness of the intervention be evaluated after implementation?

    Re-measure the same time-to-treatment metrics after the intervention and compare to baseline

    Comparing post-intervention metrics to baseline data determines whether the change achieved its intended improvement.

  11. Case 135: Reviewing a Near-Miss Patient Identification Error

    A nurse nearly administered blood intended for a different patient with a similar last name, catching the error only during a final bedside verification. No harm occurred. The unit asks you to help review this near-miss as part of ongoing quality improvement.

    1. What patient safety principle prevented harm in this case?

    Two-identifier patient verification at the bedside before administration

    Standardized double patient identifier checks are a core safety barrier designed specifically to catch this type of error before it reaches the patient.

    2. Should a near-miss with no harm still be formally reported and reviewed?

    Yes

    Near-miss reporting identifies systemic vulnerabilities before they result in actual patient harm and is a core principle of proactive safety culture.

    3. What broader patient safety concept does this case illustrate, involving communication between team members?

    The importance of structured verification and closed-loop communication (e.g., read-back)

    Structured verification steps and closed-loop communication are key safety principles that reduce reliance on memory or assumption between team members.

    4. What system-level change could further reduce this risk beyond the existing verification step?

    Barcode scanning technology for patient and product matching

    Technology-based verification adds an additional independent safety layer beyond manual identifier checks.

  12. Case 136: Handoff Communication Failure Leading to a Missed Critical Lab Result

    A critical potassium result was called to the department but was not relayed to the incoming physician during shift change, delaying treatment by several hours until the patient developed arrhythmia. You are asked to review this communication failure.

    1. What structured communication tool could have prevented this handoff failure?

    SBAR (Situation, Background, Assessment, Recommendation)

    SBAR provides a standardized framework for handoff communication that reduces the risk of critical information being lost between providers.

    2. What is the immediate clinical priority once the missed result is discovered?

    Immediately assess and treat the patient for hyperkalemia

    Patient safety takes priority over the administrative review, and the critical abnormal result must be acted upon as soon as it is recognized.

    3. What system change could ensure critical lab values are not lost during handoff?

    A standardized critical value alert system with mandatory closed-loop acknowledgment

    An automated or mandatory acknowledgment process removes reliance on verbal handoff alone for time-critical results.

    4. What should be done for the affected patient and family regarding this error?

    Transparent disclosure of the delay and its cause

    Honest, transparent disclosure to the patient and family is required whenever a system failure has affected their care.

  13. Case 137: Appraising a Trial Comparing Two Analgesic Protocols

    A published trial randomly assigns emergency department patients with acute pain to either a standard opioid protocol or a multimodal non-opioid protocol, then compares pain scores at 1 hour. Neither patients nor the assessors knew which treatment was given.

    1. What is the study design?

    Double-blind randomized controlled trial

    Random allocation to treatment groups with blinding of both patients and assessors defines a double-blind randomized controlled trial.

    2. Why is randomization important in this design?

    It minimizes selection bias and balances known and unknown confounders between groups

    Randomization helps ensure that any difference in outcome is attributable to the intervention rather than baseline differences between groups.

    3. Why is blinding of assessors specifically important for a pain outcome?

    Pain scoring is subjective, and assessor knowledge of group assignment could introduce measurement bias

    Subjective outcome measures are particularly vulnerable to bias if the assessor is aware of treatment allocation.

    4. What type of bias would be introduced if patients dropped out of one arm at a higher rate and were excluded from the analysis?

    Attrition bias

    Differential loss to follow-up between groups can distort the true treatment effect if not accounted for using an intention-to-treat analysis.

  14. Case 138: Interpreting the Test Characteristics of a New Rapid Strep Test

    A new rapid antigen test for group A streptococcal pharyngitis is studied against throat culture as the gold standard in 200 patients. Of 100 patients with a positive culture, the rapid test was positive in 85. Of 100 patients with a negative culture, the rapid test was negative in 90.

    1. Calculate the sensitivity of the rapid test.

    85% (85/100)

    Sensitivity is the proportion of true culture-positive patients correctly identified as positive by the rapid test, here 85 out of 100.

    2. Calculate the specificity of the rapid test.

    90% (90/100)

    Specificity is the proportion of true culture-negative patients correctly identified as negative by the rapid test, here 90 out of 100.

    3. Calculate the positive likelihood ratio.

    8.5 (sensitivity 0.85 divided by (1 minus specificity) 0.10)

    The positive likelihood ratio combines sensitivity and specificity to describe how much a positive result increases the odds of true disease.

    4. Given this specificity, what is a clinical implication of a negative rapid test result in a high pretest probability patient?

    A negative result may still warrant confirmatory throat culture due to imperfect sensitivity

    With 85% sensitivity, a meaningful proportion of true positive cases will be missed, so negative results in high-risk patients often require backup culture testing per guideline recommendations.

  15. Case 139: Interpreting Number Needed to Treat from a Thrombolysis Trial

    A trial of a new thrombolytic agent for a specific stroke subtype reports a reduction in poor functional outcome from 40% in the control group to 30% in the treatment group. The trial also reports a symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage rate of 6% in the treatment group versus 1% in the control group.

    1. Calculate the absolute risk reduction for poor functional outcome.

    10% (40% minus 30%)

    Absolute risk reduction is the simple difference in event rates between control and treatment groups.

    2. Calculate the number needed to treat.

    10 (1 divided by 0.10)

    Number needed to treat is the reciprocal of the absolute risk reduction and represents how many patients must be treated to prevent one additional poor outcome.

    3. Calculate the number needed to harm for symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage.

    20 (1 divided by 0.05)

    The absolute risk increase for hemorrhage is 5%, so 1 divided by 0.05 gives a number needed to harm of 20.

    4. How should NNT and NNH together inform your clinical decision, beyond statistical significance alone?

    Weigh the benefit (1 in 10 avoids poor outcome) against the harm (1 in 20 suffers hemorrhage) in the context of the individual patient's values and risk tolerance

    Clinical decision-making requires balancing benefit and harm magnitudes together with patient-specific factors, not simply relying on whether results reached statistical significance.

  16. Case 140: Evaluating Statistical versus Clinical Significance in a Blood Pressure Trial

    A large trial finds that a new antihypertensive lowers systolic blood pressure by an average of 2 mmHg more than placebo, with a p-value of 0.01. The trial enrolled 15,000 patients. There was no significant difference in rates of stroke, myocardial infarction, or death between groups.

    1. Is this result statistically significant?

    Yes, given the p-value of 0.01

    A p-value below the conventional 0.05 threshold indicates the observed difference is unlikely to be due to chance alone.

    2. Why might this result not be clinically significant despite being statistically significant?

    A 2 mmHg difference is unlikely to be meaningful to patients, and there was no improvement in patient-important outcomes like stroke or death

    Very large sample sizes can detect statistically significant but clinically trivial differences that do not translate into meaningful patient benefit.

    3. What allowed such a small difference to reach statistical significance?

    The very large sample size increased statistical power to detect small differences

    Large sample sizes reduce the standard error of the estimate, making even small effect sizes statistically detectable.

    4. How should this affect your decision to adopt this medication into practice?

    Prioritize patient-important outcomes over the surrogate blood pressure endpoint; the lack of benefit on stroke, MI, or death argues against routine adoption based on this trial alone

    Surrogate outcome improvements do not guarantee benefit in outcomes that matter to patients, and treatment decisions should be based on meaningful clinical endpoints.

Part 3: Structured Oral Station Model Answers (40 Stations)

Each station gives the 2-minute candidate reading stem, the examiner's full evolving scenario, the examiner's checklist of key decision points, and a model verbalized first-person clinical reasoning answer.

  1. Station 1: Middle-Aged Man with Crushing Chest Pain

    Reading Stem

    You are the emergency physician on shift. A 54-year-old man is brought in by paramedics with 45 minutes of crushing central chest pain radiating to his left arm, associated with diaphoresis and nausea. He has a history of smoking and hypertension. Paramedics have already applied oxygen and given aspirin en route.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals on arrival: BP 148/92, HR 96, RR 20, SpO2 96% on room air, afebrile. If the candidate asks, the patient is pale and diaphoretic but alert. If a 12-lead ECG is requested within the first few minutes, reveal 2mm ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF with reciprocal depression in I and aVL, consistent with inferior STEMI. If the candidate does not order an ECG promptly, prompt with 'the nurse is asking if you want any tests.' Once STEMI is recognized, the candidate should discuss activating the cath lab or, if unavailable, thrombolysis, and should ask about right ventricular involvement (right-sided leads) and avoid nitrates given inferior/RV involvement risk of hypotension. If nitrates are given anyway, drop the BP to 78/50 to test recognition and management of this complication with fluids. Expect antiplatelet/anticoagulation dosing (aspirin already given, consider ticagrelor or clopidogrel, heparin), and pain control with judicious opioids. Ask the candidate to state their differential (ACS, aortic dissection, PE, pericarditis, esophageal spasm) and how they ruled out dissection (symmetric pulses/BP, no tearing pain, no mediastinal widening). Candidate should arrange urgent transfer for PCI and discuss disposition to CCU or transfer centre.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Verbalizes a broad chest pain differential before committing to a diagnosis
    • Orders ECG within the first 2 to 3 minutes of the encounter
    • Recognizes inferior STEMI and considers right ventricular infarction before giving nitrates
    • Activates cath lab or discusses thrombolysis if PCI unavailable, with correct antiplatelet and anticoagulant choices
    • Manages the hypotension complication safely with fluids rather than more nitrates, and arranges appropriate disposition

    Model Verbalized Answer

    I'd start with a quick primary survey to confirm this is not an immediately life-threatening airway or breathing problem, then focus on the chest pain differential, which includes acute coronary syndrome, aortic dissection, pulmonary embolism, and pericarditis. Given the classic presentation and risk factors, I'm getting a 12-lead ECG within minutes while continuing oxygen, aspirin, and monitoring. If this shows inferior ST elevation, I'm concerned about right ventricular involvement, so I'll get right-sided leads and avoid nitrates and other preload-reducing drugs until that's clarified. I'd activate the cath lab immediately for primary PCI, give a P2Y12 inhibitor and anticoagulation per protocol, and treat pain with small titrated opioid doses. If the blood pressure drops, I'd suspect RV infarct physiology and treat with a fluid bolus rather than more nitrates. Disposition would be urgent transfer for reperfusion with CCU admission afterward.

  2. Station 2: Young Woman with Pleuritic Chest Pain and Leg Swelling

    Reading Stem

    A 32-year-old woman presents with sudden-onset pleuritic right-sided chest pain and shortness of breath that started two hours ago. She recently returned from a 14-hour flight and takes an oral contraceptive. She denies trauma or prior cardiac history.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 110/70, HR 118, RR 26, SpO2 91% on room air, afebrile, mild right calf swelling and tenderness on exam if asked. The candidate should recognize risk factors for venous thromboembolism (recent long flight, oral contraceptives) and calculate or discuss a Wells score. If they order an ECG, show sinus tachycardia without S1Q3T3. If they request a chest x-ray, it is unremarkable. Prompt for their choice of imaging (CT pulmonary angiogram) and management of oxygen and anticoagulation while awaiting results. Midway through, drop SpO2 to 85% and BP to 82/55 with worsening tachycardia to simulate a large PE causing hemodynamic compromise; the candidate should recognize this as a massive/high-risk PE and discuss thrombolysis versus catheter-directed therapy versus surgical embolectomy, and involve critical care and interventional teams. If the candidate anchors on musculoskeletal pain or panic attack and delays workup, prompt with worsening vitals to force reconsideration. Expect discussion of contraindications to thrombolysis (recent surgery, bleeding risk) and appropriate resuscitation with careful fluids given right heart strain risk.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Identifies VTE risk factors (recent travel, oral contraceptives) and applies a validated risk score
    • Orders appropriate confirmatory imaging such as CT pulmonary angiogram while stabilizing the patient
    • Recognizes hemodynamic deterioration as a sign of massive PE rather than dismissing it
    • Discusses systemic thrombolysis or alternative reperfusion strategies with contraindications considered
    • Involves critical care or interventional specialists early and gives careful, limited fluid resuscitation

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given the pleuritic pain, tachycardia, hypoxia, and clear VTE risk factors of recent air travel and oral contraceptive use, pulmonary embolism sits at the top of my differential alongside pneumothorax, pneumonia, and musculoskeletal pain. I'd start oxygen, get IV access, and order an ECG and chest x-ray while arranging a CT pulmonary angiogram, keeping the patient on a monitor. If she becomes hypotensive with worsening hypoxia, I'm treating this as a high-risk massive PE with right heart strain, so I'd start careful fluid boluses only, avoid overloading the right ventricle, and strongly consider systemic thrombolysis after checking for contraindications like recent surgery or bleeding risk. I would call critical care and interventional radiology or cardiology early to discuss catheter-directed therapy or embolectomy as alternatives. Throughout, I'm reassessing her hemodynamics closely and preparing for possible cardiac arrest given the risk of PEA arrest in massive PE.

  3. Station 3: Diabetic Man with Atypical Anginal Symptoms

    Reading Stem

    A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes and known coronary artery disease presents with two days of vague epigastric discomfort, fatigue, and mild shortness of breath on exertion. He denies classic chest pain, which he attributes to indigestion.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 132/84, HR 88, RR 18, SpO2 95%, afebrile. If the candidate probes for atypical anginal equivalents (fatigue, dyspnea, epigastric discomfort in a diabetic), reward this line of questioning. ECG shows subtle T-wave inversions in the anterior leads (V2-V4) without frank ST elevation, consistent with NSTEMI/unstable angina. If troponin is ordered, first draw is mildly elevated; a repeat troponin 2 to 3 hours later rises further, confirming myocardial injury. The candidate should recognize diabetic patients often present atypically ('silent ischemia') due to autonomic neuropathy. Expect discussion of risk stratification (HEART score or similar), antiplatelet and anticoagulant therapy, and early cardiology consultation for possible urgent versus early invasive angiography depending on ongoing symptoms or hemodynamic instability. If the candidate wants to discharge based on reassuring initial ECG and normal-appearing patient, prompt with the repeat troponin rise to correct the trajectory. Discuss glucose management considerations and medication reconciliation (metformin hold if contrast planned).

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes atypical presentation of cardiac ischemia in a diabetic patient
    • Orders serial ECGs and serial troponins rather than relying on a single normal test
    • Correctly identifies NSTEMI/unstable angina from trending troponin and ECG changes
    • Uses a validated risk score to guide disposition and discusses early invasive versus conservative strategy with cardiology
    • Addresses medication considerations such as holding metformin before contrast imaging

    Model Verbalized Answer

    In a diabetic patient with vague epigastric discomfort and fatigue, I have to keep acute coronary syndrome high on my differential even without classic chest pain, since autonomic neuropathy can blunt typical anginal symptoms. I'd get an ECG and initial troponin right away, but a single normal set doesn't rule this out, so I'm planning serial ECGs and a repeat troponin in a few hours. If the repeat troponin rises with subtle anterior T-wave changes, I'm calling this NSTEMI and starting dual antiplatelet therapy and anticoagulation per protocol while consulting cardiology. I'd risk-stratify using a HEART score to help decide between an early invasive strategy and conservative management, and I would hold metformin if contrast imaging is anticipated. Disposition would be admission to a monitored cardiac unit rather than discharge, given the objective evidence of ongoing myocardial injury.

  4. Station 4: Palpitations and Presyncope in a Young Athlete

    Reading Stem

    A 24-year-old competitive runner presents with sudden palpitations and lightheadedness during a training run 30 minutes ago. Symptoms have partially resolved but he still feels his heart racing. He has no significant past medical history.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 100/64, HR 190 regular, RR 18, SpO2 98%. ECG shows a regular narrow-complex tachycardia at 190 bpm with no clearly visible P waves, consistent with SVT (likely AVNRT). If the candidate attempts vagal maneuvers (Valsalva, carotid massage after checking for bruits/contraindications), the rhythm converts to sinus at 78 bpm after one attempt; if not attempted, prompt for the next step. If vagal maneuvers fail or are skipped, the candidate should give IV adenosine with correct dosing and technique (rapid push followed by saline flush, with warning to patient about transient symptoms) while attached to a monitor with defibrillator pads ready. If the candidate hesitates or wants to just observe, deteriorate the patient to hypotension (BP 78/50) to force recognition of unstable SVT requiring synchronized cardioversion. Ask the candidate to discuss post-conversion ECG review for pre-excitation (WPW delta waves) since young athletes with SVT warrant this evaluation, and discuss outpatient cardiology/electrophysiology referral and safety-netting regarding future episodes and driving/exercise restrictions until cleared.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes regular narrow-complex tachycardia as likely SVT and assesses hemodynamic stability first
    • Attempts vagal maneuvers correctly before or in parallel with preparing pharmacologic treatment
    • Administers adenosine correctly if vagal maneuvers fail, with monitor and defibrillator ready
    • Recognizes hemodynamic instability and escalates promptly to synchronized cardioversion
    • Reviews post-conversion ECG for pre-excitation and arranges appropriate cardiology follow-up

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This is a regular narrow-complex tachycardia at 190 with borderline blood pressure, so my first priority is deciding if he's hemodynamically stable enough for vagal maneuvers and adenosine rather than needing immediate cardioversion. I'd put him on a monitor, get IV access, and try a Valsalva maneuver first since it's low-risk and often effective in young patients. If that fails, I'd give adenosine as a rapid IV push followed immediately by a saline flush, warning him he may feel chest pressure or flushing briefly, with pads on in case of any bradyarrhythmia. If at any point he becomes hypotensive or shows signs of poor perfusion, I would move straight to synchronized cardioversion rather than repeating medications. After conversion, I'd carefully review the resting ECG for a delta wave suggesting WPW, since that changes both acute drug choices and long-term management, and I'd arrange electrophysiology follow-up along with counseling on activity restrictions until cleared.

  5. Station 5: Elderly Woman in Acute Respiratory Distress

    Reading Stem

    A 79-year-old woman with a history of heart failure and hypertension is brought in by EMS with acute severe shortness of breath that woke her from sleep. She is sitting bolt upright, using accessory muscles, with pink frothy sputum noted by paramedics.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 190/100, HR 118, RR 34, SpO2 84% on room air, afebrile, bibasilar crackles to the mid-lung fields, JVD present, cool extremities. This is acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema (flash pulmonary edema) likely from hypertensive emergency or acute decompensated heart failure. The candidate should start high-flow oxygen or non-invasive ventilation (CPAP/BiPAP) immediately, sit patient upright, and give sublingual or IV nitroglycerin to reduce afterload/preload given the elevated blood pressure, along with consideration of IV furosemide. If the candidate delays NIV/oxygen for a full history, deteriorate SpO2 to 78% and mental status to prompt recognition of impending respiratory failure requiring more aggressive airway support or intubation. Reveal ECG showing sinus tachycardia with LVH pattern, no acute ischemic changes, but ask the candidate to still rule out an ischemic trigger. If nitroglycerin is given appropriately, improve BP to 150/88 and SpO2 to 92% on CPAP within a few minutes. Discuss disposition to ICU or high-acuity monitored bed and identification of the underlying trigger (missed medications, dietary indiscretion, ischemia, arrhythmia).

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema and starts oxygen or non-invasive ventilation immediately
    • Positions patient upright and gives nitroglycerin to reduce afterload given hypertension
    • Considers diuretic therapy appropriately while monitoring response
    • Rules out an ischemic or arrhythmic trigger with ECG and further workup
    • Recognizes signs of impending respiratory failure and escalates to more advanced airway support if needed

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This presentation of sudden orthopnea, frothy sputum, hypoxia, and hypertension in a known heart failure patient points strongly to acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema, so my first move is sitting her upright and starting high-flow oxygen or CPAP right away rather than taking a long history first. Given her high blood pressure, I'd give sublingual or IV nitroglycerin to reduce preload and afterload, and consider IV furosemide once I've reassessed her volume status. I want an ECG to make sure this isn't being driven by an acute ischemic event or arrhythmia, since treating the trigger matters as much as treating the pulmonary edema itself. If she doesn't improve with CPAP and nitrates, or if her mental status declines, I would move toward intubation rather than waiting, since respiratory fatigue can happen quickly in flash pulmonary edema. She would need ICU-level monitoring given the severity of this presentation and the need to identify what precipitated the episode.

  6. Station 6: Young Man with Severe Asthma Exacerbation

    Reading Stem

    A 22-year-old man with known asthma presents with progressively worsening shortness of breath and wheezing over the past 6 hours despite using his rescue inhaler repeatedly. He can only speak in short phrases.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 138/82, HR 128, RR 32, SpO2 89% on room air, using accessory muscles, diffuse wheeze bilaterally, tripod positioning. This is a severe asthma exacerbation. The candidate should start oxygen, continuous nebulized salbutamol and ipratropium, and early systemic corticosteroids (oral or IV). If they ask for a peak flow, it is severely reduced (below 40% predicted). If treatment is delayed, worsen the wheeze to a silent chest with rising CO2-type fatigue and dropping SpO2 to 82 percent, a critical exam point testing recognition that a silent chest signals near-fatal asthma, not improvement. At this point the candidate should discuss IV magnesium sulfate, consideration of IV salbutamol or epinephrine, and preparation for possible intubation with a low threshold given risk of complete airway obstruction and permissive hypercapnia strategy if intubated (long expiratory time, low respiratory rate to avoid breath-stacking and barotrauma). If the candidate wants a chest x-ray before treating, prompt them that treatment should not be delayed for imaging in a decompensating patient. Discuss ICU admission given severity.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Starts oxygen and continuous nebulized bronchodilators (salbutamol/ipratropium) without delay
    • Gives early systemic corticosteroids
    • Recognizes a silent chest as a sign of critical, near-fatal asthma rather than improvement
    • Considers IV magnesium sulfate and escalates to advanced therapies (IV salbutamol/epinephrine) in refractory cases
    • Discusses careful ventilation strategy (permissive hypercapnia, avoiding breath-stacking) if intubation is required

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This is a severe asthma exacerbation with accessory muscle use and hypoxia despite rescue inhaler use, so I'm starting oxygen and continuous nebulized salbutamol with ipratropium immediately, along with early systemic corticosteroids, without waiting for any imaging. If his wheeze suddenly quiets while he looks more fatigued and his oxygen drops further, I would treat that silent chest as a red flag for near-complete airway obstruction rather than reassurance, and escalate quickly. At that point I'd add IV magnesium sulfate and consider IV salbutamol or epinephrine, while preparing airway equipment and calling for anesthesia or critical care support given how quickly these patients can arrest. If intubation becomes necessary, I would use a permissive hypercapnia strategy with a slower respiratory rate and long expiratory time to avoid breath-stacking and barotrauma. Given the severity of this presentation, he needs ICU-level care regardless of how he responds to initial therapy.

  7. Station 7: Elderly Smoker with Progressive Dyspnea and Fever

    Reading Stem

    A 71-year-old man with a 40 pack-year smoking history presents with 3 days of worsening shortness of breath, productive cough with green sputum, and subjective fevers. He appears fatigued and is speaking in short sentences.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 108/68, HR 112, RR 28, SpO2 88% on room air, Temp 38.6C. Exam reveals decreased breath sounds and crackles at the right base, using accessory muscles. This represents a COPD exacerbation complicated by pneumonia (sepsis from a respiratory source). The candidate should start oxygen targeting appropriate saturations (being mindful of CO2 retention risk in COPD but not withholding needed oxygen), bronchodilators, corticosteroids, and antibiotics covering typical and atypical organisms, plus sepsis bundle elements (fluids, lactate, blood cultures before antibiotics if feasible without delay). If oxygen is given at very high flow without titration, show rising somnolence to test recognition of CO2 narcosis risk and prompt for titration and consideration of NIV (BiPAP) for combined hypoxic and hypercapnic respiratory failure. If NIV or careful oxygen titration is used correctly, improve mentation and vitals gradually. Chest x-ray if ordered shows right lower lobe consolidation. Discuss admission, likely to a monitored bed given degree of illness, and consider ICU if he does not stabilize with NIV.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes combined COPD exacerbation and pneumonia causing type 2 respiratory failure and possible sepsis
    • Titrates oxygen carefully in a COPD patient while still adequately treating hypoxia
    • Starts bronchodilators, corticosteroids, and appropriate antibiotics without significant delay
    • Considers non-invasive ventilation (BiPAP) for combined hypoxic/hypercapnic failure
    • Applies sepsis bundle principles (cultures, fluids, lactate) and arranges appropriate level of care disposition

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This looks like a COPD exacerbation complicated by pneumonia, so I need to manage both the airway obstruction and a possible septic source at the same time. I'd start oxygen but titrate it carefully rather than blasting high-flow oxygen, since COPD patients can retain carbon dioxide, and I'd watch closely for any change in mental status suggesting CO2 narcosis. I'm giving nebulized bronchodilators, systemic steroids, and broad antibiotics covering typical and atypical pneumonia organisms, along with sepsis-bundle elements like fluids and blood cultures drawn before antibiotics if that doesn't delay treatment. If he continues to struggle with both oxygenation and ventilation, I would move to BiPAP to support both problems while avoiding intubation if possible. Given his degree of illness, I'd admit him to a monitored bed and keep a low threshold for ICU transfer if he doesn't improve on non-invasive support.

  8. Station 8: Sudden Onset Weakness and Slurred Speech

    Reading Stem

    A 66-year-old woman is brought in by her husband after he noticed sudden right-sided facial droop, arm weakness, and slurred speech 50 minutes ago. She has a history of atrial fibrillation and is on no anticoagulation currently.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 172/94, HR 96 irregularly irregular, RR 16, SpO2 97%, glucose 6.2 mmol/L (normal, ruling out hypoglycemia mimic). NIHSS reveals facial droop, right arm drift, and expressive aphasia. The candidate should recognize this as an acute ischemic stroke within the thrombolysis window and move rapidly: last known well time, finger-stick glucose, urgent non-contrast CT head, and stroke team activation. If CT is clear of hemorrhage, discuss tPA eligibility criteria (time window, contraindications such as recent surgery, bleeding risk, BP control below 185/110 before treatment) and also consider CT angiogram for large vessel occlusion and possible mechanical thrombectomy given the AFib history suggesting cardioembolic large clot burden. If the candidate delays imaging to complete a full history, prompt with 'the clock is running, tPA window is closing' to test time-sensitivity awareness. If BP remains too high for thrombolysis, ask for correct antihypertensive choice (e.g. labetalol) to get below threshold safely. Discuss admission to stroke unit and future anticoagulation planning for her atrial fibrillation once acute period passes.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes sudden focal neurologic deficits as acute stroke and confirms last known well time immediately
    • Checks glucose promptly to rule out a stroke mimic
    • Orders urgent non-contrast CT head and activates the stroke team without delay
    • Discusses tPA eligibility criteria and blood pressure targets, and considers CT angiogram/thrombectomy for large vessel occlusion
    • Manages blood pressure safely to meet thrombolysis thresholds and arranges stroke unit admission

    Model Verbalized Answer

    With sudden facial droop, arm weakness, and aphasia, I'm treating this as an acute stroke until proven otherwise, so my first priorities are confirming the exact last known well time and checking a glucose to rule out hypoglycemia as a mimic. I want a stat non-contrast CT head and to activate the stroke team immediately, because every minute matters for thrombolysis eligibility. Given her atrial fibrillation, I'm also worried about a large vessel occlusion, so I'd request a CT angiogram at the same time to assess for a clot amenable to mechanical thrombectomy. If the CT is clear of bleeding and she's within the window, I would review contraindications carefully and get her blood pressure under the threshold required for tPA using an agent like labetalol before proceeding. She would need admission to a stroke unit for close monitoring, and once she's through the acute period, I'd start planning anticoagulation for her atrial fibrillation to prevent recurrence.

  9. Station 9: Sudden Severe Headache in a Middle-Aged Woman

    Reading Stem

    A 45-year-old woman presents with the worst headache of her life, described as sudden onset and reaching maximum intensity within seconds, associated with neck stiffness and one episode of vomiting. She has no significant past medical history.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 158/90, HR 92, RR 18, SpO2 98%, Temp 37.1C. Exam shows mild nuchal rigidity, no focal neurologic deficit, photophobia present. This is a classic thunderclap headache concerning for subarachnoid hemorrhage. The candidate should order an urgent non-contrast CT head immediately. If done within 6 hours of symptom onset, the CT is negative and the candidate must know that a negative CT within 6 hours has very high sensitivity but should still discuss either shared decision-making to forgo LP or proceeding to lumbar puncture if any uncertainty remains, looking for xanthochromia or elevated red cell counts that don't clear between tubes. If the candidate wants to discharge based on a normal CT alone outside this timeframe, prompt for LP or CT angiogram discussion. If LP is performed, reveal xanthochromia, confirming SAH despite negative CT. Discuss urgent neurosurgery/neurointerventional consultation for aneurysm workup (CT angiogram) once SAH confirmed, blood pressure control to avoid rebleeding, and avoiding premature discharge of a thunderclap headache patient without adequate workup.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes thunderclap headache as a red flag requiring urgent subarachnoid hemorrhage workup
    • Orders non-contrast CT head urgently and correctly interprets the significance of timing since onset
    • Knows when a negative CT still requires further workup such as lumbar puncture
    • Correctly interprets lumbar puncture findings (xanthochromia) to confirm SAH despite a negative CT
    • Arranges urgent neurosurgery/neurointerventional consultation and appropriate blood pressure management

    Model Verbalized Answer

    A sudden, maximal-intensity headache with neck stiffness is a thunderclap headache, and my top concern is subarachnoid hemorrhage until I can rule it out, so I'm ordering a stat non-contrast CT head right away. If that CT is done within six hours of onset and is negative, I know the sensitivity is very high, but I'd still have a shared decision-making conversation about whether to proceed with a lumbar puncture given how catastrophic a missed SAH would be. If the CT is negative outside that window or there's any uncertainty, I'd move forward with LP looking for xanthochromia or a red cell count that doesn't clear between tubes. If either the CT or LP confirms SAH, I'm getting urgent neurosurgery involved along with a CT angiogram to look for an aneurysm, and I'd control her blood pressure carefully to reduce the risk of rebleeding before definitive treatment. I would not discharge a thunderclap headache patient without completing this full workup, regardless of how well she looks in the department.

  10. Station 10: Young Man in Status Epilepticus

    Reading Stem

    A 26-year-old man with known epilepsy is brought in by EMS actively seizing for the past 8 minutes with generalized tonic-clonic movements. Paramedics report he missed his anticonvulsant medications for several days.

    Examiner Scenario

    On arrival the patient continues to seize. Vitals: HR 128, BP 148/86, SpO2 90% on room air with poor mask seal due to seizure activity, glucose 5.8 mmol/L. The candidate should manage airway/breathing/circulation first, position patient safely, give supplemental oxygen, and administer a benzodiazepine (IV lorazepam or IM midazolam if no IV access) as first-line therapy for status epilepticus. If the first dose does not terminate the seizure within 5 minutes, prompt for a second benzodiazepine dose and then a second-line agent (IV fosphenytoin, phenytoin, valproate, or levetiracetam) if seizure continues. If seizures persist despite two lines of therapy (refractory status epilepticus), the candidate should discuss anesthetic infusion (propofol or midazolam infusion) with intubation and continuous EEG monitoring in ICU. Throughout, ask the candidate to check glucose promptly (already normal here) and consider other reversible causes (electrolyte abnormalities, missed medications as given, infection, structural lesion). Discuss post-ictal management, anticonvulsant level checking, and neurology consultation plus disposition to a monitored or ICU bed given prolonged seizure activity.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Manages airway, breathing, and circulation first while positioning the patient safely during active seizure
    • Checks glucose promptly and considers reversible causes of seizure
    • Gives a benzodiazepine as first-line treatment with correct route and repeats if seizure continues
    • Escalates appropriately to second-line antiepileptic agents if benzodiazepines fail
    • Recognizes refractory status epilepticus and discusses anesthetic infusion with intubation and continuous EEG

    Model Verbalized Answer
