    My first priority in an actively seizing patient is protecting his airway and breathing, positioning him safely on his side, and giving supplemental oxygen while getting IV access if possible. I'd check his glucose right away since hypoglycemia is a quick reversible cause, and I know he's missed his anticonvulsants, which is a likely trigger here. I'm giving a benzodiazepine like IV lorazepam or IM midazolam as first-line therapy, and if the seizure hasn't stopped within about five minutes, I would repeat the dose and then move to a second-line agent such as IV levetiracetam or fosphenytoin. If he continues seizing despite two lines of therapy, that's refractory status epilepticus, and I would move toward intubation with an anesthetic infusion like propofol, along with continuous EEG monitoring in the ICU. Once the seizure is controlled, I'd check anticonvulsant drug levels, consult neurology, and arrange monitored admission given the prolonged seizure duration.

  11. Station 11: Unresponsive Elderly Man Found at Home

    Reading Stem

    A 74-year-old man is brought in by paramedics after being found unresponsive at home by a family member. Time last seen normal is unknown. He has a history of diabetes and hypertension, and paramedics note an empty pill bottle nearby.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 98/60, HR 58, RR 10 and shallow, SpO2 90% on room air, GCS 7 (E2V2M3), pupils pinpoint and sluggish. Glucose on fingerstick is 3.1 mmol/L (hypoglycemic) in addition to signs suggesting possible opioid co-ingestion (pinpoint pupils, respiratory depression). The candidate should follow an ABC approach: secure airway given low GCS and respiratory depression, consider bag-mask ventilation, give IV dextrose promptly for hypoglycemia, and consider naloxone given pupillary findings and respiratory depression consistent with opioid toxicity, while remaining ready to intubate if he does not improve or protect his airway. If glucose and naloxone are given, some improvement in GCS and respiratory rate should be shown, but not full resolution, prompting further workup: toxicology screen, head CT to rule out intracranial process (especially given unwitnessed fall risk and anticoagulant use if applicable), electrolytes, and review of medications for overdose (the empty pill bottle). Discuss ICU admission, poison control consultation, and psychiatric assessment once medically stable given possible intentional overdose.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Approaches systematically with airway, breathing, circulation, and immediately checks glucose
    • Recognizes pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression as possible opioid toxicity and gives naloxone appropriately
    • Treats hypoglycemia promptly with IV dextrose
    • Considers head imaging and toxicology workup given unknown time last seen normal and unresponsiveness
    • Plans for airway protection/intubation if the patient does not adequately improve, and arranges ICU admission with psychiatric assessment once stable

    Model Verbalized Answer

    In an unresponsive patient with an unknown down-time, I'm going through airway, breathing, and circulation first, and checking a glucose immediately since reversible hypoglycemia has to be ruled out right away. His glucose is low, so I'm giving IV dextrose right away, and his pinpoint pupils and slow shallow breathing make me suspicious for opioid toxicity, so I'd also give naloxone while supporting his breathing with bag-mask ventilation if needed. Given his low GCS and depressed respiratory drive, I'm keeping a low threshold to intubate if he doesn't improve enough to protect his airway. Because the exact time he was last normal is unknown and there's an empty pill bottle involved, I also want a head CT to rule out an intracranial cause like hemorrhage, along with a broader toxicology screen and electrolytes. Once he's stabilized, he needs ICU-level monitoring, poison control input on the ingestion, and a psychiatric assessment given the possibility this was an intentional overdose.

  12. Station 12: Acutely Agitated Elderly Woman from Long-Term Care

    Reading Stem

    An 84-year-old woman from a long-term care facility is brought in for new-onset confusion and agitation over the past day, pulling at her IV lines and yelling. Staff report she was her normal cognitive baseline three days ago.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 100/62, HR 112, RR 22, SpO2 94% on room air, Temp 38.4C. She is fluctuating in attention, disoriented, and intermittently combative, consistent with delirium (hyperactive subtype) rather than primary dementia given the acute onset. The candidate should pursue a broad delirium workup: infection screen (urinalysis, chest x-ray, blood cultures), glucose, electrolytes, medication review (new drugs, anticholinergic burden, withdrawal), and consider CT head only if focal deficits or trauma history present. Reveal urinalysis positive for a urinary tract infection as the precipitant. The candidate should prioritize non-pharmacologic de-escalation first (reorientation, quiet environment, involving familiar staff/family, avoiding physical restraints when possible) before reaching for medications, and if pharmacologic control is needed for safety, choose a low-dose antipsychotic (e.g. low-dose haloperidol or risperidone) rather than benzodiazepines, which can worsen delirium in the elderly except in withdrawal-related cases. If the candidate reaches immediately for benzodiazepines or physical restraints without attempting de-escalation, prompt with worsening agitation and confusion to illustrate the pitfall. Discuss treating the underlying UTI, avoiding new anticholinergic medications, and admission with delirium precautions.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Distinguishes acute delirium from baseline dementia based on the timeline and fluctuating course
    • Pursues a broad workup for reversible causes including infection, metabolic, and medication review
    • Attempts non-pharmacologic de-escalation before medications or restraints
    • Chooses appropriate low-dose antipsychotic rather than benzodiazepines if pharmacologic control is required in this elderly patient
    • Identifies and treats the underlying precipitant (UTI) and arranges admission with delirium precautions

    Model Verbalized Answer

    The acute onset and fluctuating course here point to delirium rather than a primary dementia process, so I want to find the reversible cause rather than just sedating her. I'm ordering a broad workup including urinalysis, chest x-ray, blood work, glucose, and a careful medication review, since infection and drug effects are common precipitants in elderly patients. Before reaching for any medication, I'd try non-pharmacologic measures first, like reorienting her, keeping the environment calm and quiet, and involving familiar staff or family if available, and I'd avoid physical restraints unless there's no safer option. If she remains unsafe despite that, I would use a low dose of an antipsychotic like haloperidol rather than a benzodiazepine, since benzodiazepines tend to worsen delirium in the elderly outside of a withdrawal context. Once I identify her urinary tract infection as the likely trigger, I'd start appropriate antibiotics, avoid adding new anticholinergic medications, and admit her with delirium precautions in place.

  13. Station 13: Young Adult After a Suicide Attempt

    Reading Stem

    A 19-year-old university student is brought in by friends after they found her having taken an unknown quantity of acetaminophen tablets roughly 2 hours ago, following a breakup. She is medically stable but tearful and states she wishes she had died.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 118/76, HR 88, RR 16, SpO2 99%. Medically she appears well at this point, but acetaminophen overdose can cause delayed hepatotoxicity, so the candidate must obtain an acetaminophen level (timed appropriately, at least 4 hours post-ingestion for accurate interpretation on the nomogram) along with liver enzymes, INR, and consider activated charcoal if within the appropriate time window. If the level plotted on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram is above the treatment line, the candidate should start N-acetylcysteine. In parallel, the candidate must conduct a thorough suicide risk assessment: intent, plan, access to means, prior attempts, protective factors, and current safety, rather than focusing only on the medical overdose. If the candidate discusses discharge without a formal psychiatric assessment, prompt with 'she says she still wants to die and has more pills at home' to test recognition that she requires continuous observation and psychiatric evaluation before any disposition decision, including removing access to means (pills) and involving family/friends appropriately with her consent considerations. Discuss admission for both the medical overdose and psychiatric safety, with 1:1 observation as needed.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes acetaminophen overdose as a potentially serious ingestion requiring level testing at the correct time and considers activated charcoal or NAC as indicated
    • Uses the Rumack-Matthew nomogram appropriately to guide NAC treatment decisions
    • Conducts a thorough suicide risk assessment covering intent, plan, means, and prior attempts, not just the medical overdose
    • Does not discharge the patient without formal psychiatric evaluation given ongoing suicidal ideation
    • Arranges continuous safety observation and appropriate admission addressing both medical and psychiatric needs

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Even though she looks medically well right now, acetaminophen overdose can cause delayed liver injury, so I need an accurately timed acetaminophen level along with liver enzymes and INR, and I'd plot the level on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram to decide if she needs N-acetylcysteine. At the same time, I'm not going to let the medical workup distract from a thorough suicide risk assessment, asking about her intent, whether this was planned, access to further means, and any prior attempts. Given that she's telling me she still wants to die and has more pills accessible at home, I would not consider discharge until she's had a formal psychiatric evaluation and I've ensured continuous safety observation in the department. I'd also involve her support system where appropriate and make sure means restriction is addressed before she leaves any care setting. My disposition would be admission addressing both the potential hepatotoxicity and her psychiatric safety, with 1:1 observation while she awaits psychiatric assessment.

  14. Station 14: Middle-Aged Man with Chronic Suicidal Ideation and a Firearm at Home

    Reading Stem

    A 52-year-old man presents at his family's insistence after they overheard him discussing a specific plan to end his life using a firearm he owns. He has a history of depression and recent job loss, and denies any current intent to harm himself while in the department.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals are unremarkable and there is no acute medical issue. This station tests a pure psychiatric risk assessment rather than a medical resuscitation. The candidate should explore his mental state in depth: current mood, hopelessness, specific plan details, timeline, access to the firearm, alcohol or substance use, protective factors (family, future plans, religious or cultural beliefs), and any recent change in behavior such as giving away possessions. If the candidate accepts his in-department denial of intent at face value without probing further given the collateral history from family, prompt with additional collateral detail (he recently updated his will and gave away a valued possession) to test whether the candidate appropriately weighs collateral information over reassurance alone. The candidate should discuss means restriction specifically and concretely, meaning coordinating with family or authorities to remove the firearm from the home, not just verbal safety planning. Expect discussion of whether involuntary/formal mental health legislation hold criteria are met given ongoing risk despite his denial, and appropriate psychiatric consultation before any discharge decision. Discuss safety planning and follow-up arrangements if he is ultimately assessed as lower risk after full evaluation.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Conducts a detailed suicide risk assessment including specific plan, timeline, and access to lethal means
    • Weighs collateral history from family appropriately rather than relying solely on the patient's in-the-moment denial
    • Specifically addresses concrete means restriction (removing the firearm) rather than only verbal reassurance
    • Considers criteria for involuntary psychiatric hold if risk remains high despite patient's denial
    • Involves psychiatric consultation before any discharge decision and arranges appropriate safety planning and follow-up

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Because his family brought him in specifically due to a detailed plan involving a firearm, I'm taking that collateral information very seriously even though he's denying current intent to me directly. I would ask in detail about his mood, hopelessness, whether he still has access to the firearm, any substance use, and specific behavioral changes like giving away possessions or updating his will, which the family has already flagged as concerning. Given that collateral information suggests ongoing high risk despite his verbal denial, I wouldn't simply accept reassurance at face value, and I would consider whether criteria for an involuntary psychiatric hold are met to keep him safe. Regardless of the legal pathway, I'm making sure means restriction happens concretely, meaning I'd coordinate with his family or local authorities to have the firearm removed from the home rather than relying on a verbal promise. I would not make any discharge decision without a formal psychiatric consultation, and if he's ultimately assessed as lower risk, I'd still ensure a detailed safety plan and prompt follow-up are arranged before he leaves.

  15. Station 15: High-Speed Motor Vehicle Collision

    Reading Stem

    A 34-year-old man is brought in by EMS as a restrained driver in a high-speed head-on collision with prolonged extrication. He is alert but complaining of abdominal pain and difficulty breathing. EMS report significant vehicle deformity.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 92/58, HR 128, RR 28, SpO2 91% on room air, GCS 14. The candidate should run a standard trauma primary survey (ABCDE). Airway is patent, but breath sounds are decreased on the left with hyperresonance to percussion, suggesting tension pneumothorax; if the candidate does not act quickly, drop BP to 70/40 and worsen respiratory distress to force recognition and immediate needle decompression or finger thoracostomy followed by chest tube placement. Once decompressed, improve vitals. Continue survey: abdomen is distended and tender, concerning for intra-abdominal hemorrhage; the candidate should recognize hemorrhagic shock physiology, activate massive transfusion protocol if instability persists, give blood products preferentially over large crystalloid volumes, and arrange emergent surgical consultation with likely need for laparotomy given peritonitic abdomen and instability (FAST exam if performed shows free fluid). Pelvis exam should be checked once for stability (stable here) and a full secondary survey completed for other injuries. Discuss trauma team activation, damage control resuscitation principles, and transfer or OR disposition.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Runs a systematic ABCDE primary survey rather than jumping to isolated injuries
    • Recognizes and immediately treats tension pneumothorax with needle or finger decompression before chest tube placement
    • Recognizes signs of hemorrhagic shock and prioritizes blood products over large-volume crystalloids
    • Performs FAST exam and correctly identifies intra-abdominal hemorrhage requiring emergent surgical involvement
    • Activates trauma team/massive transfusion protocol as appropriate and arranges timely surgical disposition

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given the mechanism and his vitals, I'm running a structured primary survey starting with airway, which is patent, then breathing, where I'm hearing decreased breath sounds on the left with hyperresonance, which makes me concerned for a tension pneumothorax. I would not wait for imaging here, I'd perform immediate needle decompression or finger thoracostomy followed by a chest tube, since delaying this can lead to cardiac arrest. Moving to circulation, his abdomen is distended and tender with hypotension and tachycardia, which points to hemorrhagic shock likely from intra-abdominal bleeding, so I'm activating the trauma team and considering massive transfusion protocol, prioritizing blood products over large crystalloid volumes to avoid dilutional coagulopathy. I'd get a FAST exam to look for free fluid and involve trauma surgery immediately given his instability, since this pattern strongly suggests he needs to go to the operating room rather than for further imaging alone. Throughout, I'm continuing a secondary survey for other injuries once the immediate life threats are addressed, and preparing for rapid transfer to the OR.

  16. Station 16: Fall from Height with Head and Limb Injuries

    Reading Stem

    A 45-year-old construction worker fell approximately 6 meters from scaffolding and is brought in immobilized on a backboard with a cervical collar. He is confused, complaining of severe leg pain, and has an obviously deformed lower leg.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 108/70, HR 110, RR 22, SpO2 95% on supplemental oxygen, GCS 12 (confused, opens eyes to voice, localizes pain). The candidate should proceed through ABCDE with full spinal precautions given fall mechanism. Airway and breathing are intact; circulation shows an open, deformed left tibia/fibula fracture with brisk bleeding and reduced distal pulses, requiring direct pressure/hemorrhage control, realignment, and urgent orthopedic consultation given open fracture and possible vascular compromise (consider compartment syndrome monitoring). Disability shows GCS 13 with a fall from height, warranting CT head and full cervical spine imaging given high-risk mechanism, and the candidate should maintain full spinal precautions until cleared. If the candidate removes the collar early without clearance criteria being met, prompt with 'the patient still has midline neck tenderness' to test correct application of clinical decision rules (such as Canadian C-spine rules) rather than premature clearance. Exposure reveals no other obvious injuries but the candidate should log-roll to check the back. Discuss tetanus status, antibiotics for open fracture, pain control, and trauma/ortho/neurosurgery consultation with appropriate imaging-guided disposition.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Applies full ABCDE trauma assessment with spinal precautions given the high-risk fall mechanism
    • Recognizes and manages the open fracture with hemorrhage control, realignment, and assessment of distal perfusion for possible compartment syndrome or vascular injury
    • Orders appropriate head and cervical spine imaging given the mechanism and GCS, and does not clear the spine prematurely without meeting decision rule criteria
    • Performs a log-roll as part of the exposure/back examination
    • Gives appropriate open fracture management (antibiotics, tetanus status) and arranges orthopedic/neurosurgical consultation

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given a fall from height with altered mental status, I'm keeping full spinal precautions in place while running my ABCDE assessment. Airway and breathing look fine, but on circulation I see an open, deformed lower leg fracture with active bleeding, so I'd apply direct pressure, realign the limb to help perfusion, and check distal pulses closely for any vascular compromise or early signs of compartment syndrome. Given his GCS of 13 and the high-risk mechanism, I want a CT head and cervical spine imaging, and I would not remove his collar until he meets clear clinical decision rule criteria, especially since he still has midline neck tenderness. As part of exposure, I'd log-roll him to check his back for any other injuries I might otherwise miss. For the open fracture, I'm starting antibiotics, checking his tetanus status, and getting orthopedics involved urgently, while keeping neurosurgery on standby depending on his head CT findings, and I'd manage his pain throughout with appropriate titrated analgesia.

  17. Station 17: Elderly Woman After a Fall onto an Outstretched Hand

    Reading Stem

    A 78-year-old woman presents after slipping on ice and falling onto her outstretched right hand. She has immediate wrist pain, swelling, and an obvious 'dinner fork' deformity. She takes no blood thinners and has osteoporosis.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals are normal. Exam shows a classic dorsally displaced distal radius fracture (Colles fracture) with dinner-fork deformity, intact median nerve function on sensory testing if checked (candidate should specifically test for median nerve/acute carpal tunnel signs), and palpable radial pulse. If the candidate skips neurovascular exam before and after any procedure, prompt with 'do you want to check anything before you proceed?' X-ray if ordered confirms dorsally angulated distal radius fracture without intra-articular extension. The candidate should discuss procedural sedation or hematoma block for pain control, closed reduction technique, and post-reduction neurovascular recheck plus repeat x-ray to confirm adequate alignment. Discuss splinting (sugar-tong or volar splint, not a full cast acutely due to swelling risk) and orthopedic follow-up. Given her age and osteoporosis, the candidate should also raise osteoporosis screening/treatment and falls risk assessment as part of secondary injury prevention before discharge.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Performs and documents a neurovascular exam before and after any reduction attempt
    • Correctly identifies the fracture pattern (Colles-type distal radius fracture) from history and exam/imaging
    • Uses appropriate analgesia/anesthesia for reduction (hematoma block or procedural sedation) and describes correct reduction technique
    • Obtains post-reduction neurovascular check and repeat imaging to confirm alignment, then applies an appropriate splint
    • Addresses osteoporosis screening and falls risk assessment as part of secondary prevention given her age and fracture pattern

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This fall onto an outstretched hand with a dinner-fork deformity is a classic presentation of a distal radius fracture, so I'd get an x-ray to confirm the pattern and displacement, but before doing anything else I want to document a full neurovascular exam, specifically checking median nerve function given its proximity to this injury. For the reduction itself, I'd use a hematoma block or procedural sedation for adequate analgesia, then perform closed reduction with traction and correction of the dorsal angulation. After reduction, I'm rechecking neurovascular status again and getting a repeat x-ray to confirm the alignment is acceptable before splinting her in a sugar-tong or volar splint rather than a full circumferential cast, given the swelling risk. I'd arrange orthopedic follow-up for ongoing fracture care, and given her age and this fragility-type fracture, I'd also use this visit to raise osteoporosis screening and a falls risk assessment, since this is a good opportunity to prevent future fractures.

  18. Station 18: Deep Hand Laceration from a Kitchen Knife

    Reading Stem

    A 29-year-old line cook presents with a deep laceration across the palm of his dominant hand from a kitchen knife 1 hour ago. He reports numbness in his index finger and difficulty gripping objects since the injury.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals are normal. On exam there is a 4 cm deep laceration across the palm with active oozing controlled by pressure. The candidate should perform a thorough neurovascular and tendon function exam before any local anesthesia is given (testing two-point discrimination/sensation in each digit, capillary refill, and specific flexor tendon function against resistance for each finger). Findings should reveal reduced sensation in the index finger's radial digital nerve distribution and weakness of finger flexion against resistance, concerning for both digital nerve injury and partial flexor tendon injury. If the candidate anesthetizes the wound and explores/irrigates/repairs without having documented this baseline exam first, prompt with 'what did you find on your exam before you numbed the hand?' to test sequencing. Given suspected tendon and nerve injury, the candidate should recognize this requires exploration in the operating room by a hand surgeon rather than simple ED closure, and should avoid blind clamping of bleeders (risk of further nerve injury), instead using direct pressure and elevation for hemostasis. Discuss tetanus status, antibiotics if heavily contaminated, splinting in a position of safety, and urgent hand surgery consultation with appropriate analgesia while awaiting consult.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Performs a detailed neurovascular and tendon function exam before administering local anesthesia
    • Correctly identifies signs suggesting digital nerve and flexor tendon injury on exam
    • Avoids blind clamping of bleeding vessels in the hand and uses direct pressure/elevation instead
    • Recognizes that suspected tendon/nerve injury requires urgent hand surgery evaluation rather than simple primary closure in the ED
    • Addresses tetanus prophylaxis, antibiotics if indicated, appropriate splinting, and analgesia while awaiting specialist care

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Before I do anything to control bleeding or numb the hand, I want to fully document a neurovascular and tendon function exam, checking sensation in each digit and testing flexor tendon function against resistance, since local anesthetic or exploration could mask these findings afterward. Given his numbness in the index finger and weak grip, I'm concerned about both a digital nerve injury and a partial flexor tendon laceration, which changes my management significantly. For bleeding control, I'd use direct pressure and elevation rather than blindly clamping any visible vessels, since that risks causing further nerve damage in a confined space like the hand. Because I suspect tendon and nerve involvement, I don't think this is appropriate for simple closure in the emergency department, and I'd get hand surgery involved urgently for operative exploration and repair. While waiting for that consultation, I'd make sure his tetanus status is addressed, give antibiotics if the wound looks contaminated, splint the hand in a position of safety, and provide adequate analgesia.

  19. Station 19: Adult with Partial Thickness Burns from a House Fire

    Reading Stem

    A 38-year-old man is brought in after being rescued from a house fire with burns to both arms and his anterior chest, along with a hoarse voice and soot around his mouth and nose. He was in the enclosed space for several minutes before rescue.

    Examiner Scenario

    Vitals: BP 128/80, HR 108, RR 24, SpO2 97% on room air (note carbon monoxide can cause falsely normal pulse oximetry). Exam shows partial thickness burns to both arms and anterior chest, along with facial soot, singed nasal hairs, and hoarseness, all signs of possible inhalational injury and impending airway compromise. The candidate should prioritize early airway assessment and have a low threshold for early intubation given the risk of rapidly progressive airway edema, even before visible distress worsens, since delayed intubation becomes far more difficult once swelling sets in. If the candidate delays airway management to first calculate burn surface area, prompt with worsening stridor and voice change to reinforce urgency. The candidate should calculate total body surface area burned using the rule of nines (roughly 27 percent here: 9 percent each arm plus 9 percent for half the anterior trunk) and begin fluid resuscitation using a recognized formula (e.g. Parkland formula) with correct volume and timing (half in first 8 hours from time of injury, not time of arrival). Carbon monoxide poisoning should be considered given the enclosed space exposure, with a carboxyhemoglobin level checked and high-flow oxygen or consideration of hyperbaric oxygen if severe. Discuss burn wound care, pain control, tetanus status, and transfer to a burn center given the total body surface area and inhalational injury.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes signs of inhalational injury (soot, singed nasal hairs, hoarseness) and has a low threshold for early, proactive airway management
    • Understands that pulse oximetry can be falsely reassuring in carbon monoxide poisoning and checks a carboxyhemoglobin level
    • Calculates total body surface area burned accurately using the rule of nines
    • Calculates fluid resuscitation correctly using a recognized formula, with correct timing from time of injury
    • Arranges appropriate burn center transfer given the extent of burns and inhalational injury, along with wound care, analgesia, and tetanus prophylaxis

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given the soot around his mouth, singed nasal hairs, and hoarse voice after being in an enclosed fire, my top priority is airway management, since inhalational injury can progress to complete airway obstruction as swelling develops, so I want a low threshold to intubate early rather than waiting for visible distress to worsen. I also know his normal pulse oximetry reading could be falsely reassuring if he has carbon monoxide poisoning, so I'd check a carboxyhemoglobin level and start high-flow oxygen regardless of his saturation reading. Once the airway is secured, I'd calculate his total body surface area burned using the rule of nines, which comes to around 27 percent given both arms and half his anterior trunk, and use that to calculate fluid resuscitation with the Parkland formula, making sure I calculate the timing from when the injury actually occurred rather than from his arrival time. I'd also address burn wound care, adequate analgesia, and his tetanus status. Given the extent of his burns and the inhalational injury, he needs transfer to a dedicated burn center rather than staying in a general emergency department.

  20. Station 20: Elderly Man with Pain Out of Proportion to Exam

    Reading Stem

    A 78 year old man with atrial fibrillation on no anticoagulant presents with sudden severe diffuse abdominal pain that started 3 hours ago. He is nauseated and has vomited once. His abdomen looks unremarkable on inspection. Vital signs on arrival: HR 118 irregular, BP 128/78, RR 20, temp 37.6C.

    Examiner Scenario

    If asked, the abdomen is soft with mild diffuse tenderness but no peritonism initially, pain is described by the patient as far worse than the exam suggests. Bowel sounds are reduced. If the candidate asks about history, reveal known atrial fibrillation, no anticoagulation due to a past fall, and no prior abdominal surgery. Lactate returns elevated at 4.2. If the candidate orders a CT angiogram of the abdomen, reveal findings consistent with acute superior mesenteric artery embolic occlusion with no evidence of perforation yet. If the candidate does not consider mesenteric ischemia and instead pursues a benign workup, at minute 8 have the patient develop overt peritonism and hypotension to force recognition of a surgical emergency. Reward candidates who recognize pain out of proportion to exam plus atrial fibrillation as a red flag early, who order lactate and CT angiography promptly, who involve general surgery and vascular surgery urgently, and who start resuscitation with fluids and broad spectrum antibiotics while arranging emergent laparotomy or angiography. Penalize candidates who anchor on a benign diagnosis such as gastroenteritis or constipation given the atypical exam. Disposition is emergent operative or interventional radiology consultation, not discharge or routine admission.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes pain out of proportion to physical exam as a red flag
    • Identifies atrial fibrillation without anticoagulation as a major risk factor for embolic mesenteric ischemia
    • Orders serum lactate and CT angiography of the abdomen promptly
    • Initiates resuscitation with IV fluids and broad spectrum antibiotics while arranging urgent surgical or vascular consultation
    • Avoids anchoring on a benign diagnosis and reassesses when the exam changes

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This man has severe pain that is out of proportion to a relatively benign feeling abdomen, and he has atrial fibrillation without anticoagulation, so I am immediately worried about acute mesenteric ischemia from an embolic source. I would start two large bore IVs, give crystalloid, send a lactate and full bloodwork, and get a CT angiogram of the abdomen as my next step since that is the test that will confirm or exclude arterial occlusion. While waiting I would start broad spectrum antibiotics because bowel ischemia carries a high risk of translocation and sepsis, and I would call general surgery and vascular surgery early since time to reperfusion drives bowel viability. If the CT confirms SMA occlusion I would push for emergent angiography or laparotomy rather than watching and waiting. I am deliberately not reassured by a soft abdomen in this population because delayed diagnosis of mesenteric ischemia carries very high mortality.

  21. Station 21: Cirrhotic Man with Hematemesis

    Reading Stem

    A 54 year old man with known alcoholic cirrhosis presents after vomiting a large volume of bright red blood at home. He appears pale and diaphoretic. Vital signs are HR 128, BP 88/54, RR 24, oxygen saturation 96 percent on room air. His wife states he has had two prior admissions for similar bleeding.

    Examiner Scenario

    On exam the patient has scleral icterus, spider angiomata, and a distended abdomen with shifting dullness consistent with ascites. If the candidate asks for repeat vitals after initial fluids, BP improves to 96/60 but remains tachycardic. If the candidate requests bloodwork, reveal hemoglobin 68, INR 2.1, platelets 82. If the candidate calls for blood products, allow transfusion of packed red cells and consider correction of coagulopathy, and improve vitals modestly. If the candidate mentions octreotide and IV antibiotics such as ceftriaxone, confirm these are given and note this is standard for suspected variceal hemorrhage in cirrhosis. If the candidate calls gastroenterology for urgent endoscopy, state that endoscopy is being arranged. If at any point the candidate proposes aggressive crystalloid resuscitation to a normal blood pressure, have the patient rebleed more briskly to teach permissive hypotension and blood product based resuscitation. If airway protection is not addressed in a patient with ongoing hematemesis and altered mentation, have the patient vomit again with oxygen desaturation to prompt consideration of intubation before endoscopy. Correct disposition is ICU admission with urgent gastroenterology endoscopy, not discharge or routine ward admission.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes variceal hemorrhage as the most likely cause given known cirrhosis
    • Resuscitates with blood products rather than large volume crystalloid, avoiding over-resuscitation
    • Starts octreotide and prophylactic antibiotics such as ceftriaxone
    • Considers airway protection given ongoing hematemesis and risk of aspiration
    • Arranges urgent gastroenterology consultation for endoscopy and ICU level disposition

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given his known cirrhosis and large volume hematemesis with hypotension and tachycardia, I am treating this as a variceal upper GI bleed until proven otherwise. My resuscitation strategy is blood product based rather than large crystalloid volumes, aiming for a permissive systolic pressure rather than normalizing it fully, since overshooting can worsen portal pressure and rebleeding. I would start octreotide to reduce splanchnic blood flow and give IV ceftriaxone since antibiotic prophylaxis reduces mortality and rebleeding in cirrhotic variceal bleeds. I am watching his airway closely because ongoing hematemesis with any mental status change raises aspiration risk and may need early intubation before endoscopy. I would correct his coagulopathy and thrombocytopenia as appropriate and call gastroenterology urgently for endoscopy, with disposition to the ICU given his hemodynamic instability.

  22. Station 22: Unconscious Young Adult Found by Roommate

    Reading Stem

    A 24 year old woman is brought in by paramedics after being found unresponsive on her bathroom floor with an empty pill bottle nearby. She is minimally responsive to voice. Vitals are HR 58, BP 100/64, RR 8, oxygen saturation 91 percent, pupils pinpoint bilaterally.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate assesses airway and breathing, note shallow slow respirations and confirm pinpoint pupils, both suggestive of opioid toxicity. If the candidate gives naloxone, describe partial improvement in respiratory rate and level of consciousness within minutes, and note the patient becomes more alert but agitated. If the candidate asks about the pill bottle, reveal it was labeled acetaminophen with codeine and appears mostly empty, and the roommate estimates the patient may have taken it two hours ago. If the candidate orders an acetaminophen level and time since ingestion is confirmed, provide a level that plots above the Rumack-Matthew nomogram treatment line, prompting the candidate to start N-acetylcysteine. If the candidate does not ask about co-ingestants or check an acetaminophen level, at minute 9 introduce rising liver enzymes on repeat bloodwork to signal missed hepatotoxic ingestion. If the candidate gives a large single dose of naloxone rather than titrating, have the patient develop acute opioid withdrawal with agitation and vomiting, testing understanding of titrated dosing. Expect consideration of ECG for QT prolongation given combination products, and discussion of psychiatric assessment and admission for intentional overdose once medically stable.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes the opioid toxidrome from pinpoint pupils, bradypnea, and depressed consciousness
    • Titrates naloxone to effect rather than giving a large bolus that precipitates withdrawal
    • Actively investigates co-ingestants and checks an acetaminophen level given the combination product
    • Applies the Rumack-Matthew nomogram appropriately and starts N-acetylcysteine when indicated
    • Arranges psychiatric assessment and safe disposition once medically stabilized

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Her pinpoint pupils, slow shallow breathing, and depressed level of consciousness are classic for an opioid toxidrome, so my first move is supporting her airway and breathing while giving small titrated doses of naloxone rather than a large bolus, since I want to reverse respiratory depression without precipitating severe withdrawal. Once she is more awake I need to find out exactly what she took, and the fact that the bottle was acetaminophen with codeine means I have to actively look for acetaminophen toxicity as well as opioid toxicity. I would send an acetaminophen level timed appropriately and plot it on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram, and if it falls above the treatment line I would start N-acetylcysteine without delay since liver injury is time sensitive. I am also getting an ECG given combination products can prolong the QT interval. Once she is medically stabilized I would arrange a psychiatric assessment given this appears to be an intentional overdose, and ensure she is not discharged until both medical and safety concerns are addressed.

  23. Station 23: Collapsed Marathon Runner in Summer Heat

    Reading Stem

    A 29 year old man collapses near the finish line of a marathon on a hot humid day. He is confused and combative when paramedics arrive. On arrival his temperature is 41.2C rectally, HR 150, BP 92/58, and his skin is hot and dry.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate asks for a rapid temperature check, confirm rectal temperature 41.2C and emphasize this must be obtained rectally rather than tympanic for accuracy. If the candidate initiates aggressive external cooling such as ice water immersion or evaporative cooling with fans and tepid water, describe temperature trending down over several minutes with reassessment. If the candidate delays cooling to first obtain imaging or extensive labs, keep the temperature elevated and have the patient develop a brief seizure to underscore the danger of delayed cooling in heat stroke. If the candidate asks about labs, reveal potassium 6.2, creatinine rising, CK markedly elevated, and mild coagulopathy consistent with rhabdomyolysis and early multiorgan stress. If the candidate gives aggressive IV fluids and monitors urine output, allow improvement. If the candidate reaches for antipyretics such as acetaminophen, correct this by having the patient's temperature remain unchanged since antipyretics do not work on hyperthermia from heat stroke. Reward candidates who recognize this as exertional heat stroke, prioritize rapid cooling to a target below 39C before extensive workup, treat hyperkalemia if present, and recognize rhabdomyolysis requiring aggressive fluid resuscitation. Disposition is ICU admission for ongoing monitoring of organ function.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Obtains a core rectal temperature and recognizes exertional heat stroke
    • Prioritizes rapid active cooling such as ice water immersion or evaporative cooling over further workup
    • Recognizes that antipyretics are ineffective in heat stroke and does not rely on them
    • Identifies and treats complications including hyperkalemia and rhabdomyolysis
    • Arranges ICU admission for ongoing monitoring of organ dysfunction

    Model Verbalized Answer

    A collapsed marathon runner with altered mental status and a core temperature over 41 degrees on a hot day is exertional heat stroke until proven otherwise, and this is a true emergency where cooling cannot wait. My immediate priority is rapid active cooling, ideally ice water immersion or aggressive evaporative cooling with fans and tepid water, and I want a rectal temperature to guide me since peripheral measurements are unreliable here. I am not going to reach for antipyretics because this is not a hypothalamic set point problem like infectious fever, it is a failure of thermoregulation, so acetaminophen will not help and could worsen his coagulopathy. While cooling is underway I would send bloodwork looking for hyperkalemia, rising creatinine, and an elevated CK from rhabdomyolysis, and start aggressive IV fluids to protect his kidneys and support urine output. I would treat any dangerous hyperkalemia immediately and admit him to the ICU given the risk of multiorgan dysfunction.

  24. Station 24: Child with Sudden Swelling After Eating Peanuts

    Reading Stem

    A 6 year old girl develops facial swelling, hives, and repetitive coughing within 10 minutes of eating a cookie containing peanuts at a birthday party. Her mother reports she has never had a known reaction before. On arrival she has audible stridor and looks anxious. Vitals are HR 140, BP 88/50, RR 32, oxygen saturation 93 percent.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate asks about airway, describe inspiratory stridor and mild lip and tongue swelling. If the candidate gives intramuscular epinephrine promptly into the lateral thigh, describe improvement in stridor and blood pressure within a few minutes, and specify weight based dosing when asked. If the candidate delays epinephrine to instead give antihistamines or steroids first, have the child's stridor worsen and oxygen saturation drop to prompt recognition that epinephrine is first line, not antihistamines. If the candidate asks about additional treatment, allow addition of oral or IV corticosteroids and an H1 blocker as adjuncts only after epinephrine. If the candidate considers repeat epinephrine dosing, describe a partial response requiring a second IM dose about 8 minutes later, illustrating that up to a third of anaphylaxis cases need repeat dosing. If the candidate asks about observation period, expect discussion of a biphasic reaction risk requiring observation for several hours before discharge. Correct management includes IM epinephrine as first line, oxygen, IV fluids for hypotension, repeat dosing as needed, adjunct antihistamine and steroid, and an epinephrine autoinjector prescription with allergy follow up before discharge, with observation for biphasic reaction.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes anaphylaxis from the combination of skin, respiratory, and cardiovascular findings after a known trigger exposure
    • Gives intramuscular epinephrine into the lateral thigh as first line treatment without delay
    • Uses antihistamines and corticosteroids only as adjuncts, never as a substitute for epinephrine
    • Repeats epinephrine dosing if response is incomplete and monitors closely
    • Observes for biphasic reaction and arranges an epinephrine autoinjector and allergy follow up before discharge

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This is anaphylaxis given hives, facial and airway swelling, stridor, and hypotension after a known allergen exposure, so my very first drug is intramuscular epinephrine into the lateral thigh, dosed to her weight, and I am giving it immediately rather than waiting to see if antihistamines help first. I want high flow oxygen on her, IV access for fluids given her hypotension, and continuous monitoring while I reassess her airway. If her stridor and blood pressure do not fully improve within 5 to 10 minutes I would repeat the IM epinephrine dose since a meaningful proportion of anaphylaxis cases need more than one dose. Once she is stabilized I would add an antihistamine and corticosteroid as adjuncts, understanding they do not replace epinephrine and act more slowly. Before discharge I would keep her for an observation period because of the risk of a biphasic reaction, and send her home with an epinephrine autoinjector, a clear action plan, and allergy follow up.

  25. Station 25: Adult with Anaphylaxis After Wasp Sting and Biphasic Reaction

    Reading Stem

    A 45 year old man is stung by a wasp while gardening and develops widespread urticaria, throat tightness, and dizziness within 15 minutes. Paramedics gave one dose of IM epinephrine en route. On arrival he is more comfortable but still has scattered hives. Vitals are HR 102, BP 108/70, RR 18, oxygen saturation 98 percent.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate reassesses the airway and skin, confirm resolving hives and no current stridor, and note the patient feels much better after the prehospital epinephrine. If the candidate considers this resolved and prepares immediate discharge without an observation period, proceed to minute 6 where the patient suddenly develops recurrent throat tightness, wheeze, and hypotension, representing a biphasic anaphylactic reaction, testing whether the candidate recognizes this risk and reacts appropriately with repeat epinephrine. If the candidate had already planned an observation period of several hours, acknowledge this is standard practice and the recurrence is caught promptly by monitoring. Once the second reaction is treated with repeat IM epinephrine and supportive care, allow full resolution. If the candidate considers an epinephrine infusion for refractory symptoms, that is appropriate here since he required repeat dosing. Expect discussion of why beta blocker or ACE inhibitor use, if present, could make anaphylaxis harder to treat, and expect the candidate to ask about his medication history. Correct final management includes extended observation of at least several hours given the biphasic reaction, prescription of two epinephrine autoinjectors, referral to allergy and immunology, and clear return precautions.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Reassesses airway, breathing, and circulation after prehospital epinephrine rather than assuming resolution
    • Recognizes the risk of biphasic anaphylaxis and plans an adequate observation period
    • Responds immediately to the biphasic recurrence with repeat epinephrine and reassessment
    • Asks about medications such as beta blockers that can blunt response to epinephrine
    • Arranges extended observation, autoinjector prescription, and allergy follow up before discharge

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Even though he looks better after the prehospital epinephrine, I know that anaphylaxis can recur hours later as a biphasic reaction, so I am planning an extended observation period rather than discharging him early. I would also ask specifically about beta blocker or ACE inhibitor use since those medications can blunt the response to epinephrine and change my approach if he deteriorates again. If he develops recurrent throat tightness and hypotension during observation, I would treat it exactly as I would the initial episode, with prompt repeat intramuscular epinephrine and reassessment of his airway and circulation, and I would consider an epinephrine infusion if he needs repeated doses. Before discharge I want him observed long enough that I am confident the reaction has truly settled. Finally I would send him home with two epinephrine autoinjectors, clear instructions on when to use them, and a referral to allergy and immunology for further evaluation.

  26. Station 26: Woman with Vaginal Bleeding and Positive Pregnancy Test

    Reading Stem

    A 31 year old woman presents with vaginal spotting and right lower quadrant pain for one day. Her last menstrual period was 7 weeks ago and a urine pregnancy test is positive. She has no known prior ultrasound this pregnancy. Vitals are HR 96, BP 110/72, RR 16.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate asks about pain character, describe constant right sided pain with mild tenderness on exam but no peritonism yet. If the candidate orders a quantitative beta hCG and pelvic ultrasound, reveal a beta hCG of 2200 and an ultrasound showing no intrauterine pregnancy with a right adnexal mass and a small amount of free fluid, consistent with a likely ectopic pregnancy. If the candidate recognizes this as a pregnancy of unknown location or probable ectopic and consults gynecology urgently, allow this to proceed appropriately. If the candidate reassures the patient that this is likely a normal early pregnancy without further workup, at minute 8 have the patient develop worsening pain, dizziness, and a drop in blood pressure to simulate tubal rupture, forcing recognition of a ruptured ectopic and hemorrhagic shock. If the candidate then resuscitates with fluids, sends a type and crossmatch, and arranges emergent gynecology surgical intervention, allow stabilization. Expect discussion of Rh status and consideration of Rh immunoglobulin if the patient is Rh negative. Correct management is urgent gynecology consultation for suspected ectopic pregnancy, serial monitoring if stable and beta hCG is low with no clear rupture, or emergent surgery if there are signs of rupture or hemodynamic instability.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes ectopic pregnancy as a must not miss diagnosis in early pregnancy bleeding with pain
    • Orders quantitative beta hCG and pelvic ultrasound to localize the pregnancy
    • Consults gynecology urgently for a probable ectopic pregnancy rather than reassuring without workup
    • Recognizes signs of rupture and hemorrhagic shock and resuscitates accordingly with type and crossmatch
    • Considers Rh status and the need for Rh immunoglobulin in an Rh negative patient

    Model Verbalized Answer

    A positive pregnancy test with vaginal bleeding and unilateral pelvic pain at 7 weeks makes ectopic pregnancy my leading concern until it is excluded, so I want a quantitative beta hCG and a pelvic ultrasound right away to see whether there is an intrauterine pregnancy. If the ultrasound shows no intrauterine pregnancy with an adnexal mass and free fluid, I am treating this as a probable ectopic pregnancy and calling gynecology urgently rather than reassuring her that this is likely normal. I am watching closely for any sign of tubal rupture such as worsening pain, dizziness, or hypotension, because that would mean she needs immediate resuscitation with IV fluids, a type and crossmatch, and emergent surgery. I would also check her Rh status and give Rh immunoglobulin if she is Rh negative, since that matters for future pregnancies regardless of this pregnancy's outcome. Until gynecology has definitively assessed her I am not comfortable discharging her home.

  27. Station 27: Pregnant Woman with Severe Headache at 34 Weeks

    Reading Stem

    A 27 year old woman at 34 weeks gestation presents with a severe headache, visual spots, and epigastric discomfort for several hours. She has had no prenatal complications noted so far. Vitals are BP 168/108, HR 90, RR 18, with 2+ pitting edema in her legs.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate asks for a repeat blood pressure, confirm sustained severe hypertension above 160/110 on a second reading. If the candidate orders urinalysis and bloodwork, reveal significant proteinuria, elevated liver enzymes, and a platelet count of 95, consistent with severe pre-eclampsia with features suggestive of evolving HELLP syndrome. If the candidate starts IV magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis and an antihypertensive such as labetalol or hydralazine to control severe range blood pressure, allow blood pressure to improve into a safer range. If the candidate delays magnesium and antihypertensive treatment to pursue extensive additional workup first, have the patient develop a generalized tonic clonic seizure at minute 9 to simulate eclampsia, requiring the candidate to manage the airway, give magnesium, and stabilize before urgent delivery planning. If the candidate calls obstetrics urgently and discusses the need for delivery given severe features, this is correct regardless of gestational age at this severity. Reward candidates who recognize severe range blood pressure with symptoms as an emergency requiring magnesium sulfate regardless of whether an actual seizure has occurred, who treat blood pressure promptly, and who involve obstetrics immediately for consideration of delivery.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes severe pre-eclampsia from severe range blood pressure with headache, visual changes, and epigastric pain
    • Starts magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis without waiting for an actual seizure
    • Treats severe range hypertension promptly with an appropriate antihypertensive such as labetalol or hydralazine
    • Manages eclamptic seizure with airway protection and magnesium if it occurs
    • Involves obstetrics urgently given severe features and the likely need for delivery

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Severe range blood pressure with headache, visual changes, and epigastric pain at 34 weeks tells me this is severe pre-eclampsia until proven otherwise, and I need to treat this as an emergency rather than wait for a seizure to happen. I would start magnesium sulfate immediately for seizure prophylaxis, since that is indicated based on severity of features alone, and give an antihypertensive like labetalol or hydralazine to bring her blood pressure out of the severe range safely. I am sending bloodwork and urine looking for proteinuria, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets because this pattern raises concern for HELLP syndrome, which changes urgency. If she seizes despite my efforts I would protect her airway, ensure she is receiving magnesium, and treat this as eclampsia. I am calling obstetrics urgently because severe pre-eclampsia at this gestational age with these features usually means delivery needs to be seriously considered very soon.

  28. Station 28: Febrile Infant Under 90 Days Old

    Reading Stem

    A 6 week old boy is brought in by his parents for fever measured at home to 38.4C rectally. He has been feeding slightly less than usual but has no cough, rash, or vomiting. He appears well in his mother's arms. Vitals in the department confirm temperature 38.3C, HR 160, RR 40.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate performs a full septic workup discussion, describe a well appearing infant on exam with no focal source found. If the candidate orders blood cultures, urine obtained by catheterization, inflammatory markers, and considers a validated risk-stratification approach such as Step by Step, PECARN, or Rochester criteria appropriate for this 29 to 90 day age band, confirm this is the correct approach, since he is not a neonate under 28 days who would require an automatic full workup and admission regardless of any test result. Allow discussion of the workup results and reveal a positive urinalysis suggestive of a urinary tract infection with clean catch not otherwise contaminated. If the candidate proposes discharge home without antibiotics or without any workup due to the infant looking well, interject that even well-appearing infants in this age band require a structured risk-stratification workup before any disposition decision, and have blood culture eventually grow a pathogen overnight to underscore the risk of skipping the workup entirely. Correct management includes recognizing this age group is risk-stratified using validated criteria rather than either a blanket neonatal-style admission or reassurance from appearance alone, and that this particular infant needs empiric antibiotics and admission because his workup found a urinary tract infection, not simply because of his age.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes that infants 29 to 90 days old are risk-stratified using validated criteria (such as Rochester or Step by Step) rather than managed with the same blanket regardless-of-appearance rule as neonates under 28 days
    • Obtains blood culture, catheterized urine studies, and considers lumbar puncture for CSF
    • Applies validated risk stratification criteria appropriately rather than relying on gestalt alone
    • Starts empiric antibiotics after cultures are obtained rather than deferring treatment
    • Admits the infant for observation rather than discharging home

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Even though this baby looks well, at 6 weeks old he falls in the 29 to 90 day age band where I cannot rely on appearance alone to exclude serious bacterial infection, so I am pursuing a structured workup including blood cultures, a catheterized urine sample, inflammatory markers, and consideration of a lumbar puncture for CSF studies. I would use a validated approach such as Step by Step or PECARN criteria to help risk stratify him, since he is not a neonate under 28 days who would need an automatic full workup and admission regardless of any test result. Once his urinalysis suggests a urinary tract infection, that finding on its own supports starting empiric antibiotics after cultures are obtained and admitting him for observation, rather than the age cutoff alone driving that decision. If his full workup had come back reassuring and he met low risk criteria, a more selective approach could reasonably be considered for an infant his age. I am planning to admit him now because his urinalysis result places him outside low risk, and I would keep him admitted pending full culture results and clinical course.

  29. Station 29: Woman with Injuries Inconsistent with Stated Mechanism

    Reading Stem

    A 33 year old woman presents with facial bruising and a wrist injury, stating she fell down the stairs. Her partner is present and answers most questions for her. On exam she has bruising in different stages of healing and appears anxious and avoids eye contact.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate asks to speak with the patient alone, describe the partner initially resisting but eventually agreeing to step out after the candidate explains this is routine practice for all patients. If the candidate validates her disclosure without judgment and assesses immediate safety including whether children are in the home and whether the partner has access to weapons, allow her to share that there are two children at home and the partner owns a firearm, both of which raise the risk level significantly. If the candidate fails to separate her from the partner and asks about the injury mechanism in his presence, she minimizes and denies any concern, and the encounter ends without disclosure or a safety plan, representing a missed opportunity that should be identified afterward. If disclosure occurs, expect the candidate to discuss documentation of injuries, referral to social work or a domestic violence advocate, and collaborative safety planning that respects her autonomy rather than mandating an immediate action she is not ready for, while discussing local reporting obligations regarding children in the home.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes injury patterns and partner behavior as red flags for intimate partner violence
    • Separates the patient from her partner to allow private, confidential disclosure
