    • Involves social work or a domestic violence advocate and documents findings thoroughly
    • Develops a collaborative safety plan that respects patient autonomy while addressing any child safety reporting obligations

    Model Verbalized Answer

    The pattern of injuries at different stages of healing along with her partner speaking for her and her guarded body language raises my concern for intimate partner violence, so my first step is finding a reason to speak with her privately, separate from her partner. Once we are alone I would ask open, nonjudgmental questions about her safety at home rather than leading questions, and if she discloses abuse I would validate that disclosure and let her know I believe her and that help is available. I need to assess immediate risk, including whether children are in the home and whether her partner has access to weapons, since both significantly change the urgency of safety planning. I would document her injuries carefully and objectively and involve social work or a domestic violence advocate to help build a safety plan that she is comfortable with, rather than forcing an action she is not ready to take. Given that children are in the home, I would also consider my reporting obligations regarding child safety while continuing to support her autonomy in her own decisions.

  30. Station 30: Toddler with Severe Dehydration from Gastroenteritis

    Reading Stem

    A 14 month old girl presents with 3 days of vomiting and watery diarrhea. Her mother reports she has had minimal urine output today and seems very sleepy. On exam she has sunken eyes, dry mucous membranes, and delayed capillary refill. Vitals are HR 170, BP 78/44, RR 34.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate assesses for shock, confirm delayed capillary refill of 4 seconds, mottled skin, and lethargy consistent with decompensated hypovolemic shock. If the candidate gives a rapid isotonic fluid bolus, describe modest improvement in heart rate and mental status, and expect the candidate to reassess after each bolus rather than giving one bolus and moving on. If repeat boluses are needed, allow up to 2 to 3 boluses with reassessment between each, watching for signs of fluid overload such as new crackles or hepatomegaly. If the candidate checks a point of care glucose, reveal a low value consistent with hypoglycemia from poor intake, requiring dextrose correction. If the candidate does not reassess after the first bolus and instead moves on to other tasks, have the child's mental status remain poor and heart rate stay elevated to prompt reassessment. If the candidate considers electrolyte and venous blood gas testing, reveal a metabolic acidosis and mild hyponatremia consistent with the degree of dehydration. Correct management includes recognizing decompensated shock, rapid isotonic fluid boluses with frequent reassessment, correction of hypoglycemia, and admission for ongoing rehydration and monitoring, with consideration of a pediatric intensive care unit if she does not stabilize after initial boluses.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes decompensated hypovolemic shock from history and exam findings
    • Gives rapid isotonic fluid boluses with reassessment after each one rather than a single bolus alone
    • Checks and corrects point of care glucose given risk of hypoglycemia in a poorly feeding, vomiting child
    • Monitors for signs of fluid overload while resuscitating aggressively
    • Arranges appropriate admission, including pediatric intensive care if she remains unstable after initial resuscitation

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This child has decompensated hypovolemic shock from severe gastroenteritis, shown by her tachycardia, low blood pressure, delayed capillary refill, and lethargy, so my priority is rapid isotonic fluid resuscitation rather than maintenance fluids. I would give a fluid bolus and reassess her heart rate, capillary refill, and mental status right after, repeating boluses as needed while watching for signs of fluid overload like new crackles or an enlarging liver. Given her poor intake and vomiting I want a point of care glucose checked right away, and if she is hypoglycemic I would correct that promptly since it can worsen her mental status independently. I would also send a venous blood gas and electrolytes to understand her acid base and sodium status, which helps guide ongoing fluid management. Given how sick she is, I am planning to admit her, and if she does not respond adequately to initial boluses I would escalate to pediatric intensive care rather than continuing to manage her on a general ward.

  31. Station 31: Young Adult with Fever, Rash, and Neck Stiffness

    Reading Stem

    A 19 year old university student presents with fever, severe headache, and neck stiffness for 6 hours. His roommate noticed a spreading purpuric rash on his legs this morning. He looks unwell and is reluctant to move his neck. Vitals are HR 132, BP 90/58, RR 26, temp 39.4C.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate examines the skin, confirm a rapidly spreading nonblanching purpuric rash on the lower extremities and trunk. If the candidate recognizes this as likely meningococcemia and starts empiric antibiotics such as ceftriaxone immediately without waiting for lumbar puncture or imaging, allow the patient to begin stabilizing over the following minutes with fluid resuscitation. If the candidate delays antibiotics to first obtain a CT head and lumbar puncture, have the patient's blood pressure drop further and rash spread more extensively to simulate the danger of delaying antibiotics in suspected meningococcemia, since empiric treatment should never wait for confirmatory testing in this presentation. If the candidate considers droplet or airborne precautions and notifies public health given the diagnosis, this is correct and should be reinforced as necessary given the contagious nature and public health reporting obligations for meningococcal disease. If the candidate asks about contacts, reveal his roommate and girlfriend as close contacts who would need prophylactic antibiotics. If the candidate performs blood cultures before antibiotics without delaying treatment meaningfully, that is appropriate. Expect discussion of aggressive fluid resuscitation for septic shock, ICU level care, and coordination with public health for contact prophylaxis and case reporting.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes the combination of fever, meningismus, and purpuric rash as likely meningococcemia
    • Starts empiric antibiotics immediately without delaying for lumbar puncture or imaging
    • Resuscitates aggressively for septic shock while obtaining blood cultures without meaningfully delaying antibiotics
    • Implements appropriate droplet precautions and recognizes the public health reporting obligation
    • Identifies close contacts requiring prophylactic antibiotics

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Fever, neck stiffness, and a rapidly spreading purpuric rash in a young adult is meningococcemia until proven otherwise, and this is a situation where I am giving empiric antibiotics like ceftriaxone immediately rather than waiting for a lumbar puncture or CT head, because any delay in antibiotics increases mortality significantly. I would draw blood cultures quickly if it does not meaningfully delay treatment, but I am not holding antibiotics for that. Given his hypotension and tachycardia I would resuscitate him aggressively as I would any septic shock patient, with fluids and likely escalation to vasopressors and ICU care if he does not respond. I would put droplet precautions in place immediately given the risk of transmission, and I know this is a reportable disease so I would notify public health promptly. I would also identify close contacts like his roommate and girlfriend so they can receive prophylactic antibiotics as soon as possible.

  32. Station 32: Woman with Pleuritic Chest Pain After Long Flight

    Reading Stem

    A 46 year old woman presents with sudden pleuritic chest pain and shortness of breath that began a few hours after a 12 hour international flight. She has mild right calf swelling and tenderness. Vitals are HR 118, BP 122/78, RR 24, oxygen saturation 94 percent on room air.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate calculates a pretest probability score such as the Wells criteria, reveal findings supporting a moderate to high pretest probability given recent immobilization, tachycardia, and unilateral leg swelling. If the candidate orders a CT pulmonary angiogram, reveal findings of a segmental pulmonary embolism without evidence of right heart strain on the study, and normal troponin, consistent with a low-risk PE given the absence of any RV dysfunction or biomarker elevation despite normal vitals. If the candidate starts anticoagulation promptly with a direct oral anticoagulant or heparin, allow stabilization. If the candidate considers thrombolysis at this stage, redirect by revealing the patient remains hemodynamically stable with no right ventricular strain, meaning thrombolysis is not indicated and anticoagulation alone is appropriate, testing whether the candidate over-treats a low-risk, hemodynamically stable PE. If the candidate does not consider PE at all and instead treats for musculoskeletal pain, have the patient develop worsening hypoxia and tachycardia at minute 9 to force recognition. Expect discussion of risk stratification tools such as the PESI score to help decide on outpatient versus inpatient management, and consideration of provoked versus unprovoked PE given the recent flight as a transient risk factor influencing duration of anticoagulation.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Uses a validated pretest probability tool such as Wells criteria to guide the diagnostic pathway
    • Confirms the diagnosis with CT pulmonary angiogram and assesses for right heart strain and troponin elevation
    • Correctly classifies a PE with no RV strain and a normal troponin as low-risk, distinguishing it from submassive (intermediate-risk) PE which requires RV dysfunction or biomarker elevation
    • Starts appropriate anticoagulation without unnecessary escalation to thrombolysis in a stable patient
    • Uses a risk stratification tool such as PESI to help decide on disposition and considers duration of anticoagulation given the provoked nature of this event

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Given her recent long flight, unilateral leg swelling, tachycardia, and pleuritic chest pain, I have a moderate to high pretest probability for pulmonary embolism using something like the Wells criteria, so I am proceeding to a CT pulmonary angiogram to confirm the diagnosis. If that confirms a segmental PE without right heart strain and her troponin is normal, I am classifying her as low-risk PE, since she has neither RV dysfunction on imaging nor a positive biomarker, and I am not going to reach for thrombolysis, since that carries bleeding risk that is not justified here. My management is anticoagulation, either with a direct oral anticoagulant or heparin depending on other factors, started promptly once the diagnosis is confirmed. I would use a tool like the PESI score to help decide whether she can be managed as an outpatient or needs admission, and given her overall stability and low-risk classification she may be a candidate for outpatient management with close follow up. I would also note this appears to be a provoked event related to prolonged immobility, which matters for how long she ultimately needs to stay on anticoagulation.

  33. Station 33: Facial Trauma with Impending Airway Compromise

    Reading Stem

    A 34 year old man arrives after a motorcycle collision with significant facial trauma including a fractured mandible and bleeding into the oropharynx. He is agitated, has gurgling respirations, and is difficult to understand. Vitals are HR 122, BP 128/82, RR 28, oxygen saturation 90 percent on high flow oxygen.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate performs suction of the airway, describe ongoing brisk oral bleeding that quickly re-accumulates, making visualization difficult. If the candidate identifies this as a difficult airway due to facial trauma, bleeding, and distorted anatomy, and calls for backup including anesthesia or the most experienced airway provider available along with preparation of a surgical airway kit, allow this preparation to proceed appropriately. If the candidate attempts a standard rapid sequence intubation without a clear backup plan or surgical airway readiness, describe a failed first attempt due to blood obscuring the view, forcing the candidate to move to a rescue strategy, testing whether they had a plan in place before starting. If the candidate uses video laryngoscopy with continuous suction available and has a bougie ready, allow successful intubation on either the first or second attempt. If oxygen saturation continues to fall during attempts, expect the candidate to call for a surgical airway such as cricothyrotomy given a cannot intubate cannot oxygenate scenario. Expect discussion of preoxygenation, positioning, and having a structured backup plan clearly verbalized before the first attempt, consistent with a difficult airway algorithm approach.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes this as a predicted difficult airway due to facial trauma, bleeding, and distorted anatomy
    • Calls for additional skilled airway help and prepares a surgical airway kit before attempting intubation
    • Verbalizes a structured backup plan before the first intubation attempt rather than improvising after failure
    • Uses appropriate airway adjuncts such as video laryngoscopy, continuous suction, and a bougie
    • Escalates to a surgical airway if a cannot intubate cannot oxygenate situation develops

    Model Verbalized Answer

    This is a predicted difficult airway given his facial trauma, active oral bleeding, and distorted anatomy, so before I do anything I want to call for the most experienced airway provider available and have a surgical airway kit opened and ready at the bedside. I would preoxygenate as best I can, position him appropriately, and use continuous suction along with video laryngoscopy and a bougie ready to go, since blood is likely to obscure my view. I am verbalizing my backup plan out loud before I ever attempt intubation, because if my first attempt fails I do not want to be improvising in the moment. If I cannot intubate and cannot adequately oxygenate him, I am moving to a surgical airway without hesitation rather than making repeated attempts at oral intubation. Throughout this I am also thinking about who else needs to be in the room, including anesthesia or ENT, given how high risk this airway is.

  34. Station 34: Rapidly Progressive Angioedema on an ACE Inhibitor

    Reading Stem

    A 58 year old man on lisinopril presents with progressive lip and tongue swelling that started 2 hours ago. His voice now sounds muffled and he is drooling. He denies hives or itching. Vitals are HR 104, BP 138/86, RR 22, oxygen saturation 96 percent.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate examines the oropharynx, describe significant tongue and soft palate edema with a muffled voice and increasing work of breathing, but no urticaria, supporting ACE inhibitor induced angioedema rather than allergic anaphylaxis. If the candidate gives epinephrine, antihistamines, and steroids reflexively as if this were anaphylaxis, allow these to be given but reveal minimal to no improvement in the swelling over the following minutes, since bradykinin mediated angioedema does not reliably respond to these treatments, and the true priority is airway management. If the candidate recognizes progressive airway compromise and prepares for early, awake intubation given predicted rapid worsening, or calls for anesthesia or ENT for awake fiberoptic intubation while the airway is still manageable, reward this as the correct approach. If the candidate waits to intubate until the airway is nearly obstructed, describe a much more difficult, high risk airway attempt with significant desaturation, illustrating why early intervention before complete obstruction is critical in progressive angioedema. If icatibant or fresh frozen plasma is mentioned as a bradykinin pathway specific treatment where available, acknowledge this as an appropriate adjunct while emphasizing airway security remains the priority. Correct disposition is ICU admission for close airway monitoring regardless of whether intubation occurs.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Distinguishes ACE inhibitor induced angioedema from allergic anaphylaxis based on absence of urticaria and lack of response to epinephrine
    • Recognizes progressive airway swelling as an indication for early, controlled airway management rather than waiting
    • Calls for awake or fiberoptic intubation with experienced airway support while the airway is still manageable
    • Considers bradykinin pathway specific treatments such as icatibant or FFP where available as adjuncts, not substitutes for airway security
    • Arranges ICU admission for close airway monitoring regardless of whether intubation was performed

    Model Verbalized Answer

    The absence of hives or itching along with his ACE inhibitor use makes me think this is bradykinin mediated angioedema rather than classic allergic anaphylaxis, which matters because epinephrine, antihistamines, and steroids are unlikely to reliably reverse this swelling. Given that his tongue and soft palate swelling are progressing and his voice is already muffled, I am not going to wait until he is in extremis to secure his airway, I want to call for an experienced airway provider now for an early, controlled, likely awake or fiberoptic intubation while he can still cooperate and while anatomy is still manageable. If a bradykinin specific treatment like icatibant or fresh frozen plasma is available I would consider it as an adjunct, but I am not relying on it to avoid needing to protect his airway. I would still give epinephrine, antihistamines, and steroids as reasonable adjuncts in case there is any allergic component, but my main focus is airway security. Whether or not he ends up intubated, I am admitting him to an ICU setting for close airway monitoring given how quickly this can progress.

  35. Station 35: Procedural Sedation for Shoulder Dislocation Reduction

    Reading Stem

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate performs a pre-sedation assessment including airway examination, fasting status, and ASA classification, note he is ASA I but has a recent full meal, and expect discussion of how this affects risk versus benefit rather than being an absolute contraindication to emergency procedural sedation. If the candidate discusses monitoring requirements including continuous pulse oximetry, capnography if available, blood pressure, and dedicated personnel for airway management, confirm these are set up appropriately. If the candidate chooses a sedation agent such as ketamine, propofol, or a ketamine-propofol combination and dosing is discussed reasonably, allow the procedure to proceed with adequate sedation achieved. If the candidate fails to prepare airway equipment and reversal or rescue plans before sedating, interject that during the procedure the patient develops transient oxygen desaturation, testing whether the candidate has suction, bag valve mask, and airway equipment ready and responds appropriately with airway repositioning and support. If the candidate successfully reduces the shoulder and monitors recovery until the patient returns to baseline mental status before discharge, this is correct.     Examiner Checklist

    • Performs a proper pre-sedation assessment including airway exam, fasting status, and risk classification

    • Has airway rescue equipment ready and responds promptly to any desaturation during the procedure
    • Monitors recovery to baseline before discharge and documents a post-reduction neurovascular exam

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Before I sedate him I want a full pre-sedation assessment, including his airway exam, that he ate about an hour ago, and confirming he is otherwise healthy, which I would weigh as increased aspiration risk that changes my approach rather than an absolute contraindication in this urgent situation. I would set up continuous pulse oximetry, capnography if it is available, blood pressure monitoring, and make sure I have a dedicated person focused only on airway and sedation monitoring separate from the person doing the reduction. For the agent I would consider ketamine given its relative preservation of airway reflexes, or a ketamine-propofol combination, dosed carefully and titrated to effect. Before I give anything I want suction, bag valve mask, and airway equipment at the bedside in case he desaturates or needs airway support during the procedure. After the reduction I would monitor him until he is back to his baseline mental status, do a careful neurovascular exam of the limb, and apply a sling before considering him safe for discharge.

  36. Station 36: Older Woman with Painful Red Eye and Blurred Vision

    Reading Stem

    A 68 year old woman presents with a painful red right eye and blurred vision that started suddenly this evening after being in a dimly lit movie theater. She reports seeing halos around lights and has nausea and a headache. Her right eye looks injected with a hazy cornea.

    If the candidate checks visual acuity, describe significantly reduced acuity in the affected eye. If the candidate palpates the globe or measures intraocular pressure, reveal a firm globe on palpation or a measured pressure markedly elevated, consistent with acute angle closure glaucoma. If the candidate examines the pupil, describe a mid dilated, poorly reactive pupil in the affected eye. If the candidate starts topical treatment such as a topical beta blocker, alpha agonist, and considers acetazolamide to lower intraocular pressure, along with urgent ophthalmology consultation, allow improvement in symptoms and pressure over time. If the candidate misdiagnoses this as simple conjunctivitis and prescribes topical antibiotics for discharge home, have the patient return in the scenario narrative with worsening vision loss and pain to illustrate the danger of a missed diagnosis and permanent vision loss risk. If the candidate discusses avoiding pupil dilating drops given the diagnosis, this is correct since dilation would worsen angle closure. Correct management is recognizing this as an ophthalmologic emergency, starting pressure lowering treatment while awaiting ophthalmology, and arranging emergent ophthalmology evaluation for definitive treatment such as laser iridotomy.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Recognizes acute angle closure glaucoma from the combination of painful red eye, halos, nausea, and a hazy cornea

    Model Verbalized Answer

    A painful red eye with a hazy cornea, halos around lights, nausea, and a mid dilated poorly reactive pupil in an older patient strongly suggests acute angle closure glaucoma, which I consider a true ophthalmologic emergency because delayed treatment risks permanent vision loss. I would check her visual acuity and try to assess intraocular pressure or at least globe firmness on palpation to support the diagnosis. I am deliberately avoiding any pupil dilating drops since that would worsen the angle closure. My initial treatment would include topical agents such as a beta blocker and alpha agonist along with acetazolamide to start lowering the intraocular pressure while I arrange emergent ophthalmology consultation, since she will likely need definitive treatment such as laser iridotomy. I would not treat this as simple conjunctivitis and send her home with antibiotic drops, because that would delay definitive care and put her vision at real risk.

  37. Station 37: EMS Handover for a Suspected STEMI with Deteriorating Vitals

    Reading Stem

    Paramedics call ahead with a patient experiencing crushing chest pain for 45 minutes with a prehospital ECG showing ST elevation in the anterior leads. On arrival, the paramedic gives a verbal handover while the patient is being transferred to the monitor. The patient appears pale and diaphoretic.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate actively listens to the full prehospital handover including vitals trend, treatments given such as aspirin and nitroglycerin, and response to those treatments, allow the handover to convey that blood pressure has been trending down over the last several minutes. If the candidate repeats the ECG immediately on arrival rather than relying solely on the prehospital tracing, confirm ongoing anterior ST elevation and now new hypotension. If the candidate recognizes cardiogenic shock physiology developing and avoids further nitroglycerin given the hypotension, this is correct since nitrates can worsen hypotension in a hypotensive STEMI. If the candidate activates the cardiac catheterization lab immediately based on the prehospital ECG without waiting for a repeat ECG in the department, that is also an acceptable and often preferred practice to save time to reperfusion, and should be recognized as appropriate. If the candidate delays activation to repeat unnecessary tests, interject that time to reperfusion is being lost and ask the candidate to reconsider. Expect discussion of starting inotropic or vasopressor support cautiously if hypotension worsens, clear closed loop communication with the cath lab team, and continued reassessment during the handover process rather than treating handover as a passive event.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Actively engages with the EMS handover, capturing vitals trends and treatments already given rather than treating it passively
    • Confirms the STEMI with a repeat ECG in the department or activates the cath lab promptly based on the prehospital ECG
    • Recognizes evolving cardiogenic shock and avoids nitroglycerin in the setting of hypotension
    • Activates the cardiac catheterization lab without unnecessary delay, prioritizing time to reperfusion
    • Communicates clearly and in closed loop fashion with the receiving team and considers cautious hemodynamic support if needed

    Model Verbalized Answer

    I am listening carefully to the full prehospital handover, not just the ECG finding, because the trend that his blood pressure has been dropping over the last several minutes changes my management significantly and suggests early cardiogenic shock. Given the clear anterior ST elevation on the prehospital ECG and his ongoing symptoms, I would activate the cardiac catheterization lab immediately rather than waiting to repeat an ECG in the department, since time to reperfusion is critical and repeating it should not delay activation. Because he is now hypotensive, I am specifically avoiding further nitroglycerin, which could worsen his blood pressure and push him further into shock. I would prepare for cautious fluid or vasopressor support if his pressure continues to drop while arranging transfer to the cath lab. Throughout this I am communicating clearly in closed loop fashion with both the paramedics and the cath lab team so nothing gets lost in the handover.

  38. Station 38: Simultaneous Arrival of Two Critical Patients After a Collision

    Reading Stem

    Two patients arrive simultaneously from a two vehicle collision. Patient A is a 40 year old man who is talking but has an obviously deformed open femur fracture with brisk bleeding. Patient B is a 25 year old woman who is unresponsive with shallow breathing and a rapid, thready pulse. Only one resuscitation bay and team are immediately available.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate quickly performs a rapid triage assessment of both patients rather than fully assessing one before glancing at the other, confirm that patient A, despite dramatic bleeding, is talking and protecting his airway, while patient B has an unprotected airway and signs of shock, making her the higher immediate priority for the resuscitation bay. If the candidate directs a team member to apply a tourniquet and direct pressure to patient A's leg while personally moving to manage patient B's airway and circulation, allow this parallel task delegation to proceed effectively. If the candidate becomes fixated on patient A's dramatic bleeding and neglects patient B, have patient B's oxygen saturation and mental status decline further to illustrate the cost of misallocated attention in a multiple patient scenario. If the candidate calls for additional resources such as more staff, a second bay, or trauma team activation for both patients, this reflects appropriate recognition of the scale of the situation. Expect ongoing reassessment and reprioritization as both patients' conditions evolve, along with clear task delegation and communication using structured language so the team understands who is responsible for what.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Performs rapid simultaneous triage rather than fully managing one patient before assessing the other
    • Correctly identifies which patient is the higher immediate priority based on airway and shock status rather than visual drama of injury
    • Delegates tasks effectively to manage both patients in parallel using available staff
    • Calls for additional resources appropriate to the scale of the situation
    • Continuously reassesses and reprioritizes as both patients' conditions evolve

    Model Verbalized Answer

    With two critical patients arriving at once I need to rapidly triage both before committing fully to either one, and even though patient A's open femur fracture looks dramatic, he is talking and protecting his airway, while patient B is unresponsive with a compromised airway and signs of shock, which makes her my higher immediate priority. I would delegate a team member to apply a tourniquet and direct pressure to patient A's leg while I personally move to manage patient B's airway and circulation, so both patients are being addressed in parallel rather than sequentially. I am calling for additional staff and resources right away given the scale of this situation, because trying to manage both patients with a single team will not work. I am giving clear, specific instructions rather than vague requests, so everyone knows exactly what they are responsible for. Throughout this I am reassessing both patients frequently, because priorities can shift quickly and I do not want to become fixated on the more visually dramatic injury at the expense of the sicker patient.

  39. Station 39: Missed Torsion Diagnosis Requiring Disclosure and Quality Review

    Reading Stem

    A 15 year old boy was seen yesterday by a colleague for scrotal pain and discharged with a diagnosis of epididymitis. He returns today with worsening pain, and on your exam you find a high riding, horizontal testis with absent cremasteric reflex, concerning for testicular torsion missed on the prior visit.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate immediately arranges urgent urology consultation and, if available, bedside or formal ultrasound with Doppler to confirm torsion without delaying surgical consultation for imaging, allow urology to be paged and the patient taken emergently to the operating room, though with a testis that may not be salvageable given the delay. If the candidate recognizes the need for open, honest disclosure to the patient and family about the missed diagnosis on the prior visit, expect the candidate to verbalize a disclosure conversation that is honest, empathetic, avoids blaming the previous physician personally, and does not minimize what happened. If the candidate avoids disclosure or suggests withholding information about the prior visit, interject that this is not appropriate practice and ask the candidate to reconsider. If the candidate discusses reporting this case through a formal quality improvement or morbidity and mortality process rather than simply mentioning it informally to the colleague, this reflects correct systems based thinking. Expect discussion of how to approach the colleague using a nonpunitive, systems focused framework aimed at understanding what led to the missed diagnosis rather than assigning individual blame, and how this could inform a broader process change such as a standardized red flag checklist for scrotal pain presentations.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Prioritizes urgent surgical or urology involvement for actual patient care first, without delaying for administrative concerns
    • Recognizes the need for honest, empathetic disclosure of the missed diagnosis to the patient and family
    • Avoids blaming the previous physician personally during disclosure or in initial discussion
    • Reports the case through a formal quality improvement or morbidity and mortality process rather than handling it informally
    • Approaches the case using nonpunitive, systems based thinking aimed at preventing recurrence

    Model Verbalized Answer

    My first priority is still this patient's care, so I am getting urology involved emergently and pursuing imaging only if it will not meaningfully delay operative management, since testicular torsion is time critical regardless of what happened yesterday. Separately, I know this family deserves honest disclosure that torsion was not diagnosed on the prior visit, and I would have that conversation directly and empathetically, acknowledging what happened without minimizing it and without personally blaming my colleague in front of the family. After the immediate care is handled, I would make sure this case gets reviewed through our formal quality improvement or morbidity and mortality process rather than just mentioning it informally to my colleague, because a single conversation does not create systems level learning. My goal in that review is understanding what factors contributed to the missed diagnosis, such as whether a standardized approach to scrotal pain was followed, rather than assigning blame to one individual. Ultimately I want this case to drive a concrete process change, like a checklist or a clearer pathway for scrotal pain presentations, so the same error is less likely to happen again.

  40. Station 40: Applying a Clinical Decision Rule to a Low Risk Chest Pain Patient

    Reading Stem

    A 45 year old man with atypical chest pain and a normal ECG asks whether he needs further cardiac testing before going home. He read online about a blood test that can rule out a blood clot in the lungs and asks if that applies to him, since his main complaint today is pleuritic chest pain, not classic cardiac pain.

    Examiner Scenario

    If the candidate clarifies the actual presenting complaint and pretest probability for pulmonary embolism using a validated tool such as the PERC rule or Wells criteria, allow the scenario to establish that he is genuinely low risk with no major risk factors. If the candidate correctly explains that a decision rule like PERC is only valid when applied to patients who are already low pretest probability, and that a negative PERC in a truly low risk patient allows PE to be excluded without further testing, this reflects correct application of the rule and its intended population. If the candidate proposes applying PERC indiscriminately to a higher risk patient or misunderstands that it only rules out disease in an appropriately selected low risk population, interject with a data point revealing he actually has several risk factors that push him out of the low risk category, testing whether the candidate recognizes the rule no longer applies and further testing such as D-dimer or CT is needed. If the candidate discusses the underlying evidence, such as the derivation and validation studies behind the rule and its sensitivity in the appropriate population, this demonstrates genuine critical appraisal rather than rote rule application. Expect the candidate to communicate this clearly and honestly to the patient, explaining both what the test can and cannot tell them and what shared decision making looks like for further workup or reassurance.

    Examiner Checklist

    • Clarifies the presenting complaint and establishes pretest probability before applying any clinical decision rule
    • Correctly explains that a rule such as PERC is only valid in an appropriately selected low pretest probability population
    • Recognizes when a patient does not meet criteria for a rule to be applied and adjusts the workup accordingly
    • Demonstrates understanding of the evidence behind the decision rule rather than applying it by rote
    • Communicates the reasoning and limitations of testing clearly to the patient in a shared decision making conversation

    Model Verbalized Answer

    Before I answer his question about the blood clot test, I need to actually establish his pretest probability for pulmonary embolism, because a rule like PERC is only valid when it is applied to patients who are already low risk to begin with, it is not a universal rule out test for anyone with chest pain. If his history and exam genuinely put him in a low risk category with no major risk factors, then a negative PERC would actually be enough to exclude PE without needing a D-dimer or CT scan, and I would explain that reasoning to him clearly. If instead he has risk factors I was not initially aware of that push him out of the low risk category, then PERC no longer applies to him and I would need to move on to a D-dimer or imaging depending on his risk level. I want to understand the actual evidence behind this rule, including the population it was validated in, rather than just applying it mechanically to every chest pain patient. Finally I would explain all of this to him honestly, including what the test can and cannot tell us, so we can make a shared decision about further workup together.

Part 4: Supplementary MCQ Answer Key (496 Questions)

Bonus/transition practice reflecting the CFPC's 2026-2027 SAMP-to-MCQ format transition. Each entry states the keyed correct letter followed by a 3 to 4 sentence explanation.

Chapter 2: Cardiovascular Emergencies (57 Questions)

  1. Q1. A 58-year-old man presents with 20 minutes of crushing substernal chest pain radiating to the left arm, diaphoresis, and nausea. His initial ECG shows no ST changes. What is the most appropriate immediate next step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Obtain serial ECGs and serial troponins while treating empirically for acute coronary syndrome): A single normal ECG does not exclude acute coronary syndrome, since ischemic changes can evolve over time. Serial ECGs and troponins are the correct enhanced-skills approach to catch evolving infarction while empiric ASA, nitrates, and monitoring are started. Discharging a patient with an active anginal pattern risks missing an evolving MI. Stress testing and CT angiography are not appropriate in the acute unstable setting.

  2. Q2. Which historical feature most increases the pretest probability that chest pain represents acute coronary syndrome rather than a musculoskeletal cause?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Pain that is exertional and relieved within minutes by rest or nitroglycerin): The correct answer identifies classic exertional angina physiology, where myocardial oxygen demand exceeds supply and resolves with rest or nitrates. Reproducible chest wall tenderness, pleuritic pain, and fleeting sharp pains are all classic features that argue against cardiac ischemia. Recognizing these discriminating features is a key feature of this priority topic.

  3. Q3. A 45-year-old woman with no cardiac history has 2 hours of tearing chest pain radiating to the back, with a blood pressure of 190/110 and pulse discrepancy between arms. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Acute aortic dissection): Tearing pain radiating to the back with unequal blood pressures between limbs is the classic presentation of aortic dissection. Pericarditis typically causes positional, pleuritic pain without a pulse deficit. Costochondritis and panic attacks do not produce hemodynamic asymmetry or this pain quality, making dissection the diagnosis that must be actively excluded first.

  4. Q4. In a patient with suspected acute coronary syndrome and ongoing chest pain, which medication combination is standard initial emergency department management prior to disposition decisions?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Aspirin, nitroglycerin, and oxygen only if hypoxic): The correct answer reflects current guidelines: aspirin for antiplatelet effect, nitroglycerin for symptom relief if not hypotensive, and oxygen reserved for documented hypoxia rather than routine use. Routine oxygen in normoxic ACS patients has no proven benefit and may cause harm. Thrombolysis requires ECG-confirmed STEMI criteria, not empiric use, and steroids/antibiotics have no role in ACS.

  5. Q5. A young healthy patient presents with sharp chest pain that improves when leaning forward and worsens when lying flat, one week after a viral illness. A pericardial friction rub is heard. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Acute pericarditis): Positional pleuritic pain relieved by leaning forward, a recent viral prodrome, and a friction rub are classic key features of acute pericarditis. STEMI pain is typically constant and not positional. Pneumothorax and pulmonary embolism present with dyspnea and hypoxia rather than this specific postural pain pattern, making pericarditis the best fit.

  6. Q6. Which ECG finding is most specific for acute pericarditis rather than STEMI in a patient with chest pain?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Diffuse concave ST elevation across multiple leads with PR depression): Diffuse concave ST elevation with associated PR depression across most leads is the classic pericarditis pattern, reflecting global epicardial inflammation rather than a single vessel territory. STEMI produces localized ST elevation matching a coronary distribution with reciprocal changes elsewhere. Recognizing this distinction prevents unnecessary catheterization lab activation.

  7. Q7. A 30-year-old with sudden pleuritic chest pain and dyspnea after a long flight has a Wells score suggesting moderate probability for pulmonary embolism. What is the most appropriate next investigation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (D-dimer testing, followed by CT pulmonary angiography if elevated): For moderate pretest probability, a validated D-dimer followed by CT pulmonary angiography if positive is the correct enhanced-skills pathway to confirm or exclude PE. Chest x-ray cannot rule out PE, and catheterization addresses coronary disease, not thromboembolism. Anticoagulating without any objective testing is inappropriate for moderate risk patients unless CT is unavailable and clinical suspicion is very high.

  8. Q8. Which chest pain presentation warrants the highest suspicion for esophageal rupture (Boerhaave syndrome) rather than a cardiac cause?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Severe retrosternal pain immediately after forceful vomiting, with subcutaneous emphysema): Severe pain immediately following forceful vomiting, especially with subcutaneous emphysema on exam, is the classic key feature pattern for Boerhaave syndrome, a surgical emergency. Exertional pain suggests cardiac ischemia, palpation-reproducible pain suggests musculoskeletal cause, and a gradual multi-day course suggests an infectious process rather than acute rupture.

  9. Q9. A 65-year-old diabetic woman presents with fatigue, nausea, and diaphoresis but denies chest pain. Her ECG shows inferior ST elevation. What key feature does this case illustrate?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Diabetic and elderly patients may present with atypical or silent anginal equivalents): This case demonstrates the well-recognized key feature that diabetic, elderly, and female patients often present with atypical anginal equivalents such as nausea and fatigue rather than classic chest pain. The inferior ST elevation confirms an evolving MI despite the atypical presentation. Assuming cardiac disease is excluded simply because chest pain is absent would miss a life-threatening diagnosis.

  10. Q10. What is the recommended maximum door-to-balloon time target for a patient with STEMI presenting to a hospital with primary PCI capability?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (90 minutes): Ninety minutes is the accepted benchmark target for door-to-balloon time when primary PCI is available on site, minimizing myocardial damage from prolonged ischemia. Longer delays worsen outcomes and would typically prompt consideration of fibrinolysis or transfer protocols instead. Recognizing and tracking this target is part of appropriate STEMI system-of-care management.

  11. Q11. A patient with cocaine-induced chest pain and ECG changes is being managed in the emergency department. Which medication should be specifically avoided?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Beta-blockers, due to risk of unopposed alpha-adrenergic coronary vasoconstriction): Beta-blockers are contraindicated in cocaine-associated chest pain because blocking beta receptors leaves alpha-adrenergic vasoconstriction unopposed, worsening coronary spasm. Benzodiazepines are first-line to reduce sympathetic drive, while aspirin and nitroglycerin remain appropriate for suspected ischemia and vasospasm respectively. This is a classic and frequently tested exception to standard ACS pharmacotherapy.

  12. Q12. A patient with chest pain has a HEART score calculated as low risk. What is the most appropriate disposition per current evidence-based practice?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Discharge home with close outpatient follow-up, without routine admission): A validated low HEART score identifies patients at very low short-term risk of major adverse cardiac events, supporting safe discharge with outpatient follow-up rather than automatic admission. Skipping follow-up entirely would be inappropriate since some residual risk remains. Emergent catheterization is reserved for high-risk or STEMI-equivalent presentations, not low-risk scores.

  13. Q13. Which physical exam finding, if present in a patient with chest pain, should raise concern for cardiac tamponade rather than isolated pericarditis?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Jugular venous distension, muffled heart sounds, and hypotension (Beck's triad)): Beck's triad of jugular venous distension, muffled heart sounds, and hypotension signals hemodynamically significant pericardial effusion progressing to tamponade, a true emergency requiring urgent pericardiocentesis. A friction rub alone or positional relief with stable vitals suggests uncomplicated pericarditis. Chest wall tenderness points toward a musculoskeletal cause rather than tamponade.

  14. Q14. A patient presents with chest pain and the ECG shows new ST depression in leads V1 to V4 with tall R waves. What should this pattern raise suspicion for?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Posterior wall myocardial infarction, requiring posterior leads for confirmation): Anterior ST depression with tall R waves in V1 to V4 is a reciprocal pattern classically representing posterior STEMI, and posterior leads (V7 to V9) should be obtained to confirm ST elevation there. Treating this as benign early repolarization would miss an active infarction requiring reperfusion therapy. This key feature is commonly tested because posterior MI is easily missed on a standard 12-lead.

  15. Q15. A 70-year-old man with known coronary artery disease presents with an ECG showing ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. Which coronary artery is most likely occluded?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Right coronary artery): ST elevation in the inferior leads II, III, and aVF classically localizes to right coronary artery occlusion in most patients, since the RCA supplies the inferior wall. The LAD supplies the anterior wall (V1-V4), and the circumflex supplies the lateral wall (I, aVL, V5-V6). Recognizing this territorial mapping guides the clinician to anticipate right ventricular involvement and check right-sided leads.

  16. Q16. In a patient with an inferior STEMI, why is it important to obtain a right-sided ECG (V4R) before giving nitroglycerin?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Right ventricular infarction causes preload dependence, and nitrates can cause profound hypotension): Right ventricular infarction, often accompanying inferior STEMI, makes the patient preload-dependent, and nitrates or diuretics can precipitate severe hypotension by reducing venous return. Checking V4R for ST elevation identifies this subset before administering preload-reducing medications. This is a well-recognized key feature distinguishing safe from unsafe inferior MI management.

  17. Q17. A patient with an anterior STEMI develops a new systolic murmur and acute pulmonary edema on day 3. What mechanical complication should be suspected first?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Papillary muscle rupture causing acute mitral regurgitation): A new systolic murmur with acute pulmonary edema days after an anterior MI is the classic key feature presentation of papillary muscle rupture causing severe acute mitral regurgitation, a surgical emergency. Myocarditis and chronic valve disease would not present abruptly in this temporal relationship to a known infarct. Prompt echocardiography and cardiac surgery consultation are required.

  18. Q18. Which class of medication has been shown to reduce mortality when started early in a patient with confirmed acute myocardial infarction and no contraindications?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, and statins): Beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, and statins each have proven mortality benefit in the post-MI setting when contraindications are absent, and are standard evidence-based secondary prevention therapy. Calcium channel blockers are not first-line and prophylactic antiarrhythmics have shown harm in past trials. Corticosteroids have no established benefit and can impair healing.

  19. Q19. A 55-year-old man with an NSTEMI is being risk-stratified using the GRACE score to guide timing of invasive coronary angiography. What does a high GRACE score indicate?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Higher risk of in-hospital and post-discharge mortality, favoring earlier invasive management): A high GRACE score identifies NSTEMI patients at elevated risk of death or reinfarction, supporting an early invasive strategy with angiography within 24 hours rather than a conservative approach. It does not suggest lower disease likelihood or a non-cardiac cause. Risk stratification tools like GRACE are key features tested for guiding timely, appropriate escalation of care.

  20. Q20. Which of the following best describes the mechanism of a type 2 myocardial infarction?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Myocardial oxygen supply-demand mismatch without primary plaque rupture, such as from severe anemia or tachyarrhythmia): Type 2 MI results from a supply-demand mismatch, for example severe anemia, sepsis, or tachyarrhythmia increasing demand while supply is limited, without a primary culprit plaque rupture. Type 1 MI is the classic plaque rupture and thrombosis mechanism. Recognizing type 2 MI changes management toward correcting the underlying precipitant rather than emergent revascularization alone.

  21. Q21. A patient status post recent MI develops recurrent chest pain with diffuse ST elevation and a pericardial friction rub two weeks later. What condition does this represent?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Dressler syndrome, a post-MI autoimmune pericarditis): Dressler syndrome is a delayed autoimmune pericarditis occurring weeks after MI, presenting with recurrent pleuritic chest pain, fever, and a friction rub, distinct from reinfarction. Reinfarction would show new regional ECG changes and rising troponins rather than a diffuse pericarditis pattern. Recognizing this key feature avoids unnecessary repeat catheterization when anti-inflammatory therapy is the appropriate treatment.

  22. Q22. In the emergency department, what is the target time from patient contact to fibrinolysis administration when primary PCI is not available within guideline-recommended transfer time?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (30 minutes): A 30-minute door-to-needle target for fibrinolysis is the accepted benchmark when timely transfer for primary PCI is not feasible, minimizing myocardial damage from delayed reperfusion. Longer delays substantially worsen outcomes for a fully reversible process. This benchmark is a core key feature of STEMI systems-of-care management for family physicians in enhanced-skills EM roles.

  23. Q23. Which finding on troponin testing best supports a diagnosis of acute myocardial infarction rather than chronic myocardial injury?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A rising and/or falling pattern on serial measurements with at least one value above the 99th percentile): A dynamic rise and/or fall pattern distinguishes acute myocardial injury from a chronically elevated but stable troponin, which suggests conditions like renal failure or chronic heart failure instead. A single value cannot establish the temporal pattern needed for an acute MI diagnosis. This distinction is essential to avoid mislabeling chronic troponin elevation as an acute coronary event.

  24. Q24. A patient with an acute inferior MI develops complete heart block with a stable blood pressure and adequate perfusion. What is the most appropriate initial management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Close monitoring with transcutaneous pacing pads applied, reserving pacing for hemodynamic instability): Complete heart block complicating inferior MI is often transient due to AV nodal ischemia and may resolve with reperfusion, so stable patients are closely monitored with pacing pads ready rather than immediately paced or given epinephrine. Permanent pacemaker placement is not an emergency department procedure and is reserved for persistent block. This reflects the correct escalation-based approach to a common MI complication.

  25. Q25. Which lifestyle and pharmacologic combination represents guideline-based secondary prevention counseling after a myocardial infarction?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Smoking cessation, cardiac rehabilitation referral, and dual antiplatelet therapy adherence): Smoking cessation, cardiac rehabilitation, and adherence to dual antiplatelet therapy are core evidence-based secondary prevention measures that reduce recurrent events after MI. Early unrestricted strenuous activity or stopping medications prematurely both increase risk of reinfarction or stent thrombosis. Structured follow-up is essential even after an uncomplicated hospital course.

  26. Q26. A patient with a prior MI and reduced ejection fraction develops recurrent syncope. What is the most important initial concern to address?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Risk of ventricular tachyarrhythmia related to underlying scar and reduced ejection fraction): Recurrent syncope in a patient with prior MI and reduced ejection fraction should raise strong concern for ventricular tachyarrhythmia arising from myocardial scar, a potentially life-threatening cause. Attributing this to vasovagal syncope or an unrelated cause would miss a high-risk diagnosis needing urgent cardiology evaluation and possible defibrillator consideration. This distinction is a critical key feature in post-MI risk assessment.

  27. Q27. Which of the following best differentiates unstable angina from NSTEMI?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Absence of detectable myocardial injury biomarker elevation in unstable angina, versus a positive troponin in NSTEMI): Unstable angina and NSTEMI share similar clinical presentations and ECG changes, but unstable angina by definition lacks the biomarker evidence of myonecrosis seen with a positive troponin in NSTEMI. Neither condition shows the ST elevation defining STEMI, and fibrinolysis is not indicated for either. This biomarker-based distinction guides risk stratification and management intensity.

  28. Q28. A patient on dual antiplatelet therapy after a recent drug-eluting stent presents needing urgent non-cardiac surgery. What is the most appropriate approach?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Consult cardiology before stopping antiplatelet therapy, given high stent thrombosis risk if stopped prematurely): Premature discontinuation of dual antiplatelet therapy after a drug-eluting stent carries a serious risk of acute stent thrombosis, so cardiology input is essential to balance surgical bleeding risk against thrombotic risk. Simply stopping both agents or ignoring stent status entirely could precipitate a life-threatening MI. This collaborative, risk-balanced approach is the correct key feature for perioperative management.

  29. Q29. A hemodynamically stable patient has a narrow-complex regular tachycardia at 180 beats per minute that terminates abruptly with intravenous adenosine. What was the most likely underlying rhythm?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia (AVNRT)): Abrupt termination with adenosine, which transiently blocks the AV node, is classic for a reentrant supraventricular tachycardia such as AVNRT that depends on the AV node as part of its circuit. Atrial fibrillation slows but does not typically terminate abruptly with adenosine. Ventricular tachycardia and sinus tachycardia do not reliably terminate with adenosine, making AVNRT the best answer.

  30. Q30. A patient presents in ventricular fibrillation with no pulse. What is the single most important immediate intervention?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Immediate defibrillation as soon as the rhythm is confirmed): Ventricular fibrillation is a pulseless shockable rhythm requiring immediate unsynchronized defibrillation, which is the single intervention proven to restore organized rhythm and survival. Synchronized cardioversion is used for organized tachyarrhythmias with a pulse, not pulseless VF. Amiodarone is an adjunct after failed shocks, and pacing has no role in a fibrillating heart.

  31. Q31. A hemodynamically unstable patient has a wide-complex regular tachycardia with a palpable pulse. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Synchronized cardioversion): An unstable patient with a wide-complex tachycardia and a pulse requires prompt synchronized cardioversion to restore a stable rhythm, timing the shock to the QRS to avoid inducing VF. Unsynchronized defibrillation is reserved for pulseless rhythms. Oral medications and observation are inappropriate given hemodynamic instability requiring immediate electrical intervention.

  32. Q32. Which ECG feature helps distinguish ventricular tachycardia from supraventricular tachycardia with aberrant conduction?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Presence of AV dissociation with independent P waves and fusion or capture beats): AV dissociation, along with fusion or capture beats, strongly supports a ventricular origin because the atria and ventricles are firing independently rather than via the conduction system. A narrow QRS argues against VT altogether, since VT is wide-complex by definition. Simple rate or QT interval findings do not reliably differentiate these two rhythms.

  33. Q33. A patient with atrial fibrillation of unknown duration and a rapid ventricular response is hemodynamically stable. What must be assessed before attempting cardioversion?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Duration of atrial fibrillation and stroke risk, since cardioversion after 48 hours risks embolization without prior anticoagulation or transesophageal echocardiogram): Atrial fibrillation lasting more than 48 hours, or of unknown duration, carries a real risk of atrial thrombus, so cardioversion without prior anticoagulation or a transesophageal echocardiogram to exclude thrombus can precipitate embolic stroke. Stable patients therefore warrant rate control and risk assessment first rather than immediate cardioversion. This key feature prevents an iatrogenic stroke.

  34. Q34. A patient with new atrial fibrillation and a rapid ventricular rate but stable blood pressure needs rate control. Which medication class is first-line in a patient without heart failure?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Beta-blockers or non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers): Beta-blockers or non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as diltiazem are standard first-line rate control agents for atrial fibrillation in patients without heart failure. Epinephrine and atropine would worsen tachycardia rather than control it. Anticoagulation addresses stroke risk but does not control ventricular rate on its own.

  35. Q35. A patient with a wide-complex tachycardia that is regular and monomorphic, in the setting of known prior myocardial infarction and scar, is most likely having what rhythm?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Monomorphic ventricular tachycardia): A regular, monomorphic wide-complex tachycardia in a patient with known infarct scar strongly suggests scar-related monomorphic VT, a common and expected complication of prior MI. Multifocal atrial tachycardia is classically irregular with varying P wave morphology, not monomorphic. Given the high pretest probability from prior MI, VT should be assumed until proven otherwise.

  36. Q36. Torsades de pointes is most classically associated with which underlying abnormality?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A prolonged QT interval): Torsades de pointes, a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a twisting QRS axis, arises specifically from QT interval prolongation, whether congenital or drug/electrolyte-induced. A short PR interval and isolated first-degree block are unrelated conduction abnormalities. Recognizing QT prolongation as the driver directs treatment toward magnesium and removing offending QT-prolonging agents.

  37. Q37. What is the first-line emergency treatment for stable torsades de pointes?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Intravenous magnesium sulfate): Intravenous magnesium sulfate is first-line therapy for torsades de pointes, stabilizing the myocardial membrane even when serum magnesium is normal. Cardioversion is reserved for unstable or degenerating rhythms rather than as an automatic first step. Calcium channel blockers are not indicated and potassium alone will not reliably terminate the arrhythmia.

  38. Q38. A patient develops symptomatic bradycardia with a heart rate of 35 and hypotension. Atropine has failed to improve the rate. What is the next appropriate step?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Transcutaneous pacing, with transvenous pacing or dopamine/epinephrine infusion as needed): When atropine fails to correct symptomatic bradycardia, transcutaneous pacing is the next step, escalating to transvenous pacing or a chronotropic infusion such as dopamine or epinephrine if pacing is delayed or ineffective. Beta-blockade would worsen bradycardia, and defibrillation is for shockable pulseless rhythms, not bradycardia. Observation is inappropriate given hemodynamic compromise.

  39. Q39. Which type of second-degree AV block is characterized by progressively lengthening PR intervals before a dropped beat, and is generally benign?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Mobitz type I (Wenckebach)): Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, shows progressive PR prolongation culminating in a dropped QRS and is usually benign, often related to increased vagal tone or AV nodal disease. Mobitz type II and third-degree block carry higher risk of progression to complete heart block and often require pacing. First-degree block does not drop beats at all, distinguishing it from these types.

  40. Q40. A patient with atrial fibrillation and a CHADS-65 or CHA2DS2-VASc score indicating elevated stroke risk should be started on which therapy for long-term stroke prevention?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Oral anticoagulation): Patients with atrial fibrillation and an elevated validated stroke risk score should be anticoagulated, since oral anticoagulants substantially reduce stroke risk compared to aspirin alone, which is no longer considered adequate monotherapy. Being asymptomatic between episodes does not reduce embolic risk. Antiarrhythmic therapy controls rhythm or rate but does not substitute for anticoagulation in appropriate candidates.

  41. Q41. A young patient with Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome develops atrial fibrillation with a very rapid, irregular, wide-complex rhythm. Which medication should be avoided?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (AV nodal blocking agents such as adenosine, beta-blockers, or calcium channel blockers): In WPW with atrial fibrillation, AV nodal blocking agents can preferentially block the normal AV node pathway, forcing conduction entirely down the accessory pathway and precipitating ventricular fibrillation. Procainamide is a safer pharmacologic alternative that slows the accessory pathway, and cardioversion remains appropriate if the patient becomes unstable. This is a critical and frequently tested contraindication.

  42. Q42. A post-cardiac arrest patient who achieves return of spontaneous circulation remains comatose. What is the recommended target temperature management strategy?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Targeted temperature management maintaining a controlled temperature, historically 32 to 36 degrees Celsius, avoiding fever): Targeted temperature management, maintaining a controlled range and strictly avoiding fever, is recommended for comatose post-arrest patients to reduce secondary neurologic injury. Deliberately allowing hyperthermia or rapid rewarming above normal would worsen neurologic outcomes. This key feature reflects current post-resuscitation care guidelines for family physicians managing the immediate post-ROSC period.

  43. Q43. A patient with a regular narrow-complex tachycardia at 150 beats per minute has a sawtooth flutter-wave pattern on ECG, most prominent in the inferior leads. What is the most likely rhythm?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Atrial flutter with 2:1 AV conduction): A sawtooth flutter-wave pattern, especially prominent inferiorly, at a ventricular rate near 150 (often exactly half of 300) is the classic key feature of atrial flutter with 2:1 conduction. Ventricular fibrillation is chaotic and pulseless, not organized and regular. Bradycardic and first-degree block descriptions do not match a rate of 150 with flutter waves.

  44. Q44. A 68-year-old man with a history of heart failure presents in severe respiratory distress, sitting upright, with diffuse crackles and a blood pressure of 190/100. What is the most appropriate first-line treatment?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation and intravenous nitroglycerin): In acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema with hypertension, non-invasive positive pressure ventilation combined with intravenous nitroglycerin reduces preload and afterload while improving oxygenation, often avoiding intubation. A fluid bolus would worsen pulmonary edema, and beta-blockers are not first-line in the acute decompensated setting. Intubation is reserved for patients failing non-invasive measures.

  45. Q45. Which finding on chest x-ray is most characteristic of cardiogenic pulmonary edema?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Bilateral perihilar infiltrates with Kerley B lines and cardiomegaly): Bilateral perihilar infiltrates, Kerley B lines representing interstitial edema, and cardiomegaly are the classic key feature radiographic findings of cardiogenic pulmonary edema. Unilateral lobar consolidation instead suggests pneumonia, while pneumothorax and widened mediastinum point toward entirely different diagnoses. Recognizing this pattern supports the clinical diagnosis alongside history and exam.

  46. Q46. A patient with acute pulmonary edema and a systolic blood pressure of 80 mmHg develops worsening respiratory distress. Why should nitroglycerin be used cautiously in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Nitroglycerin further reduces preload and can worsen hypotension in a hypotensive patient): Nitroglycerin is a venodilator that reduces preload, which is beneficial in hypertensive pulmonary edema but can precipitate dangerous hypotension when the patient is already hypotensive, potentially indicating cardiogenic shock instead. In this scenario inotropic support or mechanical circulatory support may be needed rather than nitrates. Recognizing this blood pressure-dependent decision point is a key feature of pulmonary edema management.

  47. Q47. Which laboratory or point-of-care test is most useful to help distinguish cardiogenic pulmonary edema from a primary pulmonary process when the diagnosis is unclear?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) level): BNP is released in response to ventricular wall stress and is characteristically elevated in cardiogenic pulmonary edema, helping distinguish it from a primary pulmonary etiology when the clinical picture is ambiguous. Blood glucose, lipase, and urinalysis findings are not relevant to differentiating cardiac from pulmonary causes of dyspnea. BNP should be interpreted alongside the full clinical picture rather than in isolation.

  48. Q48. A patient with acute pulmonary edema improves after non-invasive ventilation and diuresis. What discharge consideration is most important before sending the patient home?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Ensuring adequate follow-up and medication reconciliation, since acute decompensated heart failure often warrants a period of observation or admission): Most patients with acute decompensated heart failure benefit from a period of observation or admission with careful medication reconciliation and close follow-up, rather than immediate discharge despite symptomatic improvement, given the risk of early relapse. Stopping cardiac medications or liberalizing sodium intake would predispose to recurrent decompensation. This reflects safe transition-of-care practice for this priority topic.

  49. Q49. A patient with flash pulmonary edema is found to have severe bilateral renal artery stenosis. What underlying mechanism explains this presentation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Sudden increase in afterload and fluid retention from renin-angiotensin activation causing rapid pulmonary edema): Bilateral renal artery stenosis can trigger marked renin-angiotensin-aldosterone activation, causing severe hypertension, fluid retention, and abrupt afterload increase that precipitates flash pulmonary edema, sometimes with a relatively preserved ejection fraction. This is distinct from an infectious or dehydration-related process. Recognizing this etiology is important because it may require directed renal imaging and specific therapy.

  50. Q50. Which medication class should generally be avoided as monotherapy in acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema due to risk of worsening outcomes if not paired with other therapy?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Diuretics used alone without addressing afterload in a severely hypertensive patient): While diuretics remain useful, relying on diuretics alone without addressing markedly elevated afterload in a hypertensive patient may be less effective than combining with vasodilator therapy and positive pressure ventilation. Non-invasive ventilation, nitroglycerin in hypertensive patients, and oxygen for hypoxia are all appropriate core components of therapy. Balancing preload, afterload, and ventilation together produces the best outcomes.

  51. Q51. A patient in acute pulmonary edema fails to improve with non-invasive ventilation and nitroglycerin, becoming increasingly somnolent and hypercapnic. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation): A patient failing non-invasive ventilation with worsening mental status and hypercapnia is at risk of respiratory failure and aspiration, and requires prompt endotracheal intubation rather than continued reliance on non-invasive support. Discharge would be unsafe given ongoing decompensation, and oral diuretics act too slowly for this acute deterioration. Recognizing failure of non-invasive ventilation and escalating appropriately is a key management decision point.

  52. Q52. Which historical clue in a patient with new pulmonary edema should raise suspicion for an ischemic trigger rather than simple volume overload?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Associated chest pain or anginal equivalent symptoms preceding the dyspnea): Chest pain or an anginal equivalent preceding acute pulmonary edema should prompt evaluation for acute coronary syndrome as the precipitant, since ischemia causing acute left ventricular dysfunction requires urgent cardiac workup in addition to standard pulmonary edema treatment. Sodium non-adherence and gradual peripheral edema instead suggest a volume-overload etiology. Distinguishing the trigger changes both workup and disposition.

  53. Q53. A patient with acute pulmonary edema has a known severe aortic stenosis. Why should nitroglycerin be used with particular caution in this setting?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Preload reduction in severe aortic stenosis can cause a critical drop in cardiac output and hypotension): In severe aortic stenosis, the fixed outflow obstruction makes cardiac output highly preload-dependent, so reducing preload with nitroglycerin can cause a dangerous fall in cardiac output and blood pressure. This is a distinct and important exception, similar in concept to right ventricular infarction, that changes standard pulmonary edema management. Careful hemodynamic monitoring and cautious dosing are required if nitrates are used at all.

  54. Q54. What physical exam finding, if present alongside pulmonary edema, suggests a primary valvular cause such as acute mitral regurgitation rather than isolated left ventricular failure?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A new holosystolic murmur radiating to the axilla): A new holosystolic murmur radiating to the axilla is characteristic of acute mitral regurgitation, a mechanical cause of pulmonary edema that requires urgent echocardiography and possible surgical evaluation. A long-standing unchanged murmur or simple peripheral edema does not suggest an acute valvular event. Identifying a new murmur changes the diagnostic and management pathway substantially.

  55. Q55. In an elderly patient with acute pulmonary edema and new atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response, what is an appropriate initial treatment priority?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Controlling the ventricular rate while treating the pulmonary edema concurrently, since tachycardia can both cause and worsen decompensation): Rapid atrial fibrillation can both precipitate and be caused by acute decompensated heart failure, so rate control alongside standard pulmonary edema therapy is appropriate rather than treating either issue in isolation. Unsynchronized defibrillation is reserved for pulseless rhythms, not atrial fibrillation with a pulse. Sequencing therapy to address both problems together produces better outcomes than treating one and ignoring the other.

  56. Q56. Which of the following best describes the pathophysiology of high-altitude pulmonary edema, distinguishing it from cardiogenic pulmonary edema?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction leading to uneven perfusion and capillary leak, typically with a normal heart): High-altitude pulmonary edema results from hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction causing uneven regional blood flow and capillary stress failure, typically occurring in individuals with structurally normal hearts, unlike cardiogenic pulmonary edema. It is not an infectious process and is not simply unmasked valvular disease. Recognizing this distinct non-cardiogenic mechanism guides treatment toward descent, oxygen, and specific vasodilator therapy rather than standard heart failure treatment.

  57. Q57. A patient with acute pulmonary edema secondary to a hypertensive emergency has a blood pressure of 220/130. What is an appropriate initial blood pressure reduction target in the first hour?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A reduction of approximately 20 to 25 percent from the initial mean arterial pressure): Guideline-based management of hypertensive emergency recommends a controlled reduction of roughly 20 to 25 percent of mean arterial pressure in the first hour to avoid precipitating cerebral or coronary hypoperfusion from overly rapid correction. Aggressive normalization or reduction below 90 systolic risks ischemic complications. This careful, graduated approach is a key feature when hypertensive emergency coexists with pulmonary edema.

Chapter 3: Respiratory Emergencies (28 Questions)

  1. Q58. A 22-year-old with known asthma presents with severe respiratory distress, a silent chest on auscultation, and a normal pCO2 on venous blood gas. What does this normal pCO2 most likely indicate?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Impending respiratory failure, since a normalizing pCO2 in severe asthma suggests fatigue rather than improvement): In severe asthma, tachypnea typically causes a low pCO2, so a normalizing or rising pCO2 despite ongoing severe distress and a silent chest signals respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure, not improvement. This is a classic and dangerous pitfall if pCO2 is interpreted in isolation from the clinical picture. Recognizing this key feature should prompt aggressive escalation of care, including consideration of intubation.

  2. Q59. Which finding on physical exam of a severe asthma exacerbation should raise the most concern rather than reassurance?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A silent chest with minimal air movement on auscultation): A silent chest indicates minimal air movement due to severe airway obstruction, and paradoxically signals a more critical patient than one with loud wheezing, since wheeze requires some airflow to be generated. Mild tachypnea and audible wheeze with accessory muscle use, while concerning, are less ominous than absent breath sounds. This key feature is frequently misunderstood by learners as reassuring when it is actually a red flag.
