  3. Q60. What is the first-line bronchodilator therapy for an acute moderate to severe asthma exacerbation in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Inhaled short-acting beta-agonist combined with ipratropium bromide): Inhaled short-acting beta-agonists combined with ipratropium bromide are first-line bronchodilator therapy for acute asthma exacerbations, providing rapid combined beta-agonist and anticholinergic bronchodilation. Long-acting beta-agonists are not appropriate for acute rescue therapy, and beta-blockers would worsen bronchospasm. Antibiotics are not indicated unless a specific bacterial trigger is identified.

  4. Q61. A patient with a known history of COPD presents with worsening dyspnea, increased sputum purulence, and increased sputum volume. What is the most appropriate additional therapy beyond bronchodilators and steroids?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Antibiotic therapy targeting likely respiratory pathogens): The classic Anthonisen criteria of increased dyspnea, sputum volume, and sputum purulence identify COPD exacerbations most likely to benefit from antibiotic therapy in addition to bronchodilators and corticosteroids. High-flow 100 percent oxygen risks worsening hypercapnia in COPD by blunting hypoxic respiratory drive. Antifungal therapy has no role, and withholding bronchodilators would worsen the exacerbation.

  5. Q62. Why should supplemental oxygen be carefully titrated rather than given at a high flow rate in a patient with an acute COPD exacerbation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Excessive oxygen can worsen hypercapnia and respiratory acidosis by blunting hypoxic drive and increasing ventilation-perfusion mismatch): In COPD, chronically elevated CO2 can shift respiratory drive to rely more on hypoxia, so excessive supplemental oxygen may blunt this drive and worsen ventilation-perfusion mismatching, leading to dangerous hypercapnia and acidosis. Oxygen is not contraindicated outright, but should be titrated to a conservative target saturation. This key feature prevents iatrogenic harm from well-intentioned oxygen therapy.

  6. Q63. A patient in acute respiratory distress from a COPD exacerbation has worsening hypercapnia and acidosis despite maximal medical therapy and is becoming drowsy. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP)): Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation is strongly indicated for COPD exacerbations with hypercapnic respiratory acidosis, reducing work of breathing and often avoiding intubation if initiated before severe decompensation. High-dose opioids would dangerously worsen respiratory drive, and discharge is clearly unsafe in this deteriorating patient. Drowsiness from hypercapnia, unless it precludes airway protection, is itself an indication to act rather than withhold support.

  7. Q64. Which oral or systemic medication has been shown to reduce relapse and improve recovery when given during an acute asthma or COPD exacerbation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Systemic corticosteroids): Systemic corticosteroids reduce airway inflammation and have been shown to reduce relapse rates and hospital length of stay in both acute asthma and COPD exacerbations, making them a standard component of therapy. Inhaled antibiotics have no role in acute exacerbations, and decongestants do not address the underlying bronchospasm or inflammation. Magnesium may be used as an adjunct in severe asthma specifically, not as first-line for all patients or for COPD.

  8. Q65. A young patient with a severe asthma exacerbation fails to improve after continuous nebulized bronchodilators and systemic corticosteroids. What adjunct therapy has evidence for benefit in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Intravenous magnesium sulfate): Intravenous magnesium sulfate has evidence supporting its use as an adjunct bronchodilator in severe asthma exacerbations refractory to initial standard therapy, working through smooth muscle relaxation. Antihistamines and iron have no bronchodilator role, and beta-blockers would worsen bronchospasm and are contraindicated. This adjunct is specifically reserved for severe or refractory cases rather than routine mild exacerbations.

  9. Q66. Which spirometric or clinical feature best distinguishes COPD from asthma in a patient with chronic respiratory symptoms?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Largely fixed airflow obstruction with limited reversibility after bronchodilator, typically in a patient with significant smoking history): COPD is characterized by largely fixed, poorly reversible airflow obstruction usually in the setting of significant smoking or occupational exposure history, whereas asthma typically shows greater bronchodilator reversibility and can have a variable course. Full reversibility in every case is more typical of asthma, not COPD. Recognizing this key distinguishing feature helps guide both diagnosis and long-term management strategy.

  10. Q67. A patient with a known asthma exacerbation is being considered for discharge from the emergency department. Which finding would suggest the patient is safe for discharge?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Peak flow greater than 70 percent of predicted or personal best, sustained after bronchodilator therapy with no accessory muscle use): A sustained peak flow above roughly 70 percent of predicted or personal best, resolution of accessory muscle use, and stable vital signs after treatment support safe discharge with an action plan and follow-up. Persistent accessory muscle use, very low peak flow, or ongoing need for continuous nebulizers all indicate the patient remains too unstable for discharge. Objective peak flow measurement is a key feature supporting safe disposition decisions.

  11. Q68. Which historical feature in a patient with COPD exacerbation should prompt evaluation for a triggering pulmonary embolism rather than a simple infective exacerbation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain and hypoxia disproportionate to the degree of wheeze or sputum change): Sudden pleuritic pain and hypoxia out of proportion to typical infective exacerbation features should raise suspicion for pulmonary embolism as a COPD exacerbation trigger, since COPD patients are already at elevated baseline risk for thromboembolism. A gradual productive cough or a typical infective response to antibiotics instead supports a straightforward infective exacerbation. This distinction changes the diagnostic workup significantly.

  12. Q69. A patient with severe asthma is being considered for intubation due to imminent respiratory arrest. Which induction agent is preferred for its bronchodilatory properties?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Ketamine): Ketamine has intrinsic bronchodilatory properties and is generally the preferred induction agent for rapid sequence intubation in severe asthma, in addition to providing hemodynamic stability. Succinylcholine and etomidate are chosen for other properties, not bronchodilation. This is a well-recognized pharmacologic key feature for airway management in status asthmaticus.

  13. Q70. Which ventilator strategy is recommended once a severe asthma patient requires intubation, to avoid a life-threatening complication of air trapping?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Permissive hypercapnia with a lower respiratory rate and prolonged expiratory time to reduce breath stacking): In severe asthma, aggressive ventilation with high rates and short expiratory times risks dynamic hyperinflation and breath stacking, which can cause barotrauma and hemodynamic collapse, so permissive hypercapnia with prolonged expiratory time and lower rates is the recommended strategy. Normalizing CO2 rapidly at the expense of adequate exhalation time is dangerous. Recognizing and preventing breath stacking is a critical key feature in ventilated status asthmaticus.

  14. Q71. A COPD patient on long-term home oxygen presents with an exacerbation and is found to have a chronically elevated bicarbonate on venous blood gas. What does this finding most likely represent?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Metabolic compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis from long-standing CO2 retention): A chronically elevated bicarbonate in a COPD patient represents renal metabolic compensation for long-standing chronic respiratory acidosis from CO2 retention, and should be interpreted in the context of the patient's known baseline rather than as a new acute process. This finding actually supports chronic CO2 retention rather than arguing against it. Recognizing baseline compensation helps correctly interpret acute blood gas changes during an exacerbation.

  15. Q72. A 60-year-old smoker presents with acute dyspnea, unilateral decreased breath sounds, hyperresonance to percussion, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Tension pneumothorax): Unilateral hyperresonance, decreased breath sounds, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side are the classic key features of tension pneumothorax, a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate needle or finger thoracostomy decompression rather than imaging delay. Pneumonia would show dullness with consolidation, not hyperresonance. Pulmonary edema and effusion would not cause tracheal deviation in this direction.

  16. Q73. What is the most appropriate immediate management of suspected tension pneumothorax with hemodynamic instability?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Immediate needle or finger thoracostomy decompression without waiting for chest x-ray confirmation): Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis in an unstable patient, and immediate needle or finger thoracostomy decompression is required without delaying for imaging confirmation, since delay risks cardiac arrest from obstructive shock. Fluids alone do not address the underlying mechanical problem. Observation is inappropriate given hemodynamic instability from a rapidly progressive process.

  17. Q74. A patient presents with acute dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and a recent long-haul flight. A CT pulmonary angiogram confirms a pulmonary embolism. The patient is hemodynamically stable with no right heart strain. What is the appropriate initial treatment?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Therapeutic anticoagulation): Hemodynamically stable pulmonary embolism without right heart strain is appropriately treated with therapeutic anticoagulation alone, reserving thrombolysis or embolectomy for massive or high-risk PE with hemodynamic instability or significant right heart strain. Withholding anticoagulation entirely would leave the patient at risk of clot extension and recurrent embolism. Risk-stratifying PE severity determines the correct treatment intensity.

  18. Q75. A patient with massive pulmonary embolism presents in shock with a systolic blood pressure of 75 mmHg despite fluids. What is the most appropriate treatment?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Systemic thrombolysis, given hemodynamic instability from massive PE): Massive pulmonary embolism causing hemodynamic instability despite fluid resuscitation is an indication for systemic thrombolysis to rapidly reduce clot burden and right ventricular strain, unless a contraindication exists. Anticoagulation alone acts too slowly for this immediately life-threatening presentation. This risk-stratified escalation from stable to massive PE management is a core key feature of this priority topic.

  19. Q76. Which bedside tool is most useful to rapidly differentiate cardiac versus pulmonary causes of acute dyspnea in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Point-of-care ultrasound assessing cardiac function, lung B-lines, and inferior vena cava characteristics): Point-of-care ultrasound allows rapid bedside assessment of cardiac function, presence of B-lines suggesting pulmonary edema, pleural effusions, and pneumothorax, making it a powerful tool to differentiate causes of acute dyspnea quickly. An ECG alone, complete blood count, or glucose level do not directly visualize cardiopulmonary structures in this way. Integrating point-of-care ultrasound into the assessment reflects current enhanced-skills EM practice.

  20. Q77. A patient presents with acute dyspnea and a history significant for a recent long bone fracture repair. Petechiae are noted on the chest and axillae. What diagnosis should be strongly considered?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Fat embolism syndrome): The triad of recent long bone fracture, respiratory distress, and petechiae, classically on the chest and axillae, is the key feature presentation of fat embolism syndrome, typically occurring 24 to 72 hours after injury. Pneumonia and asthma exacerbation would not typically produce this petechial pattern tied temporally to fracture repair. Recognizing this distinct syndrome prevents misattribution to a more common respiratory cause.

  21. Q78. A patient with acute dyspnea has a normal chest x-ray, normal oxygen saturation, and significant anxiety with numbness and tingling in the fingers. What is the most likely diagnosis after excluding serious causes?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Hyperventilation syndrome): Perioral and finger paresthesias with anxiety, a normal chest x-ray, and normal oxygenation in the setting of acute dyspnea are classic for hyperventilation syndrome causing respiratory alkalosis, but this remains a diagnosis of exclusion after ruling out serious causes such as PE, pneumothorax, or cardiac disease. Assuming hyperventilation without adequate workup risks missing a life-threatening diagnosis presenting atypically. Once excluded, reassurance and controlled breathing techniques are appropriate.

  22. Q79. A patient with acute dyspnea and a large unilateral pleural effusion undergoes thoracentesis. Which fluid characteristic would suggest an exudative rather than transudative process using Light's criteria?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Pleural fluid to serum protein ratio greater than 0.5): Light's criteria classify an effusion as exudative if the pleural fluid to serum protein ratio exceeds 0.5, among other criteria, pointing toward causes like infection, malignancy, or inflammation rather than simple transudative processes like heart failure. A low ratio with normal LDH instead suggests a transudate. Correctly classifying the effusion guides the subsequent diagnostic workup.

  23. Q80. Which patient population with acute dyspnea should prompt consideration of a right-to-left cardiac shunt (such as patent foramen ovale) causing platypnea-orthodeoxia syndrome?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A patient whose dyspnea and hypoxia worsen specifically when moving from supine to upright position): Platypnea-orthodeoxia syndrome describes dyspnea and hypoxia that specifically worsen with upright positioning and improve when supine, classically from a right-to-left shunt whose flow is positionally dependent. Symptoms unaffected by position or improving with standing do not fit this specific and less common syndrome. Recognizing this rare but testable pattern prompts consideration of structural cardiac shunt evaluation.

  24. Q81. A patient with acute dyspnea and known cirrhosis has significant orthodeoxia and clubbing. What pulmonary complication of liver disease should be considered?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Hepatopulmonary syndrome): Hepatopulmonary syndrome, caused by intrapulmonary vascular dilation in the setting of liver disease, classically presents with dyspnea, orthodeoxia, and clubbing, and should be considered in cirrhotic patients with unexplained hypoxia. A simple viral infection or isolated pneumonia would not explain the chronic pattern of orthodeoxia and clubbing. Recognizing this hepatopulmonary link changes the diagnostic approach and specialist referral pathway.

  25. Q82. Which feature of a patient's dyspnea history should raise concern for methemoglobinemia as an underlying cause?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Cyanosis and low oxygen saturation on pulse oximetry that does not improve with supplemental oxygen, alongside a normal PaO2 on arterial blood gas): Methemoglobinemia classically causes cyanosis with a pulse oximetry reading that plateaus around the mid-80s percent and fails to improve with supplemental oxygen, despite a normal calculated PaO2 on arterial blood gas, reflecting the co-oximetry discordance characteristic of abnormal hemoglobin. Straightforward asthma or a case improving proportionally with oxygen does not fit this pattern. Recognizing this discordance prompts co-oximetry testing and treatment with methylene blue if severe.

  26. Q83. A patient with acute dyspnea after starting a new antiarrhythmic medication is found to have bluish-gray discoloration of the skin and low oxygen saturation unresponsive to oxygen. What is the most likely diagnosis and initial treatment?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Methemoglobinemia, treated with intravenous methylene blue if severe or symptomatic): Certain medications, including some antiarrhythmics and local anesthetics, can precipitate methemoglobinemia, presenting with cyanosis unresponsive to oxygen, and severe or symptomatic cases are treated with intravenous methylene blue. This differs from anaphylaxis, which would show additional features like urticaria or hypotension. Carbon monoxide poisoning presents differently on co-oximetry and this presentation better fits a drug-induced methemoglobinemia.

  27. Q84. A patient with acute dyspnea has a widened alveolar-arterial (A-a) gradient on arterial blood gas. What does this finding suggest about the underlying cause?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A parenchymal or vascular lung problem impairing gas exchange, such as pulmonary embolism or pneumonia, rather than a purely extrapulmonary cause): A widened A-a gradient indicates impaired gas exchange within the lung itself, such as from pulmonary embolism, pneumonia, or pulmonary edema, helping distinguish these parenchymal or vascular causes from purely extrapulmonary causes like opioid-induced hypoventilation or a psychogenic cause, which typically have a normal A-a gradient. This calculation is a useful key feature to help focus the differential diagnosis of dyspnea. It should be interpreted alongside the full clinical context rather than in isolation.

  28. Q85. A previously healthy 25-year-old presents with sudden-onset dyspnea and pleuritic chest pain at rest, with a tall, thin body habitus and no trauma history. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Primary spontaneous pneumothorax): A tall, thin young patient with sudden pleuritic pain and dyspnea at rest, without trauma, is the classic key feature presentation of primary spontaneous pneumothorax from rupture of a subpleural bleb. MI is uncommon at this age without risk factors, and pneumonia or pulmonary edema would have a more gradual onset with additional systemic or cardiac findings. Chest x-ray confirms the diagnosis and guides whether observation or chest tube drainage is required.

Chapter 4: Neurological Emergencies (43 Questions)

  1. Q86. A 70-year-old presents with acute left-sided weakness and slurred speech that began 90 minutes ago. Non-contrast CT head shows no hemorrhage. What is the most important next consideration?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Determine eligibility for thrombolytic therapy based on the established time window and exclusion criteria): A normal non-contrast CT in acute ischemic stroke is expected early on, since infarction is not always visible immediately, and the priority is rapidly assessing eligibility for thrombolysis within the appropriate time window. Waiting 24 hours would forfeit the opportunity for time-sensitive reperfusion therapy. Discharge is inappropriate given an active, potentially treatable acute neurologic deficit.

  2. Q87. Which of the following is an absolute contraindication to intravenous thrombolysis in acute ischemic stroke?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Evidence of intracranial hemorrhage on non-contrast CT): Intracranial hemorrhage on imaging is an absolute contraindication to thrombolysis, since administering a thrombolytic in this setting would worsen bleeding and outcomes. Elevated but treatable blood pressure, advanced age alone, or mild baseline disability are not automatic absolute contraindications and require individualized assessment. Correctly identifying true absolute contraindications is essential before proceeding with time-sensitive thrombolytic therapy.

  3. Q88. A patient presents with sudden severe headache described as the worst of their life, along with neck stiffness, but a normal non-contrast CT six hours after onset. What is the next most appropriate step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Lumbar puncture to look for xanthochromia and red blood cells, since CT sensitivity decreases with time from onset): CT sensitivity for subarachnoid hemorrhage decreases as time passes from symptom onset, so a normal CT beyond the very early window does not exclude subarachnoid hemorrhage, and lumbar puncture looking for xanthochromia is the appropriate next step. Discharging this classic thunderclap headache presentation without further workup risks missing a life-threatening bleed. Lumbar spine MRI is unrelated to this intracranial concern.

  4. Q89. A patient with acute ischemic stroke symptoms has a blood pressure of 210/115 and is being considered for thrombolysis. What is the recommended blood pressure target before administering thrombolytic therapy?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Below 185/110 mmHg): Blood pressure must be controlled below 185/110 mmHg before administering thrombolytic therapy in acute ischemic stroke, since higher pressures increase the risk of hemorrhagic transformation. Overly aggressive reduction below normal ranges, however, risks worsening cerebral perfusion in the setting of acute ischemia. This specific threshold is a key feature that must be met before proceeding with thrombolysis.

  5. Q90. Which stroke syndrome presentation is most consistent with an occlusion of the middle cerebral artery on the dominant (typically left) side?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Contralateral face and arm weakness greater than leg weakness, with aphasia): Middle cerebral artery occlusion classically causes face and arm weakness greater than leg weakness, since the MCA territory supplies most of the lateral cortex, with aphasia occurring when the dominant hemisphere is involved. Isolated leg weakness instead suggests anterior cerebral artery territory involvement. Pure sensory loss and bilateral vision loss do not fit the classic MCA syndrome pattern.

  6. Q91. A patient presents with sudden-onset vertigo, ataxia, dysarthria, and crossed sensory findings (ipsilateral face, contralateral body). Which vascular territory is most likely involved?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Posterior circulation (vertebrobasilar) territory): Vertigo, ataxia, dysarthria, and crossed sensory findings are classic key features of posterior circulation or brainstem stroke syndromes, such as lateral medullary syndrome, reflecting vertebrobasilar territory involvement. Anterior cerebral artery strokes cause leg-predominant weakness rather than this brainstem pattern. Recognizing posterior circulation stroke is important since these can be more subtle and easily misattributed to peripheral vertigo.

  7. Q92. A patient with acute stroke symptoms has an unclear time of symptom onset because they were last seen normal the night before and woke up with a deficit. What imaging approach may help determine eligibility for reperfusion therapy?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (MRI with diffusion-weighted and FLAIR sequence mismatch to estimate stroke age): In wake-up strokes with unknown onset time, MRI diffusion-FLAIR mismatch can help estimate how long ago the stroke occurred, potentially allowing thrombolysis or thrombectomy in appropriately selected patients despite unclear onset. Skull x-ray has no role in evaluating brain parenchyma. Carotid ultrasound assesses extracranial vessels but does not establish stroke timing.

  8. Q93. A patient presents with acute onset of an isolated third cranial nerve palsy including a dilated, non-reactive pupil. What diagnosis must be urgently excluded?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Posterior communicating artery aneurysm causing compressive third nerve palsy): An acute third nerve palsy with pupillary involvement (a dilated, non-reactive pupil) is a classic key feature of compressive posterior communicating artery aneurysm and requires urgent vascular imaging, since aneurysmal compression preferentially affects the peripherally located pupillary fibers. A benign viral neuropathy would more typically spare the pupil. Bell's palsy affects the facial nerve, causing different findings, not an isolated third nerve palsy.

  9. Q94. Which stroke mimic should be specifically excluded with a bedside glucose check before attributing sudden focal neurologic deficits to an acute stroke?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Hypoglycemia): Hypoglycemia is a well-known stroke mimic that can cause focal neurologic deficits indistinguishable from acute stroke, and a bedside glucose check is a rapid, essential step before proceeding further in the stroke pathway. Hypertension, chronic anemia, and a remote old injury do not typically produce acute new focal deficits in this manner. Correcting hypoglycemia promptly may rapidly reverse the deficit, avoiding unnecessary thrombolysis.

  10. Q95. A patient with a large hemispheric ischemic stroke develops progressive decreased level of consciousness and signs of herniation over the following two days. What is this complication called and what is an appropriate management consideration?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Malignant cerebral edema, potentially requiring decompressive hemicraniectomy): Malignant cerebral edema with mass effect and herniation is a recognized life-threatening complication of large hemispheric infarcts, and decompressive hemicraniectomy has been shown to improve survival in appropriately selected patients, particularly younger patients. This is not a benign or expected recovery pattern, nor is it simply a medication effect or infection. Early recognition and neurosurgical consultation are critical key features in this scenario.

  11. Q96. A patient presenting with transient ischemic attack symptoms that fully resolved within 20 minutes is being risk-stratified for early stroke recurrence. What tool is commonly used for this purpose?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (ABCD2 score): The ABCD2 score, incorporating age, blood pressure, clinical features, duration, and diabetes, is a validated tool used to stratify short-term stroke recurrence risk after a TIA and guide urgency of further workup and disposition. The Glasgow Coma Scale assesses level of consciousness, not stroke recurrence risk. APGAR and Wells scores are used in entirely different clinical contexts (newborn assessment and PE risk, respectively).

  12. Q97. Which finding on CT angiography in a patient with acute large vessel occlusion stroke would make them a candidate for mechanical thrombectomy in addition to standard therapy?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Occlusion of a proximal large vessel such as the internal carotid or proximal middle cerebral artery, within an appropriate time window and with favorable imaging): Mechanical thrombectomy is indicated for proximal large vessel occlusions, such as the internal carotid or proximal MCA, in eligible patients within the appropriate time window and with favorable imaging characteristics, in addition to thrombolysis if not contraindicated. Small distal occlusions or an absence of any occlusion would not warrant this intervention. Chronic small vessel disease is a separate, unrelated imaging finding. Recognizing large vessel occlusion candidacy is essential for timely referral to a thrombectomy-capable center.

  13. Q98. A patient presents with sudden monocular vision loss described as a curtain coming down, lasting several minutes and then resolving completely. What is this transient phenomenon called and what does it suggest?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Amaurosis fugax, suggesting transient retinal ischemia often from carotid artery disease): Amaurosis fugax describes transient monocular vision loss from retinal ischemia, classically from an embolic source such as carotid artery disease, and warrants urgent vascular workup similar to a TIA. A benign migraine aura typically involves more complex visual phenomena and is bilateral or affects the visual field rather than one eye entirely. This is a recognized key feature requiring urgent stroke-pathway evaluation rather than dismissal as benign.

  14. Q99. A patient with acute ischemic stroke has atrial fibrillation identified as the likely embolic source. When is anticoagulation typically initiated after the acute stroke?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Delayed initiation, often days after the stroke depending on infarct size, to reduce the risk of hemorrhagic transformation): Anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation-related stroke is typically delayed, often by several days depending on infarct size and hemorrhagic transformation risk, to balance recurrent embolism risk against bleeding risk into the newly infarcted tissue. Immediate anticoagulation within the first hour would increase hemorrhagic transformation risk. Withholding anticoagulation entirely, or waiting a full year, would leave the patient unnecessarily exposed to recurrent embolic stroke.

  15. Q100. Which of the following best describes a lacunar stroke syndrome presentation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Pure motor or pure sensory deficit from a small vessel infarct, often in the internal capsule or thalamus, without cortical signs like aphasia or neglect): Lacunar strokes classically produce pure motor or pure sensory syndromes from small vessel disease affecting deep structures like the internal capsule or thalamus, without cortical signs such as aphasia, neglect, or visual field cuts. Combined cortical signs instead suggest a larger territory cortical infarct. Recognizing the lacunar pattern helps localize the vascular pathology and anticipate a generally more favorable prognosis.

  16. Q101. A 45-year-old presents with a sudden, severe headache reaching maximum intensity within seconds, described as the worst headache of their life. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Non-contrast CT head, followed by lumbar puncture if the CT is negative and suspicion remains): A thunderclap headache reaching maximum intensity within seconds is a classic red-flag key feature for subarachnoid hemorrhage, warranting urgent non-contrast CT and, if negative, lumbar puncture to look for xanthochromia given imperfect CT sensitivity over time. Discharging with only analgesics or delaying to outpatient MRI risks missing a life-threatening bleed. Empiric migraine therapy is inappropriate without first excluding this dangerous diagnosis.

  17. Q102. Which historical feature of a headache should be considered a red flag warranting urgent neuroimaging rather than a diagnosis of primary headache disorder?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (New headache in a patient over 50 years old with no prior headache history): New onset headache after age 50 is a recognized red flag, since it raises concern for secondary causes such as giant cell arteritis, mass lesion, or other structural pathology rather than a new primary headache disorder at this age. A long-standing unchanged pattern, or a headache improving with routine measures, is more reassuring. This key feature should prompt further investigation rather than symptomatic treatment alone.

  18. Q103. A 68-year-old presents with a new temporal headache, jaw claudication, and scalp tenderness. What diagnosis must be considered urgently to prevent vision loss?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Giant cell (temporal) arteritis): Temporal headache with jaw claudication and scalp tenderness in an older patient is the classic key feature triad for giant cell arteritis, which can cause irreversible vision loss if untreated, making urgent corticosteroid therapy and temporal artery biopsy referral essential. Tension headache and new migraine at this age would not include jaw claudication or scalp tenderness. Cluster headache typically has autonomic features rather than this specific presentation.

  19. Q104. What is the appropriate initial treatment for suspected giant cell arteritis while awaiting temporal artery biopsy confirmation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (High-dose corticosteroids started immediately, without waiting for biopsy results): Corticosteroids should be started immediately on clinical suspicion of giant cell arteritis, without waiting for biopsy confirmation, since delay risks irreversible vision loss and biopsy results remain positive for a period even after steroids are started. Withholding treatment pending biopsy would risk permanent harm. Antibiotics have no role, and NSAIDs alone are insufficient for this vasculitis.

  20. Q105. A patient with a known history of migraine presents with a headache similar to prior episodes but now has a new focal neurologic deficit that does not resolve. What should this prompt?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Urgent evaluation for stroke or other structural cause, since a new persistent deficit should not be assumed to be a typical migraine aura): A new, persistent focal neurologic deficit, especially one that does not resolve as a typical aura would, should prompt urgent stroke workup rather than being attributed reflexively to the patient's known migraine history. Assuming a benign cause based solely on history risks missing an evolving stroke. This distinction between typical aura and a true red-flag deficit is an important key feature in headache assessment.

  21. Q106. A young obese woman presents with headache, transient visual obscurations, and papilledema on fundoscopic exam, with a normal CT head. What diagnosis should be considered?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Idiopathic intracranial hypertension (pseudotumor cerebri)): Headache with transient visual obscurations and papilledema in a young obese patient, with a structurally normal CT, is classic for idiopathic intracranial hypertension, requiring lumbar puncture for opening pressure measurement and ophthalmology involvement to protect vision. Tension and cluster headache do not typically cause papilledema. A normal CT does not exclude this diagnosis, since the pathology is related to intracranial pressure rather than a structural mass.

  22. Q107. A patient with a headache is found to have a sixth cranial nerve palsy causing diplopia on lateral gaze, along with signs of elevated intracranial pressure. What does this cranial nerve finding often represent?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A non-localizing false localizing sign of raised intracranial pressure, due to the long intracranial course of the sixth nerve): Sixth nerve palsy is a well-recognized false localizing sign of elevated intracranial pressure because of the nerve's long intracranial course, making it vulnerable to stretch from raised pressure regardless of the underlying lesion's actual location. It does not definitively localize a mass to the nerve's brainstem nucleus. Recognizing this key feature avoids over-interpreting the cranial nerve finding as precise anatomic localization.

  23. Q108. A patient presents with severe unilateral periorbital headache, ipsilateral lacrimation, and conjunctival injection, occurring in clusters over several weeks. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Cluster headache): Severe unilateral periorbital pain with ipsilateral autonomic features such as lacrimation and conjunctival injection, occurring in cyclical clusters, is the classic key feature presentation of cluster headache. Migraine without aura and tension headache lack these prominent autonomic features. Trigeminal neuralgia causes brief, electric shock-like pains rather than this sustained autonomic headache pattern.

  24. Q109. Which first-line abortive treatment is most appropriate for an acute cluster headache attack in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (High-flow 100 percent oxygen by non-rebreather mask): High-flow 100 percent oxygen is a well-established, rapid, first-line abortive treatment for acute cluster headache attacks with a strong evidence base. Oral acetaminophen alone is typically insufficient for the severity of cluster headache pain. Antibiotics and lumbar puncture have no role in typical, recurrent cluster headache without red-flag features.

  25. Q110. A patient with headache and fever has neck stiffness and photophobia. What is the most appropriate initial management priority?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Prompt empiric antibiotics (and consideration of lumbar puncture) for suspected bacterial meningitis, without unnecessary delay): Fever, neck stiffness, and photophobia raise strong concern for bacterial meningitis, a time-critical diagnosis where empiric antibiotics should not be delayed for lumbar puncture or imaging if there is any anticipated delay, since delayed antibiotics worsen outcomes. Reassurance and outpatient follow-up would be dangerously inadequate. This urgency is a critical key feature of headache with concerning associated features.

  26. Q111. A postpartum patient develops a positional headache that worsens when upright and improves when lying flat, several days after epidural anesthesia. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Post-dural puncture headache): A postural headache, worse upright and relieved supine, following recent epidural or spinal anesthesia is the classic key feature of post-dural puncture headache from ongoing CSF leak. Pre-eclampsia headache is not typically positional in this manner, and subarachnoid hemorrhage would not characteristically improve so distinctly with position. Recognizing the temporal and positional pattern guides toward conservative management or an epidural blood patch if severe.

  27. Q112. Which feature of a headache in a pregnant patient beyond 20 weeks gestation should prompt immediate blood pressure measurement and urinalysis?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Any new or unusual headache, given the possibility of pre-eclampsia): Any new or unusual headache in a pregnant patient beyond 20 weeks should prompt blood pressure measurement and urinalysis to screen for pre-eclampsia, since headache is a recognized warning symptom of this potentially life-threatening condition. Restricting concern only to febrile or chronic headaches would miss many true pre-eclampsia presentations. This low threshold for screening is an important key feature in pregnant patients with headache.

  28. Q113. A patient with a headache and recent scuba diving history presents with confusion and focal neurologic deficits shortly after surfacing. What diagnosis should be considered?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Arterial gas embolism from decompression illness): Neurologic symptoms including headache and confusion occurring shortly after surfacing from a dive are classic for arterial gas embolism, a neurologic emergency form of decompression illness requiring urgent recompression therapy. A coincidental viral or migraine process would not have this striking temporal relationship to diving. Recognizing this diving-specific etiology is essential to arrange timely hyperbaric treatment.

  29. Q114. A patient taking frequent over-the-counter analgesics for chronic headache reports the headache has become progressively more frequent despite increasing medication use. What is the most likely explanation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Medication overuse headache): Progressively worsening headache frequency despite escalating analgesic use is characteristic of medication overuse headache, a common and often underrecognized cause of chronic daily headache. While red flags should always be considered, a new brain tumor is a far less statistically likely explanation for this specific pattern. Management involves gradual analgesic withdrawal rather than assuming a rare cause without other supporting features.

  30. Q115. A patient presents in active generalized tonic-clonic seizure that has been continuous for 10 minutes. What is the most appropriate first-line medication?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intravenous or intramuscular benzodiazepine such as lorazepam or midazolam): Benzodiazepines are first-line therapy for status epilepticus, given intravenously or intramuscularly depending on access, to rapidly terminate ongoing seizure activity. Oral medications cannot be reliably given during active convulsions, and propofol is typically reserved for refractory status epilepticus requiring intubation. A seizure lasting this long meets criteria for status epilepticus and requires active treatment rather than observation.

  31. Q116. A patient continues to seize despite two appropriate doses of benzodiazepine. What is the most appropriate next step in management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A second-line antiepileptic such as intravenous fosphenytoin, valproate, or levetiracetam): After benzodiazepine failure, guidelines recommend moving to a second-line intravenous antiepileptic such as fosphenytoin, valproate, or levetiracetam rather than repeating benzodiazepines indefinitely, which increases respiratory depression risk without added benefit. Discharge is inappropriate given ongoing refractory status epilepticus. Oral medications are not appropriate for this acute, potentially life-threatening scenario requiring rapid intravenous loading.

  32. Q117. A patient who appears to have stopped convulsing after treatment for status epilepticus remains unresponsive with subtle eye deviation and twitching. What should be considered?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Non-convulsive status epilepticus, warranting EEG monitoring): Subtle findings such as eye deviation or twitching in a persistently unresponsive patient after apparent seizure cessation should raise concern for non-convulsive status epilepticus, which requires EEG monitoring to confirm and guide ongoing antiepileptic treatment. Assuming full recovery or simple post-ictal state could allow ongoing unrecognized seizure activity to cause further neurologic injury. This key feature prevents under-treatment of a subtle but serious condition.

  33. Q118. A young adult with a first-time unprovoked generalized seizure has returned to baseline mental status. What is an appropriate emergency department workup?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Blood glucose, electrolytes, and consideration of neuroimaging, with neurology follow-up arranged): A first unprovoked seizure warrants basic metabolic workup including glucose and electrolytes, along with consideration of neuroimaging to exclude a structural cause, and appropriate neurology follow-up rather than immediately starting long-term antiepileptic medication in the emergency department. Completely forgoing workup or follow-up would miss potentially treatable underlying causes. This measured, stepwise approach is the correct key feature for a first seizure presentation.

  34. Q119. A patient with known epilepsy on phenytoin presents with a breakthrough seizure. Which laboratory test is most useful to help explain the breakthrough episode?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Phenytoin level, to assess for subtherapeutic dosing or non-adherence): Checking a phenytoin level is a key and directly relevant step to identify subtherapeutic levels from non-adherence, medication interactions, or altered metabolism as a cause of breakthrough seizure in a patient with known epilepsy on this medication. Glucose alone would miss the medication-specific issue central to this scenario. Lipase and an inappropriately applied pregnancy test are not relevant to this specific presentation.

  35. Q120. A pregnant patient at 34 weeks gestation presents with a generalized tonic-clonic seizure and hypertension. What is the most likely diagnosis and first-line treatment?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Eclampsia, treated first-line with intravenous magnesium sulfate): A seizure with hypertension in a pregnant patient beyond 20 weeks is eclampsia until proven otherwise, and magnesium sulfate is the first-line treatment of choice, both to control the seizure and to prevent recurrence, rather than standard antiepileptic drugs. Attributing this to unrelated epilepsy or a febrile cause would miss this obstetric emergency requiring urgent delivery planning. This distinction is a critical key feature in this population.

  36. Q121. A patient with alcohol use disorder presents with a seizure two days after their last drink. What is the most likely cause and an important preventive consideration for future episodes?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Alcohol withdrawal seizure, with benzodiazepines used both acutely and to prevent further withdrawal seizures): Alcohol withdrawal seizures classically occur within the first two to three days after cessation and are best managed and prevented with benzodiazepines rather than assuming a new primary epilepsy diagnosis. While glucose should be checked, alcohol withdrawal is the far more likely explanation in this clinical context, and benzodiazepines play a central role in both acute seizure treatment and withdrawal prevention. Recognizing this etiology avoids unnecessary long-term antiepileptic therapy.

  37. Q122. A child presents with a seizure during a high fever, lasting 3 minutes, with full return to baseline afterward and no focal features. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Simple febrile seizure): A brief, generalized seizure associated with fever, with rapid return to baseline and no focal features, is characteristic of a simple febrile seizure in a young child and is generally benign. Lumbar puncture is not automatically required in every febrile seizure but is considered based on additional risk factors or exam findings. This does not meet criteria for status epilepticus given the brief duration and full recovery.

  38. Q123. Which historical feature would make a febrile seizure classified as complex rather than simple, warranting closer evaluation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Focal features, duration longer than 15 minutes, or recurrence within 24 hours): Complex febrile seizures are defined by focal features, prolonged duration beyond 15 minutes, or recurrence within 24 hours, and these features warrant a more thorough workup than a simple febrile seizure. A brief, fully generalized seizure with rapid recovery fits the simple category instead. Recognizing these distinguishing features helps determine the appropriate level of further investigation.

  39. Q124. A patient with a witnessed seizure has tongue biting on the lateral tongue and urinary incontinence, followed by a period of confusion. What do these findings support?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A true epileptic seizure rather than a psychogenic non-epileptic event): Lateral tongue biting, urinary incontinence, and a post-ictal confusional period are features that support a true epileptic seizure, since these findings are less commonly associated with psychogenic non-epileptic events or simple syncope. Syncope and anxiety attacks would not typically produce these specific findings together. While no single feature is entirely definitive, this combination raises confidence in a genuine epileptic event.

  40. Q125. A patient recovering from a seizure has unilateral weakness that resolves over the following hour. What is this transient post-ictal phenomenon called?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Todd's paralysis): Todd's paralysis is a transient, self-resolving post-ictal focal weakness following a seizure, typically resolving within hours, and should be recognized as distinct from a new acute stroke. While stroke must still be considered if the deficit persists or the history is atypical, the classic resolving pattern within an hour supports Todd's paralysis. This is a well-recognized and frequently tested key feature to avoid unnecessary thrombolysis in a stroke mimic.

  41. Q126. A patient with known epilepsy who has been seizure-free for two years on medication asks about driving regulations after a single breakthrough seizure. What is the general principle regarding disposition and reporting?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A seizure-related driving restriction period typically applies, and physicians often have reporting obligations depending on jurisdiction): Most jurisdictions require a defined seizure-free interval before resuming driving after a breakthrough seizure, and physicians often have specific reporting obligations to transportation or licensing authorities, which is an important medicolegal key feature of seizure management. A long prior seizure-free period does not exempt the patient from renewed restriction after a breakthrough event. Understanding these obligations is part of comprehensive emergency management of seizure patients.

  42. Q127. A patient taking a new antibiotic develops a first-time seizure. Which class of antibiotic is most classically associated with lowering the seizure threshold?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Fluoroquinolones and certain beta-lactams such as high-dose penicillins or carbapenems): Fluoroquinolones and certain beta-lactams, particularly at high doses or in renal impairment, are recognized to lower the seizure threshold and should be considered as a potential contributing cause in a new seizure temporally associated with starting one of these medications. Topical treatments and probiotics have no meaningful systemic seizure risk. Recognizing medication-induced seizure risk is an important key feature when evaluating a new seizure with a temporally associated new prescription.

  43. Q128. A patient with a seizure disorder presents after a breakthrough seizure and is found to have a supratherapeutic level of their antiepileptic medication with new nystagmus and ataxia. What does this suggest?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Antiepileptic drug toxicity, which can itself sometimes provoke breakthrough seizures or mimic other neurologic conditions): Supratherapeutic antiepileptic levels with nystagmus and ataxia indicate drug toxicity, which paradoxically can sometimes be associated with breakthrough seizures or can mimic other neurologic presentations, and warrants dose adjustment and further evaluation rather than being dismissed as irrelevant. This is not evidence of a new structural lesion based on this finding alone. Correctly attributing these findings to toxicity avoids unnecessary escalation of unrelated antiepileptic therapy.

Chapter 5: Altered Mental Status and Behavioral Emergencies (42 Questions)

  1. Q129. An unresponsive patient is brought in by paramedics with pinpoint pupils and slow, shallow respirations. What is the most appropriate immediate treatment to consider?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Naloxone administration for suspected opioid toxicity): Pinpoint pupils with respiratory depression is the classic key feature toxidrome of opioid overdose, and naloxone should be given promptly since it is a low-risk, rapidly acting reversal agent. Glucose alone would not address respiratory depression from opioids. Flumazenil is used for benzodiazepine reversal, not the described opioid toxidrome, and carries its own risks including precipitating seizures.

  2. Q130. Which mnemonic-based approach represents the standard initial assessment structure for a patient with decreased level of consciousness of unknown cause?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Airway, breathing, circulation, followed by immediate bedside glucose, and consideration of naloxone if opioid toxicity is suspected): The correct initial approach to undifferentiated decreased level of consciousness always begins with airway, breathing, and circulation, followed rapidly by bedside glucose checking and consideration of reversible causes such as opioid toxicity, before more time-consuming investigations like CT. Skipping straight to imaging or history without securing the ABCs first risks missing an immediately life-threatening and reversible cause. This structured approach is a foundational key feature of this priority topic.

  3. Q131. A patient with decreased level of consciousness is found to have a blood glucose of 1.8 mmol/L. What is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Intravenous dextrose administration): Severe hypoglycemia with decreased consciousness requires prompt intravenous dextrose, since oral glucose is unsafe in a patient who cannot protect their airway and insulin would dangerously worsen hypoglycemia. Waiting for formal lab confirmation would delay a time-critical, easily reversible treatment. This represents one of the most important reversible causes to identify and treat immediately in this priority topic.

  4. Q132. A patient with decreased level of consciousness has a Glasgow Coma Scale score of 7. What is the most appropriate airway management consideration?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (The patient likely requires airway protection with intubation, given inability to reliably protect the airway at this GCS level): A GCS of 8 or less has traditionally been used as a threshold suggesting the patient cannot adequately protect their airway and often requires intubation, and a GCS of 7 falls within this concerning range requiring careful airway assessment and likely intervention. Simple nasal cannula oxygen would not address airway protection concerns. The specific intubation technique should be tailored to the clinical scenario rather than mandating one method in every case.

  5. Q133. Which of the following toxic ingestions classically causes decreased level of consciousness with a specific antidote that should be considered based on history and clinical picture?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Benzodiazepine overdose, with flumazenil considered cautiously in select cases): Benzodiazepine overdose can cause significant decreased consciousness, and flumazenil is a specific antidote, though it must be used cautiously due to risks of precipitating seizures, particularly in patients with chronic benzodiazepine use or co-ingestion of pro-convulsant substances. Viral gastroenteritis, simple dehydration, and vasovagal episodes do not have a specific reversal antidote in this way. Recognizing when a specific antidote applies is an important key feature of toxicologic causes of decreased LOC.

  6. Q134. A patient with decreased level of consciousness has asterixis on exam and a history of cirrhosis. What is the most likely underlying cause?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Hepatic encephalopathy): Asterixis in a patient with known cirrhosis is a classic key feature of hepatic encephalopathy, related to elevated ammonia and other metabolic derangements from impaired liver clearance. Opioid toxicity and isolated stroke would not typically produce asterixis in this clinical context. Recognizing hepatic encephalopathy directs management toward identifying and correcting precipitants such as infection, bleeding, or constipation, and considering lactulose therapy.

  7. Q135. A patient with decreased level of consciousness and a history of poorly controlled diabetes has fruity breath odor, deep rapid breathing, and significant hyperglycemia. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Diabetic ketoacidosis): Fruity breath odor, Kussmaul respirations, and significant hyperglycemia are the classic key feature triad of diabetic ketoacidosis, requiring fluid resuscitation, insulin therapy, and careful electrolyte monitoring, particularly potassium. Hypoglycemia would present oppositely with low glucose rather than hyperglycemia. Simple alcohol intoxication would not explain the hyperglycemia and characteristic breath odor together.

  8. Q136. Which vital sign abnormality pattern, along with decreased level of consciousness, should raise concern for elevated intracranial pressure (Cushing's triad)?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular respirations): Cushing's triad, consisting of hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular respirations, is a late and ominous sign of significantly elevated intracranial pressure and impending herniation. Hypotension with tachycardia instead suggests a shock state rather than elevated ICP. Recognizing this specific triad prompts urgent neuroimaging and measures to reduce intracranial pressure, such as head elevation and hyperventilation in select scenarios.

  9. Q137. A patient with decreased level of consciousness has an elevated serum ammonia unrelated to liver disease, and is taking valproic acid for a seizure disorder. What should be considered?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Valproate-induced hyperammonemic encephalopathy): Valproic acid can cause hyperammonemic encephalopathy even without underlying liver disease, and this should be considered in a patient on this medication presenting with decreased consciousness and elevated ammonia. Assuming this is coincidental or automatically implies cirrhosis would miss a medication-related and potentially reversible cause. Recognizing this specific drug-induced etiology guides appropriate medication adjustment and supportive care.

  10. Q138. A patient found unresponsive outdoors in cold weather has a core temperature of 28 degrees Celsius. What modification to standard resuscitation should be considered?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Prolonged resuscitation efforts and gradual rewarming, since severe hypothermia can mimic clinical death and neuroprotect against hypoxic injury): Severe hypothermia can markedly slow metabolism and provide some neuroprotection, meaning patients may survive with good outcomes despite prolonged downtime, so resuscitation efforts are typically prolonged and the patient is not pronounced dead until adequately rewarmed (the principle that a patient is not dead until warm and dead). Rapid, uncontrolled rewarming risks dangerous arrhythmias. This key feature significantly changes standard resuscitation decision-making in hypothermic cardiac arrest.

  11. Q139. A patient with decreased level of consciousness following a reported fall has an epidural hematoma with a classic lucid interval on history. What does this pattern typically indicate regarding the injured vessel?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Arterial bleeding, most commonly from the middle meningeal artery): The classic lucid interval, where a patient briefly regains consciousness before rapidly deteriorating, reflects the typically arterial bleeding pattern of epidural hematoma, most often from the middle meningeal artery, which can expand rapidly and cause herniation. A purely venous or chronic subdural process would not typically produce this rapid, dramatic lucid interval pattern. Recognizing this key feature should prompt urgent neuroimaging and neurosurgical consultation.

  12. Q140. A patient with decreased level of consciousness is found to have carboxyhemoglobin levels significantly elevated after a house fire exposure. What is an important point regarding pulse oximetry in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Standard pulse oximetry can read falsely normal or high despite significant carbon monoxide poisoning, and co-oximetry should be used instead): Standard pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin and can therefore read falsely reassuring in carbon monoxide poisoning, making co-oximetry essential to accurately assess true oxygen-carrying capacity. Assuming standard oximetry is reliable in this setting risks underestimating the severity of poisoning. This is a frequently tested key feature pitfall in toxicologic causes of decreased consciousness.

  13. Q141. A patient with decreased level of consciousness after a suspected overdose has a widened QRS on ECG. Which class of ingestion should be strongly suspected and what is a key treatment?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Tricyclic antidepressant overdose, treated with sodium bicarbonate for cardiac sodium channel blockade): Widened QRS in the setting of suspected overdose is a classic key feature of tricyclic antidepressant toxicity from sodium channel blockade, and sodium bicarbonate is the specific treatment to narrow the QRS and reduce arrhythmia risk. Acetaminophen toxicity does not classically cause QRS widening, and N-acetylcysteine addresses hepatotoxicity, not cardiac conduction. Benzodiazepine and opioid overdoses do not typically cause this ECG finding, making TCA toxicity the best answer.

  14. Q142. A patient with decreased level of consciousness has significant hyponatremia on laboratory testing. What is an important principle when correcting the sodium level?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Sodium should be corrected gradually, since overly rapid correction risks osmotic demyelination syndrome): Overly rapid correction of chronic hyponatremia carries a serious risk of osmotic demyelination syndrome, so correction should proceed in a controlled, gradual manner guided by established correction rate limits, particularly in chronic or unclear-duration hyponatremia. Symptomatic severe hyponatremia does require treatment, but with careful attention to correction rate rather than uncontrolled rapid correction. This key feature prevents an iatrogenic neurologic complication from overly aggressive treatment.

  15. Q143. An elderly hospitalized patient develops acute fluctuating confusion, inattention, and disorganized thinking over the past day. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Delirium): Acute onset, fluctuating course, and inattention are the classic key features distinguishing delirium from dementia, which develops gradually over months to years rather than acutely. Normal aging does not cause this degree of acute cognitive disturbance. Delirium should always prompt a thorough search for an underlying medical cause rather than being attributed to a primary psychiatric disorder.

  16. Q144. Which underlying causes should be systematically considered first in an elderly patient presenting with new delirium?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Infection, medication effects or interactions, and metabolic derangements): Delirium in elderly patients is most commonly driven by identifiable and often reversible causes such as infection (particularly urinary tract infection), medication effects, or metabolic derangements, and a thorough workup for these is the correct initial approach. Assuming a primary psychiatric or personality-based cause without medical workup risks missing a treatable underlying condition. This systematic search for reversible causes is a foundational key feature of delirium management.

  17. Q145. An agitated, delirious elderly patient requires pharmacologic management for safety. Which medication class is generally preferred as first-line for this purpose?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Low-dose antipsychotics such as haloperidol, used cautiously with attention to QT interval): Low-dose antipsychotics are generally preferred over benzodiazepines for agitated delirium in most elderly patients, since benzodiazepines can worsen confusion and paradoxically increase agitation, except in cases of alcohol withdrawal delirium. QT interval should be monitored given antipsychotic risk of prolongation. Physical restraints should be a last resort after non-pharmacologic and pharmacologic measures, not a first-line sole intervention.

  18. Q146. A patient in alcohol withdrawal delirium (delirium tremens) requires pharmacologic treatment. Which medication class is the preferred first-line agent in this specific scenario?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Benzodiazepines): Unlike most other causes of delirium, alcohol withdrawal delirium (delirium tremens) is specifically treated first-line with benzodiazepines, which address both the underlying withdrawal physiology and associated agitation, and can also prevent withdrawal seizures. Antipsychotics alone do not adequately treat the withdrawal seizure risk. Opioids and beta-blockers do not address the core GABA-related withdrawal pathophysiology, making this an important exception to typical delirium management.

  19. Q147. Which non-pharmacologic intervention is considered a foundational first step in managing an elderly patient with delirium, before considering medication?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Reorientation strategies, minimizing environmental disruption, ensuring hearing and vision aids are available, and correcting sensory deficits): Non-pharmacologic strategies such as reorientation, minimizing environmental disruption, ensuring sensory aids like glasses and hearing aids are used, and maintaining normal sleep-wake cycles are foundational first-line interventions for delirium, often reducing the need for medication. Overstimulation or withholding supportive family presence would likely worsen delirium rather than help. Heavy sedation is not a preferred management strategy and can worsen outcomes.

  20. Q148. A patient with acute agitation requires assessment of decision-making capacity regarding refusing emergency treatment. What is the correct approach in this urgent situation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Capacity should still be formally assessed even in urgent situations, since agitation alone does not automatically mean incapacity): Agitation alone does not automatically equate to lacking decision-making capacity, and a proper capacity assessment, even if rapid given the urgency, is still required before overriding a patient's refusal. Simply honoring any refusal regardless of capacity could allow a genuinely incapable patient to make harmful decisions. This careful, individualized capacity assessment is an important medicolegal key feature in managing agitated patients.

  21. Q149. An agitated patient with delirium is found to have a new focal neurologic deficit on exam. What should this prompt in the workup?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Urgent neuroimaging to exclude a structural intracranial cause such as stroke or hemorrhage): A new focal neurologic deficit in a delirious patient should never be assumed to be a typical feature of delirium alone, and warrants urgent neuroimaging to exclude a structural cause such as stroke, hemorrhage, or mass lesion. Immediate psychiatric admission without addressing this red flag would miss a potentially life-threatening neurologic emergency. This distinction between typical delirium and a focal deficit requiring imaging is a key safety feature.

  22. Q150. Which validated bedside tool is commonly used to help diagnose delirium at the bedside by assessing acute onset, fluctuating course, inattention, and altered level of consciousness?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Confusion Assessment Method (CAM)): The Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) is a validated bedside tool specifically designed to assess the core diagnostic features of delirium: acute onset and fluctuating course, inattention, and either disorganized thinking or altered level of consciousness. The Glasgow Coma Scale assesses level of consciousness alone and does not capture the full delirium diagnostic picture. APGAR and Wells scores are used for entirely different clinical purposes.

  23. Q151. An agitated patient requires chemical sedation for safety, and their history includes prolonged QT interval on a prior ECG. What should be prioritized before choosing an antipsychotic?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Obtaining or reviewing a recent ECG and considering an agent with a lower QT-prolonging risk if feasible given the clinical urgency): A known history of QT prolongation should prompt careful consideration of ECG review and antipsychotic choice when feasible, since certain agents carry higher torsades risk, though urgent safety needs may still require rapid sedation with close monitoring. Deliberately choosing the highest-risk agent would be inappropriate when safer alternatives exist. Withholding all sedation despite genuine safety risk is also inappropriate, so a balanced, risk-aware approach is correct.

  24. Q152. A patient with delirium related to sepsis is found to have hypoactive rather than hyperactive features, appearing quiet, withdrawn, and lethargic. What is important to recognize about this presentation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Hypoactive delirium is common, often underrecognized, and carries similarly serious implications as hyperactive delirium): Hypoactive delirium, characterized by lethargy and withdrawal rather than agitation, is common, frequently underrecognized, and carries prognostic significance similar to hyperactive delirium, requiring the same thorough workup for underlying causes such as sepsis. Assuming only agitated presentations represent true delirium would miss many cases. Recognizing this subtype is an important key feature since it is easily overlooked compared to the more obviously disruptive hyperactive form.

  25. Q153. A patient with acute agitation from suspected sympathomimetic toxicity (such as cocaine or methamphetamine) requires sedation. Which medication class is generally preferred as first-line?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Benzodiazepines): Benzodiazepines are generally preferred as first-line sedation for sympathomimetic toxidrome-related agitation, addressing the underlying excess sympathetic and central nervous system stimulation effectively and safely. Beta-blockers alone can cause unopposed alpha-adrenergic effects, similar to the concern in cocaine-related chest pain. Antipsychotics may lower seizure threshold in this context and opioids do not address the underlying stimulant physiology, making benzodiazepines the preferred choice.

  26. Q154. A patient with newly diagnosed dementia and superimposed acute agitation is being evaluated. What historical detail helps confirm this is delirium superimposed on dementia rather than dementia progression alone?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (An acute change in baseline cognitive function occurring over hours to days, distinct from the patient's prior stable baseline): An acute change over hours to days from the patient's known stable baseline strongly suggests delirium superimposed on underlying dementia, which requires the same thorough search for a reversible medical cause as delirium in a cognitively normal patient. A gradual, stable, multi-year decline is more consistent with dementia progression alone rather than an acute superimposed process. Family history of dementia does not help distinguish an acute change from chronic progression.

  27. Q155. Which medication class, when newly started or increased in an elderly patient, is a particularly common and important reversible contributor to new delirium that should be reviewed?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Anticholinergic medications): Anticholinergic medications are a well-recognized and common reversible contributor to delirium in elderly patients, given increased sensitivity to central anticholinergic effects with aging, making thorough medication review essential in any new delirium presentation. Topical creams, standard vitamin doses, and artificial tears do not carry meaningful systemic anticholinergic burden. Identifying and discontinuing unnecessary anticholinergic medications is a key modifiable step in delirium management.

  28. Q156. An agitated patient requires physical restraint for immediate safety after less restrictive measures have failed. What is an essential ongoing requirement once restraints are applied?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Frequent reassessment, documentation, and removal of restraints as soon as safely possible, following institutional and legal requirements): Once restraints are used as a last resort after less restrictive measures fail, ongoing frequent reassessment, clear documentation, and prompt removal once safe are essential, following applicable institutional and legal requirements, since restraints carry their own risks. Leaving restraints indefinitely without reassessment would violate both safety and medicolegal standards. Restraints should never be a default first-line intervention rather than a genuine last resort.

  29. Q157. A patient presents after a suicide attempt with a specific plan, access to lethal means, and ongoing intent. What is the most appropriate immediate management priority?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Ensure immediate safety through close observation and removal of means, followed by formal psychiatric assessment): A patient with specific plan, access to means, and ongoing intent represents high acute suicide risk, requiring immediate safety measures such as close observation and removal of means, followed by formal psychiatric evaluation before any consideration of discharge. Discharging with only a future outpatient appointment or relying on reassurance alone would be unsafe given this level of expressed risk. Immediate safety and structured psychiatric assessment are the correct key feature priorities.

  30. Q158. Which historical and clinical factors are most associated with increased short-term suicide risk and should be specifically asked about during risk assessment?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Specific plan, access to lethal means, prior attempts, and current intent): Specific plan, access to lethal means, history of prior attempts, and current expressed intent are core, well-established risk factors that should be specifically and directly asked about during a thorough suicide risk assessment. Demographic details like marital status or occupation alone, without assessing these core factors, are insufficient for adequate risk stratification. Directly and non-judgmentally asking about suicidal ideation does not increase risk and is an essential key feature of assessment.

  31. Q159. A patient who denies current suicidal ideation but has a recent suicide attempt and ongoing significant risk factors is requesting to leave against medical advice. What is the most appropriate approach?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A capacity assessment should be performed, and involuntary hold procedures considered per local mental health legislation if the patient lacks capacity or poses significant ongoing risk): Denial of current ideation does not eliminate genuine concern given a recent attempt and ongoing risk factors, and a proper capacity assessment along with consideration of involuntary hold provisions under local mental health legislation is the correct approach if warranted. Automatically allowing departure or automatically restraining without assessment are both inappropriate extremes. This individualized legal and clinical assessment is a key feature of managing suicide risk in the emergency department.

  32. Q160. Which patient population has been shown to have a particularly elevated risk of completed suicide, warranting heightened clinical vigilance?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Older adult men, particularly those who are recently widowed or socially isolated): Older adult men, especially those recently widowed or socially isolated, have a notably elevated risk of completed suicide, often using more lethal means, even though other groups such as adolescent females may have higher rates of attempts. Strong social support and married status are generally protective rather than risk factors. Recognizing this demographic risk pattern helps direct heightened vigilance and appropriate screening in this population.

  33. Q161. A patient presents with suicidal ideation and has ingested an unknown quantity of acetaminophen four hours ago. What is the most appropriate immediate step regarding the ingestion?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Obtain an acetaminophen level at the appropriate time point and use the Rumack-Matthew nomogram to guide N-acetylcysteine treatment): A timed acetaminophen level plotted on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram is essential to determine need for N-acetylcysteine therapy, since acetaminophen toxicity can be clinically silent early on despite being potentially fatal if untreated. Assuming safety based on appearance alone risks missing significant hepatotoxic ingestion. Medical stabilization and toxicologic workup must occur before or alongside psychiatric assessment, not instead of it.

  34. Q162. Which screening tool is commonly used in emergency settings to help stratify suicide risk and guide disposition decisions?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)): The Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale is a validated, widely used tool specifically designed to assess suicidal ideation and behavior severity, helping guide risk stratification and disposition planning in emergency settings. The Glasgow Coma Scale, APGAR, and Wells scores are validated tools for entirely different clinical purposes unrelated to suicide risk. Using an appropriate validated tool supports more consistent, structured risk assessment.
