  35. Q163. A patient with chronic suicidal ideation related to a personality disorder presents again after a low-lethality self-harm gesture, now stating they feel better and want to go home. What is an important principle in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Each presentation should still be individually assessed for acute risk factors, since chronic ideation does not exclude an acute escalation in risk): Even patients with chronic suicidal ideation related to a personality disorder can experience genuine acute escalations in risk, so each presentation warrants individualized assessment rather than reflexively dismissing repeat presentations. Automatically labeling behavior as attention-seeking without proper assessment risks missing true escalating risk. Conversely, automatic involuntary admission without individualized assessment is also not appropriate; the correct approach is careful, non-judgmental, case-by-case evaluation.

  36. Q164. A patient who attempted suicide by hanging is now stable but has hoarse voice and neck swelling. What complication should be considered and evaluated urgently?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Airway or vascular injury to the neck, such as laryngeal injury or carotid artery dissection): Hoarseness and neck swelling after a hanging attempt should raise concern for significant structural injury including laryngeal trauma or carotid artery dissection, both of which can be delayed or evolve and require urgent imaging and specialist evaluation. Assuming this is benign or simply anxiety-related risks missing a serious, potentially life-threatening injury. Careful medical evaluation of the physical injury must accompany psychiatric assessment in near-lethal suicide attempts.

  37. Q165. Which factor is generally considered protective against suicide risk and should be incorporated into a comprehensive risk assessment?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Strong social support, religious or cultural beliefs against suicide, and responsibility for dependent children): Strong social support, protective religious or cultural beliefs, and a sense of responsibility toward dependents are recognized protective factors that should be weighed alongside risk factors in a comprehensive suicide risk assessment. Social isolation, financial strain, and relationship loss are instead recognized risk factors that increase concern rather than protect against it. Balancing both risk and protective factors provides a more complete clinical picture than considering risk factors alone.

  38. Q166. A patient with suicidal ideation is being considered for discharge with outpatient follow-up rather than admission. What safety planning element is essential before discharge?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A collaborative safety plan including means restriction, identified coping strategies, support contacts, and clear follow-up arrangements): A collaborative, structured safety plan addressing means restriction, coping strategies, support contacts, and concrete follow-up arrangements is an essential evidence-based component before discharging a patient with suicidal ideation, since a verbal promise alone (a no-suicide contract) has not been shown to be reliably protective. Providing only a crisis line number without broader planning is insufficient. Ensuring clear follow-up, rather than leaving arrangements to the patient alone, is a key feature of safe discharge planning.

  39. Q167. A patient who overdosed on a calcium channel blocker in a suicide attempt is bradycardic and hypotensive. What treatment should be considered in addition to standard supportive care?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (High-dose insulin euglycemic therapy, along with calcium and vasopressor support as needed): High-dose insulin euglycemic therapy, along with calcium administration and vasopressor support, is a recognized specific treatment for severe calcium channel blocker toxicity causing bradycardia and hypotension. Naloxone, flumazenil, and N-acetylcysteine are specific antidotes for entirely different toxidromes (opioid, benzodiazepine, and acetaminophen respectively) and would not address this cardiotoxicity. Recognizing the specific ingestion class guides appropriately targeted therapy beyond generic supportive care.

  40. Q168. A physician is documenting a suicide risk assessment in the emergency department chart. What is an important medicolegal principle to follow regarding documentation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Document the specific risk factors assessed, the clinical reasoning behind the disposition decision, and the safety plan discussed): Thorough documentation of the specific risk and protective factors assessed, the clinical reasoning supporting the disposition decision, and details of any safety plan is an important medicolegal principle that supports both good patient care and defensible practice. Recording only the final disposition without supporting reasoning would be inadequate documentation. Specific, detailed documentation is preferred over vague statements in this high-risk clinical scenario.

  41. Q169. A patient presents with suicidal ideation in the context of a first-episode major depressive episode with psychotic features, including command auditory hallucinations to harm themselves. What does this specific feature indicate about acute risk?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Command hallucinations directing self-harm significantly increase acute risk and warrant urgent psychiatric evaluation and likely admission): Command auditory hallucinations instructing self-harm represent a significant, well-recognized acute risk factor requiring urgent psychiatric evaluation and often inpatient admission, since compliance with such commands can occur unpredictably. Dismissing hallucinations as benign or unrelated to risk would be a dangerous underestimation. Psychotic depression is a recognized and serious clinical entity, not evidence against a genuine depressive illness.

  42. Q170. A patient who survived a serious suicide attempt expresses relief at having survived and states they no longer wish to die. What is an important principle regarding this statement in ongoing risk assessment?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Expressed relief can be reassuring but does not eliminate the need for careful follow-up assessment, since risk can fluctuate and this alone should not be the sole basis for discharge): Expressed relief after surviving an attempt is a genuinely positive and relevant clinical sign, but risk can still fluctuate over time, so it should be incorporated into, rather than serve as the sole basis for, a comprehensive ongoing assessment and safety plan before any discharge decision. Completely disregarding this statement would ignore meaningful clinical information. Basing discharge solely on one statement without broader assessment would be an incomplete and potentially unsafe approach.

Chapter 6: Trauma and Musculoskeletal Injury (57 Questions)

  1. Q171. A 28-year-old restrained driver is brought in after a high-speed collision. He has a linear bruise across his lower abdomen from the seatbelt and is tachycardic with diffuse abdominal tenderness. Primary survey is otherwise stable. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Obtain CT abdomen and pelvis or arrange laparotomy given the seatbelt sign): a seatbelt sign with tachycardia and abdominal tenderness after high-speed deceleration is strongly associated with hollow viscus and mesenteric injury. These injuries can be missed on initial exam and are notoriously difficult to detect with FAST alone. Prompt CT imaging or, if unstable, laparotomy is required to avoid delayed diagnosis of bowel perforation. Discharge or repeat exam alone risks missing a life-threatening intra-abdominal injury.

  2. Q172. A 35-year-old man arrives after a motorcycle crash with severe chest pain and shortness of breath. He is hypotensive, has distended neck veins, and absent breath sounds on the right with tracheal deviation to the left. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Perform immediate needle or finger thoracostomy decompression): the combination of hypotension, distended neck veins, absent unilateral breath sounds, and tracheal deviation is classic for tension pneumothorax, a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate decompression. Waiting for a chest x-ray delays a time-critical intervention and can lead to cardiovascular collapse. Antibiotics and fluid boluses do not address the underlying mechanical problem. Decompression should occur before any imaging in a decompensating patient.

  3. Q173. A 45-year-old pedestrian struck by a car at moderate speed arrives with an unstable pelvis on exam, significant tenderness, and bruising over the perineum. His heart rate is 128 beats per minute, blood pressure is 84/50, and he remains restless despite initial fluid administration. What is the most appropriate immediate intervention?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Apply a pelvic binder at the level of the greater trochanters): an unstable pelvic fracture with hemodynamic compromise requires immediate mechanical stabilization to reduce pelvic volume and control hemorrhage. Applying the binder at the level of the greater trochanters provides optimal compression of the pelvic ring. An internal exam or delaying stabilization for imaging can worsen ongoing hemorrhage. Definitive imaging and further management follow once the pelvis is stabilized.

  4. Q174. A 50-year-old man involved in a high-speed frontal collision arrives with a widened mediastinum noted on his supine chest x-ray. He is hemodynamically stable with a normal heart rate, no chest wall deformity, and no palpable subcutaneous emphysema on exam. What is the most appropriate next investigation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Obtain a CT angiogram of the chest to assess the aorta): a widened mediastinum after high-speed deceleration raises concern for blunt traumatic aortic injury, which requires definitive vascular imaging. CT angiography is the standard next step to confirm or exclude aortic injury in a stable patient. Repeating plain films or echocardiography alone lacks the sensitivity needed to rule out this life-threatening injury. Discharge would be inappropriate given the significant mechanism and imaging finding.

  5. Q175. A 24-year-old is alert, oriented, and fully ambulatory after a low-speed rear-end collision at a stop sign. He has no midline cervical tenderness, no distracting painful injury elsewhere, and can rotate his neck actively 45 degrees in each direction without any pain. What is the most appropriate management of his cervical spine?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Clinically clear the cervical spine without imaging per the Canadian C-Spine Rule): this patient meets low-risk criteria and demonstrates full range of motion without pain, allowing clinical clearance without imaging under the validated Canadian C-Spine Rule. Ordering CT or plain films in this low-risk group exposes the patient to unnecessary radiation and cost. Continued collar use and admission are unnecessary once the rule supports clearance. The rule is specifically designed to safely reduce unneeded cervical spine imaging in alert, stable patients.

  6. Q176. A hypotensive trauma patient with a heart rate of 130 beats per minute has a FAST exam positive for free fluid in Morison's pouch after a high-speed motor vehicle collision. He remains tachycardic and hypotensive despite two liters of crystalloid over twenty minutes. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Activate the operating room for emergent laparotomy): a positive FAST with ongoing hemodynamic instability despite fluid resuscitation indicates active intra-abdominal hemorrhage requiring emergent surgical control. Delaying with repeat imaging or discharge risks fatal hemorrhage. The FAST exam in this context is used to rapidly triage unstable patients directly to the operating room rather than for outpatient follow up. Continued fluid resuscitation alone will not control the source of bleeding.

  7. Q177. A 60-year-old man has a flail segment on the right chest wall with visible paradoxical movement after a fall from height at a construction site. His oxygen saturation is 88 percent on room air, his respiratory rate is 32 breaths per minute, and his distress is increasing. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Provide supplemental oxygen, analgesia, and prepare for possible intubation): flail chest with hypoxia and respiratory distress is managed with good analgesia, supplemental oxygen, and close monitoring for respiratory failure requiring intubation. External binders can worsen ventilation by restricting chest wall movement and are not recommended. Needle decompression is reserved for tension pneumothorax, not simple flail chest with pulmonary contusion. Adequate pain control improves ventilation and reduces the risk of secondary pneumonia.

  8. Q178. A 30-year-old man struck his head in a fall from a ladder at home and now has a Glasgow Coma Scale that dropped from 14 to 9 over one hour, along with a new dilated and sluggish right pupil. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Obtain an urgent CT head and consult neurosurgery immediately): a falling Glasgow Coma Scale with a new unilateral dilated pupil suggests an expanding intracranial mass lesion causing uncal herniation, a neurosurgical emergency. Immediate CT imaging and neurosurgical consultation are required to guide urgent decompression. Observation alone risks irreversible brain injury from ongoing herniation. MRI is not the appropriate initial modality in the acute trauma setting due to time and accessibility constraints.

  9. Q179. A 22-year-old football player has left flank pain and gross hematuria after receiving a direct blow to the flank during a tackle in a game. He is hemodynamically stable with normal vital signs and no other associated injuries identified on a thorough examination. What is the most appropriate next investigation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Obtain a CT abdomen and pelvis with intravenous contrast): gross hematuria after blunt flank trauma warrants CT imaging with contrast to evaluate for renal injury, which is the most sensitive and specific test available. Retrograde urethrogram is used for suspected urethral injury, not renal trauma. Plain x-ray does not adequately assess renal or retroperitoneal structures. Immediate laparotomy is unnecessary in a hemodynamically stable patient without other indications for surgery.

  10. Q180. A 26-year-old has an obvious knee dislocation after a high-speed motorcycle crash, with an absent dorsalis pedis pulse on the affected side. The limb below the knee is cool, pale, and has delayed capillary refill compared to the other leg. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Attempt immediate reduction and urgent vascular surgery consultation): knee dislocation carries a high risk of popliteal artery injury, and an absent pulse with a cool, pale limb indicates limb-threatening vascular compromise. Immediate reduction may restore perfusion, but urgent vascular surgery consultation is essential regardless of the response to reduction. Delaying care for imaging or outpatient follow up risks limb loss. Ice and elevation do not address the underlying vascular emergency.

  11. Q181. A 40-year-old stabbing victim has decreased breath sounds and dullness to percussion at the left lung base with hypotension and tachycardia. Chest x-ray shows a large left pleural effusion consistent with a significant hemothorax from the penetrating injury. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Insert a large-bore chest tube for drainage and monitor output): a large hemothorax with hypotension requires prompt chest tube drainage both to reexpand the lung and to quantify ongoing blood loss, which guides the decision for thoracotomy. Needle decompression is used for tension pneumothorax, not hemothorax. Conservative management with repeat imaging delays definitive drainage in an unstable patient. Discharge is clearly inappropriate given the significant chest injury and instability.

  12. Q182. A 33-year-old man has a stab wound to the left chest with hypotension, muffled heart sounds, and distended neck veins on examination. Bedside echocardiography confirms a moderate pericardial fluid collection with early signs of right ventricular collapse. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Perform pericardiocentesis or arrange emergent thoracotomy): Beck's triad with confirmed pericardial fluid on echocardiography after penetrating chest trauma indicates cardiac tamponade, a rapidly fatal condition requiring immediate decompression. Oxygen alone and further imaging delay life-saving intervention. A large fluid bolus may provide temporary benefit but does not treat the underlying tamponade physiology. Emergent pericardiocentesis or thoracotomy is required without delay in this unstable patient.

  13. Q183. A 29-year-old woman who is 32 weeks pregnant is involved in a low-speed motor vehicle collision and is hemodynamically stable with a normal primary survey and no vaginal bleeding. She reports normal fetal movement since the accident occurred. What is the most appropriate additional management specific to her pregnancy?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Position her in left lateral tilt and arrange fetal monitoring): in a pregnant trauma patient beyond 20 weeks, positioning in left lateral tilt relieves aortocaval compression and improves venous return, and fetal monitoring is required to detect placental abruption or fetal distress. Supine positioning can precipitate maternal hypotension. Imaging should not be delayed when clinically indicated, as fetal risk from undiagnosed maternal injury outweighs radiation concerns. Immediate discharge without fetal monitoring risks missing a significant obstetric complication.

  14. Q184. A 38-year-old trauma patient remains hypotensive with a blood pressure of 82/48 and tachycardic despite two liters of crystalloid, with ongoing visible hemorrhage from an open pelvic injury sustained in a fall from height. What is the most appropriate next step in management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Activate the massive transfusion protocol with balanced blood products): persistent hypotension despite crystalloid with ongoing visible hemorrhage indicates hemorrhagic shock requiring early balanced blood product resuscitation rather than continued crystalloid. Massive transfusion protocols provide rapid, coordinated delivery of red cells, plasma, and platelets in balanced ratios. Waiting for a full crossmatch delays life-saving treatment in an emergency. A single unit of red cells is inadequate for ongoing major hemorrhage.

  15. Q185. A trauma patient involved in a rollover motor vehicle collision has completed a primary survey with no life threats identified and is now hemodynamically stable with a patent airway and clear breath sounds bilaterally. What is the most appropriate next step in his overall trauma assessment?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Perform a thorough secondary survey including a log roll to inspect the back): after the primary survey addresses immediate life threats, a complete secondary survey including a log roll is essential to identify missed injuries such as posterior wounds, spinal deformity, or penetrating trauma. Skipping this step risks overlooking significant injuries. Repeating only airway and breathing assessment is insufficient once the primary survey is complete. Transfer or discharge before a full secondary survey is premature in trauma care.

  16. Q186. A 20-year-old falls onto an outstretched hand while skateboarding and has focal tenderness in the anatomical snuffbox on the radial side of the wrist. Initial wrist x-rays in all standard views are read as normal with no visible fracture line. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Immobilize in a thumb spica splint and arrange repeat imaging in ten to fourteen days): scaphoid fractures are frequently occult on initial x-ray due to their blood supply and fracture pattern, so clinical suspicion with snuffbox tenderness warrants splinting despite normal imaging. Repeat imaging or MRI at follow up confirms or excludes the fracture. Discharging without immobilization risks avascular necrosis if an occult fracture is missed. Surgical referral without further imaging is premature without a confirmed fracture.

  17. Q187. A 68-year-old woman falls on an outstretched hand while walking her dog and has a dorsally angulated distal radius fracture with obvious deformity, marked swelling, and intact neurovascular status distally. She rates her pain as nine out of ten. What is the most appropriate initial management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Perform closed reduction under hematoma block and apply a sugar-tong splint): a displaced, angulated distal radius fracture with intact neurovascular status is typically managed with closed reduction and splinting in the emergency department, followed by orthopedic follow up. A simple sling without reduction leaves the deformity uncorrected. Discharge without any reduction or immobilization risks malunion and functional impairment. Open reduction internal fixation is not the initial emergency department management for most closed distal radius fractures.

  18. Q188. A 6-year-old falls from monkey bars at the playground and has a swollen, deformed elbow with severe pain, an absent radial pulse, and cool, pale fingers on the affected side. His mother reports he was previously healthy with no prior injuries. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Urgently consult orthopedics for reduction given the vascular compromise): a supracondylar fracture with an absent pulse and a cool, pale hand indicates vascular compromise requiring urgent orthopedic reduction to restore perfusion. Delaying with splinting alone or reassurance risks permanent limb ischemia and Volkmann contracture. Outpatient MRI is inappropriate given the acute vascular emergency. Prompt intervention is required to prevent irreversible neurovascular damage in this pediatric injury.

  19. Q189. A 25-year-old twists his ankle while landing awkwardly during a basketball game and can bear weight for four steps in triage. He has tenderness only over the anterior talofibular ligament with no bony tenderness at either malleolus. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Manage as a soft tissue sprain without ankle x-ray per the Ottawa Ankle Rules): this patient meets the Ottawa Ankle Rules criteria for no imaging, as he can bear weight and has no bony tenderness at the malleoli, with tenderness isolated to the ligament. Ordering an x-ray in this low-risk scenario is unnecessary and increases cost and radiation exposure. Casting is not indicated for an isolated ligament sprain without fracture. Orthopedic referral is unnecessary for an uncomplicated ankle sprain.

  20. Q190. A 19-year-old punches a wall in anger and has tenderness and swelling over the fifth metacarpal neck, with 30 degrees of angulation seen on x-ray but no rotational deformity noted on careful clinical exam. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Apply an ulnar gutter splint and arrange orthopedic follow up): boxer's fractures with acceptable angulation and no rotational deformity are managed nonoperatively with an ulnar gutter splint and outpatient follow up. Surgical fixation is reserved for significant rotational deformity or unacceptable angulation. Discharging without any immobilization risks further displacement and pain. A full arm cast is unnecessarily restrictive for this distal metacarpal injury.

  21. Q191. A 30-year-old has a tibial shaft fracture from a skiing accident and now reports severe pain out of proportion to the injury, with pain on passive stretch of the toes and a tense, swollen calf compartment on palpation. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Urgently measure compartment pressures and prepare for emergent fasciotomy): pain out of proportion to injury with pain on passive stretch is an early and reliable sign of compartment syndrome, a surgical emergency requiring prompt pressure measurement and fasciotomy if confirmed. Elevation and increased analgesia can mask worsening ischemia and delay diagnosis. Doppler ultrasound is not the appropriate diagnostic tool for compartment syndrome. Delayed recognition risks irreversible muscle and nerve damage.

  22. Q192. A 16-year-old falls off his bicycle onto his shoulder while riding to school and has a midshaft clavicle fracture with mild displacement on x-ray but intact overlying skin and completely normal neurovascular status. He is otherwise well and in no significant distress. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Manage with a simple sling and outpatient orthopedic follow up): most midshaft clavicle fractures, even with mild displacement, are managed nonoperatively with a simple sling for comfort and outpatient orthopedic follow up. Rigid figure-of-eight braces have not been shown to improve outcomes and cause discomfort. Emergent surgical fixation is reserved for severely displaced, open, or neurovascularly compromised fractures. A shoulder spica cast is not standard management for this injury.

  23. Q193. A 22-year-old presents to the emergency department after a fall with the arm held in slight abduction and external rotation, unable to move the shoulder actively, with exam findings consistent with an anterior dislocation. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Perform procedural sedation and closed reduction with axillary nerve checks before and after): anterior shoulder dislocations are managed with procedural sedation and closed reduction, with careful documentation of axillary nerve function before and after the procedure since this nerve is commonly injured. Discharging for outpatient reduction delays relief and risks worsening neurovascular injury. MRI is not required before an urgent reduction attempt. Surgical referral without an attempt at closed reduction is not first-line management.

  24. Q194. An 82-year-old woman falls at home and now has a shortened, externally rotated leg with significant groin pain and a complete inability to bear weight, with an intertrochanteric hip fracture confirmed on x-ray. What is the most appropriate management approach?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Provide analgesia such as a nerve block and arrange early surgical fixation): hip fractures in elderly patients are associated with significant morbidity and mortality, and current evidence supports early analgesia, such as a fascia iliaca nerve block, along with early surgical fixation to reduce complications of prolonged immobility. Bed rest for weeks increases risks of pneumonia, thromboembolism, and further deconditioning. Discharge with crutches is inappropriate for a confirmed fracture. Hip spica casting is not standard treatment for adult hip fractures.

  25. Q195. A 35-year-old has an open tibial fracture with a visible bone fragment protruding through a laceration after a fall from a ladder at a work site. The wound is heavily contaminated with dirt and debris. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Administer intravenous antibiotics, update tetanus status, and arrange urgent surgical debridement): open fractures require prompt intravenous antibiotics, tetanus prophylaxis as indicated, and urgent surgical debridement to reduce the risk of infection and osteomyelitis. Primary closure in the emergency department is contraindicated for contaminated open fractures. Splinting alone with oral antibiotics is insufficient management for this injury. Delaying antibiotics increases infection risk and should not wait for surgical availability.

  26. Q196. A 45-year-old recreational athlete feels a sudden pop in his calf while playing tennis and now cannot plantarflex his foot against resistance, with a palpable gap in the tendon and a positive Thompson test on exam. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Immobilize in plantarflexion and refer for orthopedic follow up): a positive Thompson test with inability to plantarflex against resistance confirms an Achilles tendon rupture, which is initially immobilized in slight plantarflexion pending orthopedic evaluation for definitive management. Reassuring the patient as a mild strain risks worsening the injury with continued weight bearing. Encouraging activity can further retract the torn tendon ends. X-ray does not visualize tendon integrity and is not the definitive diagnostic test.

  27. Q197. A 17-year-old falls onto an outstretched hand during a football game and has an obviously deformed elbow with the forearm shortened, the olecranon prominent posteriorly, and significant swelling around the entire joint. He reports severe pain and is holding the arm supported against his chest. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Assess neurovascular status and perform prompt closed reduction under sedation): elbow dislocations require careful neurovascular assessment, particularly of the ulnar and median nerves and brachial artery, followed by prompt closed reduction under procedural sedation to restore perfusion and reduce pain. Splinting in the deformed position or delaying reduction risks ongoing neurovascular compromise. MRI is unnecessary before an urgent reduction attempt. A sling alone does not address the dislocation requiring reduction.

  28. Q198. A 50-year-old presents with a hot, swollen, exquisitely tender knee, subjective fever, and inability to bear weight after what he describes as a minor fall at home, raising concern beyond a simple fracture. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Perform joint aspiration for synovial fluid analysis and culture): a hot, swollen, tender joint with fever and inability to bear weight raises concern for septic arthritis, which must be evaluated with joint aspiration for cell count, gram stain, and culture before management decisions are finalized. Immobilization alone or x-ray without aspiration risks missing a joint infection requiring urgent treatment. Starting antibiotics without obtaining fluid first can compromise culture yield and diagnostic accuracy. Prompt aspiration is essential given the significant morbidity of untreated septic arthritis.

  29. Q199. A 2-year-old refuses to bear weight on one leg after a minor fall while playing at daycare, with a subtle nondisplaced spiral fracture of the tibia seen on x-ray and no other injuries identified on examination. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Immobilize in a long leg cast or splint as a toddler's fracture): a nondisplaced spiral tibial fracture in a young child with a plausible minor fall mechanism is consistent with a toddler's fracture, a benign injury pattern managed with immobilization. Automatically investigating for abuse is not warranted when the mechanism and fracture pattern are consistent, though clinicians should remain alert to inconsistent findings. Surgical fixation is not required for this stable, nondisplaced fracture pattern. Discharging without any immobilization risks displacement and prolonged pain.

  30. Q200. A 24-year-old cuts his palm on broken glass and has a laceration overlying the flexor tendon sheath at the base of his finger. Sensation is intact but he has weakness in flexion at the distal interphalangeal joint. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Explore the wound for tendon injury and refer for surgical repair): weakness in distal interphalangeal flexion with a laceration over the flexor tendon sheath suggests a partial or complete flexor tendon injury requiring wound exploration and surgical referral. Simply closing the skin without exploration risks missing a significant tendon injury that worsens with delayed repair. A simple dressing alone does not address the suspected tendon injury. X-ray is not useful for evaluating soft tissue tendon integrity.

  31. Q201. A 30-year-old sustains a laceration over the fourth metacarpophalangeal joint after punching another person in the mouth during an altercation. The wound is visibly contaminated with saliva and located directly over the joint space. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Leave the wound open, irrigate thoroughly, and start prophylactic antibiotics): a clenched-fist human bite injury over a joint carries a high risk of deep infection and joint contamination, so the wound should be left open, irrigated thoroughly, and treated with prophylactic antibiotics covering oral flora. Primary closure of this type of contaminated wound increases the risk of abscess formation. Skin adhesive is inappropriate for a contaminated bite wound near a joint. Withholding antibiotics ignores the well-established infection risk of these injuries.

  32. Q202. A 55-year-old presents with a deep, dirty puncture wound to the foot from stepping on a rusty nail while gardening and cannot recall the date of his last tetanus vaccination. The wound is contaminated with soil and appears several hours old. What is the most appropriate management regarding tetanus prophylaxis?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Administer both tetanus toxoid and tetanus immunoglobulin given the uncertain history): a deep, dirty puncture wound is tetanus-prone, and with an uncertain immunization history, both tetanus toxoid and tetanus immunoglobulin should be given to provide both active and passive protection. Withholding prophylaxis for a small wound ignores the significant tetanus risk of contaminated puncture wounds. Waiting until infection develops is far too late, as tetanus prophylaxis is preventive. Immunoglobulin alone without toxoid does not establish lasting active immunity.

  33. Q203. A 20-year-old has a laceration through the margin of the upper eyelid after falling onto a metal railing at a construction site, with the wound extending close to the lacrimal punctum. There is mild surrounding bruising but no globe injury. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Refer to ophthalmology or plastic surgery for specialized lid margin repair): lacerations involving the eyelid margin require precise realignment by a specialist to avoid notching, malposition, or damage to the lacrimal drainage system. Simple sutures or adhesive placed by a non-specialist risk permanent cosmetic and functional deformity. Discharging with only a patch and no formal repair leaves the injury inadequately treated. Specialized techniques are needed to properly align the delicate eyelid margin structures.

  34. Q204. A 40-year-old presents 30 hours after sustaining a linear laceration on his forearm from a kitchen knife accident. The wound edges are not devitalized and there is no obvious purulence, but the significant delay raises infection concern. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Irrigate thoroughly and consider delayed closure or healing by secondary intention): wounds presenting more than approximately 18 to 24 hours after injury have an increased risk of infection if closed primarily, so thorough irrigation with delayed closure or secondary intention healing is generally preferred. Closing regardless of the time delay increases the risk of wound infection and dehiscence. Skin adhesive is not appropriate for a wound with elevated infection risk. Referring only after infection develops misses the opportunity for appropriate initial management.

  35. Q205. A 26-year-old has a laceration crossing the vermillion border of the lower lip after falling face-first onto a concrete step, with the wound edges slightly separated and mild ongoing oozing. What is the most important initial step in repairing this laceration?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Place the first suture precisely at the vermillion border to align it): the vermillion border is a critical cosmetic landmark, and even a slight step-off is highly noticeable, so the first suture should precisely realign this border before the remainder of the laceration is closed. Closing the mucosal side first without first aligning the border risks a visible cosmetic mismatch. Adhesive alone does not provide adequate realignment for a laceration crossing this landmark. Delaying closure for swelling to resolve increases infection risk and is not standard practice.

  36. Q206. A 35-year-old mechanic presents with a small, seemingly minor puncture wound on his index finger from an accidental discharge of a high-pressure grease gun, with mild swelling and pain clearly out of proportion to the wound size. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Urgently refer for surgical exploration and debridement given the injection mechanism): high-pressure injection injuries can cause extensive deep tissue damage along fascial planes despite a deceptively minor external wound, requiring urgent surgical exploration and debridement. Simple suturing of the puncture site fails to address the extensive underlying injury. Discharge with oral antibiotics alone significantly underestimates the severity of this injury. The external appearance of the wound does not reflect the extent of internal tissue damage.

  37. Q207. A 28-year-old steps on a broken beer bottle at the beach and has a plantar foot laceration with a persistent sensation of something still lodged inside despite thorough visual inspection of the wound. What is the most appropriate next step before wound closure?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Obtain an x-ray or ultrasound to evaluate for a retained foreign body): glass fragments are often radiopaque and can be identified on plain x-ray, or with ultrasound if not visualized on x-ray, and this should be done before wound closure when a retained foreign body is suspected. Closing the wound without evaluating for a retained fragment risks ongoing infection, pain, or migration of the foreign body. A pressure dressing alone does not address a suspected retained object. Discharge without evaluation ignores the patient's specific concern of a retained foreign body.

  38. Q208. A 33-year-old requires repair of a deep finger laceration sustained while slicing vegetables, and local anesthesia is planned using a digital nerve block at the base of the affected finger before exploration and closure. What is the most appropriate anesthetic approach to use?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Use plain lidocaine without epinephrine for the digital block): standard teaching for digital nerve blocks has traditionally recommended plain lidocaine without epinephrine due to concern for vasoconstriction compromising blood supply to the digit. High-dose epinephrine or epinephrine-containing bupivacaine at the digit base raises theoretical concern for digital ischemia in this teaching. Avoiding local anesthesia altogether and using general sedation is unnecessary and inappropriate for a simple finger laceration repair.

  39. Q209. A 19-year-old has a deep laceration over the cheek near the angle of the mandible after falling onto broken glass at a party. Clear fluid is noted draining from the wound when he is asked to chew gum. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Assess for parotid duct injury and refer to a specialist for repair): a deep cheek laceration near the angle of the mandible with clear fluid production during chewing raises strong concern for parotid duct injury, which requires specialist assessment and repair to prevent salivary fistula or sialocele. Standard layered closure in the emergency department risks missing and worsening a ductal injury. Assuming the fluid is normal saliva without further evaluation overlooks a significant structural injury. Skin adhesive is inadequate for a wound with suspected deep structure involvement.

  40. Q210. A 45-year-old presents with a small cat bite puncture wound to the dorsum of the hand that appears clean with only minimal surrounding swelling and no visible discharge four hours after the bite occurred. What is the most appropriate management given the type of bite?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Start prophylactic antibiotics given the high infection risk of cat bites): cat bites, due to their narrow, deep puncture wounds and inoculation of oral flora, carry a particularly high infection risk and warrant prophylactic antibiotics regardless of the wound's initial appearance. Primary closure of puncture wounds from bites is generally avoided due to infection risk. Waiting for visible infection before treating ignores the well-documented high infection rate of cat bites. Assuming no antibiotics are needed based on appearance alone underestimates this specific bite risk.

  41. Q211. A 29-year-old has a deep laceration over the anterior shin with a fragment of visible bone at the base of the wound after a bicycle accident on a gravel trail. There is only minimal surrounding soft tissue swelling. What is the most appropriate management approach?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Manage as an open fracture with antibiotics, tetanus update, and orthopedic consultation): visible bone at the base of a laceration indicates an open fracture, which requires the same management as any open fracture, including antibiotics, tetanus prophylaxis, and orthopedic consultation, rather than treatment as a simple skin laceration. Closing the skin alone with oral antibiotics fails to address the underlying open fracture appropriately. A simple dressing without antibiotics or orthopedic involvement significantly undertreats this injury. Referral only for cosmetic skin closure ignores the underlying bony injury.

  42. Q212. A 50-year-old has a scalp laceration with brisk, difficult-to-control bleeding after a fall down a flight of stairs, representing a significant mechanism of head trauma with a brief loss of consciousness reported by a witness. What is the most appropriate initial management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Apply direct pressure to control bleeding and assess for associated head injury): scalp lacerations can bleed briskly due to the rich vascular supply, so initial management includes direct pressure for hemostasis alongside assessment for underlying skull fracture or intracranial injury given the significant mechanism. A tight circumferential bandage risks compromising circulation and does not substitute for proper wound assessment. Closing the wound before assessing for head injury risks missing a serious underlying injury. Discharge before bleeding is controlled and head injury is assessed is premature.

  43. Q213. A 27-year-old has a small laceration over the dorsum of the wrist with full passive range of motion of all fingers noted on exam, but the mechanism of injury involved a sharp kitchen blade during an altercation. What is the most appropriate examination step?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Test tendon function against resistance through full range of motion): partial tendon lacerations can allow preserved passive range of motion while still having significantly reduced strength against resistance, so active testing against resistance is essential to avoid missing a partial tendon injury. Assuming an intact tendon based on passive motion alone risks missing a partial laceration that can progress to complete rupture. X-ray does not assess tendon integrity. Deferring tendon assessment to follow up risks a missed injury with a sharp mechanism.

  44. Q214. A 30-year-old sustains 40 percent total body surface area burns in a workplace explosion approximately two hours before arrival to the emergency department. The team begins calculating intravenous fluid resuscitation using the standard Parkland formula. What is the most appropriate way to time the fluid administration?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Calculate the 24 hours from the time of injury, giving half in the first eight hours from injury): the Parkland formula calculates fluid requirements from the time of injury, not the time of hospital arrival, with half the calculated volume given in the first eight hours from injury and the remainder over the following sixteen hours. Calculating from arrival time can lead to under-resuscitation if there was a delay in presentation. Giving the entire calculated volume as a single bolus risks fluid overload and pulmonary edema. Delaying resuscitation until oliguria develops ignores the preventive purpose of early fluid calculation.

  45. Q215. A 45-year-old is rescued from a burning house with visible facial soot, singed nasal hairs, and a notably hoarse voice, though he is currently breathing comfortably without stridor or significant respiratory distress. His oxygen saturation is currently 97 percent on room air. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Proceed with early intubation before airway edema progresses further): signs such as facial soot, singed nasal hairs, and hoarseness indicate possible inhalation injury, and airway edema can progress rapidly, so early intubation is recommended before the airway becomes difficult or impossible to secure. Waiting for respiratory distress to develop risks a failed airway once edema has progressed significantly. Humidified oxygen alone does not address the risk of impending airway obstruction. Discharge instructions are inappropriate given the significant inhalation injury risk indicators present.

  46. Q216. A 38-year-old has a full-thickness circumferential burn to the forearm sustained in an industrial accident, with absent distal pulses on Doppler exam and worsening paresthesia and pain in the fingers over the past hour. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Perform an escharotomy to relieve the constricting eschar): a circumferential full-thickness burn causing vascular compromise, evidenced by absent pulses and worsening paresthesia, requires escharotomy to relieve the constricting, non-compliant eschar and restore perfusion. Elevation or compression bandaging does not address the mechanical constriction causing ischemia. Additional intravenous fluids alone do not relieve the compartment-like pressure from the eschar. Delayed intervention risks irreversible limb ischemia and loss of function.

  47. Q217. A 26-year-old spills an industrial alkali cleaning chemical on his forearm at work and presents with significant pain, skin discoloration, and a slippery texture to the skin at the site of contact. His coworkers brought him in immediately after the incident occurred. What is the most appropriate initial management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Remove contaminated clothing and irrigate copiously with water): chemical burns are managed with prompt removal of contaminated clothing followed by copious water irrigation to dilute and remove the offending agent, continuing for an extended period depending on the chemical. Neutralizing agents can generate an exothermic reaction and worsen tissue damage, so they should never be applied. Delaying irrigation for transport prolongs chemical contact and tissue injury. Ice is not appropriate for chemical burn management and does not address ongoing chemical exposure.

  48. Q218. A 40-year-old man rescued from a house fire reports headache, nausea, and mild confusion, with a normal pulse oximetry reading of 99 percent on room air. Carbon monoxide poisoning is strongly suspected given the significant exposure history. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Administer 100 percent high-flow oxygen despite the normal pulse oximetry): standard pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin and will read falsely normal in carbon monoxide poisoning, so treatment with high-flow oxygen should be based on clinical suspicion and history of exposure rather than the oximetry reading. Withholding oxygen based on a falsely reassuring reading risks ongoing hypoxic tissue injury. A chest x-ray does not assess for carbon monoxide toxicity. Discharge based on symptomatic improvement alone risks missing significant carboxyhemoglobin elevation requiring further monitoring.

  49. Q219. A 33-year-old has scattered small burns in several areas after a grease fire. Estimating the exact percentage using the rule of nines is difficult due to the scattered pattern. What is the most appropriate method to estimate total body surface area affected?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Use the patient's palm, including fingers, as approximately one percent of body surface area): the palmar surface method, using the patient's own palm including fingers as roughly one percent of total body surface area, is a practical and validated method for estimating scattered or irregularly distributed burns where the rule of nines is less accurate. Assuming a fixed arbitrary percentage does not reflect the actual burn distribution. Using the rule of nines alone while ignoring small scattered patches underestimates total burn area. Imaging is not used to calculate burn surface area.

  50. Q220. A 24-year-old spills hot coffee on her forearm while cooking dinner, resulting in a red, moist, blistered area that is exquisitely painful to light touch, covering approximately 3 percent of her total body surface area. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Clean the wound, apply an appropriate dressing, and provide analgesia with outpatient follow up): a superficial partial-thickness burn covering a small total body surface area is managed as an outpatient with wound cleaning, an appropriate dressing, adequate analgesia, and follow up rather than admission. Parkland formula resuscitation is reserved for larger burns typically exceeding an established threshold of total body surface area. Escharotomy is not indicated for a superficial partial-thickness burn without circumferential involvement or vascular compromise. Withholding analgesia is inappropriate given the significant pain associated with partial-thickness burns.

  51. Q221. A 35-year-old electrician sustains a high-voltage electrical injury while working on a power line, with small entry and exit wound burns visible on the hand and foot, though he now reports significant diffuse muscle pain. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Obtain cardiac monitoring and screen for rhabdomyolysis given the electrical mechanism): high-voltage electrical injuries can cause significant internal tissue damage, cardiac arrhythmias, and rhabdomyolysis despite deceptively small external entry and exit wounds, so cardiac monitoring and laboratory screening are essential. Treating only the visible wounds significantly underestimates the potential for deep tissue and cardiac injury. Discharge once external wounds are dressed ignores the risk of delayed arrhythmia or renal injury from myoglobinuria. A chest x-ray alone does not adequately screen for these electrical injury complications.

  52. Q222. A 50-year-old has a full-thickness burn involving the entire palm of the hand and extending across the volar wrist joint after grabbing a hot pan handle while cooking dinner at home. He reports significant pain and reduced grip strength since the injury. What is the most appropriate disposition?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Arrange transfer to a specialized burn center given the location and depth): full-thickness burns involving the hands, joints, face, feet, or genitalia meet established criteria for transfer to a specialized burn center regardless of the total body surface area involved. Discharge or general ward admission does not provide the specialized wound care and rehabilitation these injuries require. Outpatient management with only topical dressings is insufficient for a full-thickness burn involving a functionally critical area. Early specialized care improves both functional and cosmetic outcomes for these high-risk burn locations.

  53. Q223. A 42-year-old road worker sustains a burn from hot asphalt tar adhering firmly to the skin of his forearm during a paving project on a hot summer afternoon. Coworkers immediately called for emergency medical assistance after the incident occurred. What is the most appropriate initial management of the adherent tar?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Cool the tar with water first, then remove it gently using mineral oil): adherent tar burns are managed by first cooling the tar with water to stop the ongoing thermal injury, followed by gentle removal using a petroleum-based solvent such as mineral oil rather than forceful peeling. Forcibly peeling the tar off immediately can avulse the underlying skin and worsen the wound. Leaving the tar in place permanently prevents proper assessment and treatment of the burn beneath it. Applying direct heat would worsen the thermal injury rather than aid removal.

  54. Q224. A 3-year-old presents to the emergency department with a sharply demarcated, symmetric stocking-pattern burn to both feet with no splash marks noted, and the history provided by the caregiver is inconsistent with the observed injury pattern. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Suspect non-accidental injury and initiate a child protection assessment): a sharply demarcated, symmetric stocking-pattern burn without splash marks combined with an inconsistent history is a classic pattern suggestive of intentional immersion injury, warranting a child protection assessment. Accepting an inconsistent explanation without further investigation risks failing to protect a child from ongoing abuse. Treating the burn alone without addressing the concerning pattern misses a critical safeguarding responsibility. Routine follow up alone does not fulfill the clinician's duty to investigate suspected non-accidental injury.

  55. Q225. A 29-year-old splashes an industrial cleaning chemical directly into his eye while working in a warehouse and presents immediately afterward with severe pain, tearing, and significantly blurred vision in the affected eye. He reports the chemical container had no visible safety label. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Begin immediate copious irrigation with saline before further examination): chemical eye injuries require immediate copious irrigation to dilute and remove the offending agent, as ongoing chemical exposure causes progressive tissue damage that outweighs the value of an initial visual acuity assessment. Performing visual acuity testing before irrigation unnecessarily delays a time-critical intervention. Topical antibiotic ointment does not address the acute chemical exposure. Patching without irrigation and delaying ophthalmology follow up risks worsening, potentially permanent, ocular damage.

  56. Q226. A 32-year-old with a well-documented sulfa allergy noted clearly in his chart sustains a partial-thickness burn to the dorsum of the hand while cooking at home. The burn is moderately painful with intact sensation throughout. What is the most appropriate topical dressing choice for his wound?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Use an alternative non-sulfa dressing given the documented allergy): silver sulfadiazine contains a sulfonamide component and should be avoided in patients with a documented sulfa allergy, requiring an alternative non-sulfa burn dressing instead. Applying it regardless of the allergy risks a significant allergic reaction. Assuming the allergy is unrelated to burn care is incorrect, as the cream itself contains the offending sulfa component. Delaying all dressing until allergy testing is arranged is unnecessary and delays appropriate wound care that can proceed safely with an alternative product.

  57. Q227. A 20-year-old presents with red, painful, non-blistered skin across both shoulders and upper back after several hours of prolonged sun exposure at the beach, with no evidence of blistering or skin breakdown on exam. What is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Provide supportive care with analgesia and moisturizing lotion): superficial, first-degree burns such as uncomplicated sunburn without blistering are managed conservatively with analgesia, moisturizing lotion, and supportive care rather than burn-specific dressings or fluid resuscitation. Silver sulfadiazine is reserved for partial-thickness burns with blistering or skin breakdown, not intact superficial burns. Parkland formula calculations apply to larger, deeper burns, not a limited superficial sunburn. Burn center admission is not indicated for a superficial burn without blistering or significant surface area involvement.

Chapter 7: Abdominal and Gastrointestinal Emergencies (29 Questions)

  1. Q228. A 74-year-old man arrives by ambulance with sudden severe back and abdominal pain, a pulsatile abdominal mass on palpation, and a systolic blood pressure of 78 mmHg despite two liters of crystalloid. He is diaphoretic, confused, and appears critically unwell. Which is the most appropriate immediate next step in management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Activate vascular surgery for emergent operative repair without delaying for CT): A hemodynamically unstable patient with a pulsatile mass and flank pain has a ruptured AAA until proven otherwise. CT wastes critical time and risks arrest in transit. Bedside ultrasound can confirm the aneurysm at the bedside, but definitive management is immediate surgical intervention. Antihypertensives are inappropriate in a hypotensive, shocked patient.

  2. Q229. A 27-year-old woman with a positive urine pregnancy test presents with six weeks of amenorrhea, right lower quadrant pain, and light vaginal spotting for one day. Her vital signs are within normal limits and her abdominal exam shows only mild focal tenderness. Which investigation should be prioritized to evaluate for ectopic pregnancy?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Quantitative beta-hCG paired with transvaginal pelvic ultrasound imaging): This combination is the standard first-line workup for suspected ectopic pregnancy in a stable patient. Transvaginal ultrasound can localize an intrauterine or adnexal pregnancy, while the quantitative beta-hCG helps interpret findings relative to the discriminatory zone. CT is inappropriate due to radiation exposure in early pregnancy, and laparoscopy is reserved for confirmed or unstable cases.

  3. Q230. A 15-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with sudden severe left testicular pain that began 90 minutes ago, associated with nausea and vomiting. Examination reveals a high-riding, horizontally oriented testicle with an absent cremasteric reflex and a swollen, tender scrotum. Which action should occur first?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Emergent urology consultation for surgical exploration without awaiting imaging): Classic history and exam findings for testicular torsion warrant immediate surgical exploration since testicular viability falls sharply after six hours. Waiting for Doppler ultrasound in a clear clinical presentation risks unnecessary delay and loss of the testicle. Manual detorsion can be attempted while awaiting the surgeon but does not replace definitive surgical fixation.

  4. Q231. A 22-year-old man reports periumbilical pain that migrated to the right lower quadrant over the past 18 hours, now accompanied by anorexia, a low-grade fever of 38.1°C, nausea, and localized rebound tenderness at McBurney point, along with a mildly elevated white blood cell count. What is the most appropriate next step?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Surgical consultation guided by clinical findings and abdominal CT imaging): This presentation is classic for acute appendicitis, and CT abdomen and pelvis with contrast confirms the diagnosis and guides surgical planning when the clinical picture is not unequivocal. Discharge without surgical involvement risks perforation. Antibiotics alone are only appropriate in select uncomplicated cases chosen jointly with surgery, not as an emergency department default.

  5. Q232. A 45-year-old woman with known gallstones presents with 12 hours of constant right upper quadrant pain radiating to the right shoulder, a temperature of 38.4°C, mild nausea, and a positive Murphy sign elicited during deep palpation on examination. Which imaging modality is the appropriate first-line investigation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Right upper quadrant ultrasound to assess the gallbladder and bile ducts): Ultrasound is the first-line test for suspected acute cholecystitis, showing gallstones, wall thickening, pericholecystic fluid, and a sonographic Murphy sign. It is fast, radiation-free, and widely available in the emergency department. HIDA scan and MRCP are reserved for equivocal ultrasound findings or suspected choledocholithiasis, not as initial tests.

  6. Q233. An 80-year-old woman with a history of atrial fibrillation on anticoagulation presents with severe abdominal pain that is markedly disproportionate to a soft, benign-appearing abdominal exam, along with persistent vomiting and one recent episode of bloody diarrhea. Which diagnosis must be prioritized in her workup?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Acute mesenteric ischemia from embolic occlusion requiring urgent CT angiography): Pain out of proportion to exam findings, atrial fibrillation as an embolic source, and bloody diarrhea are classic red flags for mesenteric ischemia. CT angiography is the investigation of choice and must not be delayed, since bowel infarction and mortality rise rapidly with time. The other diagnoses do not explain this high-risk symptom cluster.

  7. Q234. A 19-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with two days of diffuse abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, and deep rapid breathing, along with a fruity odor on her breath. Her abdomen is mildly tender diffusely without any peritoneal signs on examination. Which bedside test should be obtained immediately?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Point-of-care glucose and capillary or serum ketone measurement right away): Abdominal pain is a common presenting symptom of diabetic ketoacidosis, and Kussmaul breathing along with the patient's diabetic history should prompt immediate glucose and ketone testing. Missing DKA by anchoring on a primary gastrointestinal cause delays critical fluid and insulin therapy. Imaging and other labs can follow once DKA is excluded or confirmed.

  8. Q235. A 24-year-old woman presents with sudden onset severe left lower quadrant pain, nausea, and vomiting that began abruptly one hour ago, with a known 6 cm ovarian cyst identified on prior imaging. Pelvic exam reveals significant adnexal tenderness on the left side. Which test best evaluates for ovarian torsion?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Pelvic ultrasound with Doppler flow assessment of the affected ovary): Ultrasound with Doppler is the standard investigation for suspected ovarian torsion, evaluating ovarian size, cyst characteristics, and blood flow, though normal flow does not fully exclude intermittent torsion. Gynecology should be involved promptly given the risk of ovarian loss. CA-125 and diagnostic peritoneal lavage have no role in this acute evaluation.

  9. Q236. A 58-year-old man presents with two days of left lower quadrant pain, a low-grade fever of 37.9°C, and mild leukocytosis on laboratory testing, but with no peritoneal signs, guarding, or systemic toxicity on examination. Which management approach is most appropriate for this uncomplicated presentation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (CT abdomen and pelvis with contrast, then outpatient oral antibiotics if uncomplicated): CT confirms the diagnosis and excludes complications such as abscess or perforation. Uncomplicated diverticulitis in a reliable patient without systemic toxicity can often be managed with oral antibiotics and outpatient follow-up. Surgery and inpatient intravenous antibiotics are reserved for complicated or severe cases, and discharge without any workup risks missing a complication.

  10. Q237. A 62-year-old man on chronic NSAIDs presents with sudden severe diffuse abdominal pain that began two hours ago and a rigid, board-like abdomen on palpation. An upright chest radiograph shows free air beneath the right hemidiaphragm. What is the most appropriate next step in his management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Immediate surgical consultation for likely perforated viscus and operative repair): Free air on an upright film in a patient with peritonitis confirms perforation and mandates urgent surgical involvement, along with fluid resuscitation and broad-spectrum antibiotics. Further imaging or medical trials would only delay definitive operative management in an unstable surgical abdomen. Discharge is contraindicated with these findings.

  11. Q238. A 50-year-old woman presents with epigastric pain radiating to the back that began after a large meal, along with vomiting and a lipase level five times the upper limit of normal. Right upper quadrant ultrasound shows gallstones without any common bile duct dilation. What is the most appropriate initial management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation with supportive care and close monitoring): Gallstone pancreatitis without evidence of cholangitis or ductal obstruction is managed with fluid resuscitation, analgesia, and supportive care, with cholecystectomy addressed after recovery. ERCP is reserved for concurrent cholangitis or persistent biliary obstruction, not routine gallstone pancreatitis. Antibiotics are not indicated unless infected necrosis or cholangitis is present.

  12. Q239. A 70-year-old woman with a prior abdominal surgery presents with two days of crampy abdominal pain, progressive distension, obstipation, and bilious vomiting. Abdominal X-ray shows multiple dilated small bowel loops with air-fluid levels and no free air. Which management step is most appropriate at this time?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Nasogastric decompression with intravenous fluids and CT abdomen imaging): Adhesive small bowel obstruction is managed with bowel rest, nasogastric decompression, fluid resuscitation, and CT imaging to assess severity and look for signs of ischemia or complete obstruction. Oral intake would worsen distension and vomiting. Surgical consultation is warranted if there are signs of strangulation or failure to improve with conservative measures.

  13. Q240. A 30-year-old woman reports she uses condoms consistently and had a normal menstrual period three weeks ago, then presents to the emergency department with new lower abdominal pain and mild vaginal spotting. Which practice is recommended regardless of her stated contraceptive history and reported cycle?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Obtain a urine or serum beta-hCG test in all women of reproductive age): Contraceptive history and reported menstrual dates are unreliable for excluding pregnancy, and missing an ectopic pregnancy has severe consequences. Every woman of reproductive age presenting with abdominal pain should have a pregnancy test regardless of her stated history. This is a fundamental safety practice in emergency abdominal pain assessment.

  14. Q241. An 85-year-old man presents with several days of mild, poorly localized abdominal discomfort and normal vital signs, but his family reports he has been eating less, sleeping more, and is more confused than his usual baseline over the past two days. Which principle should guide his emergency assessment?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Elderly patients have blunted pain and peritoneal signs, warranting a lower imaging threshold): Older adults frequently present atypically, with minimal pain, normal vital signs, and vague symptoms such as confusion or reduced oral intake, even with serious pathology like mesenteric ischemia or perforation. Clinicians should have a lower threshold for imaging in this population rather than relying on the degree of reported pain or vital sign normalcy.

  15. Q242. A 68-year-old hypotensive man with sudden abdominal and back pain that began 30 minutes ago arrives in the resuscitation bay with a blood pressure of 80/50 mmHg. Before vascular surgery arrives, which bedside tool can rapidly help confirm the suspected diagnosis without leaving the department?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Point-of-care bedside ultrasound to identify an abdominal aortic aneurysm): Bedside point-of-care ultrasound can rapidly identify an aortic aneurysm without moving an unstable patient out of the resuscitation area, supporting immediate management decisions while surgical teams mobilize. Formal CT requires transport and is unsafe in hemodynamic instability. Barium enema and tagged red cell scans have no role in AAA diagnosis.

  16. Q243. A 60-year-old man with known cirrhosis and prior variceal bleeding presents with large-volume hematemesis over the past hour and a blood pressure of 82/50 mmHg with a heart rate of 128 beats per minute. Which initial management priority takes precedence over early endoscopy in this unstable patient?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Aggressive resuscitation with crystalloid and blood products plus airway protection): In a hemodynamically unstable variceal bleed, immediate priorities are securing the airway given risk of aspiration and hemorrhagic shock, along with fluid and blood product resuscitation. Endoscopy is essential but occurs after initial stabilization. Nasogastric lavage has limited diagnostic value and oral iron is irrelevant to acute massive bleeding.

  17. Q244. A patient with known esophageal varices presents with ongoing hematemesis and worsening tachycardia despite initial fluid resuscitation in the emergency department. In addition to standard resuscitation, which two adjunctive therapies are recommended before endoscopy in this suspected variceal hemorrhage?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intravenous octreotide infusion and prophylactic broad-spectrum antibiotic therapy): Octreotide reduces splanchnic blood flow and portal pressure, decreasing variceal bleeding, while prophylactic antibiotics reduce mortality and rebleeding risk in cirrhotic patients with GI hemorrhage. Neither vitamin K nor a low-residue diet addresses the acute bleeding physiology, and oral omeprazole with loperamide is inadequate for this presentation.

  18. Q245. A 45-year-old man presents with two episodes of melena over the past day but is hemodynamically stable on repeat vital signs, with a normal hemoglobin and a normal serum urea level on laboratory testing. Which tool helps determine whether he can be safely discharged for outpatient endoscopy?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Glasgow-Blatchford score used to risk-stratify for outpatient versus admission): The Glasgow-Blatchford score uses hemoglobin, urea, blood pressure, heart rate, and clinical features to identify low-risk upper GI bleed patients who can be safely managed with outpatient endoscopy rather than admission. The other listed tools assess unrelated clinical problems and have no role in GI bleed risk stratification.

  19. Q246. A 72-year-old man presents with several episodes of large-volume painless bright red rectal bleeding over the last several hours and a drop in blood pressure to 88/56 mmHg. He is too hemodynamically unstable to tolerate a bowel preparation. Which imaging modality is most appropriate for him right now?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (CT angiography to localize an actively bleeding lower GI source): In hemodynamically significant lower GI bleeding where bowel preparation is not feasible, CT angiography can rapidly localize active extravasation and guide interventional radiology or surgical management. Unprepped colonoscopy has poor visualization in active bleeding, and barium enema and ultrasound are not appropriate for localizing acute hemorrhage.

  20. Q247. A 55-year-old woman presents with melena over the past two days and a history of chronic NSAID use for osteoarthritis. She is hemodynamically stable while awaiting endoscopy later this evening. Which medication is appropriately started while she awaits this definitive endoscopic evaluation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Intravenous proton pump inhibitor infusion started prior to endoscopy): Intravenous PPI therapy started before endoscopy can reduce the stigmata of recent hemorrhage and the need for endoscopic intervention in suspected peptic ulcer bleeding. Tranexamic acid is not routinely recommended for GI bleeding given trial evidence of harm without benefit, and heparin prophylaxis is inappropriate during active bleeding.

  21. Q248. A trauma bay patient with massive upper gastrointestinal hemorrhage and ongoing hypotension requires transfusion of multiple blood products while surgical and endoscopic teams are mobilized. Which transfusion strategy best reflects current massive transfusion protocol principles for this critically bleeding patient?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Balanced ratio of red cells, plasma, and platelets given together): Modern massive transfusion protocols recommend a balanced approach, commonly near a 1:1:1 ratio of red cells, plasma, and platelets, to avoid dilutional coagulopathy and better mimic whole blood replacement. Giving any single component alone risks worsening coagulopathy and does not adequately address ongoing hemorrhagic shock.

  22. Q249. A patient presents to the emergency department reporting several days of dark stools but with no witnessed blood and an otherwise unremarkable history. Which bedside examination step is essential to confirm true melena and help assess the severity of this presentation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Digital rectal examination to directly visualize and characterize the stool): A digital rectal exam confirms the presence, color, and consistency of blood, distinguishing true melena from other causes of dark stool such as iron or bismuth ingestion. Bowel sounds, percussion for dullness, and organ palpation do not confirm the presence or character of gastrointestinal bleeding.

  23. Q250. A patient presents with new rectal bleeding five days after undergoing a colonoscopy with polypectomy for a large colonic polyp. He is hemodynamically stable with only a mild hemoglobin drop on repeat testing. Which management approach is most appropriate for him at this point?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Admission for observation with gastroenterology consultation for possible repeat colonoscopy): Post-polypectomy bleeding, though often self-limited, warrants admission for monitoring and gastroenterology involvement since rebleeding or need for repeat endoscopic hemostasis can occur. Assuming spontaneous resolution or skipping monitoring risks missing a patient who deteriorates, and surgery is not first-line for this typically endoscopically manageable complication.

  24. Q251. A 68-year-old man on chronic warfarin therapy presents with severe hematemesis over the past two hours and an INR result of 4.5 on point-of-care testing. Along with resuscitation, which intervention is most appropriate to reverse his coagulopathy as rapidly as possible?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate given with intravenous vitamin K): In life-threatening bleeding on warfarin, four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate provides rapid factor replacement and reverses the INR faster than plasma alone, combined with intravenous vitamin K for sustained reversal. Oral vitamin K alone acts too slowly, and simply stopping the drug without active reversal does not address ongoing coagulopathy during active hemorrhage.

  25. Q252. A 70-year-old man with a history of abdominal aortic aneurysm repair three years ago presents with a small herald bleed of bright red blood per rectum earlier today, followed suddenly by massive hematemesis in the waiting room. Which diagnosis must be urgently considered in this patient?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Aortoenteric fistula requiring emergent surgical and vascular surgery evaluation): A herald bleed followed by massive hemorrhage in a patient with a prior aortic graft is the classic presentation of an aortoenteric fistula, a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate surgical and vascular surgery involvement. The other listed conditions do not explain this specific history and pattern of bleeding.

  26. Q253. A previously healthy 3-year-old boy presents with painless, bright red to maroon rectal bleeding that started this morning, without fever, vomiting, or other associated symptoms. Which investigation best identifies the classic pediatric cause of this specific painless bleeding presentation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Technetium-99m pertechnetate scan to detect ectopic gastric mucosa): Painless lower gastrointestinal bleeding in a young child is classically caused by a Meckel diverticulum containing ectopic gastric mucosa, which is best identified using a technetium-99m pertechnetate scan that binds gastric mucosa.
