Upper endoscopy would not visualize this distal small bowel abnormality, and plain films or pancreatic enzymes do not aid this diagnosis.

  27. Q254. A resident asks whether tranexamic acid should be routinely given to all patients presenting with acute gastrointestinal bleeding, based on older extrapolated trauma literature and outdated departmental protocols. What is the appropriate evidence-based response to this specific clinical question?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (No, large trial evidence has not shown benefit and suggests possible harm): The large HALT-IT trial found that tranexamic acid did not reduce mortality in gastrointestinal bleeding and was associated with an increased risk of venous thromboembolic events, so it is not recommended as routine therapy. It is not universally contraindicated in all bleeding disorders, but its use in GI bleeding specifically is not supported.

  28. Q255. A cirrhotic patient with a prior variceal bleed is maintained on a non-selective beta blocker at home for secondary prophylaxis against rebleeding. He now presents to the emergency department with a new acute variceal hemorrhage. What is the role of his home beta blocker in this acute setting?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (The beta blocker is for chronic prevention, not acute hemorrhage control): Non-selective beta blockers reduce portal pressure over time and are used for primary and secondary prevention of variceal bleeding, but they play no role in controlling an active acute hemorrhage. Acute management still requires resuscitation, octreotide, antibiotics, and endoscopy, independent of the patient's chronic beta blocker therapy.

  29. Q256. A patient presents with suspected upper gastrointestinal bleeding based on abdominal pain and dark stool history, but with no witnessed hematemesis or melena confirmed yet on arrival. Endoscopy is not immediately available overnight. Which statement best reflects the current evidence on nasogastric lavage in this specific setting?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Nasogastric lavage has limited sensitivity and does not replace endoscopy): A clear nasogastric aspirate does not reliably exclude an upper GI bleed, particularly if bleeding has stopped or originates beyond the pylorus, so a negative result cannot rule out serious pathology. It should not delay resuscitation or definitive endoscopic evaluation, and a bloody aspirate points to an upper, not lower, source.

Chapter 8: Toxicology, Environmental Emergencies, and Anaphylaxis (43 Questions)

  1. Q257. A 20-year-old woman presents to the emergency department six hours after an intentional single acute acetaminophen overdose, reporting no nausea or vomiting and remaining fully asymptomatic on examination. Which step is most appropriate to guide the potential need for antidote therapy in this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Plot the 4-hour serum acetaminophen level on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram): A serum acetaminophen level drawn at or after 4 hours post-ingestion plotted on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram determines whether N-acetylcysteine therapy is indicated, since risk is based on level and time rather than symptoms at presentation. Waiting for elevated liver enzymes to treat would allow preventable hepatotoxicity to progress.

  2. Q258. A patient found unresponsive on the street with pinpoint pupils, cyanotic lips, and a respiratory rate of only 4 breaths per minute is about to be given naloxone by paramedics. Which approach to dosing minimizes precipitating acute opioid withdrawal while still restoring adequate ventilation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Titrate naloxone slowly in small incremental doses to restore breathing only): Small titrated doses aim to restore adequate respiratory drive while minimizing the risk of precipitating severe acute withdrawal, which can be distressing and increase aspiration risk. A large bolus is more likely to cause abrupt withdrawal. Withholding naloxone in a patient with dangerously low respiratory rate risks hypoxic injury or death.

  3. Q259. A patient who intentionally overdosed on amitriptyline presents with a widened QRS complex of 130 milliseconds on ECG, altered mental status, and a blood pressure of 84/50 mmHg. Which treatment is most appropriate to address this specific cardiotoxicity?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Intravenous sodium bicarbonate boluses to narrow the QRS complex): Sodium bicarbonate increases extracellular sodium and alkalinizes the serum, both of which counteract tricyclic antidepressant sodium channel blockade and narrow the QRS complex, reducing risk of ventricular arrhythmia. Class IA antiarrhythmics are contraindicated as they share a similar mechanism and can worsen toxicity. Calcium does not address this specific toxidrome.

  4. Q260. A patient presents with dry flushed skin, dilated pupils, urinary retention, agitated confusion, and markedly absent bowel sounds several hours after ingesting an unknown household substance found empty at the scene. Which toxidrome best fits this specific clinical presentation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Anticholinergic toxidrome, classically described as dry and delirious): Dry flushed skin, mydriasis, urinary retention, delirium, and decreased bowel sounds are the classic features of anticholinergic toxicity, often remembered as dry as a bone, red as a beet, mad as a hatter. Cholinergic toxicity would instead present with excessive secretions and miosis, and the sympathomimetic and opioid patterns do not match these findings.

  5. Q261. A farm worker presents with excessive salivation, lacrimation, urination, miosis, bradycardia, and diffuse muscle fasciculations shortly after an occupational pesticide spraying exposure earlier that afternoon. Which two treatments together address both the muscarinic and nicotinic effects of this specific toxicity?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Atropine for muscarinic symptoms plus pralidoxime for nicotinic effects): Organophosphate poisoning causes both muscarinic symptoms, treated with atropine, and nicotinic effects such as fasciculations and weakness, addressed by pralidoxime which reactivates acetylcholinesterase if given early. Using only one agent leaves either the secretions or the neuromuscular toxicity inadequately treated, and this presentation is not an opioid toxidrome.

  6. Q262. A patient rescued from a smoky house fire has a measured carboxyhemoglobin level of 30 percent with altered mental status and a headache, though the bedside pulse oximetry reading displays a reassuring 98 percent. Which initial treatment is most appropriate for this patient?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (High-flow 100% oxygen immediately, with hyperbaric oxygen considered): Pulse oximetry is unreliable in carbon monoxide poisoning since it cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, so a normal reading is falsely reassuring. High-flow oxygen accelerates carbon monoxide elimination, and hyperbaric oxygen should be considered for significant neurologic symptoms or high carboxyhemoglobin levels. Hydroxocobalamin treats cyanide toxicity, not carbon monoxide poisoning specifically.

  7. Q263. A patient with a history of chronic aspirin overuse for chronic pain presents with tinnitus, tachypnea, and a mixed respiratory alkalosis together with an underlying metabolic acidosis on arterial blood gas testing. Which treatment enhances renal elimination of this specific toxin?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Intravenous sodium bicarbonate given to alkalinize both the urine and serum): Alkalinizing the urine ionizes salicylate molecules, trapping them in the renal tubule and enhancing elimination, while alkalinizing the serum also reduces salicylate entry into the central nervous system. Acetazolamide would acidify the serum and worsen toxicity despite alkalinizing urine, and calcium chloride has no established role in salicylate elimination.

  8. Q264. A patient with a confirmed beta blocker overdose presents with profound bradycardia at 32 beats per minute and hypotension that remains refractory to atropine and intravenous fluid boluses already given. Which specific antidote should be administered next in this refractory presentation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Intravenous glucagon bolus followed by a continuous glucagon infusion): Glucagon bypasses the blocked beta receptor by directly activating adenylate cyclase, improving heart rate and contractility in beta blocker toxicity refractory to atropine. High-dose insulin euglycemia therapy is also used but is typically added alongside glucagon rather than replacing it. Simply stopping the home medication does not treat the acute overdose already ingested.

  9. Q265. A patient presents after an acute large digoxin overdose with nausea, visual halos and disturbances, and life-threatening hyperkalemia together with a ventricular dysrhythmia visible on the cardiac monitor. Which treatment specifically and definitively reverses this digoxin toxicity?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Digoxin-specific antibody fragments administered intravenously as the definitive antidote): Digoxin immune Fab fragments directly bind and neutralize digoxin, rapidly reversing both the cardiac toxicity and the associated hyperkalemia. Calcium administration has traditionally been cautioned against in digoxin toxicity due to concern for worsening cardiac contraction, so Fab fragments remain the definitive antidote rather than standard hyperkalemia measures alone.

  10. Q266. A patient on a chronic SSRI who recently started a second serotonergic agent for migraine prevention presents with agitation, hyperreflexia, inducible clonus, and hyperthermia that developed rapidly over several hours. Which diagnosis best fits, and how does it differ from neuroleptic malignant syndrome?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Serotonin syndrome, distinguished by rapid onset, hyperreflexia, and clonus): Serotonin syndrome typically develops within hours of adding a serotonergic agent and features neuromuscular hyperactivity such as clonus and hyperreflexia, contrasting with neuroleptic malignant syndrome, which develops over days and presents with lead-pipe rigidity and hyporeflexia. Malignant hyperthermia is triggered by anesthetic agents, and anticholinergic toxicity does not cause clonus or hyperreflexia.

  11. Q267. A patient rescued from a house fire has severe unexplained lactic acidosis, altered mental status, and a normal carboxyhemoglobin level on laboratory testing, raising strong suspicion for concomitant cyanide toxicity from burning synthetic materials. Which treatment should be given to this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intravenous hydroxocobalamin to bind and neutralize the cyanide): Hydroxocobalamin binds cyanide to form cyanocobalamin, which is renally excreted, and is preferred in smoke inhalation victims because it does not induce methemoglobinemia like older cyanide antidote kit components. Severe unexplained lactic acidosis after smoke inhalation should raise suspicion for cyanide toxicity requiring this specific antidote rather than bicarbonate or charcoal alone.

  12. Q268. A toddler found with an open pill bottle ingested a large number of iron supplement tablets several hours ago and now has vomiting, abdominal pain, and hematemesis together with a widened anion gap metabolic acidosis. Which treatment is appropriate for this significant iron toxicity?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Intravenous deferoxamine chelation therapy for significant iron toxicity): Deferoxamine chelates free iron, allowing renal excretion, and is indicated in significant iron toxicity with systemic symptoms or acidosis. Activated charcoal does not bind iron effectively and has no role here. Whole bowel irrigation is actually useful when abdominal imaging shows a significant radiopaque tablet burden, rather than always being withheld.

  13. Q269. A patient with a suspected pure benzodiazepine overdose after a witnessed single-agent ingestion is being considered by the treating team for flumazenil administration to reverse sedation. Which concern most limits the routine use of this reversal agent in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Flumazenil can precipitate seizures, especially with co-ingestants or dependence): Flumazenil can precipitate withdrawal seizures in patients who are benzodiazepine-dependent or who have co-ingested a proconvulsant medication such as a tricyclic antidepressant, making its routine use risky and generally reserved for select supervised scenarios. It is an intravenous benzodiazepine receptor antagonist, not an opioid receptor agent.

  14. Q270. A patient presents with blurred vision, an elevated osmolar gap, and a high anion gap metabolic acidosis several hours after a suspected accidental antifreeze ingestion found at his workshop. Which treatment blocks the formation of the toxic metabolites in this case?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Fomepizole to inhibit alcohol dehydrogenase and block toxic metabolites): Fomepizole competitively inhibits alcohol dehydrogenase, preventing the conversion of ethylene glycol or methanol into their toxic metabolites, and is the preferred antidote alongside consideration of hemodialysis in severe cases. Activated charcoal poorly adsorbs these small alcohols, and naloxone has no role in this specific toxidrome.

  15. Q271. A patient develops perioral numbness, a metallic taste, seizures, and sudden cardiovascular collapse shortly after receiving a regional nerve block with a large volume of local anesthetic during an orthopedic procedure. Which specific treatment should be administered for this toxicity?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Intravenous lipid emulsion therapy to bind the local anesthetic): Intravenous lipid emulsion acts as a lipid sink that binds lipophilic local anesthetic molecules, drawing them away from cardiac and neural tissue, and is the specific treatment for local anesthetic systemic toxicity alongside standard resuscitation. Activated charcoal is irrelevant for an injected drug, and naloxone does not reverse local anesthetic toxicity.

  16. Q272. A marathon runner collapses near the finish line with a rectal core temperature of 41.5°C, confusion, and hot dry skin, having stopped sweating despite the hot and humid race conditions. Which cooling method is most effective and practical for rapid temperature reduction in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Evaporative cooling with tepid water misting plus fans and ice packs): Evaporative cooling, combining misting with tepid water and fanning, is highly effective and practical for rapidly lowering core temperature in heat stroke, often supplemented with ice packs to major vessel areas. Antipyretics are ineffective in heat stroke since the hypothalamic set point is not the primary problem, unlike in fever.

  17. Q273. A patient with confirmed exertional heat stroke from a long outdoor training session is being actively cooled and monitored in the emergency department resuscitation bay. Which specific laboratory complication should be actively monitored given the well-known risk of muscle breakdown in this condition?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Creatine kinase level to detect rhabdomyolysis and renal risk): Exertional heat stroke commonly causes rhabdomyolysis from muscle breakdown, so creatine kinase should be monitored along with renal function and urine output to detect and manage the risk of acute kidney injury from myoglobin release. The other listed labs are not the primary expected complication of this condition.

  18. Q274. A hiker presents to the emergency department with a frostbitten hand that appears pale, waxy, hard, and completely insensate after several hours of prolonged exposure to subzero temperatures without adequate gloves. Which management step is appropriate once the risk of accidental refreezing is fully eliminated?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Rapid rewarming in a warm water bath at 37 to 39°C): Rapid rewarming in circulating warm water within this temperature range is the standard of care for frostbite once refreezing risk is eliminated, and it should not be delayed by slower passive methods. Vigorous rubbing causes further mechanical tissue damage, and dry heat sources risk uneven and excessive burns to insensate tissue.

  19. Q275. A hypothermic patient with a measured core temperature of 28°C is found unresponsive outdoors after an overnight exposure and has a barely palpable pulse on initial assessment. Which precaution is essential during extrication and transport to avoid triggering a lethal complication?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Handle the patient very gently to avoid triggering ventricular fibrillation): Severely hypothermic hearts are electrically irritable, and rough handling or jostling can precipitate ventricular fibrillation that is often refractory to defibrillation until the core temperature is raised. Medications should be given at longer intervals or withheld at very low temperatures, and chest compressions are reserved for a patient in confirmed cardiac arrest.

  20. Q276. A group of hikers is struck simultaneously by a single lightning bolt on an exposed ridge, and several appear to have no palpable pulse while one appears fully alert with only minor superficial burns. Which triage principle applies uniquely to this mass lightning casualty scenario?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Reverse triage, prioritizing apparently dead patients since cardiac arrest may be reversible): Unlike typical trauma triage, lightning strike victims in apparent cardiac arrest often have a primary respiratory arrest with a heart that can restart spontaneously, so resuscitation efforts should prioritize these patients over those who are already breathing and stable. This reverse triage approach maximizes survivors in a mass lightning casualty event.

  21. Q277. A recreational scuba diver develops migratory joint pain, mild confusion, and progressive dyspnea within an hour of a rapid uncontrolled ascent from a deep dive earlier that morning. Which treatment is considered definitive for this suspected case of decompression sickness?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Hyperbaric oxygen therapy administered as soon as possible): Hyperbaric oxygen therapy is the definitive treatment for decompression sickness, reducing bubble size and improving tissue oxygenation, and should be arranged as quickly as possible after 100% normobaric oxygen and fluid resuscitation are started. Transport should avoid increased altitude, since reduced cabin pressure worsens bubble expansion and symptoms.

  22. Q278. A diver surfaces rapidly after a regulator malfunction and immediately develops sudden focal neurologic deficits, confusion, and a brief loss of consciousness on the boat deck. Which diagnosis is most likely here, and what immediate positioning is recommended for this patient?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Arterial gas embolism, with the patient placed supine or left lateral): Sudden focal neurologic symptoms immediately upon surfacing suggest arterial gas embolism from pulmonary barotrauma, and positioning the patient supine or in the left lateral position helps reduce further embolization to the brain while arranging emergent hyperbaric therapy. Nitrogen narcosis resolves with descent and does not cause this abrupt post-ascent presentation.

  23. Q279. A patient reports severe right ear pain and muffled hearing that began during a rapid airplane descent after falling asleep before landing without swallowing or equalizing. Otoscopic exam shows a retracted, erythematous tympanic membrane with a small area of hemorrhage. Which condition best explains this presentation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Middle ear barotrauma from inadequate pressure equalization during descent): Rapid pressure changes during aircraft descent can cause middle ear barotrauma when the Eustachian tube fails to equalize pressure, leading to a retracted, erythematous, or hemorrhagic tympanic membrane and pain. This differs from otitis externa or cholesteatoma, which have different clinical histories and exam findings unrelated to recent altitude change.

  24. Q280. A climber at high altitude develops progressive ataxia, a severe worsening headache, and increasing confusion over several hours after a rapid ascent completed without adequate acclimatization days. Which management step is most critical for this specific presentation of high altitude cerebral edema?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Immediate descent along with dexamethasone and supplemental oxygen): High altitude cerebral edema is a medical emergency, and immediate descent is the single most important intervention, supported by dexamethasone and supplemental oxygen while descent is arranged. Continuing ascent or remaining at altitude risks rapid neurological deterioration and death, and acetazolamide alone is inadequate for established cerebral edema.

  25. Q281. A construction worker sustains an obvious high-voltage electrical injury with a visible entry wound on his hand and an exit wound on his foot after contact with an overhead power line. He is currently asymptomatic with a normal initial ECG tracing. Which management step is still indicated for him?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Cardiac monitoring and screening for rhabdomyolysis given the voltage exposure): High-voltage electrical injuries can cause delayed arrhythmias and significant deep tissue and muscle damage leading to rhabdomyolysis, even when the initial ECG and exam appear reassuring, so cardiac monitoring and creatine kinase or urine myoglobin screening are indicated. Immediate discharge risks missing a delayed cardiac or renal complication.

  26. Q282. A child is rescued by a lifeguard after a brief unwitnessed submersion in a backyard pool, coughing on arrival but now alert with normal oxygen saturation and no visible respiratory distress on repeated examination. Which management approach is most appropriate for this child?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Observe for a period of several hours to monitor for delayed respiratory decline): Even asymptomatic-appearing submersion patients require an observation period, since pulmonary symptoms and hypoxia can develop over several hours after aspiration of water. Immediate discharge risks missing a delayed deterioration, while prophylactic antibiotics and chest tubes are not routinely indicated without evidence of infection or pneumothorax.

  27. Q283. A patient presents with painful, itchy, purplish skin lesions on several fingers that developed gradually after repeated exposure to cold, damp outdoor conditions over recent weeks, without any history of true tissue freezing. Which diagnosis best fits, and how does it differ from frostbite?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Chilblains, a nonfreezing cold injury distinct from frostbite): Chilblains, or pernio, is a nonfreezing cold injury causing painful, itchy, erythematous to purplish lesions from repeated cold and damp exposure, distinct from frostbite which involves actual tissue freezing. Trench foot affects the feet from prolonged wet cold exposure, and Raynaud phenomenon is a vasospastic condition rather than a direct cold injury requiring rewarming in ice water.

  28. Q284. A patient presents with progressive swelling, worsening pain, and spreading ecchymosis of the calf several hours after a witnessed pit viper bite while hiking, along with early coagulopathy demonstrated on laboratory testing. Which treatment is indicated based on these specific findings?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Antivenom administration given the progressive local and systemic findings): Progressive local swelling with systemic effects such as coagulopathy after a pit viper envenomation are indications for antivenom therapy to prevent worsening tissue damage and bleeding complications. Incision and suction techniques and tourniquets are outdated and harmful practices that are no longer recommended in modern snakebite management.

  29. Q285. Two patients present to triage after prolonged strenuous exertion in high ambient heat, both with elevated core temperature and clinical signs of dehydration on examination. One has completely normal mental status while the other is confused and disoriented to time and place. Which finding distinguishes heat stroke from heat exhaustion here?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Central nervous system dysfunction, which defines heat stroke): Heat stroke is defined by hyperthermia combined with central nervous system dysfunction such as confusion, ataxia, or seizures, whereas heat exhaustion involves similar systemic symptoms without significant neurologic impairment. Sweating can be present or absent in either condition, and neither the specific temperature number alone nor duration of exposure alone defines the distinction.

  30. Q286. A patient develops diffuse urticaria, lip and tongue swelling, audible wheeze, and a drop in blood pressure within minutes of a bee sting while gardening in the backyard. Which is the correct first-line treatment and route of administration for this patient right now?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Intramuscular epinephrine injected into the anterolateral thigh): Intramuscular epinephrine in the anterolateral thigh is the first-line and most rapidly absorbed treatment for anaphylaxis, and should never be delayed for antihistamines or steroids, which are adjunctive only. Subcutaneous injection has slower and less reliable absorption compared to the intramuscular route in the thigh.

  31. Q287. A patient develops sudden severe hypotension and audible bronchospasm shortly after receiving intravenous contrast during a scheduled CT scan, without any urticaria, flushing, or other visible skin changes noted by the technologist. Which statement about this specific presentation is correct?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Anaphylaxis can occur without skin findings and still requires epinephrine): Anaphylaxis diagnostic criteria include presentations with acute involvement of two or more organ systems, or isolated hypotension after a known allergen exposure, even without cutaneous findings. Waiting for skin changes to appear before treating with epinephrine risks a fatal delay in this life-threatening reaction.

  32. Q288. A patient treated successfully in the emergency department with intramuscular epinephrine for anaphylaxis from a food exposure appears fully recovered and asymptomatic after one hour of observation. Which recommendation regarding further observation reflects appropriate practice for this specific presentation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (An observation period is recommended given the risk of a biphasic reaction): A biphasic anaphylactic reaction can recur hours after apparent resolution of initial symptoms, even without further allergen exposure, so an observation period is recommended following initial treatment. Patients should also be discharged with an epinephrine auto-injector prescription and an action plan rather than antihistamines alone.

  33. Q289. A patient with confirmed anaphylaxis after a wasp sting remains persistently hypotensive despite receiving two separate doses of intramuscular epinephrine and two liters of intravenous crystalloid fluids in the resuscitation bay. Which next step is most appropriate for this refractory presentation right now?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Start a continuous intravenous epinephrine infusion with close cardiac monitoring): Refractory anaphylaxis despite repeated intramuscular epinephrine doses and fluid resuscitation warrants a continuous intravenous epinephrine infusion under close cardiac monitoring for more titratable and sustained delivery. Discontinuing epinephrine would remove the most effective treatment, and corticosteroids or antihistamines are adjunctive rather than primary rescue therapies.

  34. Q290. A patient on chronic beta blocker therapy for hypertension develops anaphylaxis after a wasp sting and remains persistently hypotensive despite standard intramuscular epinephrine doses given twice already. Which adjunct medication may help overcome this relative epinephrine resistance in beta-blocked patients?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intravenous glucagon to bypass the blocked beta-adrenergic receptors): Glucagon has inotropic and chronotropic effects independent of beta receptors, making it useful in patients on beta blockers whose anaphylaxis responds poorly to epinephrine. Corticosteroids and antihistamines do not address this specific resistance mechanism, and calcium gluconate is not an established treatment for this scenario.

  35. Q291. A teenager recovers well in the emergency department after treatment for peanut-induced anaphylaxis at a birthday party and is now medically ready for discharge home with his parents. Which discharge plan components are most appropriate for this specific patient before he leaves?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Two epinephrine auto-injectors, an action plan, and allergy specialist referral): Best practice discharge for anaphylaxis includes prescribing two epinephrine auto-injectors in case a repeat dose is needed, providing a written anaphylaxis action plan, and referring to an allergy specialist for further evaluation and confirmatory testing. A single auto-injector or no specialist follow-up leaves the patient inadequately prepared for future exposures.

  36. Q292. A patient presents to the allergy clinic with a history of several recurrent anaphylactic episodes over the past year, and no clear trigger has been identified despite a thorough history, skin testing, and dietary review. Which statement about this specific clinical scenario is correct?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Idiopathic anaphylaxis is a recognized entity warranting allergy referral): Idiopathic anaphylaxis is a recognized diagnosis when no trigger can be identified despite appropriate evaluation, and these patients should still be referred to an allergist for further workup and management planning, including consideration of mast cell disorders. Dismissing recurrent objective anaphylactic episodes as panic attacks risks missing a serious and potentially recurrent condition.

  37. Q293. A 15 kg toddler presents to the emergency department with anaphylaxis, including hives and audible stridor, shortly after accidentally eating a snack containing tree nuts at daycare. Which epinephrine dosing approach is appropriate for this specific weight-based pediatric patient?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A weight-appropriate intramuscular epinephrine dose from a pediatric auto-injector or ampule): Pediatric anaphylaxis treatment requires a weight-based intramuscular epinephrine dose, typically delivered via a pediatric-strength auto-injector or drawn up from an ampule for smaller children, rather than an adult fixed dose. Epinephrine has no effective oral formulation, and subcutaneous administration has less reliable absorption than the intramuscular route.

  38. Q294. A patient undergoing an elective surgical procedure develops sudden hypotension, audible bronchospasm, and diffuse flushing shortly after the surgical drapes and sterile gloves are applied, before any anesthetic medications have actually been given. Which trigger should be strongly suspected in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Latex allergy as the likely trigger given the timing of exposure): The timing of reaction onset shortly after latex glove and drape contact, before any medications were administered, strongly suggests latex allergy as the trigger for perioperative anaphylaxis. Anesthetic agents remain a possible cause but are less likely given the exposure timeline described, and a vasovagal episode would not typically include bronchospasm and true hypotensive flushing together.

  39. Q295. A junior resident on shift wants to treat a patient in active anaphylaxis with intravenous diphenhydramine and corticosteroids first, planning to give epinephrine only if the patient fails to improve after these initial medications. What is the correct teaching point regarding this proposed approach?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Epinephrine must be given first, since antihistamines and steroids are only adjunctive): Epinephrine is the only medication proven to reduce mortality in anaphylaxis and must never be delayed for antihistamines or corticosteroids, which only provide adjunctive symptom relief for itching or potential biphasic reaction prevention. Treating with antihistamines or steroids first risks a fatal delay in definitive therapy.

  40. Q296. A hypotensive patient with confirmed anaphylaxis and a blood pressure of 78/45 mmHg is being actively treated with intramuscular epinephrine and intravenous fluids in the resuscitation bay. Which positioning is generally recommended unless significant respiratory distress or active vomiting is present in this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Supine position with legs elevated to support venous return): Placing the hypotensive anaphylaxis patient supine with legs elevated helps improve venous return and blood pressure, unless significant respiratory distress or vomiting makes this position unsafe, in which case a position of comfort is used instead. Sitting fully upright risks worsening hypotension through reduced venous return in a hemodynamically unstable patient.

  41. Q297. A patient with active anaphylaxis has rapidly progressive tongue and lip swelling over the past ten minutes, now with a muffled voice and new audible inspiratory stridor on examination. Which airway management principle applies to this rapidly progressing and potentially catastrophic presentation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Early airway management should be considered before complete obstruction develops): Progressive angioedema with stridor and voice change signals impending complete airway obstruction, and early intervention, potentially by the most experienced available provider, is safer than waiting for further deterioration or desaturation, at which point intubation becomes far more difficult or impossible. Bronchodilators alone do not address upper airway angioedema.

  42. Q298. A patient develops classic anaphylactic symptoms only on occasions when exercising within a few hours after eating a specific type of shellfish, but reliably tolerates that same shellfish or vigorous exercise independently on other days without any reaction at all. Which condition best explains this specific pattern?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Food-dependent exercise-induced anaphylaxis requiring both cofactors together): This condition requires the combination of a specific food ingestion followed by exercise within a defined window, with neither factor alone provoking symptoms, distinguishing it from classic food allergy or idiopathic anaphylaxis where a single trigger or no trigger is identified. Cholinergic urticaria is related to body temperature rise rather than a specific food and exercise combination.

  43. Q299. A patient recovering well after an episode of anaphylaxis caused by a confirmed shellfish allergy is now being discharged home from the emergency department later that evening. Beyond the standard epinephrine auto-injector prescription, which additional counseling point is important to discuss with him before discharge?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Strict allergen avoidance counseling and consideration of medical alert identification): Comprehensive discharge counseling includes strict allergen avoidance strategies such as careful label reading for hidden ingredients, discussion of relevant cross-reactivity risks, and consideration of medical alert identification in case of a future emergency when the patient cannot communicate their allergy. Omitting these points leaves the patient at ongoing risk of a repeat reaction.

Chapter 9: Obstetric, Pediatric, and Vulnerable Populations (56 Questions)

  1. Q300. A 27-year-old woman at 7 weeks gestation by last menstrual period presents to the emergency department with light vaginal spotting and mild cramping. She has never had a dating ultrasound this pregnancy. Which single historical feature most strongly raises your concern for ectopic pregnancy rather than a simple threatened miscarriage?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A documented history of prior tubal surgery or pelvic inflammatory disease): tubal damage from prior surgery or infection is a major risk factor for ectopic implantation and should heighten suspicion in any first trimester bleeder. Nausea and breast tenderness are nonspecific pregnancy symptoms seen in both normal and ectopic pregnancy. A single brief spotting episode is common in normal early pregnancy. Family history of twins has no bearing on ectopic risk.

  2. Q301. You are scanning a bleeding patient with a positive pregnancy test and an indeterminate location on transvaginal ultrasound. Which combination of sonographic findings is most consistent with a true early intrauterine pregnancy rather than a pseudosac associated with an ectopic pregnancy located elsewhere in the pelvis?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A centrally located fluid collection lacking any true double decidual sac sign): this describes a pseudosac, a fluid collection within the endometrial cavity from decidual reaction that mimics a gestational sac in ectopic pregnancy and lacks the true decidual ring. An eccentric sac with a decidual ring is a true intrauterine gestational sac. Free fluid with an empty uterus and an adnexal mass both suggest ectopic pregnancy rather than a normal intrauterine one.

  3. Q302. A hemodynamically stable 24-year-old woman at approximately 6 weeks gestation presents with mild spotting and no significant pain. Her serum beta-hCG returns at 1200 IU per litre, and transvaginal ultrasound shows an empty uterus with no adnexal abnormality identified. What is the most appropriate next step in her management today?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Repeat the beta-hCG in 48 hours and arrange close outpatient gynecology follow-up): in a stable patient with a hCG below the discriminatory zone and an empty uterus, serial hCG measurement helps distinguish early normal pregnancy, miscarriage, and ectopic pregnancy without exposing her to unnecessary surgery. Immediate laparoscopy is invasive and unwarranted without evidence of rupture. Empiric methotrexate risks harming a viable intrauterine pregnancy. Discharge with no follow-up ignores the diagnostic uncertainty present.

  4. Q303. While counseling a stable patient with an early pregnancy of uncertain location, she asks how her doctor will interpret her repeat blood test results. In a normal early intrauterine pregnancy, what is the expected minimum rise in serum beta-hCG over a 48 hour interval between two blood draws?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Roughly a doubling, though a minimum rise near 53 percent may still be normal): classic teaching describes hCG doubling roughly every 48 hours in early normal pregnancy, but studies show a minimum rise of about 53 percent can still be consistent with viability. A 15 percent rise is too low to be reassuring. hCG rises are expected well before 6 weeks. Tripling is not the standard reference threshold used clinically.

  5. Q304. A 31-year-old woman at 9 weeks gestation arrives by ambulance hypotensive, tachycardic, and diaphoretic, describing sudden severe abdominal pain and moderate vaginal bleeding over the last hour. Bedside ultrasound reveals free intraperitoneal fluid and an empty uterine cavity. What is the single highest priority action for this patient right now?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Activate the massive transfusion protocol and urgently call gynecology to the bedside): hypotension, tachycardia, severe pain, free fluid, and an empty uterus indicate a likely ruptured ectopic pregnancy with hemorrhage, a surgical emergency requiring resuscitation and immediate gynecologic involvement. Waiting for formal radiology or hCG results delays lifesaving intervention. Misoprostol has no role in a ruptured ectopic pregnancy and observation would be dangerous.

  6. Q305. A 22-year-old woman at 8 weeks gestation presents with heavy vaginal bleeding and crampy lower abdominal pain. On speculum examination her cervical os is open, and you can visualize tissue fragments sitting within the cervical canal itself. Which diagnosis best fits this particular clinical presentation and examination finding?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Inevitable abortion, or an incomplete abortion depending on how much tissue has passed): an open cervical os with tissue visible in the canal indicates the pregnancy loss is already underway or partially expelled, defining inevitable or incomplete abortion. Threatened abortion involves a closed os with a viable pregnancy. Missed abortion has a closed os with no cardiac activity but no expulsion. Complete abortion means all products have already passed and the os has closed again.

  7. Q306. Transvaginal ultrasound performed on a bleeding pregnant patient shows a gestational sac containing a fetal pole measuring 8 millimetres in crown-rump length, with no cardiac activity detected after a careful and prolonged search. What is the most appropriate clinical interpretation of this particular finding today?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (This single measurement is diagnostic of a missed abortion given the crown-rump length): per current SRU/ASRM/ACR consensus first-trimester ultrasound criteria, a crown-rump length of 7 millimetres or more with no cardiac activity on careful search is diagnostic of pregnancy failure on a single scan, with no repeat scan required. The 7 mm threshold was deliberately set to build in a safety margin below which a repeat scan in 7 to 14 days is still required. This finding does not represent a normal viable pregnancy, and it has no relationship to ectopic pregnancy rupture.

  8. Q307. You are seeing several pregnant patients with first trimester vaginal bleeding this shift, each with a different blood type on file. According to standard obstetric practice, which specific patient should routinely receive Rh immunoglobulin before being discharged home from your department today?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (An Rh-negative woman experiencing any degree of vaginal bleeding during this pregnancy): Rh immunoglobulin is recommended for Rh-negative women with any first trimester bleeding to prevent alloimmunization from fetal-maternal hemorrhage. It is not restricted to pregnancies past 20 weeks. Rh-positive women do not require immunoglobulin since alloimmunization is not a risk. It should be considered regardless of whether management is medical or surgical.

  9. Q308. A stable woman at 7 weeks gestation with a confirmed viable intrauterine pregnancy and only minimal spotting asks you what activity restrictions she should follow at home. Based on the current evidence, what is the most appropriate counseling point for you to give her before discharge today?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Pelvic rest and strict bed rest have not been shown to reduce miscarriage risk): evidence does not support bed rest or pelvic rest as effective interventions to prevent miscarriage in threatened abortion, and this reassurance avoids unnecessary activity restriction. Strict bed rest has no proven benefit. Hospital admission is not required for stable minimal bleeding. Bleeding does not always predict eventual loss since many such pregnancies continue normally.

  10. Q309. A hemodynamically stable woman with a confirmed ectopic pregnancy has a beta-hCG of 1800 IU per litre, no fetal cardiac activity on ultrasound, and an adnexal mass measuring under 3.5 centimetres. After discussing the case with gynecology, which management option may reasonably be offered to this particular patient?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Single-dose intramuscular methotrexate together with close serial beta-hCG follow-up monitoring): a stable patient with a small unruptured ectopic mass and moderate hCG may be a candidate for medical management with methotrexate, provided there are no contraindications and follow-up is ensured. Immediate surgery is not mandatory when criteria for medical therapy are met. Discharge without treatment risks rupture. Misoprostol is not an accepted treatment for ectopic pregnancy.

  11. Q310. A junior resident asks why the emergency team uses the discriminatory beta-hCG zone cautiously rather than treating it as an absolute fixed cutoff whenever evaluating a woman with early pregnancy bleeding of uncertain location. What is the best explanation you can give this resident today?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Multiple gestations and normal variation between hCG assays can shift the expected threshold): the discriminatory zone is an estimate that can vary with multiple gestations, different hCG assays, and individual patient factors, so it should guide rather than dictate decisions. It does not apply universally across all trimesters since it is specific to early pregnancy evaluation. hCG values remain clinically relevant despite bleeding. Ultrasound and hCG are complementary, not one uniformly superior to the other.

  12. Q311. A 29-year-old woman at 6 weeks gestation reports mild vaginal spotting overnight. On speculum examination her cervical os is closed, and transvaginal ultrasound confirms a viable intrauterine pregnancy with normal fetal cardiac activity present. This clinical picture is best classified under which of the following diagnostic categories today?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Threatened abortion, given the closed cervical os and confirmed ongoing viable pregnancy): mild bleeding with a closed cervical os and a confirmed viable intrauterine pregnancy on ultrasound defines threatened abortion, since the pregnancy has not yet been lost. Complete abortion requires prior expulsion of all products. Septic abortion requires evidence of infection, which is absent here. Missed abortion requires an absence of cardiac activity, which contradicts the viable pregnancy described.

  13. Q312. A woman recently diagnosed with a missed abortion at 9 weeks gestation would like to avoid surgical evacuation if possible and asks about medical management instead. Which counseling point about using misoprostol for this indication is the most clinically accurate one for you to share with her?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (It carries a small but real risk of heavy bleeding and incomplete expulsion needing follow-up): misoprostol is an effective option for medical management of missed abortion but patients should be counseled about the possibility of heavy bleeding, incomplete expulsion, and the need for follow-up or possible surgical completion. It does not work within an hour in all patients. It is not universally contraindicated in stable patients. Follow-up hCG or ultrasound is still generally recommended to confirm completion.

  14. Q313. A febrile 26-year-old woman presents several days after an incomplete abortion with foul-smelling vaginal discharge, marked uterine tenderness, and retained products of conception visible on ultrasound. Which underlying diagnosis absolutely must be considered here and treated urgently before this patient can be safely discharged?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Septic abortion requiring broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics together with urgent surgical evacuation): fever, foul discharge, uterine tenderness, and retained products after incomplete abortion indicate septic abortion, a life-threatening infection requiring prompt antibiotics and evacuation of retained tissue. Threatened abortion does not involve infection or retained products. Methotrexate has no role once infection is present. This presentation is inconsistent with normal postpartum involution.

  15. Q314. A 33-year-old primigravida presents at 30 weeks gestation for a routine prenatal visit. Her blood pressure is measured twice, four hours apart, using proper technique. Which specific pair of blood pressure values would actually meet the accepted diagnostic threshold for hypertension in pregnancy at this gestational age?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A systolic pressure of 140 mmHg or a diastolic pressure of 90 mmHg or higher): the diagnostic threshold for hypertension in pregnancy is a systolic pressure of 140 mmHg or greater or a diastolic pressure of 90 mmHg or greater, confirmed on two occasions at least four hours apart. The other listed values fall below this recognized diagnostic threshold and would not qualify as hypertensive in pregnancy.

  16. Q315. A woman at 32 weeks gestation is found to have new hypertension along with 2+ proteinuria on urine dipstick during her assessment today, but otherwise reports feeling generally well. Which additional laboratory or clinical feature would reclassify her presentation as pre-eclampsia with severe features?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A platelet count measured at less than 100,000 per microlitre on repeat testing): thrombocytopenia is one of the defined severe features of pre-eclampsia, along with markers such as elevated liver enzymes, renal impairment, pulmonary edema, and severe symptoms. Mild ankle edema is common in normal pregnancy and not a severe feature. A single episode of nausea is nonspecific. A urine output of 40 mL per hour is within a generally acceptable range and not indicative of oliguria.

  17. Q316. A 35-year-old woman with known pre-eclampsia develops new right upper quadrant pain along with markedly elevated liver enzymes, and her hemoglobin has dropped with schistocytes seen on peripheral blood smear review. Which specific diagnosis best explains this particular clinical and laboratory triad today?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (HELLP syndrome, a recognized severe variant of pre-eclampsia involving hemolysis and liver injury): hemolysis with schistocytes, elevated liver enzymes causing right upper quadrant pain, and low platelets define HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of pre-eclampsia. Acute fatty liver of pregnancy presents differently, often with hypoglycemia and coagulopathy. Gestational cholestasis causes pruritus without hemolysis. Viral hepatitis would not explain the hemolytic component or its close association with hypertension.

  18. Q317. You are preparing to start seizure prophylaxis for a woman diagnosed with severe pre-eclampsia at 34 weeks gestation in your emergency department. What is the standard intravenous loading dose of magnesium sulfate that you should order for this particular patient right now?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Roughly 4 to 6 grams given intravenously over about 15 to 20 minutes total): the standard loading dose for magnesium sulfate in severe pre-eclampsia is 4 to 6 grams given intravenously over 15 to 20 minutes, followed by a maintenance infusion. A 1 gram rapid push is far too low and given too quickly. Intramuscular dosing regimens exist but are not the described standard intravenous loading approach. A 20 gram continuous infusion would risk severe toxicity.

  19. Q318. A woman receiving a continuous magnesium sulfate infusion for eclampsia prophylaxis is reassessed and found to have absent deep tendon reflexes along with a respiratory rate of only 10 breaths per minute. What is the single most appropriate immediate action for you to take right now?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Stop the infusion immediately and give intravenous calcium gluconate right away as antidote): absent reflexes and a respiratory rate below 12 per minute are signs of magnesium toxicity, requiring immediate cessation of the infusion and calcium gluconate as the antidote. Increasing or continuing the infusion would worsen toxicity and risk respiratory arrest. Switching to oral magnesium is inappropriate and does not address the acute toxicity.

  20. Q319. While a patient remains on ongoing magnesium sulfate therapy for severe pre-eclampsia overnight on your unit, which specific set of clinical parameters must be monitored regularly at the bedside in order to detect early magnesium toxicity before respiratory depression actually develops?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Deep tendon reflexes, respiratory rate, and hourly urine output measured together at the bedside): these three parameters are the standard bedside markers used to detect early magnesium toxicity before it progresses to respiratory depression or cardiac arrest. Fasting glucose has no relationship to magnesium levels. A single sodium check does not reflect ongoing magnesium status. Fetal heart rate alone does not capture maternal toxicity risk.

  21. Q320. A 38 weeks pregnant woman with pre-eclampsia lacking severe features and a reassuring fetal status is currently being managed in your emergency department after obstetric consultation was requested. What is considered the definitive treatment for pre-eclampsia in a patient at this particular gestational age?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Delivery of both the fetus and placenta after appropriate obstetric consultation is arranged): delivery is the only definitive cure for pre-eclampsia, and at 38 weeks with reassuring fetal status this is generally the recommended course after obstetric involvement. Prolonged bed rest at home does not treat the underlying disease. Antihypertensives alone manage blood pressure but do not resolve pre-eclampsia. Diuretics are not appropriate management for this condition.

  22. Q321. A pregnant patient in your department has a blood pressure of 175/118 mmHg with an ongoing severe headache, and you need to lower it acutely and safely before she can be safely transferred. Which antihypertensive agent is commonly used first-line for this indication in most emergency departments?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Intravenous labetalol given carefully in incremental titrated doses over several minutes): intravenous labetalol is a commonly used first-line agent for acute severe hypertension in pregnancy due to its established safety and efficacy profile in this population. ACE inhibitors are contraindicated in pregnancy due to fetal renal toxicity. A single thiazide dose is not an accepted acute treatment. Nifedipine is actually used for both acute and chronic hypertension in pregnancy, not restricted to chronic use only.

  23. Q322. A previously healthy 29-year-old woman at 29 weeks gestation with no prior seizure history suddenly develops a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure in your waiting room, and her documented blood pressure moments later is 168/112 mmHg. What is the most likely diagnosis until proven otherwise here?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Eclampsia, given the new seizure occurring alongside severe hypertension in pregnancy): a new seizure in pregnancy accompanied by severe hypertension should be presumed to be eclampsia until proven otherwise, given the immediate need for magnesium sulfate and blood pressure control. Febrile seizure would require a fever history, which is absent. New-onset epilepsy is a diagnosis of exclusion in this context. Vasovagal syncope does not typically produce a generalized tonic-clonic seizure with this degree of hypertension.

  24. Q323. A medical student on your rotation asks why magnesium sulfate is generally preferred over phenytoin specifically for seizure prophylaxis and treatment in women diagnosed with eclampsia, rather than simply reaching for a more familiar anticonvulsant that is used commonly outside of pregnancy. How should you answer them?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Large randomized trials have shown magnesium sulfate more effectively reduces recurrent seizures): large randomized trials have demonstrated magnesium sulfate is superior to phenytoin for preventing recurrent eclamptic seizures and reducing maternal mortality. Phenytoin does carry drug interactions. Magnesium sulfate does carry a real risk of toxicity requiring monitoring. Phenytoin is not the preferred agent in major obstetric guidelines, which favor magnesium sulfate.

  25. Q324. A woman who delivered vaginally three days ago now presents to your emergency department with a new headache, blurred vision, and a blood pressure reading of 155/100 mmHg on repeat measurement. What should you recognize about pre-eclampsia risk in this specific postpartum setting before discharging her home?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Postpartum pre-eclampsia can genuinely develop and still requires urgent evaluation and treatment): pre-eclampsia can present or persist up to several weeks after delivery, and new hypertension with headache and visual symptoms postpartum warrants urgent assessment and treatment. It is incorrect to assume pre-eclampsia cannot occur after delivery. These symptoms should not be dismissed as a routine postpartum headache. Blood pressure monitoring remains important in the postpartum period.

  26. Q325. A pregnant woman with long-standing chronic hypertension is being evaluated for possible superimposed pre-eclampsia during this visit. Which specific laboratory finding on today's testing would be most consistent with severe pre-eclampsia rather than her chronic hypertension continuing on its own unchanged?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (New-onset proteinuria together with a rising creatinine and new thrombocytopenia on testing): these findings reflect the multiorgan involvement characteristic of severe pre-eclampsia rather than chronic hypertension alone, which typically lacks new proteinuria or hematologic abnormalities. A normal blood count and stable liver enzymes would be reassuring rather than concerning. Trace ketones with no protein is nonspecific. An unchanged hemoglobin does not indicate pre-eclampsia.

  27. Q326. A woman at only 26 weeks gestation develops severe features of pre-eclampsia including a blood pressure of 170/115 mmHg, and you have already started magnesium sulfate and appropriate antihypertensive therapy at your community hospital. What essential next step must you now also arrange for this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Urgent transfer to a facility capable of both maternal-fetal and neonatal intensive care): severe pre-eclampsia at a very preterm gestational age requires care at a center equipped for high-risk maternal and neonatal management, particularly given anticipated preterm delivery. Discharge home is unsafe given the severity of findings. Iron supplementation does not address the acute severe disease process. Severe features often do progress rapidly and require close monitoring rather than reassurance.

  28. Q327. A resident wants to give a large intravenous crystalloid bolus to a woman with severe pre-eclampsia who appears mildly oliguric on your unit tonight. Why should intravenous fluid administration generally be managed cautiously in this population rather than reaching quickly for an aggressive fluid bolus tonight?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (These particular patients carry an increased risk of pulmonary edema from capillary leak): pre-eclampsia is associated with increased capillary permeability and low oncotic pressure, raising the risk of pulmonary edema with aggressive fluid administration. Aggressive fluids do not reliably improve outcomes and can cause harm. Pre-eclampsia is not generally characterized by significant intravascular depletion requiring boluses. Fluid restriction alone has not been shown to worsen outcomes in this context.

  29. Q328. A 3-week-old infant presents to the emergency department with a rectal temperature of 38.2 C and no localizing signs. Parents report the infant is feeding well. What is the most appropriate next step in management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Full septic workup including blood, urine, and CSF cultures with empiric antibiotics): a febrile neonate under 28 days of age requires a complete sepsis evaluation and empiric antibiotics regardless of how well the infant appears, because clinical appearance is unreliable at this age for excluding serious bacterial infection. Discharge home, a brief observation period, or outpatient oral antibiotics all fail to meet the standard of care for this highest-risk age group. Neonates lack the immunologic maturity to reliably localize infection, so occult bacteremia and meningitis must be actively excluded.

  30. Q329. Which age cutoff defines the neonatal period during which any fever mandates a full sepsis evaluation regardless of clinical appearance, per standard emergency medicine practice?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (0 to 28 days of life): the neonatal period, defined as birth through 28 days, carries the highest risk of serious bacterial infection and mandates full sepsis workup for any fever regardless of appearance. The 60 and 90 day cutoffs describe later infant risk-stratification pathways that allow more selective testing in well-appearing infants. The 14-day cutoff is too narrow and does not match accepted neonatal fever protocols.

  31. Q330. A 45-day-old infant has fever of 38.4 C, appears well, and has a normal urinalysis and inflammatory markers. Using a standard low-risk criteria approach, what is the most appropriate management?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Discharge without antibiotics and arrange follow-up within 24 hours): a well-appearing 45-day-old infant who meets low-risk criteria with normal urinalysis and inflammatory markers can be safely observed as an outpatient with close follow-up, avoiding unnecessary invasive testing. Admission for empiric antibiotics is reserved for high-risk infants who do not meet low-risk criteria. Lumbar puncture is not required once validated low-risk criteria are satisfied, and empiric ceftriaxone is unnecessary in a true low-risk case.

  32. Q331. A previously healthy 18-month-old has a witnessed generalized tonic-clonic seizure lasting two minutes during a febrile illness, with full return to baseline within 15 minutes. What best describes this event and its required workup?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Simple febrile seizure that generally does not require routine neuroimaging or lumbar puncture): a generalized seizure lasting under 15 minutes with full recovery in a neurologically normal toddler meets criteria for simple febrile seizure, which has a benign course and does not routinely require imaging or lumbar puncture. Complex febrile seizures involve focal features, prolonged duration, or recurrence within 24 hours, none of which are described here. This event is not afebrile and does not meet the duration criteria for status epilepticus.

  33. Q332. Which finding in a febrile toddler should be treated as a red flag prompting immediate escalation of care rather than routine outpatient management?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Persistent lethargy with poor eye contact after fever control): altered mental status that persists despite antipyretic administration is a red flag suggesting serious underlying illness and requires urgent further evaluation. Mild rhinorrhea, transient fussiness that resolves with fever control, and decreased appetite for a single meal are common, reassuring findings in benign viral illness. Persistent lethargy after fever resolution cannot be attributed to the fever itself and needs escalation.

  34. Q333. A 4-month-old fully immunized infant has fever without an identifiable source and appears well on repeated examination. What is the recommended approach regarding occult bacteremia risk today compared with the pre-vaccine era?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Occult bacteremia risk has fallen substantially, reducing the need for routine blood cultures in well-appearing infants): widespread conjugate vaccination against Haemophilus influenzae type b and Streptococcus pneumoniae has markedly lowered the incidence of occult bacteremia in fully immunized, well-appearing infants. This has shifted practice away from routine blood cultures in this population when the infant looks well. The risk has not increased or remained unchanged, and occult bacteremia is not limited to older children.

  35. Q334. A 10-day-old neonate presents with a temperature of 38.1 C and mild irritability. Which diagnostic step is considered mandatory before any decision to discharge is entertained?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Lumbar puncture as part of a complete sepsis workup): any febrile neonate under 28 days, including this 10-day-old with fever and irritability, requires a full sepsis workup including lumbar puncture before any discharge decision because meningitis cannot be reliably excluded clinically at this age. A chest radiograph is only indicated with respiratory findings, and rapid viral antigen testing or stool culture do not replace the need for a complete sepsis evaluation. Skipping the lumbar puncture in a neonate is not an acceptable shortcut.

  36. Q335. For a well-appearing 60-day-old infant with fever and a positive urinalysis, negative inflammatory markers, and no meningeal signs, what does a standard low-risk pathway generally recommend?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Treat as urinary tract infection with oral or intravenous antibiotics without lumbar puncture if truly low risk): a well-appearing 60-day-old with a positive urinalysis and reassuring inflammatory markers who meets low-risk criteria can typically be treated for urinary tract infection without a lumbar puncture, since the risk of concurrent bacterial meningitis is very low in this setting. Performing lumbar puncture on every infant with a positive urinalysis is unnecessary once low-risk status is confirmed. Withholding antibiotics or automatic intensive care admission are not supported by this presentation.

  37. Q336. Fever without a source in a 2-year-old with normal vital signs and a reassuring examination is best characterized using which principle?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Clinical appearance and age-appropriate risk stratification guide the extent of investigation more than height of fever alone): validated age-based approaches emphasize overall clinical appearance, risk factors, and objective testing rather than temperature magnitude alone when deciding on investigation intensity. A temperature above 39 C does not automatically indicate bacterial infection, and a full sepsis workup is not required for every child regardless of age. Response to antipyretics has been shown to be an unreliable predictor of illness severity.

  38. Q337. A 6-week-old infant with fever 38.6 C has an abnormal urinalysis, elevated C-reactive protein, and mild irritability on exam. What does this combination indicate under standard risk-stratification criteria?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (High risk status warranting full sepsis workup and admission): the combination of abnormal urinalysis, elevated inflammatory markers, and clinical irritability in a 6-week-old places this infant in a high-risk category under standard criteria, requiring full evaluation and admission rather than outpatient management. Low-risk outpatient pathways apply only when all screening criteria, including normal inflammatory markers and reassuring appearance, are met. A single antibiotic dose followed by discharge does not adequately address a high-risk young infant.

  39. Q338. A physician is teaching a resident about fever thresholds in infants. Which statement about the definition of fever in a young infant is accurate?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A rectal temperature of 38.0 C or higher is generally considered fever in infants): rectal temperature measurement is the accepted standard in young infants because it most accurately reflects core body temperature, with 38.0 C as the threshold used in most risk-stratification protocols. Axillary and tympanic measurements are less accurate in this age group and are not preferred. Restricting the definition to temperatures above 39.5 C would miss clinically significant fevers in this vulnerable population.

  40. Q339. A 5-year-old presents with fever, drooling, and a muffled voice, sitting upright and refusing to lie flat. What is the most appropriate immediate priority?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Minimize agitation and arrange controlled airway management given suspected epiglottitis): drooling, muffled voice, and refusal to lie flat are classic features of epiglottitis, and the priority is avoiding agitation while arranging airway management in a controlled setting such as the operating room. Obtaining radiographs or attempting direct oral examination with a tongue depressor can precipitate complete airway obstruction and should be avoided. Oral antibiotics and discharge are entirely inappropriate given the risk of rapid airway compromise.

  41. Q340. Which combination of findings in a febrile 3-month-old would classify the infant as high risk under most validated clinical decision rules?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Ill appearance with abnormal inflammatory markers and lethargy): the combination of ill appearance, abnormal inflammatory markers, and lethargy meets high-risk criteria under validated clinical decision rules and warrants a full evaluation and likely admission. Well appearance with normal white count and reassuring urinalysis, a simple low-grade fever with reassuring vital signs, and brief fussiness with normal feeding are all reassuring low-risk features. High risk is defined by the presence of concerning clinical or laboratory findings together, not isolated normal results.

  42. Q341. A parent asks why their well-appearing febrile 4-month-old still needs blood work when the infant looks fine. What is the most accurate response?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Clinical appearance alone cannot reliably exclude serious bacterial infection in young infants, so age-based testing protocols still apply): studies have repeatedly shown that clinical gestalt alone misses a meaningful proportion of serious bacterial infections in young febrile infants, which is why standardized age-based testing protocols remain necessary even when an infant looks well. Testing is grounded in evidence rather than being an arbitrary hospital policy. Deferring all testing to a family physician or relying solely on fever severity would risk missing serious infection.

  43. Q342. During a routine emergency visit, which screening question is most appropriate for identifying possible intimate partner violence in a private setting without the partner present?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Has your partner ever hit, kicked, or otherwise physically hurt you): direct, specific behavioral screening questions asked in a private setting are more effective at eliciting disclosure of intimate partner violence than vague questions about relationship happiness or stress. Questions about general relationship satisfaction or financial arguments are too indirect to reliably identify abuse. Effective screening requires explicit language about physical acts rather than broad impressions of the relationship.

  44. Q343. A 4-month-old infant presents with a spiral fracture of the femur and the caregiver reports the injury occurred from rolling off a couch. What should this finding prompt?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Suspicion of non-accidental trauma given the injury is inconsistent with the reported mechanism and the infant's developmental stage): a spiral femur fracture in a non-mobile 4-month-old is inconsistent with a short fall from a couch and is a well-recognized red flag for physical abuse requiring further investigation. Reassurance or documentation alone without action fails to protect a potentially at-risk infant. Simple discharge with orthopedic follow-up ignores the mechanism-injury mismatch that mandates a child protection referral.

  45. Q344. Which principle should guide documentation of injuries in a suspected abuse case in the emergency record?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Use precise, objective, quoted language describing the history and physical findings without interpretive conclusions): documentation in suspected abuse cases should use direct quotes and objective descriptions of findings, since the chart may later serve as evidence in legal proceedings and interpretive conclusions can compromise its reliability. Brief summaries without quotes lose important detail, and omitting the reported mechanism weakens the record's evidentiary value. Recording personal opinions about credibility is inappropriate and outside the physician's role.

  46. Q345. A physician strongly suspects a school-aged child is being physically abused at home based on examination findings. What is the physician's legal obligation in this situation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Report the suspicion directly to the local child protection authority without delay): physicians in Canada have a legal duty to report suspected child abuse directly to child protection authorities as soon as reasonable grounds for suspicion exist, without waiting for family agreement or independent social work confirmation. Discussing concerns with the family first or waiting for a follow-up visit delays a protective response and could place the child at continued risk. Reporting is a personal, non-delegable legal obligation once reasonable suspicion is present.

  47. Q346. An elderly patient arrives with unexplained bruising in various stages of healing and appears fearful of their live-in caregiver. What should the physician prioritize?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Interview the patient privately without the caregiver present and assess for elder abuse): bruising in various stages of healing combined with fearfulness toward a caregiver are recognized indicators of elder abuse, and a private interview without the caregiver present is essential to allow safe disclosure. Attributing the findings to normal aging or discharging without further inquiry ignores meaningful red flags. Asking the caregiver directly in the patient's presence risks retaliation and prevents honest disclosure.

  48. Q347. A woman discloses ongoing physical abuse from her partner but states she is not ready to leave the relationship. What is the most appropriate emergency department response?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Provide safety planning resources and support her autonomy in decision-making): patients disclosing intimate partner violence should be offered safety planning and support while respecting their autonomy, since leaving an abusive relationship is a complex process that carries its own risks. Insisting she leave immediately can be unsafe and disempowering, and contacting the partner to mediate could escalate danger. Declining further discussion abandons the patient at a critical moment of disclosure.

  49. Q348. Which injury pattern is most concerning for physical abuse rather than an accidental household injury in a toddler?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A scald burn with well demarcated stocking-glove distribution and no splash marks): a sharply demarcated stocking-glove burn pattern without splash marks is highly suggestive of forced immersion rather than an accidental spill, which typically produces irregular borders with splash marks. A single forehead bruise, a shin abrasion, and a minor knee scrape are all common accidental findings in mobile toddlers learning to walk. Pattern and distribution of injury are key distinguishing features between abusive and accidental burns.

  50. Q349. What is the primary purpose of a safety plan developed with a patient experiencing intimate partner violence before discharge?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (To identify concrete steps and resources that reduce risk if violence escalates or the patient decides to leave): a safety plan is designed to give the patient practical, individualized strategies and resources to reduce risk regardless of whether or when they choose to leave the relationship. It is not primarily a legal document, nor is its purpose to pressure the patient into ending the relationship. Mandatory police involvement is not required in every disclosed case and depends on the patient's wishes and jurisdictional requirements.

  51. Q350. A 2-year-old presents with bruising to the ears, neck, and buttocks, areas generally protected from routine accidental injury. How should this examination finding be interpreted?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (As a highly specific finding warranting evaluation for physical abuse): bruising to the ears, neck, and buttocks occurs in areas not typically exposed to accidental trauma in toddlers and is considered a highly specific marker for physical abuse requiring further evaluation. This pattern is not a benign or common finding in active toddlers, whose accidental bruises typically occur over bony prominences like shins and foreheads. While a bleeding disorder workup may be part of the evaluation, it does not replace the need to assess for abuse.

  52. Q351. Why is it recommended to interview a patient alone, separate from a partner or caregiver, when screening for domestic violence or abuse?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (It allows disclosure without fear of retaliation or the abuser controlling the narrative): interviewing a patient alone removes the risk that a controlling or abusive partner or caregiver will intimidate the patient or dominate the account, allowing more honest disclosure. This practice is a clinical safety measure rather than simply an administrative requirement, and it should occur routinely rather than only upon patient request, since many patients cannot safely ask for privacy in front of an abuser. Visit length is not the rationale for this practice.
