  53. Q352. A caregiver provides a history for an infant's injury that changes significantly between the initial triage note and the physician assessment. What does this finding suggest?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (This inconsistency should raise concern for non-accidental injury and warrants further investigation): a changing or evolving history between different points of assessment is a recognized red flag for non-accidental injury and should prompt further investigation rather than being dismissed. Attributing the change to normal caregiver stress, recall variation, or a language barrier without further exploration risks missing a genuine safety concern. Inconsistent histories are one of the most consistently cited abuse indicators in the emergency medicine literature.

  54. Q353. When mandatory reporting legislation for suspected child abuse applies in most Canadian jurisdictions, what threshold of suspicion triggers the physician's duty to report?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Reasonable grounds to suspect abuse, even without certainty): mandatory reporting legislation in most Canadian provinces is triggered by reasonable grounds to suspect abuse, a lower threshold than definitive proof or caregiver admission. Waiting for absolute proof or a confession would delay protective action and is inconsistent with the intent of the legislation. Consulting a hospital lawyer is not a legal prerequisite to fulfilling the reporting duty.

  55. Q354. A physician is unsure whether an injury pattern in an older adult patient meets the threshold for elder abuse reporting. What is the most appropriate action?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Consult local elder protection or adult protective services resources for guidance on next steps): when uncertain whether findings meet a reporting threshold, physicians should consult local elder protection or adult protective services resources rather than delaying action or dismissing the concern. Waiting for a family member to raise the issue or assuming capacity negates concern both risk allowing ongoing harm to continue. A patient's reluctance to discuss the situation does not eliminate the physician's responsibility to seek appropriate guidance.

  56. Q355. Which statement best reflects the recommended approach to disclosing intimate partner violence findings when the patient's partner is present in the emergency department waiting area?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Separate the patient from the partner before asking sensitive screening questions): standard practice for intimate partner violence screening requires separating the patient from their partner in a private space before asking sensitive questions, ensuring safety and honest disclosure. Having the partner present to verify the account or asking questions within earshot of others directly undermines patient safety and honest reporting. Delaying screening entirely until the partner leaves the building is impractical and may miss the only opportunity to screen during that visit.

Chapter 10: Shock, Sepsis, and Thromboembolic Disease (43 Questions)

  1. Q356. A 3-year-old girl has had vomiting and watery diarrhea for two days and now appears lethargic. On examination her capillary refill is 4 seconds, radial pulses are weak and thready, mucous membranes are very dry, and her blood pressure is 70 over 40 mmHg. Which finding most clearly indicates she has progressed to decompensated shock?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Measurable hypotension together with weak, thready peripheral pulses): hypotension is a late finding in pediatric shock because children compensate with tachycardia and vasoconstriction for a prolonged period. Once blood pressure actually falls, compensatory mechanisms have failed and the child has entered decompensated shock, which carries a much higher risk of cardiac arrest. Dry mucous membranes and decreased urine output reflect ongoing dehydration but do not by themselves define decompensation. Mild tachycardia with normal mental status is more typical of early, compensated shock.

  2. Q357. A 24-year-old woman is stung by a wasp at a picnic and within ten minutes develops diffuse urticaria, facial swelling, wheeze, and a blood pressure of 78 over 42 mmHg with warm, flushed extremities. Which type of shock best explains this rapid clinical picture and its underlying pathophysiology?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Distributive shock from anaphylaxis causing widespread vasodilation and capillary leak): anaphylaxis triggers mast cell degranulation, releasing histamine and other mediators that cause massive vasodilation, increased vascular permeability, and warm flushed extremities rather than the cool extremities seen in hypovolemic or cardiogenic shock. This explains the rapid hypotension paired with urticaria and wheeze. Cardiogenic and obstructive shock are caused by mechanical or pump failure, not an allergic mediator cascade. Hypovolemic shock develops more gradually from actual volume loss rather than redistribution.

  3. Q358. A 28-year-old man arrives by ambulance after a stab wound to the abdomen. He has a palpable radial pulse, is talking appropriately, and his systolic blood pressure is 88 mmHg. The trauma team estimates definitive surgical hemorrhage control is roughly 20 minutes away. Which fluid strategy is most appropriate before he reaches the operating room?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Permissive hypotension using small, titrated crystalloid boluses to maintain perfusion): in penetrating trauma with a short transport time to definitive hemorrhage control, aggressive fluid resuscitation to a normal blood pressure can dislodge early clots and worsen ongoing bleeding. Small titrated boluses aimed at maintaining a palpable radial pulse and adequate mentation balance perfusion against the risk of exacerbating hemorrhage. Fully normalizing blood pressure before surgical control is associated with worse outcomes in this setting. Hypotonic saline is never an appropriate resuscitation fluid in hemorrhagic shock.

  4. Q359. A 60-year-old man with septic shock from pneumonia remains hypotensive after receiving 30 mL per kilogram of balanced crystalloid fluid, with a mean arterial pressure of 58 mmHg despite adequate volume resuscitation. According to current sepsis guidelines, which vasopressor should be started first to restore adequate perfusion pressure?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Norepinephrine titrated to achieve an adequate mean arterial pressure): current sepsis guidelines recommend norepinephrine as the first-line vasopressor for septic shock because of its favorable balance of alpha and beta effects and its association with fewer arrhythmias compared to alternatives. Dopamine is associated with higher arrhythmia rates and is no longer preferred. Phenylephrine and epinephrine are typically reserved for specific situations or as second-line adjuncts rather than initial therapy. Adequate fluid resuscitation should already have been attempted before starting any vasopressor.

  5. Q360. A 45-year-old woman with a suspected ruptured ectopic pregnancy has a heart rate of 118 beats per minute, a blood pressure of 118 over 78 mmHg, cool extremities, and mild anxiety, but she remains fully alert and oriented. Which statement best describes her current physiologic shock state?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (She is compensated, since tachycardia often precedes any measurable blood pressure drop): young, otherwise healthy patients can maintain a normal blood pressure through tachycardia and vasoconstriction well into significant blood loss, masking the severity of shock until compensatory mechanisms fail. Cool extremities and anxiety in this context are early clues that perfusion is already compromised despite normal vital sign thresholds. She is not free of shock simply because her blood pressure is normal, and close monitoring with repeat assessment is essential. Labeling her presentation as decompensated is incorrect since her blood pressure has not yet fallen.

  6. Q361. A previously healthy 2-year-old boy has three days of vomiting and diarrhea from presumed viral gastroenteritis. He is alert, has slightly dry lips, normal capillary refill, and is tolerating small sips of an oral electrolyte solution offered in the department. Which management approach is most appropriate for his mild to moderate dehydration?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Oral rehydration therapy given in small, frequent, tolerated volumes): a child with mild to moderate dehydration who is alert and tolerating oral fluids should be managed with oral rehydration therapy, which is as effective as intravenous fluids in this population and avoids unnecessary intravenous access. Intravenous boluses are reserved for children who cannot tolerate oral intake or who show signs of more severe dehydration or shock. Strict bowel rest is outdated and delays recovery, and nasogastric rehydration is unnecessary when oral intake is already succeeding. Early refeeding alongside rehydration is generally encouraged.

  7. Q362. A 70-year-old man with a history of coronary artery disease presents with chest pain, then develops hypotension, jugular venous distension, and bilateral crackles heard on lung auscultation, with cool and mottled extremities. Which type of shock is most consistent with this overall clinical presentation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Cardiogenic shock resulting from acute failure of the heart's pump function): the combination of chest pain, hypotension, jugular venous distension, and pulmonary crackles reflects a failing left ventricle unable to maintain forward flow, causing back-pressure into the lungs and systemic venous congestion. Hypovolemic shock would present with flat neck veins rather than distension, and distributive shock from sepsis typically causes warm rather than cool, mottled extremities. Obstructive shock from pulmonary embolism can cause similar hemodynamics but the ischemic chest pain and pulmonary edema here point toward primary pump failure.

  8. Q363. A 24-year-old man is brought in after a high-speed motor vehicle collision. He has absent breath sounds on the right side, distended neck veins, tracheal deviation to the left, and a blood pressure of 76 over 40 mmHg. Which diagnosis best explains this obstructive shock presentation and requires immediate treatment?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Tension pneumothorax requiring immediate needle or finger decompression): absent breath sounds unilaterally, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, distended neck veins, and hypotension after trauma are the classic findings of tension pneumothorax, which obstructs venous return and cardiac output and demands immediate decompression rather than imaging. Cardiac tamponade would more typically present with muffled heart sounds and a centrally positioned trachea. Hemorrhagic shock from splenic injury would not cause tracheal deviation or absent unilateral breath sounds. This presentation is not consistent with an isolated spinal cord injury.

  9. Q364. A 4-year-old child with severe gastroenteritis has sunken eyes, delayed capillary refill of 3 seconds, and lethargy consistent with significant hypovolemic shock. Intravenous access has just been obtained. Which initial fluid bolus is recommended by standard pediatric resuscitation protocols before reassessment?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Twenty millilitres per kilogram of isotonic crystalloid given rapidly): standard pediatric resuscitation protocols recommend an initial bolus of 20 mL per kilogram of an isotonic crystalloid such as normal saline or balanced solution, given rapidly and followed by reassessment of perfusion and mental status. Hypotonic fluids are avoided in acute resuscitation because they can worsen cerebral edema and electrolyte disturbances. Dextrose-containing solutions are not appropriate as a primary resuscitation fluid for hypovolemic shock. Colloid at a much larger volume is not the standard first bolus choice.

  10. Q365. A 30-year-old woman develops generalized hives, lip swelling, audible wheeze, and a blood pressure of 76 over 40 mmHg within minutes of a bee sting while gardening. Which medication should be administered immediately as first-line treatment for her distributive shock?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intramuscular epinephrine injected into the anterolateral thigh muscle): epinephrine is the first-line, life-saving treatment for anaphylaxis because it rapidly reverses vasodilation, reduces mucosal edema, and supports blood pressure through both alpha and beta adrenergic effects. Antihistamines like diphenhydramine and corticosteroids like prednisone have a delayed onset and are only adjunctive therapies, never substitutes for epinephrine. Nebulized salbutamol can help bronchospasm but does not address the underlying hypotension and vasodilation. Delaying epinephrine in favor of these other agents increases the risk of cardiovascular collapse.

  11. Q366. A 19-year-old man sustains a cervical spinal cord injury after diving into shallow water. In the emergency department his blood pressure is 82 over 50 mmHg, his heart rate is 52 beats per minute, and his extremities are warm rather than cool. Which shock type best explains this presentation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Neurogenic shock from loss of sympathetic vascular tone): a high spinal cord injury disrupts descending sympathetic pathways, causing unopposed vagal tone with bradycardia and loss of vascular tone with hypotension, while skin remains warm due to vasodilation rather than the cool skin seen in hypovolemic or cardiogenic shock. This combination of relative bradycardia with hypotension and warm extremities after spinal trauma is the hallmark of neurogenic shock. Hemorrhagic and cardiogenic shock typically cause tachycardia and cool, clammy skin. Septic shock would not be expected acutely after an isolated traumatic spinal injury.

  12. Q367. A 55-year-old man with septic shock has been started on broad-spectrum antibiotics and norepinephrine. The team wants an objective marker to track over several hours to judge whether resuscitation is actually improving tissue perfusion. Which laboratory value is most useful as a resuscitation endpoint in this situation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Serial serum lactate measurements to confirm ongoing clearance): trending lactate over time reflects whether tissue perfusion and oxygen delivery are improving with resuscitation, making it a widely used and validated endpoint in septic shock management. A single glucose or sodium value provides no information about perfusion status, and white blood cell counts change too slowly and are influenced by too many other factors to guide hour-to-hour resuscitation decisions. Persistently elevated or rising lactate despite therapy suggests inadequate resuscitation or ongoing tissue hypoperfusion. Falling lactate over serial measurements supports that current therapy is effective.

  13. Q368. A 14-month-old boy has had five days of diarrhea and poor oral intake. On examination he has sunken eyes, markedly decreased skin turgor, delayed capillary refill, and appears lethargic and difficult to rouse. Using a standard clinical dehydration scale, which combination of findings classifies him as severely dehydrated?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Sunken eyes, poor skin turgor, delayed capillary refill, and lethargy): severe dehydration on standard pediatric assessment scales requires multiple concordant abnormal findings across categories including mental status, eyes, skin turgor, and capillary refill, all of which are present in this child. Isolated or mild findings such as slightly dry mucous membranes with normal activity, normal urine output with mild thirst, or a mildly elevated heart rate with normal blood pressure are more consistent with no dehydration or only mild dehydration. The combination of multiple significant abnormal findings together is what defines the severe category.

  14. Q369. A 35-year-old man involved in a motorcycle collision has lost an estimated 1500 millilitres of blood. His heart rate is 124 beats per minute, blood pressure is 98 over 70 mmHg, and he appears anxious and mildly confused. Which class of hemorrhagic shock best matches this presentation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Class III hemorrhage with significant blood volume loss): a blood loss of roughly 1500 millilitres corresponds to approximately 30 to 40 percent of circulating volume in an average adult, which fits Class III hemorrhage, characterized by marked tachycardia, a measurable drop in blood pressure, and altered mental status such as confusion or anxiety. Class I hemorrhage causes minimal or no measurable vital sign change. Class II hemorrhage typically causes tachycardia with blood pressure still preserved and only mild anxiety, not the degree of hemodynamic and mental status change seen here. This presentation is clearly beyond the threshold for no hemorrhagic shock.

  15. Q370. A 62-year-old woman with septic shock from a urinary source remains hypotensive despite an adequate weight-based dose of norepinephrine and appropriate fluid resuscitation. The team decides to add a second vasopressor. Which agent is typically chosen as the standard second-line vasopressor in this situation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Vasopressin added as the standard evidence-based second agent): vasopressin is the recommended second-line agent added to norepinephrine in septic shock, working through a different receptor pathway that can help restore vascular tone while potentially allowing a lower catecholamine dose. Dobutamine and milrinone are inotropes generally reserved for patients with evidence of concurrent cardiac dysfunction rather than as routine second-line vasopressors. Nitroglycerin is a vasodilator and would worsen hypotension, making it inappropriate in this setting. Adding vasopressin is supported by current septic shock management guidelines.

  16. Q371. A 68-year-old man with a productive cough and fever is assessed in triage. His respiratory rate is 24 breaths per minute, he is intermittently confused, and his systolic blood pressure is 95 mmHg. Which bedside score is most useful to flag him as high risk for sepsis-related mortality?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (The quick Sequential Organ Failure Assessment score at the bedside): the quick Sequential Organ Failure Assessment score uses respiratory rate, altered mentation, and systolic blood pressure, all present in this patient, to rapidly identify patients at higher risk of poor outcomes from suspected infection outside the intensive care unit. The Glasgow Coma Scale alone does not incorporate respiratory rate or blood pressure and is not a sepsis screening tool. CURB-65 is specific to pneumonia severity rather than general sepsis risk, and the Wells score is used for pulmonary embolism probability, not sepsis.

  17. Q372. A previously healthy 34-year-old man presents with fever, severe headache, neck stiffness, and photophobia over the past twelve hours, and bacterial meningitis is strongly suspected. Blood cultures are drawn and a lumbar puncture is planned shortly. Which empiric antibiotic regimen is most appropriate to start immediately in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Ceftriaxone plus vancomycin, with dexamethasone given before or with antibiotics): empiric therapy for suspected bacterial meningitis in an immunocompetent adult should cover the most common pathogens including penicillin-resistant pneumococcus, which requires the combination of ceftriaxone and vancomycin, with adjunctive dexamethasone shown to reduce neurologic complications when given before or with the first antibiotic dose. Oral amoxicillin alone does not provide adequate coverage or route of administration for this life-threatening infection. Ciprofloxacin alone and metronidazole plus clindamycin do not adequately cover the key bacterial meningitis pathogens and are inappropriate empiric choices.

  18. Q373. A 55-year-old man with poorly controlled diabetes reports leg pain that seems far worse than the modest cellulitis-like erythema on examination, with rapidly spreading redness, skin bullae, and low-grade fever over several hours. Which action is most urgent given the suspected underlying diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Immediate surgical consultation for possible necrotizing fasciitis and debridement): pain that is disproportionate to the visible skin findings, especially with bullae, rapid spread, and systemic signs in a diabetic patient, is a red flag for necrotizing fasciitis, which requires emergent surgical debridement in addition to broad-spectrum antibiotics because imaging and antibiotics alone cannot halt the infection. Discharging the patient home, using only topical treatment, or observing overnight all risk delaying a diagnosis where mortality rises significantly with every hour of delayed surgical source control. Early surgical involvement is the single most time-critical intervention in this presentation.

  19. Q374. A 40-year-old man has a fluctuant, tender, erythematous mass on his thigh with well-defined borders, mild surrounding warmth, and no lymphangitic streaking or systemic toxicity, having developed the lesion gradually over the past week after a minor scrape. Which management step should take priority over antibiotic selection alone for this collection?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Incision and drainage performed for the fluctuant collection): a well-localized fluctuant abscess is primarily a surgical problem, and incision and drainage is the definitive treatment that most reliably resolves the infection, with antibiotics reserved as an adjunct in select cases such as surrounding cellulitis or systemic symptoms. Antibiotics alone, whether intravenous or oral, are far less effective at resolving a walled-off purulent collection without source control. Warm compresses alone are insufficient once a discrete abscess has formed and drainage is indicated. Delaying drainage in favor of antibiotics alone risks prolonged symptoms and spread of infection.

  20. Q375. A 40-year-old man who injects drugs presents with fever, a new regurgitant heart murmur, and tender painful nodules on his fingertips and toes. The team plans to start empiric antibiotics for suspected infective endocarditis. Which step should occur first whenever clinically feasible?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Drawing at least two separate sets of blood cultures): identifying the causative organism is central to both diagnosing infective endocarditis using clinical criteria and selecting targeted antibiotic therapy, so blood cultures should be obtained before antibiotics are started whenever the patient is hemodynamically stable enough to permit the short delay. Starting antibiotics first can sterilize cultures and make it far harder to identify the organism later. An electrocardiogram and an outpatient echocardiogram are useful parts of the overall workup but do not take priority over securing cultures before antibiotics begin.

  21. Q376. A 32-year-old woman returns from a camping trip in an endemic region and notices a slowly expanding, painless red rash with central clearing on her thigh, roughly ten centimetres wide, several days after removing an attached tick. Which tick-borne illness does this classic finding most strongly suggest?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Lyme disease presenting with the classic erythema migrans rash): a slowly expanding, painless, annular red rash with central clearing appearing days after a tick bite in an endemic area is the classic presentation of erythema migrans, the hallmark early finding of Lyme disease. Rocky Mountain spotted fever typically presents with fever and a petechial rash spreading from the wrists and ankles rather than a single expanding target lesion. Ehrlichiosis and babesiosis more commonly present with fever, malaise, and laboratory abnormalities rather than this specific expanding rash. Recognizing erythema migrans allows early treatment before disseminated disease develops.

  22. Q377. A 58-year-old woman meets criteria for septic shock from a presumed urinary source, with hypotension and altered mental status. Two large-bore intravenous lines are placed. Per current sepsis bundle recommendations, which sequence of actions is correct for her initial emergency department management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Draw blood cultures first, then give antibiotics within one hour): current sepsis bundle recommendations call for obtaining blood cultures before administering antibiotics, provided this does not meaningfully delay treatment, with broad-spectrum antibiotics then given within one hour of recognition of septic shock. Giving antibiotics before cultures reduces the chance of identifying the causative organism and narrowing therapy later. Waiting for lactate results or for definitive source confirmation before starting antibiotics introduces dangerous delays in a condition where mortality rises with every hour antibiotics are withheld.

  23. Q378. A 27-year-old woman returns from two months volunteering in a rural region of sub-Saharan Africa and now has cyclical fevers, chills, headache, and fatigue that began roughly two weeks after arriving home. Which test should be prioritized to screen for the most immediately dangerous cause of her illness?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Thick and thin peripheral blood smears to screen for malaria): fever in a returning traveler from a malaria-endemic region is a medical emergency until proven otherwise, and thick and thin blood smears remain the standard test to rapidly identify and speciate malaria parasites so treatment is not delayed. Stool ova and parasite testing, hepatitis A serology, and a chest radiograph may be reasonable parts of a broader travel illness workup but none of them addresses the most immediately life-threatening diagnosis suggested by her cyclical fever pattern. Malaria can progress rapidly to severe disease if diagnosis is delayed.

  24. Q379. A 29-year-old otherwise healthy nonpregnant woman is diagnosed with uncomplicated acute pyelonephritis, tolerates oral intake well, and is judged well enough for outpatient management after receiving one dose of intravenous antibiotics in the department. Which empiric oral antibiotic is commonly recommended to complete her discharge course?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Ciprofloxacin continued for seven to ten days per local resistance patterns): fluoroquinolones such as ciprofloxacin achieve adequate tissue penetration into the kidney and are commonly recommended for outpatient completion of uncomplicated pyelonephritis, with duration and choice adjusted to local resistance data. Nitrofurantoin does not achieve adequate renal tissue concentrations and is not appropriate for pyelonephritis despite being useful for simple cystitis. Doxycycline and clindamycin do not have reliable activity against the typical gram-negative uropathogens responsible for pyelonephritis and are not standard choices.

  25. Q380. A 16-year-old boy has fever, neck stiffness, photophobia, and a rapidly spreading non-blanching petechial rash across his trunk and legs, raising strong concern for meningococcal disease. Which infection control measure should be instituted immediately in addition to starting empiric antibiotics?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Droplet precautions instituted for suspected meningococcal disease transmission): Neisseria meningitidis is spread through respiratory droplets, so droplet precautions should be started immediately alongside empiric antibiotics to protect healthcare workers and other patients until the diagnosis is excluded or the patient has completed an adequate course of therapy. Airborne precautions are reserved for pathogens like tuberculosis that spread via smaller aerosolized particles, not meningococcus. Standard precautions alone are insufficient given the droplet transmission risk, and contact precautions without respiratory protection would not adequately protect against droplet spread.

  26. Q381. A febrile 45-year-old man is being evaluated in the emergency department, and the team is trying to decide how strongly to suspect infective endocarditis as the underlying cause. Which single patient factor confers the highest risk and should most raise clinical suspicion in this scenario?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Injection drug use or the presence of a prosthetic heart valve): both intravenous drug use and prosthetic heart valves are well-established major risk factors for infective endocarditis because they provide either a direct route of bloodstream bacterial entry or an abnormal surface prone to bacterial adherence and vegetation formation. Well-controlled diabetes, seasonal allergic rhinitis, and a remote uncomplicated appendectomy do not meaningfully increase the risk of endocarditis. Recognizing these high-risk factors should raise the index of suspicion earlier in the workup of an undifferentiated fever.

  27. Q382. An emergency physician is assessing a patient with a rapidly spreading, disproportionately painful leg infection after a minor skin injury. Which combination of underlying risk factors most increases this patient's likelihood of having developed true necrotizing fasciitis rather than simple cellulitis?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Diabetes mellitus combined with recent penetrating soft tissue trauma): diabetes impairs local immune response and wound healing, and penetrating trauma provides a portal of entry for aggressive polymicrobial or monomicrobial pathogens, together substantially increasing the risk of necrotizing fasciitis compared with the general population. Young age without chronic illness, well-controlled asthma with mild allergies, and a prior joint replacement in isolation are not recognized major risk factors for this aggressive soft tissue infection. Recognizing these combined risk factors should heighten suspicion and prompt earlier surgical involvement.

  28. Q383. A 74-year-old man presents with community-acquired pneumonia. He has a respiratory rate of 32 breaths per minute, new confusion, and a blood urea nitrogen above the normal threshold, giving him a high CURB-65 score. Which disposition and antibiotic approach is most appropriate for his care?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Hospital admission with combination intravenous antibiotic therapy started promptly): a high CURB-65 score reflecting confusion, elevated respiratory rate, and abnormal renal function indicates severe community-acquired pneumonia warranting hospital admission and prompt combination intravenous antibiotic therapy covering typical and atypical pathogens. Outpatient management with oral monotherapy or azithromycin alone is inappropriate given his high-risk features and would risk clinical deterioration. Withholding antibiotics until culture results return would dangerously delay treatment in a patient already showing signs of severe illness.

  29. Q384. A 38-year-old man presents with fever, severe headache, and a rash that began on his wrists and ankles before spreading centrally toward his trunk, roughly a week after a tick bite while hiking. Which classic triad should raise suspicion for Rocky Mountain spotted fever in this presentation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Fever, headache, and a rash spreading from the extremities centrally): the classic triad of Rocky Mountain spotted fever is fever, headache, and a rash that characteristically begins on the wrists and ankles before spreading centripetally toward the trunk, exactly as described in this patient after tick exposure. Cough, wheeze, and pleuritic chest pain point toward a primary respiratory process rather than a tick-borne rickettsial illness. Painless rash with bradycardia and peripheral edema, and jaundice with hepatomegaly and dark urine, describe different clinical patterns not characteristic of this disease's classic presentation.

  30. Q385. A 34-year-old woman presents with pleuritic chest pain after a long flight. Her Wells score works out to a low pretest probability for pulmonary embolism. Which next step is most appropriate to exclude PE in this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Order an age-adjusted D-dimer; if negative, PE can be safely excluded): in a patient with a low pretest probability by Wells criteria, a negative age-adjusted D-dimer has a high enough negative predictive value to exclude PE without imaging. CT pulmonary angiography carries radiation and contrast risk that is unwarranted when a validated rule-out pathway exists. Empiric anticoagulation is inappropriate before any objective testing, and a V/Q scan is unnecessary when D-dimer alone can safely exclude the diagnosis in this risk category.

  31. Q386. A 68-year-old man with a high Wells score has tachycardia, hemoptysis, and a recent hip fracture. Which statement about D-dimer testing applies best to this patient?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (D-dimer should not be used since a negative result cannot safely exclude PE here): in patients with a high pretest probability, the negative predictive value of D-dimer drops substantially and a negative result cannot be trusted to rule out PE. These patients should proceed directly to definitive imaging such as CT pulmonary angiography regardless of D-dimer result. Sensitivity of D-dimer does not meaningfully increase with clinical suspicion, and D-dimer is never a substitute for confirmatory imaging.

  32. Q387. A 72-year-old man with confirmed proximal DVT has a creatinine clearance of 25 mL/min. Which anticoagulant strategy is most appropriate given his renal function?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Unfractionated heparin transitioning to warfarin once stable): in severe renal impairment with a creatinine clearance this low, most DOACs are relatively or absolutely contraindicated due to renal clearance concerns, making unfractionated heparin bridging to warfarin the safer standard approach. Rivaroxaban and apixaban both require caution or avoidance at this level of renal dysfunction, and dabigatran is renally cleared and poorly suited to significant renal impairment.

  33. Q388. A patient with confirmed PE develops a systolic blood pressure of 75 mmHg that remains unresponsive to a fluid bolus. Which management step should be taken next?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Give systemic thrombolysis promptly if no absolute contraindication exists): a systolic pressure of 75 mmHg refractory to fluids defines massive, high-risk PE, and systemic thrombolysis is the guideline-directed treatment to rapidly reduce clot burden and reverse hemodynamic collapse. Anticoagulation alone does not act quickly enough in this unstable state, delaying treatment for further imaging risks arrest, and an IVC filter does nothing to treat the existing clot burden or hypotension.

  34. Q389. A hemodynamically stable patient with confirmed PE has echocardiographic right ventricular strain and an elevated troponin. How should this presentation be classified and initially managed?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Intermediate-risk PE managed with anticoagulation and close monitoring for deterioration): a hemodynamically stable patient with right ventricular strain and troponin elevation meets criteria for intermediate-risk, or submassive, PE, which is generally managed with anticoagulation plus vigilant monitoring for hemodynamic decline. This is not low-risk PE given the strain and biomarker findings, and it does not meet criteria for high-risk PE since the patient remains hemodynamically stable. Pulmonary angiography is not required to make this risk stratification.

  35. Q390. Which of the following represents an absolute contraindication to systemic thrombolysis in a patient with massive pulmonary embolism?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A prior history of intracranial hemorrhage at any point in the past): prior intracranial hemorrhage is a well-established absolute contraindication to thrombolysis because of the extreme risk of recurrent catastrophic bleeding. Age alone, a minor dental procedure, and well-controlled hypertension are not absolute contraindications, though significantly elevated uncontrolled blood pressure would be a relative concern.

  36. Q391. A pregnant patient at 28 weeks gestation presents with unilateral leg swelling and new dyspnea. Which initial diagnostic approach is most appropriate for her?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Compression ultrasonography of the symptomatic leg before any chest imaging): in pregnancy, starting with leg Doppler ultrasound avoids fetal radiation exposure, and a positive result confirming DVT can allow treatment without any need for chest imaging. Proceeding straight to CT pulmonary angiography or a V/Q scan exposes the fetus to unnecessary radiation before a lower-risk test is tried, and D-dimer is unreliable in pregnancy since it rises physiologically regardless of clot presence.

  37. Q392. Which anticoagulant is generally preferred for treatment of a confirmed DVT diagnosed during pregnancy?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Low molecular weight heparin, which does not cross the placenta): LMWH is the preferred agent in pregnancy because it does not cross the placenta and has an established safety profile for both mother and fetus. Rivaroxaban and dabigatran are direct oral anticoagulants that are not recommended in pregnancy due to limited safety data, and warfarin crosses the placenta and is teratogenic, particularly in the first trimester.

  38. Q393. Which clinical finding contributes the most points toward a high Wells score for suspected pulmonary embolism?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Clinical signs of DVT such as leg swelling and calf tenderness): clinical signs of DVT carry the highest point value in the Wells PE score because they directly suggest a source clot capable of embolizing. Age over 60 alone, mild tachycardia at 85 beats per minute, and unrelated allergy history do not meaningfully contribute to pretest probability for PE.

  39. Q394. A 60-year-old man with active metastatic colon cancer is newly diagnosed with acute DVT. Which anticoagulant approach is generally preferred for his long-term treatment?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A DOAC such as apixaban or LMWH, per current guideline preference): current guidelines support either LMWH or select DOACs, such as apixaban, as preferred long-term therapy for cancer-associated thrombosis, balancing efficacy and bleeding risk. Warfarin has historically shown inferior efficacy in this population, aspirin alone is inadequate treatment for confirmed DVT, and withholding anticoagulation entirely leaves the patient at ongoing risk of clot extension and embolization.

  40. Q395. A patient with submassive PE develops worsening hypoxia despite adequate therapeutic anticoagulation. Which option should be discussed with the consulting specialty team?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Catheter-directed thrombolysis considered as an escalation option in this case): worsening hypoxia despite therapeutic anticoagulation in submassive PE warrants discussion of an escalation strategy, and catheter-directed thrombolysis offers targeted clot reduction with a lower systemic bleeding risk than full-dose systemic thrombolysis. Stopping anticoagulation or reducing the dose would worsen outcomes by allowing further clot propagation, and discharging a deteriorating patient home is unsafe.

  41. Q396. A 25-year-old with no risk factors has mild pleuritic pain, entirely normal vital signs, and low clinical suspicion for PE. Which tool can safely avoid any further testing if every criterion is met?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (The Pulmonary Embolism Rule-out Criteria, known as PERC): PERC is specifically validated to be applied only in patients with a low clinical gestalt for PE, and if every criterion is met, PE can be excluded without any further testing, including D-dimer. The Wells score and Geneva score are risk stratification tools that still require a downstream test such as D-dimer or imaging, unlike PERC which can end the workup entirely in the appropriate low-risk population.

  42. Q397. Which scenario represents the most appropriate indication for placement of an inferior vena cava filter?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A patient with confirmed proximal DVT and an absolute contraindication to anticoagulation): IVC filters are indicated specifically when a patient has confirmed venous thromboembolism but cannot receive anticoagulation, since the filter mechanically prevents embolization while the underlying clot risk remains unaddressed. Filters are not indicated in patients tolerating anticoagulation well, are not recommended for routine surgical prophylaxis in the absence of confirmed clot, and are never a substitute for anticoagulation when anticoagulation can safely be given.

  43. Q398. A patient develops DVT after major orthopedic surgery, a clearly provoked event with no other ongoing risk factors present. What duration of anticoagulation is generally recommended?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Approximately 3 months of anticoagulation for this provoked event): for a DVT clearly provoked by a major transient risk factor such as recent major orthopedic surgery, guidelines generally recommend a finite course of about 3 months of anticoagulation once the provoking factor has resolved. Two weeks is too short to adequately treat an acute clot, and indefinite or mandatory 12-month courses are reserved for unprovoked or recurrent events, not clearly provoked ones.

Chapter 11: Airway Management, Procedures, and Ophthalmology (43 Questions)

  1. Q399. Which mnemonic is most commonly used at the bedside to predict a potentially difficult airway before attempting intubation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (LEMON, the bedside mnemonic used to predict difficult airway anatomy): LEMON, standing for Look externally, Evaluate 3-3-2, Mallampati, Obstruction, and Neck mobility, is the standard bedside tool used to predict a difficult airway before intubation. SAMPLE is used for gathering a focused history, AVPU is a rapid consciousness assessment tool, and OPQRST is used to characterize a pain complaint, none of which assess airway anatomy.

  2. Q400. A hypotensive trauma patient requires rapid sequence intubation. Which induction agent choice is generally preferred for its relative hemodynamic stability in this situation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Ketamine or etomidate at an appropriately reduced induction dose): both ketamine and etomidate are favored in hypotensive patients because they generally preserve hemodynamic stability better than other induction agents when dosed appropriately for the clinical situation. Propofol and thiopental both carry a significant risk of worsening hypotension through vasodilation and myocardial depression, and high-dose midazolam alone also carries substantial hypotension risk in an unstable patient.

  3. Q401. What is the standard intravenous dose of succinylcholine used for rapid sequence intubation in an average adult patient?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (1 to 1.5 mg/kg intravenously, giving rapid onset and short paralysis): this is the standard weight-based dosing range for succinylcholine in rapid sequence intubation, producing rapid onset of paralysis with a short duration of action suited to emergency airway management. A dose of 0.1 mg/kg is far too low to achieve adequate paralysis for intubation, while 5 mg/kg and especially 10 mg/kg represent substantial overdosing with unnecessary toxicity risk.

  4. Q402. Which condition is a recognized contraindication to succinylcholine use during rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Chronic renal failure with associated baseline hyperkalemia): succinylcholine causes a transient rise in serum potassium, which can be dangerous or life-threatening in patients who already have baseline hyperkalemia from chronic renal failure. An isolated ankle fracture from earlier that day, mild well-controlled asthma, and well-controlled hypertension are not recognized contraindications to succinylcholine use.

  5. Q403. A patient cannot be intubated and cannot be oxygenated despite optimal positioning and rescue airway devices. Which intervention is now indicated?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Perform an emergency surgical cricothyroidotomy without further delay): a cannot-intubate-cannot-oxygenate scenario is a true airway emergency requiring immediate surgical airway access to prevent hypoxic arrest. Repeating the same failed technique, waiting for backup, or simply giving more paralytic and reattempting all delay definitive oxygenation in a situation where time is critical.

  6. Q404. Which pre-oxygenation strategy best reduces the risk of desaturation during the apneic phase of rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (High-flow nasal oxygen combined with a non-rebreather mask for several minutes): combining high-flow nasal oxygen with a tight-fitting non-rebreather mask for several minutes maximizes denitrogenation and extends the safe apneic window during intubation. A single deep breath or low-flow nasal cannula alone provides inadequate preoxygenation, and waiting to bag-mask ventilate only after paralysis has already been given misses the critical preoxygenation window entirely.

  7. Q405. Which clinical scenario is the best indication for awake intubation rather than standard rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A predicted difficult airway with an intact gag reflex and stable breathing): awake intubation is specifically indicated when a difficult airway is anticipated and the patient can maintain spontaneous ventilation and protective reflexes throughout the procedure, allowing a controlled approach if intubation proves difficult. A fully unresponsive cardiac arrest patient requires immediate rapid sequence or unassisted intubation, a normal airway exam favors standard rapid sequence intubation, and an uncooperative agitated patient who refuses assessment is a poor candidate for an awake technique.

  8. Q406. According to the failed airway algorithm, what is the recommended next step after one failed intubation attempt when bag-mask oxygenation is still possible?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Attempt an alternate device, such as video laryngoscopy or a supraglottic airway): the failed airway algorithm calls for switching technique or device after a failed attempt, provided oxygenation can still be maintained, before escalating further. Proceeding directly to a surgical airway is reserved for cannot-intubate-cannot-oxygenate situations, abandoning management entirely is unsafe, and giving only more sedation without changing technique does not address the underlying difficulty.

  9. Q407. Which component of the LEMON difficult airway assessment refers to direct visualization of the oropharyngeal structures on examination?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Mallampati classification, which grades the visible oropharyngeal structures): the Mallampati component of LEMON specifically involves visual inspection of the oropharynx to grade how much of the soft palate, uvula, and pillars are visible. The 3-3-2 rule assesses mouth opening and hyomental distance, neck mobility assesses flexion and extension, and the external look component screens for visible trauma or anatomic abnormality, none of which involve direct oropharyngeal visualization.

  10. Q408. What is the appropriate intubating dose of rocuronium used for rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (1.2 mg/kg intravenously, the standard dose for rapid sequence intubation): a dose of 1.2 mg/kg is the accepted intubating dose of rocuronium for rapid sequence intubation, producing intubating conditions within about 60 seconds. Doses of 0.1 and 0.3 mg/kg are well below what is needed for rapid onset paralysis, while 3 mg/kg substantially exceeds the recommended dose without added clinical benefit.

  11. Q409. In which patient population should ketamine be used with particular caution as an induction agent for rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Patients with severe hypertension or active psychosis worsened by sympathomimetic effects): ketamine's sympathomimetic properties can dangerously exacerbate severe hypertension and may worsen acute psychosis, so caution is warranted in these populations. Ketamine is actually often favored in hemodynamically unstable trauma patients and in actively wheezing asthmatics due to its bronchodilating and pressor-supporting properties, and it is not typically the agent of choice for awake intubation requiring preserved respiratory drive.

  12. Q410. What is the primary purpose of applying external laryngeal manipulation, such as the BURP maneuver, during direct laryngoscopy?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (To improve vocal cord visualization by repositioning the larynx): external laryngeal manipulation, including the BURP maneuver, is applied during direct laryngoscopy to reposition the larynx and bring the vocal cords into better view for successful tube passage. It does not reduce the paralytic dose required, does not itself prevent aspiration, and plays no role in confirming tube depth after placement.

  13. Q411. In a young child requiring an emergency surgical airway, which technique is generally favored over a standard surgical cricothyroidotomy given pediatric airway anatomy?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Needle cricothyroidotomy performed with jet ventilation): the pediatric cricothyroid membrane is small and the airway anatomy differs significantly from adults, making needle cricothyroidotomy with jet ventilation the generally favored emergency technique rather than a standard surgical incision. An adult-sized surgical technique risks significant injury to pediatric airway structures, bedside tracheostomy without surgical backup is not a standard emergency approach, and blind nasotracheal intubation is not an appropriate alternative in this emergency.

  14. Q412. Which method provides the most reliable confirmation of correct endotracheal tube placement immediately after intubation?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Continuous waveform capnography throughout transport and ongoing care): continuous waveform capnography is the most reliable confirmation method because it provides ongoing, real-time evidence of tracheal placement and continued ventilation, unlike a single-point check. Auscultation alone and visualizing chest rise can both be misleading in obese patients or with esophageal intubation, and a chest radiograph is too slow to serve as the immediate confirmation method.

  15. Q413. What is the purpose of apneic oxygenation using nasal cannula oxygen during the induction and paralysis phase of rapid sequence intubation?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (To extend the safe apnea time available before desaturation occurs): apneic oxygenation delivers passive oxygen flow during the period between induction and successful intubation, extending the time available before oxygen saturation begins to fall. It does not replace the need for standard preoxygenation beforehand, it does not provide any positive pressure ventilation, and it has no effect on the required dose of the induction agent.

  16. Q414. A 45-year-old requires procedural sedation for shoulder reduction. She had a light snack two hours earlier. According to current evidence, how should recent oral intake influence the decision to proceed with sedation in the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Recent intake alone should not delay urgent sedation if risk is carefully weighed): Evidence does not support strict fasting guidelines as a prerequisite for procedural sedation in the emergency department. The decision should balance urgency of the procedure, depth of sedation planned, and airway risk factors rather than applying a fixed fasting interval. Guidelines from emergency medicine bodies emphasize individualized risk assessment over rigid nil-by-mouth rules. Options A, C, and D describe rigid fasting-based rules unsupported by current practice.

  17. Q415. An otherwise healthy 30-year-old man needs procedural sedation for a complex laceration repair requiring several minutes of stillness. He has no cardiac history. Which single agent best provides deep, short-acting sedation with rapid recovery for this brief painful procedure?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Propofol titrated intravenously with close airway and respiratory monitoring): Propofol produces rapid onset, deep sedation and a fast recovery profile that suits brief painful procedures needing stillness. It requires careful titration and vigilant respiratory monitoring because it can cause apnea and hypotension without warning. Lorazepam and diazepam have slower onset and much longer recovery, making them poor choices for brief procedures. Morphine alone lacks the reliable sedative depth needed for a still, cooperative field.

  18. Q416. A 4-year-old child requires procedural sedation for fracture reduction. Which factor is most essential to calculate correctly before administering any sedative or analgesic medication in this pediatric patient?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Weight-based dosing calculated precisely from the child's current body weight): Pediatric sedation medications carry a narrow safety margin, and dosing errors are a leading cause of adverse events in children. Every agent chosen for procedural sedation in a pediatric patient must be dosed per kilogram using an accurately measured or estimated weight. Distraction techniques and route preference matter for comfort but do not substitute for correct dosing. Toileting timing has no bearing on drug safety in this context.

  19. Q417. During procedural sedation for a moderately deep dissociative state, which combination of monitoring is the minimum standard recommended to detect early respiratory compromise before clinical deterioration is visible?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Continuous pulse oximetry plus capnography with a dedicated monitoring provider): Capnography detects hypoventilation and apnea before oxygen desaturation becomes apparent on pulse oximetry, giving an earlier warning of respiratory depression. A provider dedicated solely to monitoring, separate from the person performing the procedure, is required to respond promptly. Blood pressure checks and temperature monitoring do not reliably detect the earliest signs of respiratory compromise. Visual observation alone is insufficient once deeper sedation levels are reached.

  20. Q418. A 6-year-old receives ketamine for procedural sedation before abscess incision and drainage. Shortly after the dose, the child becomes agitated and cries inconsolably despite adequate analgesia. Which ketamine-related phenomenon best explains this presentation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (An emergence reaction occurring as the dissociative effect wears off): Emergence reactions, including agitation, vivid dreams, or crying, are a recognized adverse effect of ketamine as its dissociative state resolves and are more common in older children and adults than young children. They typically respond to a calm quiet recovery environment or a small dose of benzodiazepine if severe. Anaphylaxis and malignant hyperthermia present with distinct systemic signs not described here. Opioid withdrawal is irrelevant since no opioid was given.

  21. Q419. A hypotensive trauma patient requires brief procedural sedation for a dislocation reduction. Which sedative agent is generally preferred over propofol in this hemodynamically unstable patient because it causes less blood pressure drop?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Etomidate, which maintains hemodynamic stability better than propofol in most patients): Etomidate has minimal effect on blood pressure and cardiac output, making it a favoured choice for brief sedation in hemodynamically fragile patients. Propofol commonly causes vasodilation and myocardial depression that can worsen existing hypotension, so increasing its dose would be dangerous. Diazepam has a slow onset unsuitable for rapid brief sedation. Morphine alone does not provide adequate sedation depth for a painful reduction.

  22. Q420. A patient becomes unresponsive with a respiratory rate of 4 per minute several minutes after receiving fentanyl and midazolam for procedural sedation. Bag-mask ventilation is started immediately. Which reversal agents should be prepared if ventilation does not promptly restore adequate breathing?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Naloxone and flumazenil, targeted to the opioid and benzodiazepine given): Naloxone reverses opioid-induced respiratory depression from fentanyl, while flumazenil reverses benzodiazepine effects from midazolam, and both should be readily available whenever these agents are combined for sedation. Airway support and ventilation always come first, but reversal agents are prepared for cases where basic maneuvers are insufficient. Activated charcoal has no role in reversing intravenous sedative effects. Sodium bicarbonate and N-acetylcysteine do not address this specific drug-induced respiratory depression.

  23. Q421. An elderly patient with multiple comorbidities and a full stomach needs analgesia for a hip fracture before transfer. Which approach is generally preferable to full procedural sedation in this higher-risk patient when feasible?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A regional nerve block providing analgesia while avoiding airway risk): Regional techniques such as a fascia iliaca or femoral nerve block can provide effective analgesia for hip fracture without the airway and cardiovascular risks of deep procedural sedation, which is especially valuable in elderly patients with comorbidities and aspiration risk. Deep sedation in a patient with a full stomach carries meaningful aspiration risk. General anesthesia is not typically performed at bedside by emergency physicians. Withholding analgesia is unacceptable and inconsistent with good patient care.

  24. Q422. During moderate procedural sedation, capnography shows a flattened waveform while the oxygen saturation remains normal for the moment. What does this finding most likely indicate and what should the team do immediately?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Early apnea or airway obstruction, prompting immediate stimulation and airway repositioning): A flattened capnography waveform signals reduced or absent exhaled carbon dioxide, an early sign of apnea or airway obstruction that precedes any drop in oxygen saturation, especially in a patient receiving supplemental oxygen. The team should immediately stimulate the patient, reposition the airway, and prepare to assist ventilation. Assuming normal status or a monitor problem ignores the value of capnography as an early warning tool. Giving more opioid would worsen respiratory depression.

  25. Q423. A patient has completed procedural sedation and the reduction is finished. Which finding is most essential before this patient can safely be discharged home from the emergency department?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Return to baseline mental status with stable vital signs over time): Safe discharge after procedural sedation requires the patient to return to their baseline level of consciousness and demonstrate stable vital signs and respiratory status over a period of observation, not just a single reading. A one-time blood pressure check or a single correct verbal response does not confirm sustained recovery from sedation. A family member's physical presence in the waiting room does not address the patient's clinical readiness. Ongoing observation is needed until recovery criteria are consistently met.

  26. Q424. A child develops sudden stridor and desaturation immediately after ketamine administration for procedural sedation, consistent with laryngospasm. What is the most appropriate initial management step?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Apply jaw thrust with positive pressure ventilation using firm technique): Laryngospasm during ketamine sedation is managed initially with airway maneuvers, including a firm jaw thrust and gentle positive pressure ventilation, which often breaks the spasm. If this fails, succinylcholine may be needed to relax the laryngeal muscles. Repeating ketamine does not treat the airway obstruction and could worsen instability. Waiting without intervening or giving oral fluids in an obstructed, desaturating airway is unsafe and inappropriate.

  27. Q425. A physician is selecting the intravenous ketamine dose for dissociative sedation in a school-aged child undergoing fracture reduction. Which dosing approach reflects safe, evidence-based practice for this indication?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A weight-based intravenous dose titrated to the desired dissociative endpoint): Ketamine for procedural sedation is dosed per kilogram of body weight and titrated intravenously to reach the dissociative state needed for the procedure, allowing smaller incremental doses if more sedation is required. Age alone is an unreliable proxy for the correct dose because children of the same age can vary widely in weight. Fixed adult dosing regardless of age risks significant overdose in children. An excessive bolus increases the risk of respiratory depression and emergence reactions.

  28. Q426. A physician plans to combine fentanyl and midazolam for procedural sedation in an opioid-naive adult. Compared with using either drug alone, what is the most important safety consideration with this combination?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Combined use increases the risk of synergistic respiratory depression significantly): Opioids and benzodiazepines given together produce a synergistic, rather than simply additive, depression of respiratory drive, meaning lower doses of each can still cause significant hypoventilation. This combination requires more vigilant monitoring and smaller incremental titrated doses than either drug used alone. The combination does not eliminate respiratory risk, and midazolam does not counteract fentanyl's respiratory depressant effect. The two drugs can be used together safely with appropriate caution, so option D is incorrect.

  29. Q427. A physician is titrating fentanyl and midazolam incrementally for procedural sedation in an adult. Which titration strategy best minimizes the risk of oversedation and respiratory depression?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Give small incremental doses, allowing time to assess effect between doses): Titrating sedative and analgesic medications in small incremental doses with adequate time between administrations allows the clinician to assess the patient's response and stop before oversedation occurs. Giving the full anticipated dose upfront or increasing doses too rapidly removes this safety margin and raises the risk of respiratory depression. Sequencing full doses of one drug before adding another does not address the core issue of titrating to effect. Careful incremental titration is the standard safe practice for combination sedation.

  30. Q428. A 60-year-old woman presents with sudden severe unilateral eye pain, headache, nausea, and blurred vision after watching a movie in a dark theatre. Examination shows a mid-dilated fixed pupil and a hard eye on palpation. Which diagnosis is most likely?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Acute angle closure glaucoma from pupillary block in a susceptible eye): The classic presentation of sudden severe eye pain, headache, nausea, a mid-dilated fixed pupil, and a firm globe in a dim-light setting strongly suggests acute angle closure glaucoma from pupillary block. Darkness causes physiologic pupil dilation, which can precipitate angle closure in a predisposed eye. Conjunctivitis and migraine do not typically produce a hard, tender globe with a fixed pupil. Chronic open angle glaucoma is painless and progresses gradually, unlike this acute presentation.

  31. Q429. A patient is diagnosed with acute angle closure glaucoma in the emergency department. Which combination of initial medical treatments is appropriate while urgent ophthalmology consultation is arranged?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Topical beta blocker, alpha agonist, and acetazolamide to lower pressure): Initial emergency management of acute angle closure glaucoma targets rapid reduction of intraocular pressure using topical agents such as a beta blocker and alpha agonist along with systemic acetazolamide, while definitive treatment with laser iridotomy is arranged urgently with ophthalmology. Topical antibiotics and oral antihistamines do not address elevated intraocular pressure and are not indicated. Patching the eye and routine follow-up would delay vision-threatening treatment inappropriately given the urgency of this diagnosis.

  32. Q430. A 70-year-old man reports sudden, painless, complete loss of vision in one eye over seconds. Fundoscopy reveals a pale retina with a cherry red spot at the macula. What is the most likely diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Central retinal artery occlusion causing acute painless monocular vision loss): Sudden, painless, profound monocular vision loss with a pale retina and cherry red macular spot on fundoscopy is the classic presentation of central retinal artery occlusion, an ocular stroke. Retinal detachment more typically causes a curtain or shadow effect with flashes and floaters rather than instantaneous complete loss. Optic neuritis usually causes pain with eye movement and more gradual vision changes. Vitreous hemorrhage presents with floaters and hazy vision, not the described fundoscopic finding.

  33. Q431. A patient presents 90 minutes after sudden painless monocular vision loss found to be central retinal artery occlusion. Regarding potential treatment, what should the emergency physician understand about the time-sensitive nature of this condition?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Irreversible retinal damage can occur within a very short time window): Retinal tissue is highly sensitive to ischemia, and irreversible damage in central retinal artery occlusion can begin within roughly 90 to 100 minutes of vessel occlusion, making rapid recognition and urgent ophthalmology involvement critical. Retinal tissue does not tolerate prolonged ischemia without damage, so option A is false. Treatment outcomes are time-dependent and not guaranteed regardless of delay, and spontaneous complete resolution without intervention is not the expected course.

  34. Q432. A 55-year-old myopic patient describes new floaters, flashes of light, and a curtain-like shadow moving across the peripheral vision of one eye over the past day. Which diagnosis should be strongly suspected?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Retinal detachment presenting with flashes, floaters, and a visual field curtain): New floaters, photopsia, and a curtain-like or shadow visual field defect, particularly in a myopic patient, is a classic presentation of retinal detachment and warrants urgent ophthalmology referral. Acute angle closure glaucoma typically presents with pain and a red, hard eye rather than painless flashes and floaters. Conjunctivitis and uveitis present with discharge, redness, or photophobia rather than this specific visual field pattern. Myopia is a recognized risk factor for retinal detachment.

  35. Q433. A patient with suspected retinal detachment reports that the shadow has now progressed to involve their central vision. What does this change in symptoms most likely indicate regarding urgency of referral?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Possible macular involvement, indicating a more urgent same-day ophthalmology referral): Progression of a visual field shadow into the central vision suggests the macula may now be involved, which significantly worsens visual prognosis and increases the urgency of same-day ophthalmology evaluation and surgical repair. This progression does not indicate resolution, stabilization, or an unrelated benign process. Recognizing macular threat versus macula-sparing detachment is a key factor in how urgently the patient must be seen and treated.

  36. Q434. Two patients present with chemical eye splashes, one from an acidic cleaning product and one from an alkaline drain cleaner. Which statement about the relative severity of these injuries is accurate?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Alkali burns typically cause deeper, more progressive tissue penetration than acids): Alkali substances cause liquefactive necrosis that allows continued penetration deeper into ocular tissues over time, generally producing more severe and progressive damage than acids, which cause coagulative necrosis that tends to limit further penetration. This makes alkali exposures particularly dangerous and requires urgent, prolonged irrigation. The two injury types are not identical in severity, and acids are not universally worse than alkalis. Both acid and alkali exposures require immediate copious irrigation regardless of type.

  37. Q435. A patient arrives with an alkaline chemical splash to the eye and irrigation has been started. What is the appropriate endpoint for stopping ocular irrigation in this situation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Irrigation should continue until the conjunctival pH is neutral and stable): The proper endpoint for ocular irrigation after a chemical injury is achieving and confirming a neutral, stable conjunctival pH on repeat testing, not a fixed volume, fixed time, or symptom-based endpoint. Some alkaline injuries require very large volumes and prolonged irrigation to neutralize fully. Stopping based on pain relief alone is unreliable because pain can decrease from anesthetic drops rather than actual neutralization. A fixed time or volume may be inadequate for a severe alkali burn.

  38. Q436. A patient with facial trauma develops proptosis, a tense and firm orbit, decreased visual acuity, and an afferent pupillary defect within an hour of injury. Which diagnosis should be urgently considered?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Orbital compartment syndrome from retrobulbar hemorrhage causing rising pressure): Proptosis, a tense firm orbit, decreasing vision, and a new afferent pupillary defect after trauma are hallmark findings of orbital compartment syndrome from retrobulbar hemorrhage, a true ocular emergency that threatens the optic nerve. Simple bruising or allergic swelling would not cause a tense orbit with worsening vision and pupillary defect. Chronic dry eye disease is unrelated to acute trauma and would not explain these findings. Rapid recognition is essential since permanent vision loss can occur within a short time.

  39. Q437. A patient is diagnosed with orbital compartment syndrome causing rapidly worsening vision after trauma. Which emergency procedure should be performed without delay to relieve pressure and preserve vision?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Lateral canthotomy and cantholysis performed at the bedside without delay): When orbital compartment syndrome is causing rapidly worsening vision, lateral canthotomy and cantholysis is the emergency bedside procedure that immediately decompresses the orbit and can be vision-saving, and it should not be delayed for imaging. A pressure patch would worsen the pressure inside the orbit and is contraindicated. Imaging can be helpful but should not delay this time-critical procedure when clinical findings are clear. Antibiotics do not address the mechanical compartment pressure causing vision loss.

  40. Q438. A patient with a corneal abrasion returns two days later with worsening pain, a white infiltrate visible on the cornea, and purulent discharge. Which feature most distinguishes a corneal ulcer from a simple corneal abrasion?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A visible corneal infiltrate suggesting infection rather than epithelial defect alone): A white or hazy corneal infiltrate, especially with purulent discharge and worsening pain, indicates an infectious corneal ulcer rather than a simple abrasion, which involves only epithelial disruption without infiltrate. Simple abrasions are typically painful, so pain alone does not distinguish the two conditions. A normal clear cornea would not be expected in either an infiltrate-bearing ulcer or most symptomatic abrasions. Ulcers do not resolve spontaneously within a day and require urgent ophthalmology evaluation and culture-directed treatment.

  41. Q439. A contact lens wearer presents with eye pain, redness, and a corneal infiltrate consistent with a bacterial corneal ulcer. Which aspect of this patient's history is the most significant risk factor for this diagnosis?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Extended or overnight contact lens wear promoting bacterial corneal infection): Extended or overnight contact lens wear is a well-established major risk factor for bacterial corneal ulcers, since it reduces corneal oxygenation and allows bacteria, notably Pseudomonas, to adhere and proliferate on the lens and corneal surface. Reading glasses use, migraine family history, and seasonal allergies are not recognized risk factors for infectious corneal ulcers. Identifying contact lens wear history is essential for guiding empiric antibiotic choice and patient counseling on lens hygiene.

  42. Q440. A patient presents to the emergency department with a red eye. Which associated finding should most strongly prompt urgent same-day ophthalmology referral rather than routine primary care follow-up?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Decreased visual acuity accompanied by significant pain and photophobia): Any red eye presenting with decreased vision, significant pain, and photophobia is a red flag combination suggesting a vision-threatening process such as uveitis, keratitis, or angle closure glaucoma, and warrants urgent same-day ophthalmology assessment. Mild watery discharge with normal vision and comfort, allergic bilateral itching, and an isolated subconjunctival hemorrhage are all benign presentations that can typically be managed conservatively or in primary care. Distinguishing benign from vision-threatening red eye presentations is a core emergency medicine skill.

  43. Q441. A patient presents after blunt eye trauma with blood visible layering in the anterior chamber, consistent with traumatic hyphema. Which management principle is most important in the initial emergency department care of this patient?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Head of bed elevation and eye shield with urgent ophthalmology involvement): Traumatic hyphema is managed with head of bed elevation to help blood settle and reduce intraocular pressure, protection with a rigid eye shield to prevent further trauma, and urgent ophthalmology involvement due to risk of rebleeding and elevated intraocular pressure. Firm pressure patching is contraindicated as it can increase intraocular pressure and worsen bleeding. Resuming normal activity risks rebleeding and is not recommended in the acute period. Aspirin and other agents affecting clotting should be avoided since they increase rebleeding risk.

Chapter 12: Systems-Based Practice and EMS (55 Questions)

  1. Q442. A paramedic crew requests real-time direction from a physician while managing a critically injured patient in the field. Which term best describes this form of medical direction?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (online medical control via direct radio or phone contact): online medical control refers to real time direction from a physician communicating directly with prehospital providers during an active call. This differs from offline control, which relies on pre-established standing orders and protocols. Option A describes offline control, and options C and D describe unrelated quality assurance and outreach activities.

  2. Q443. EMS is dispatched to a house where a patient collapsed after reports of a gas leak. Before any crew member enters the residence, what must occur first?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (scene safety must be confirmed by trained hazmat personnel): a key feature of prehospital medicine is that responder safety takes priority over immediate patient contact when an unconfirmed hazard such as a gas leak is present. Entering before the scene is declared safe risks additional casualties among providers. Options A, B, and D all involve premature entry or exposure of untrained people to an unassessed hazard.

  3. Q444. A stable patient with an isolated ankle fracture is found ten minutes from a community hospital. Which prehospital approach best reflects appropriate field management?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (load and go with splinting done during rapid transport): an isolated stable injury close to an appropriate facility favors rapid transport with brief field stabilization rather than prolonged on scene procedures. Stay and play approaches are reserved for situations where field intervention changes outcome or transport is delayed. Air transport is unnecessary for a short ground distance, and delaying transport for imaging contradicts efficient prehospital care.

  4. Q445. A trauma patient meets criteria for a designated trauma centre, but the nearest hospital is a small community site fifteen minutes away. What should guide the transport decision?

    Correct Answer: D
