    The correct answer is D (bypass protocols directing transport to the appropriate trauma centre): trauma triage criteria and regional bypass protocols exist so that patients meeting trauma centre criteria are transported to the facility best equipped to manage their injuries, even if it is not the closest hospital. Always defaulting to the nearest site can delay definitive care. Patient or family preference and waiting on scene for a trauma team are not appropriate substitutes for bypass protocols.

  5. Q446. During a prehospital cardiac arrest, the paramedic contacts the base hospital physician for a specific medication order not covered by standing protocol. This exchange is an example of what?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (online medical control providing a direct physician order): a real time conversation between the paramedic and the base hospital physician resulting in a specific order is the definition of online medical control. Offline protocol development happens before calls, interfacility transfer involves moving a patient between facilities, and community education is unrelated to this scenario.

  6. Q447. Which statement best describes offline medical control in a paramedic system?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (pre-approved standing orders and protocols guide routine field care): offline medical control consists of protocols and standing orders developed in advance that allow paramedics to act without needing live physician contact for routine situations. Option A describes online control instead. Options B and C misrepresent how offline protocols function in practice.

  7. Q448. A paramedic crew is performing resuscitation on a patient found in asystole after a witnessed arrest with prolonged downtime and no return of circulation despite full advanced care. What should guide the decision to stop?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (a validated field termination of resuscitation rule and medical control): termination of resuscitation in the field should be guided by validated clinical criteria such as witnessed status, bystander CPR, and rhythm, combined with medical control involvement rather than an arbitrary decision. Continuing until any hospital arrival ignores evidence based criteria, family wishes alone are not a clinical determinant, and prehospital termination is an accepted practice under proper protocols.

  8. Q449. A rural hospital arranges transfer of an unstable patient with a bleeding disorder to a tertiary centre. What is the most important priority before the transferring crew departs?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (adequate stabilization and monitoring appropriate for the transport): interfacility transfer of an unstable patient requires that the patient be sufficiently stabilized and monitored for the transport environment before departure to reduce risk during transit. Minimal documentation, prioritizing speed over stability, and deferring decisions to a distant accepting physician all compromise safe transfer practice.

  9. Q450. Police report an unstable structure with downed electrical lines near a patient who fell. What should the responding EMS crew do while awaiting confirmation the scene is safe?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (staging at a safe distance until scene safety is declared): when a hazard such as downed electrical lines is reported, crews must stage safely until the scene is confirmed secure, since patient care cannot begin at the cost of responder safety. Sending anyone in, regardless of experience, or brief entry despite the hazard both risk additional injury. Directing bystanders to handle live electrical hazards is unsafe and inappropriate.

  10. Q451. A young patient involved in a low speed collision is alert, has no midline neck tenderness, no distracting injury, and a normal neurologic exam. What does selective spinal immobilization protocol suggest?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (clinical clearance without immobilization may be appropriate here): selective spinal immobilization protocols allow clinical clearance in low risk patients who are alert, have no midline tenderness, no distracting injury, and a normal neurologic exam. Mandatory immobilization for every collision ignores validated clinical decision rules. A collar alone without full assessment and reliance solely on vehicle damage are not appropriate approaches.

  11. Q452. During prehospital management of a cardiac arrest, the paramedic considers reversible causes while performing resuscitation. Which action reflects this key feature of prehospital arrest care?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (assessing for hypoxia, hypovolemia, and other treatable causes): a key feature of prehospital arrest management is actively considering and addressing reversible causes during resuscitation, since correcting them can change outcome. Assuming a single universal cause, withholding airway management outright, and delaying family notification unnecessarily do not reflect appropriate prehospital arrest care.

  12. Q453. A patient was ejected from a vehicle during a rollover collision but currently has normal vital signs and no obvious injury on exam. How should mechanism of injury influence field triage?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (high-risk mechanism should still prompt trauma centre transport): mechanism of injury criteria, such as ejection from a vehicle, remain an important trigger for trauma centre transport even when initial vital signs and exam appear reassuring, since serious injury can be occult. Relying on normal vitals alone or releasing the patient at the scene risks missing significant injury, and ignoring mechanism entirely contradicts trauma triage principles.

  13. Q454. Which situation most strongly supports activating air medical transport rather than ground transport?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (long ground transport time to definitive care for a critical patient): air medical transport is generally indicated when ground transport time to definitive care would be excessively long for a critically ill or injured patient. A stable minor injury near a hospital, favorable ground conditions, and patient preference for comfort are not appropriate indications for helicopter activation.

  14. Q455. A paramedic crew arrives at the emergency department with a critically ill patient. What is the most appropriate way to hand over care to the receiving team?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (a structured verbal report covering history, findings, and treatment given): an organized verbal handover ensures the receiving team receives essential clinical information promptly and accurately during a critical transition of care. A brief unstructured comment, delaying communication until documentation is typed, and assuming the chart alone suffices all risk losing important information during handover.

  15. Q456. At a multi-vehicle collision, a responder uses START triage and identifies a patient who is walking and following commands with no obvious major injury. What triage category applies?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (minor, often labeled walking wounded or green): under START triage, patients who can walk and follow commands without major injury are categorized as minor or green, indicating lowest treatment priority relative to other categories. Immediate and delayed categories apply to patients with more significant physiologic compromise, and expectant applies to patients unlikely to survive given resource limits, none of which fit this patient.

  16. Q457. A rescuer triaging a pediatric patient at a mass casualty scene uses JumpSTART instead of adult START. What is the key reason a separate pediatric algorithm exists?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (children's baseline vital signs and responses differ from adults): JumpSTART was developed because pediatric physiologic parameters, including normal respiratory rate ranges, differ meaningfully from adult norms used in standard START triage. Assuming identical norms would misclassify many children. Automatic expectant categorization and removing respiratory assessment both misrepresent how JumpSTART actually functions.

  17. Q458. During a mass casualty response, a patient has a respiratory rate over thirty per minute after airway repositioning. Under START triage, what category should this patient receive?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (immediate, tagged red for the highest treatment priority): under START triage, a respiratory rate above thirty per minute after airway repositioning places a patient in the immediate category due to significant physiologic compromise. Minor and delayed categories require far more stable respiratory status, and expectant is reserved for patients unlikely to survive given available resources rather than simply an elevated respiratory rate.

  18. Q459. Multiple agencies respond to a large scale disaster involving fire, police, and EMS services. What structure should coordinate these responders to maintain organized command?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (an incident command system with unified command coordination): a formal incident command system with unified command allows multiple responding agencies to coordinate resources and communication effectively during a large scale disaster. Independent agency action or permanent single agency control undermines coordinated response, and waiting until hospital arrival to make decisions ignores the need for real time scene coordination.

  19. Q460. A hospital receiving a large influx of casualties finds its resources becoming overwhelmed. What surge strategy should the receiving team apply to reallocate limited resources?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (continuous re-triage and reverse triage as conditions evolve): under surge conditions, ongoing reassessment and reverse triage allow limited resources to be redirected toward patients most likely to benefit as the situation changes. Treating strictly in arrival order, suspending triage, or allocating based on insurance status do not reflect appropriate disaster resource allocation principles.

  20. Q461. Responders arrive at a mass casualty scene involving an unknown chemical exposure. Before treating any patient, what should the team prioritize regarding their own safety?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (appropriate staging and protective equipment before entering the hazard zone): personal and team safety must be addressed first at a scene involving an unknown chemical exposure, including proper staging and protective equipment, before any patient contact occurs. Immediate treatment without assessing risk, sending untrained volunteers in, or ignoring protection all expose responders to unnecessary harm.

  21. Q462. In a disaster with overwhelming casualties and severely limited resources, a patient has catastrophic injuries unlikely to survive even with maximal intervention. Which triage category applies under these constrained conditions?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (expectant, reflecting resource limitations in this disaster): the expectant category is used in mass casualty situations with overwhelming demand and limited resources for patients whose injuries make survival unlikely even with maximal intervention, allowing resources to be directed elsewhere. Immediate and delayed categories assume a reasonable chance of survival with treatment, and minor clearly does not fit catastrophic injuries.

  22. Q463. A patient at a mass casualty incident has a stable but significant injury that can safely wait without immediate deterioration. Under standard triage tag colour coding, which colour is assigned?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (yellow, representing the delayed treatment priority level): a stable but significant injury that can safely wait for treatment without rapid deterioration is tagged yellow under standard triage colour coding, reflecting the delayed category. Red is reserved for immediate life threats, black for deceased or expectant patients, and green for minor walking wounded, none of which match this presentation.

  23. Q464. A regional health authority develops stockpiles, training exercises, and communication plans months before any disaster occurs. Which phase of disaster management does this activity represent?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (preparedness phase occurring well before any incident begins): building stockpiles, running training exercises, and developing communication plans in advance of any event are hallmark activities of the preparedness phase of disaster management. The response phase occurs during the active incident, recovery occurs afterward, and no phase can eliminate all future disaster risk entirely.

  24. Q465. Casualties from a hazardous materials incident arrive at a treatment area still contaminated with an unknown substance. What must occur before definitive medical treatment begins?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (decontamination must occur before treatment to protect patients and staff): contaminated casualties from a hazardous materials incident require decontamination before definitive treatment to prevent secondary contamination of treatment areas and staff. Proceeding directly to treatment, delaying decontamination until hospital arrival, or ignoring contamination status all create serious safety risks for patients and providers.

  25. Q466. A community hospital activates its mass casualty plan after a building collapse produces far more patients than normal daily volume. What best describes this operational challenge?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (a surge capacity challenge requiring expanded resource mobilization): a sudden influx of patients exceeding normal daily volume represents a surge capacity challenge requiring mobilization of additional staff, space, and supplies beyond routine operations. Treating it as routine volume, assuming more physicians alone solve the problem, or limiting the issue to prehospital personnel all understate the scope of a true surge event.

  26. Q467. During an ongoing mass casualty response, a triage officer walks back through the treatment area checking previously tagged patients. Why is this ongoing reassessment necessary?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (patient status can change and initial tags may need updating): continuous reassessment during a mass casualty response is necessary because patient conditions can improve or deteriorate over time, requiring triage tags to be updated accordingly. Assuming tags never need review, limiting reassessment only to minor patients, or ending the triage officer's role after initial tagging all risk missing clinically important changes.

  27. Q468. Multiple responding agencies at a disaster use different radio codes and terminology, creating confusion during patient handoffs. What principle of disaster management addresses this problem?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (standardized common terminology and communication across all agencies): using standardized common terminology across responding agencies reduces confusion and improves coordination during multi-agency disaster response. Allowing agencies to keep separate terminology, accepting confusion as unavoidable, or limiting understanding to only the incident commander all undermine effective communication during a mass casualty event.

  28. Q469. Survivors of a mass casualty event show signs of acute distress but no physical injury. Which early intervention is most appropriate for this group at the scene?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (psychological first aid focused on safety and practical support): psychological first aid emphasizing safety, comfort, and practical support is the appropriate early intervention for uninjured survivors showing acute distress after a mass casualty event. Formal long-term psychotherapy is not appropriate at the scene, withholding any support ignores real psychological needs, and routine sedation is not an appropriate or evidence based intervention.

  29. Q470. An ED morbidity review finds a delayed troponin turnaround contributed to a missed MI in a middle aged patient discharged home who returned in cardiac arrest hours later requiring resuscitation. What is the most appropriate next step for the department to take?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Conduct a systems based root cause analysis with lab and ED staff): a missed diagnosis linked to a delayed troponin turnaround reflects a process and systems failure, not simply an individual lapse. Root cause analysis examines the entire chain of events, from ordering to result reporting to communication, to identify fixable contributing factors. Blaming a single physician ignores the systemic gaps that allowed the delay to occur and to go unnoticed. Mandating universal repeat troponins does not address the underlying turnaround time problem and adds unnecessary testing for low risk patients.

  30. Q471. A busy triage nurse catches a wrong patient wristband scan just before an intravenous medication is administered during a hallway bed shortage, preventing harm. No dose was given and the patient was completely unaffected. How should this near miss event be handled by the department?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Submit a formal near miss report to trigger a systems review): near misses are valuable safety data because they reveal system vulnerabilities before harm actually occurs. Formal reporting allows the department to identify why the wristband scan nearly failed and to fix the underlying process. Treating this as a personal lapse rather than a systems signal discourages future reporting and misses an opportunity for prevention. Ignoring the event or focusing on individual blame is inconsistent with a proactive patient safety culture.

  31. Q472. An emergency physician chairing monthly morbidity and mortality rounds wants staff at every level of training to report adverse events honestly rather than concealing them out of fear of punishment. Which approach to running these rounds would best build that kind of culture over time?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Frame discussion around systems factors rather than individual blame): morbidity and mortality rounds are most effective at improving safety when they explore contributing systems factors rather than singling out individuals for public criticism. A blame focused approach discourages honest disclosure and undermines trust in the review process. Restricting review to only bad outcome cases or making attendance optional both reduce the educational and systemic value of the rounds. Naming individuals publicly reinforces a punitive rather than learning oriented culture.

  32. Q473. A quality audit shows the department's average door to needle time for stroke thrombolysis exceeds the national benchmark by nearly twenty minutes. The improvement team wants to test a proposed workflow change before rolling it out to the entire department. What method best fits this stated goal?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (Run a small scale Plan Do Study Act cycle first): PDSA cycles allow a proposed workflow change to be tested on a small scale, measured, and refined before wider implementation. This iterative approach reduces the risk of unintended consequences from a large scale rollout of an untested change. Waiting a full year delays needed improvement without adding useful information, and implementing hospital wide without testing risks spreading a flawed intervention. Staff opinion surveys alone do not measure the actual impact on the outcome of interest.

  33. Q474. An ED's central line associated bloodstream infection rate is found to be well above the accepted national benchmark during a routine quarterly quality audit conducted by infection control. What is the most appropriate first step in addressing this specific finding?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Compare local insertion practices against validated benchmark checklist data): when a rate exceeds an external benchmark, the appropriate first step is to examine local processes, such as insertion checklist adherence and maintenance bundle compliance, against established evidence based standards. This identifies specific, correctable gaps rather than assuming individual fault. Stopping insertions entirely is impractical and denies necessary care, and increasing prophylactic antibiotics empirically is not a proven infection prevention strategy for central lines.

  34. Q475. A critical potassium result was not communicated during overnight shift change, and the incoming physician did not learn of it until the patient deteriorated. A review traces the failure to inconsistent verbal handover practices. What change addresses the root cause?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Implement a standardized structured handoff tool with closed loop verification): inconsistent verbal handover is a well recognized cause of missed critical information at shift change. Structured tools with closed loop verification, where the receiving clinician repeats back key information, directly address this root cause. Reducing the number of handoffs or eliminating verbal communication entirely does not fix the underlying communication process and may introduce new gaps. Disciplining the receiving physician ignores that the system itself failed to reliably transmit the result.

  35. Q476. A medication error in the ED resulted in a patient receiving ten times the intended dose of an anticoagulant, causing a significant bleeding complication that required urgent transfusion and intervention. According to established open disclosure principles, what should the treating team do next?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Disclose the error to the patient promptly and apologize openly): open disclosure principles require timely, honest communication with patients who experience harm from a medical error, along with a sincere apology. Delaying disclosure until a review is complete or withholding information from the patient directly violates these principles and can further damage trust. Concealing errors to avoid litigation is both ethically inappropriate and inconsistent with a genuine patient safety culture.

  36. Q477. During a root cause analysis of a delayed sepsis diagnosis, the reviewing team uses the five whys technique to trace the failure back through triage, laboratory ordering, and results reporting steps. What best describes the actual purpose of this technique?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (It is iterative questioning used to trace the underlying systemic root cause): the five whys technique repeatedly asks why an event occurred to move past the immediate proximate cause and uncover deeper systemic contributing factors. It is a root cause analysis tool intended for quality improvement, not a mechanism for assigning individual blame or a substitute for legal proceedings. It complements rather than replaces incident reporting systems, which capture the events that trigger this type of analysis.

  37. Q478. An ED introduces a new procedural sedation safety checklist after two serious adverse events during pediatric sedations. A follow up audit three months later shows only forty percent compliance among staff. What should the quality improvement team do next?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Investigate barriers to use and redesign implementation with staff input): low compliance with a new safety checklist usually reflects implementation barriers such as workflow disruption or unclear instructions rather than staff carelessness. Engaging frontline staff to identify and address these barriers improves both usability and buy in. Discarding the checklist abandons a potentially valuable safety tool prematurely, and disciplining staff or arbitrarily shortening the checklist does not address the actual reasons for poor adherence.

  38. Q479. A department is designing a new quality improvement project targeting earlier recognition and treatment of sepsis after several delayed cases were identified. Which measure would best serve as the process measure to track improvement over time?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Time from sepsis recognition to first antibiotic dose administered): a process measure tracks a specific step in the care pathway that is directly linked to the outcome being targeted, making time to antibiotics an appropriate process measure for a sepsis improvement project. Total visit volume and unrelated length of stay do not reflect the sepsis care process itself. Patient satisfaction is a valuable but separate outcome that does not specifically measure timeliness of sepsis treatment.

  39. Q480. Incident reports filed over several months reveal a recurring pattern of medication reconciliation errors at admission, involving multiple different physicians working across different shifts and days of the week. What conclusion should the quality committee draw from this recurring pattern?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Recognize a systemic pattern requiring a standardized reconciliation process, not retraining alone): when errors recur across multiple different individuals, the pattern points to a systems level gap rather than isolated individual failures. Addressing this requires standardizing the reconciliation process itself, such as through structured tools or pharmacist involvement, rather than relying on retraining single clinicians. Treating each case as isolated or increasing discipline fails to address the shared underlying process problem that is generating the errors.

  40. Q481. A department is revising its incident reporting policy after years of low reporting rates and wants to adopt a just culture model rather than a purely punitive one going forward. What best distinguishes a just culture approach to patient safety events?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (It distinguishes human error and system flaws from reckless behaviour): a just culture framework holds individuals accountable for reckless or intentional misconduct while recognizing that most errors arise from human factors and system design flaws that deserve a supportive, learning oriented response. This is distinct from a purely punitive culture, which tends to suppress honest reporting rather than improve it. Just culture principles apply across all staff roles, not only to nursing, and accountability is not eliminated altogether.

  41. Q482. A quality review identifies a cluster of missed subtle fractures on overnight plain films that were initially read only by emergency physicians before formal radiology review the next morning. What system level change would best reduce this recurring pattern?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Establish a formal radiology overread process within twenty four hours): a recurring pattern of missed fractures on physician interpreted overnight films is best addressed through a system level safeguard, such as timely formal radiology overread, that catches discrepancies before they affect patient outcomes. Discontinuing overnight imaging denies necessary diagnostic care, and focusing on individual miss rates ignores that interpretation error rates are an expected part of practice that systems should be designed to catch. Increasing image resolution alone does not address interpretation accuracy.

  42. Q483. An emergency department director wants to know whether the department's wait times and quality indicators are actually competitive compared to similar departments elsewhere in the province. What is the most appropriate benchmarking strategy to answer this question?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Benchmark against a validated provincial emergency department quality indicator dataset): meaningful benchmarking requires comparison against standardized, validated external data across multiple similar departments, allowing for a fair assessment of relative performance. Comparing only to one's own historical data or a single anecdotal peer hospital does not provide a reliable external reference point. Declining to benchmark at all abandons a valuable tool for identifying improvement opportunities, since validated indicators do account for relevant differences between departments.

  43. Q484. A three year old with a minor head injury has a normal mental status, no loss of consciousness, and no palpable skull fracture. The PECARN rule places her in a group with less than 0.02 percent risk of clinically important traumatic brain injury. How should this evidence be applied?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (Apply PECARN to support shared decision making about observation versus CT): the PECARN rule was derived and validated to identify children at very low risk of clinically important traumatic brain injury who can safely avoid CT, and a risk this low supports a shared discussion about observation instead of imaging. Ordering a CT regardless ignores the value of a validated low risk stratification tool, and defaulting to admission is unnecessary for a child at such low measured risk. The rule has been validated across many pediatric populations rather than only its original one.

  44. Q485. A new high sensitivity troponin assay reports 95 percent sensitivity for acute myocardial infarction measured at ED presentation. A low risk chest pain patient with a normal ECG tests negative on this assay. How should this specific result be interpreted at the bedside?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (A negative highly sensitive test at low pretest probability helps rule out MI): a highly sensitive test, when negative in a patient with low pretest probability, meaningfully lowers the post-test probability of disease, consistent with the SnNout principle. Sensitivity and specificity are independent test characteristics, so high sensitivity does not imply high specificity. The false positive rate is determined by specificity, not the sensitivity figure given, so option D describes the wrong parameter entirely.

  45. Q486. A large randomized trial reports that a preventive medication has a number needed to treat of 50 to avoid one stroke over five years of continuous use. A patient in the ED asks what this statistic actually means for their own care. What is the correct interpretation?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Fifty patients must be treated for five years to prevent one stroke): the number needed to treat represents how many patients require treatment over the specified time period for one additional patient to experience the benefit, in this case one stroke prevented. It does not mean a fixed proportion of patients will be harmed, nor does a number needed to treat of 50 indicate that the drug is inherently unsafe. This statistic is calculated directly from the absolute risk reduction observed in the trial data.

  46. Q487. A statin trial reports a 50 percent relative risk reduction in myocardial infarction, but the baseline absolute risk in the control group was only 2 percent over the study period. How should a physician explain the actual benefit to a patient?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (The absolute risk reduction is only about 1 percent for this patient): a 50 percent relative risk reduction applied to a 2 percent baseline absolute risk yields an absolute risk reduction of only about 1 percent, which is the figure most relevant to an individual patient's actual benefit. Relative risk reduction alone can overstate clinical significance when baseline risk is low, and it is not true that absolute risk reduction is always larger than relative risk reduction, since the reverse is typically the case.

  47. Q488. A cohort study reports an odds ratio of 1.8 with a 95 percent confidence interval of 0.9 to 3.2 for an association between a drug exposure and an adverse outcome. How should a physician interpret this specific result when counseling a patient?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (The interval crossing one means the finding is not statistically significant): when a 95 percent confidence interval for an odds ratio includes the value one, the association is not statistically significant at the conventional threshold, regardless of the point estimate itself. This does not prove the association is definitely false, only that chance cannot be excluded as an explanation. A wider confidence interval reflects less precision, typically from a smaller sample or more variable data, not greater precision.

  48. Q489. A young otherwise healthy patient has a low pretest probability Wells score for pulmonary embolism and a subsequently negative D-dimer test result. The physician is deciding whether further imaging is required before discharge home. What does the evidence support in this specific situation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (A negative D-dimer in this low risk patient effectively rules out PE): in a patient with a low pretest probability Wells score, a negative D-dimer has been validated to safely exclude pulmonary embolism without further imaging, avoiding unnecessary CT pulmonary angiography and its associated radiation and contrast risks. D-dimer testing is specifically useful in this low risk group, and empiric anticoagulation without confirmed diagnosis is not supported by this combination of findings.

  49. Q490. A retrospective cohort study concludes that a new sepsis bundle reduced overall mortality, but patients were not randomized to receive either the bundle or usual care during the study period. What limitation should a physician consider when appraising this specific claim?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (Lack of randomization means residual confounding may explain part of the benefit): without randomization, patients who received the sepsis bundle may have differed systematically from those who received usual care in ways that independently affected mortality, so confounding cannot be excluded as an alternative explanation for the observed benefit. Retrospective design does not eliminate confounding, and retrospective studies are not inherently superior to randomized trials for establishing causal effects on mortality. Cohort studies of this type can still report relative risk estimates.

  50. Q491. A clinical finding on physical exam is reported to have a positive likelihood ratio of 10 for a particular diagnosis. A physician elicits this finding on a patient with intermediate pretest probability. What does this likelihood ratio tell the physician?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (A positive result substantially increases the post test probability of disease): a positive likelihood ratio of 10 is considered large and, when applied to an intermediate pretest probability, meaningfully shifts the post-test probability toward the diagnosis being considered. A likelihood ratio does not directly represent sensitivity, and a likelihood ratio near one would indicate the finding is not diagnostically useful, the opposite of what is described. Positive likelihood ratios apply specifically to positive test results, not negative ones.

  51. Q492. The Ottawa ankle rule has near 100 percent sensitivity for clinically important fracture in the adult population it was originally validated in. An ED physician wants to use this rule to reduce unnecessary ankle radiographs in eligible patients. How should this sensitivity figure be applied clinically?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (High sensitivity supports using the rule to safely rule out fracture and avoid imaging): a clinical decision rule with very high sensitivity for a serious outcome is best used to rule out that outcome when the rule is negative, since few true cases will be missed. High sensitivity does not support ruling in a diagnosis, that role belongs to high specificity, and applying the rule against clear clinical judgment when it conflicts with exam findings undermines the intended use of the tool.

  52. Q493. A pharmacovigilance study reports a number needed to harm of 20 for a serious bleeding complication associated with a new anticoagulant. A physician is explaining this statistic to a patient before prescribing. What is the correct interpretation?

    Correct Answer: D

    The correct answer is D (For every 20 patients treated, one experiences the attributable adverse outcome): the number needed to harm expresses how many patients must be exposed to a treatment for one additional patient to experience the adverse outcome attributable to that treatment. A lower number needed to harm, such as 20 compared to 200, actually indicates greater potential harm, not greater safety. The statistic can be meaningfully compared alongside number needed to treat when weighing benefits against risks, and it is not restricted to fatal outcomes.

  53. Q494. A single center case series describes eight patients with an unusual presentation of a rare emergency diagnosis and proposes a new treatment approach based on their outcomes. How should a physician appraise this type of evidence before changing practice?

    Correct Answer: B

    The correct answer is B (A case series generates hypotheses but has significant inherent limitations): a case series describes outcomes in a small group of patients without a comparison group, making it useful for generating hypotheses but unable to establish causal treatment relationships on its own. It sits low on the hierarchy of evidence, not at the top, and findings from a small case series should be corroborated by controlled studies before being adopted into practice guidelines.

  54. Q495. A clinical decision rule was derived and externally validated in an adult population at a single tertiary center. A physician is considering applying it to a pediatric patient at a rural community ED. What should guide this decision?

    Correct Answer: A

    The correct answer is A (Consider whether the derivation and validation population resembles the current patient): applying any clinical decision rule requires assessing whether the current patient reasonably matches the population in which the rule was derived and validated, since performance can differ meaningfully across age groups and clinical settings. Applying a rule without regard to population differences risks inaccurate risk stratification, and rules validated only in adults cannot be assumed to generalize automatically to pediatric patients.

  55. Q496. A meta-analysis pools results from several small trials of differing design and reports a high I squared statistic indicating substantial heterogeneity between them. How should a physician interpret the pooled effect estimate from this analysis?

    Correct Answer: C

    The correct answer is C (High heterogeneity suggests caution interpreting the pooled effect estimate given trial differences): a high I squared statistic indicates that the variability between trial results is unlikely to be due to chance alone, meaning the pooled estimate may mask meaningful differences in study populations, interventions, or methods. This should prompt caution rather than added confidence in the combined result. Heterogeneity remains relevant regardless of overall sample size, and it should be explored rather than disregarded when interpreting a meta-analysis.
